Sources

The registry of sources the research entries actually cite. It records what was consulted and written down - it is not a verified bibliography, and nothing may be added here that has not genuinely been consulted. Where an entry names no source it says so, rather than borrowing one.

Works cited

kitamoto-mushiro-niwa

北本市史 民俗編, the farm yard measured in straw mats (https://kdas.jp/test2/detail_display.php?t_cd=1&acc_cd=3&aclc_cd=0&chap=6&hp_page=74&bc_cd=1)

What it is: A page of 北本市史 民俗編, the folklore volume of the published city history of Kitamoto in Saitama prefecture, read in that city's own digital archive. The passage records local farming custom, among it how big a farmhouse's work yard was, counted in the straw mats that could be laid out on it, with the conversion of two mats to one tsubo.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the one source we could read that states a farm work yard's size directly rather than leaving it to be inferred from a house plan, and it adds the reason the size varies - the yard tracked how much grain the household had to thresh and dry - which is why our yards scale with the holding. Its limits are era and place: a twentieth-century folklore volume recording remembered practice in one district of the Kanto plain, whose crop was barley, and a remembered "usually forty to sixty mats" is the range of a custom rather than a survey measurement.

Used for: the one directly-stated work-yard size - 「Ordinarily forty to sixty mats; among them there were also houses that went past a hundred mats. Incidentally, two mushiro (straw mats) come to one tsubo.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「普通四〇〜六〇枚、中には百枚を越す家もあった。ちなみに、ムシロ二枚が一坪にあたる」) = 20-30 tsubo (66-99 sq m) ordinarily, past 50 tsubo for a few; and that the yard tracks the household's cropped acreage (a barley district)

kodaira-niwa

小平市史, the 回田新田 farmstead's measured yard (https://adeac.jp/kodaira-lib/text-list/d100010/ht002170)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the one directly measured yard - 約七十坪 (231 sq m) of swept working ground inside a 700-tsubo homestead: the upper end of the range, a large Musashino dry-field holding

yonekura-mushiro

Yonekura district museum note, Okayama, on the straw mats a farm kept (https://townweb.e-okayamacity.jp/yonegura/minna/hakubutukan/hakubutu012.htm)

What it is: An exhibit page from the 米倉町内博物館, a small neighborhood museum of old farm, fishing and household tools kept by residents of the Yonekura district of Okayama City and published on the city's community web pages. The page describes the mushiro, the woven rice-straw mat - how it was made on a mat loom, its size, and what a farm did with it. No author or date is given.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for a single household number, that a farm kept about fifty straw mats, which matters because mats spread out to sun-dry grain need floor space and a work yard has to be drawn large enough to hold them. Its value is that it is an independent local account agreeing with a count recorded elsewhere; its limits are what kind of thing it is - a community museum's note written from local memory rather than a survey - and how narrow it is, speaking for one district of Okayama and for the last farming generations before machines took the work over.

Used for: ~50 mats per farm - the independent second line that agrees with Kitamoto's count

tobunken-mushiro

今石みぎわ, むしろ織り, Tokyo National Research Institute for Cultural Properties (https://tobunken.repo.nii.ac.jp/record/3166/files/06_55_Imaishi.pdf)

What it is: 今石みぎわ (Imaishi Migiwa), "莚と莚織りの技術" (The straw mat and the technique of mat weaving), a research report in the National Research Institute for Cultural Properties, Tokyo's series on intangible cultural heritage, 2012, freely downloadable. Half of it records the craft as it survived in a three-household hamlet in Yamaguchi, measured and photographed from 2010; the other half is a documentary study of mat looms in Edo-period sources - the Kaga agricultural treatise of 1707, the illustrated encyclopedia of 1713, a craftsmen's picture scroll and a farmhouse survey of about 1830 - and of the Meiji machines that replaced them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the measured size of the ordinary straw mat - about three shaku by six, 90 by 180 cm - which is what turns a count of mats in a period document into an area on our maps. Its limits are that the measurement is of a present-day weaving of a craft that was once universal, so it is a modern measurement of a premodern form; the same paper, though, carries the premodern corroboration - the 1707 Kaga treatise it quotes gives the same three-shaku width, and the paper's point is that the loom was essentially unchanged from at least the early 1700s - so the figure is better founded than a single village's practice would be. The one size variation it reports is the Ryukyu rush mat at three shaku two sun.

Used for: the mat's measured size - 幅3尺 x 長6尺 (90 x 180 cm, 1.62 sq m) - which converts a mat count into an area

kamikanai-1771-houses

上金井村 (Meiwa 8, 1771) main-house and holding histograms, 日本建築学会論文報告集 418 (https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/aijax/418/0/418_KJ00004075360/_pdf)

What it is: 津田良樹 (Tsuda Yoshiki), a peer-reviewed paper in the Architectural Institute of Japan's journal of planning (1990), on the size and plan of Edo-period farmhouses as recorded in the billeting documents made when the shogun's procession to Nikko was lodged along the road: five villages in Shimotsuke across three periods, about thirty houses each, with the houses' sizes read from the procession records and, for Kamikanai in 1771, each household's assessed holding from the village's tax ledger beside them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the shape of the whole house-size distribution of a village, from a documentary base that counts the small and the poor whom surviving buildings leave out, which is what our farmhouse sizes are spread by. Its limits are that these are highway villages, chosen because they lodged the shogunal procession, not a random draw; that the coupling of house size to holding - about ten tsubo more for five koku more - is stated for Iwahara in the Kyoho period, while at Kamikanai the paper finds the relation not marked, with sizable houses among the landless, so the two findings must be kept apart as the research page keeps them; and that it is one province of eastern Japan.

Used for: the SHAPE of the distribution - 31 commoner main houses fitting a lognormal of median 22.5 tsubo, sigma_ln 0.46, the headman detached at 3.1x; and the ADDITIVE coupling to holding size (5 koku more buys about 10 tsubo more)

kikoba-kenchi

木古庭村 (Miura, Sagami) Bunroku 1594 and Genroku 1699 検地帳, 日本建築学会計画系論文集 222 (https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/aijsaxx/222/0/222_KJ00003747888/_pdf)

What it is: A 1974 research paper in the Transactions of the Architectural Institute of Japan (number 222), the second part of a study called "近世村方文書よりみた相模国三浦郡木古庭村の屋敷と家屋" ("The homesteads and houses of Kikoba village, Miura district, Sagami province, seen through early-modern village documents"). It works through that one village's own paperwork, including a 1594 land survey and a homestead-area register submitted to the magistrate in 1691, to reconstruct how large peasant house plots and houses were and who held them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the one thing a homestead layout most needs and can least often find - the measured spread of house-lot areas across a whole village, from a register of 1699 - so the sizes and the spread of the plots on our maps rest on it; it also shows that the small end of the distribution was occupied, since every household in the village owned a main house. Its limits are that it is one village of 58 households in one province, so the spread is a pattern rather than a national rate; that the lots run from about 20 to 100 bu with peaks at 30 and 50, and the paper gives no separate figure for the great holders' lots - its "twice" and "three times" count buildings per household, not lot area - so any larger lot on our maps is our reading and not its measurement; and that the public copy is a scanned Japanese paper we read for its tables and its stated figures.

Used for: homestead-lot areas 20-100 bu with peaks at 30 and 50 - the right-skew corroboration (the paper's "2x" counts buildings per household, not lot area, so it gives no separate figure for great holders); and that by Genroku every peasant held a homestead, so the small end is occupied rather than empty

santome-shinden-allotment

三富新田 (Genroku 9, 1696) founding allotment, 日本建築学会論文報告集 436 (https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/aijax/436/0/436_KJ00004077200/_pdf)

What it is: A 1992 peer-reviewed paper by Yoshiro Yamamori in the Journal of the Architectural Institute of Japan, Planning Series, no. 436, on the row settlements of the Musashino plateau west of Tokyo. It reconstructs how Santome Shinden (三富新田), a planned reclamation colony finished in 1696, was surveyed and handed out: every settler got a long strip running back from the road, with a thatched house set at the road end of it.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our homesteads normally vary in size, and this paper is the attested counter-case for the setting where they do not, because a planned colony issued every household an identical house lot even though the finished strips themselves came out uneven. Its limits are that it is a modern architectural study reading the survey record rather than an Edo document, and that it describes one deliberately planned colony on one plateau, so it is evidence for what a laid-out settlement looked like and never for an ordinary village that grew on its own.

Used for: the counter-form - a planned shinden colony issued every settler an identical homestead, 屋敷の規模はまったくの均等配分: the uniform yard_sizes="allotted" knob

ndl-kokumori

National Diet Library reference on 石盛 (the assessed yield per tan) (https://crd.ndl.go.jp/reference/entry/index.php?id=1000296656&page=ref_view)

What it is: Another entry in the National Diet Library's Collaborative Reference Database, on kokumori (石盛), the assessed yield per tan of land fixed at the cadastral survey. It gives the grades that had largely settled into use: 1.5 koku for upper paddy, 1.3 for middle, 1.1 for lower and 0.9 for the poorest, while noting that this varied by region.

Why it applies, and its limits: The middle-paddy figure is the first input to the yard area we derive from a household's crop, so we need a period number for what a tan of ordinary paddy was reckoned to yield, and this is one. Two honest limits: it is a tax assessment rather than a measured harvest, and the entry itself says the numbers differed from region to region, so we use the middle grade as a plausible ordinary case and not as a precise yield.

Used for: 1.3 koku/tan for 中田 - the first input of the crop-derived yard area

irri-drying-floor

IRRI Rice Knowledge Bank, sun drying and drying-floor area (https://www.knowledgebank.irri.org/grainQuality/module_4/popups/pu_drying.htm)

What it is: A page from the Rice Knowledge Bank of the International Rice Research Institute, the agricultural research organization based in the Philippines, explaining how paddy is dried in the sun: how thin the grain is spread and how much floor a given tonnage of it needs.

Why it applies, and its limits: It carries the only plain statement of area we could find on a public page - grain spread a few centimeters deep, five hundred square meters of drying floor handling six tons of paddy - a depth-to-area ratio from which the size of the drying yard in front of a house is derived. Its limits: this is modern extension advice for today's farmers and varieties, written in the tropics, and we flag it as such wherever it is used; and its ratio is for a whole lot spread at once on a hard floor, while our households dry on racks first and spread in batches, so the yard on the map is a fraction of the ratio's answer, worked out in the record rather than taken from the page. Sun-drying grain on a floor is nonetheless an old technique whose arithmetic does not change with the century, so we take the ratio from it and nothing else.

Used for: the spread depth ("thin layers of ideally 2-4 cm") and the only direct area statement ("500 square meters of drying floor can handle 6 tons of paddy") - MODERN practice, flagged as such, used only to convert a crop volume into a floor area

sugiura-1973-fuzoku

Sugiura Tadashi, 「On the outbuildings of farmhouses in rural settlements: the case of Miyazaki town, Miyagi prefecture」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「農村集落における農家の付属建物について - 宮城県宮崎町の例」), Tōhoku Chiri 25(3): 145-152, 1973 (https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/tga1948/25/3/25_3_145/_article/-char/ja)

What it is: A peer-reviewed geography article by Sugiura Tadashi, "農村集落における農家の付属建物について - 宮城県宮崎町の例" ("On the outbuildings of farmhouses in rural settlements: the case of Miyazaki town, Miyagi prefecture"), published in Tōhoku Chiri 25(3), 1973, pages 145-152. It counts, household by household, which outbuildings each farmstead in the surveyed town actually had, and what stood inside the older main houses.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a household-by-household count of the outbuildings of three hamlets, by function, which is the strongest kind of evidence the record has for what stood on a farmstead and is what populates our farmyards. Its limits are that the survey was made in July 1972, a quarter century after the postwar land reform and well into mechanization, when the draft ox and horse had largely left Tohoku farms, so the barn and livestock-shed frequencies are a floor rather than a premodern figure and some sheds are twentieth-century equipment; that it is the cold, snowy northeast and not the whole country; that it is three hamlets of one town, 87 households, a sample the author himself calls unusually rich in outbuildings; and that its table gives average counts of buildings per household, which the record reads as the share of households owning one - the two agree only where no household kept two.

Used for: the per-household inventory of farmstead outbuildings by function (Table 5) and what stood inside the old main house (Table 6) - privy 0.87, firewood shed 0.76, straw shed 0.68, barn 0.58, work shed 0.55, livestock shed 0.55, kura 0.24, manure shed 0.24, bath 0.29, coop 0.16-0.28, household shrine 0.03; scope 1972 Tōhoku, stated wherever quoted

kotobank-shitsuden

Seisenban Nihon Kokugo Daijiten (Shogakukan), Nihon Dai-hyakka Zensho / Nipponica (Shogakukan) and Kaitei Shinpan Sekai Dai-hyakka Jiten (Heibonsha), s.v. 湿田, aggregated at kotobank (https://kotobank.jp/word/%E6%B9%BF%E7%94%B0-74168)

What it is: Three reference works shown together on kotobank for 湿田 (shitsuden, the wet paddy) - Shogakukan's Seisenban Nihon Kokugo Daijiten and Nipponica, and Heibonsha's revised Sekai Dai-hyakka Jiten. They define it as a badly drained paddy that stays waterlogged even when rice is not growing in it, and set out what that costs: no winter crop, heavy going for the farm work, soil that is colder and starved of air, and unstable, generally low yields.

Why it applies, and its limits: This is the class our blue plots belong to, and these entries are what let a reader clicking one of them learn why a wet field is a poorer field rather than simply a wetter one. They are tertiary summaries that characterize a type rather than measure any particular field, so which of a settlement's plots are wet is our own placement, made from the ground the map puts them on.

Used for: shitsuden defined - 水はけが悪く、水稲を栽培していないときでも過湿な状態の水田 ("a paddy with poor drainage that stays waterlogged even when rice is not being grown"), no winter crop; and its penalties - 農作業が困難, 地温が夏は乾田より低く酸素不足, 収量は不安定, 湿田の生産性は概して低い

kotobank-kanden

Digital Daijisen (Shogakukan) and Kaitei Shinpan Sekai Dai-hyakka Jiten (Heibonsha), s.v. 乾田, aggregated at kotobank (https://kotobank.jp/word/%E4%B9%BE%E7%94%B0-49311)

What it is: The Digital Daijisen and Heibonsha's revised Sekai Dai-hyakka Jiten entries for 乾田 (kanden, the dry paddy), shown together on kotobank: a paddy that drains well and dries out to the condition of a dry field when the water is let off, together with the national program that converted waterlogged paddy into it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the contrast term for the wet paddy - the dry paddy that drains and can carry a winter crop - and the scale of the Meiji conversion, which is what tells us that much of the premodern paddy was the wet kind. Its limits are that these are tertiary dictionary summaries; that the two-thirds figure is hedged in the source itself as what is commonly said; and that it is a modern figure about a modern program - the entry gives no premodern share of wet paddy, so the step from "two thirds were converted after Meiji" to "a premodern map carries wet paddy" is the record's inference and not the source's statement.

Used for: the contrast term - 水はけのぐあいがよく、水を入れないときには乾いて畑の状態になる田 ("dries out to a dryland-field state"); and the Meiji national conversion program - 明治以降，国家的事業として湿田の乾田化が行われ，全国の水田の2/3以上は乾田となったといわれる

kotobank-yatsuda

Encyclopedia entry s.v. 谷津田 / 谷地田, aggregated at kotobank (https://kotobank.jp/word/%E8%B0%B7%E6%B4%A5%E7%94%B0-400607)

What it is: The encyclopedia entry for 谷津田 / 谷地田 (yatsuda, the valley-bottom paddy) on kotobank, from Heibonsha's Sekai Dai-hyakka Jiten, describing paddy lying in the low ground of valleys cut into a plateau: wetland, opened up long ago, and in many cases dependent on rainfall because such valleys are not well supplied with water sources.

Why it applies, and its limits: Where our maps run fields up a valley floor, this is the field type they are, and the entry gives both the setting and its character - old, wet, and often rain-fed rather than fed by a channel, which bears on whether we draw a supply ditch to it. It is a tertiary summary describing a landform-and-field type rather than measuring one, so we take the character of the ground from it and no dimension.

Used for: the valley-bottom paddy and its wetland character - 台地が開析されてできた谷間の低地すなわち谷地に分布する水田 ... 一般に湿地で古く開発された

kotobank-fukada

Digital Daijisen and Seisenban Nihon Kokugo Daijiten (Shogakukan), s.v. 深田, aggregated at kotobank (https://kotobank.jp/word/%E6%B7%B1%E7%94%B0-123763)

What it is: Two Shogakukan dictionaries, the Digital Daijisen and the Seisenban Nihon Kokugo Daijiten, shown together on kotobank for the word 深田 (fukada), which both define as a paddy with deep mud, a swamp paddy, the opposite of a shallow one.

Why it applies, and its limits: A dictionary is the right authority for what a word means, and this word settles a distinction our paddy classes depend on: fukada names how deep the mud underfoot is, not where in the landscape the field sits. Nothing in these entries ties the word to a valley bottom, and none of them attaches a depth to it, so we use it for the sense of the term and take our depths from elsewhere.

Used for: fukada as a degree of mud rather than a slope position - どろの深い田。沼田 ("a paddy with deep mud; a swamp paddy"), opposed to 浅田 (asada). Recorded with its LIMIT: nothing read equates fukada with the valley-bottom paddy, and no fetched source attaches a knee- or waist-deep figure to the word itself

fao-rice-water

Thierry Facon, "Water Management in Rice in Asia: Some Issues for the Future", FAO (https://www.fao.org/4/x6905e/x6905e0g.htm)

What it is: "Water Management in Rice in Asia: Some Issues for the Future", a paper by Thierry Facon published by the UN Food and Agriculture Organization, surveying how rice irrigation in Asia is run and where it is heading. Along the way it describes the ordinary mechanics of a paddy landscape: water passing from plot to plot so that irrigation and drainage cannot be told apart, and runoff being taken up again further down.

Why it applies, and its limits: That plot-to-plot cascade is the behavior our field layouts depend on, and it is a consequence of flat, bunded, gravity-fed paddies rather than of any modern works. The limits are that the paper is written about contemporary irrigation systems and their reform, and that what we draw from the cascade - which plot ends up wettest - is our own inference, labeled as such where we use it, not something the paper says.

Used for: the plot-to-plot cascade that the wettest-plot-on-the-drain inference rests on - "water flows from one plot to another and no distinction can be made between irrigation and drainage"; and drainage reuse - surface runoff "can be re-used, i.e., recycled within the system". NOT used for the inference itself, which the entry labels an inference

maff-suitou-mizu

MAFF (Japan, Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries), 水稲栽培のポイント (https://www.maff.go.jp/j/seisan/gijutsuhasshin/techinfo/attach/pdf/suitou-2.pdf)

What it is: An extension leaflet from the Japanese Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries, 水稲栽培のポイント ("points for paddy rice cultivation"), setting out how deep the water is held in a paddy at each stage of the season: somewhat deep until the transplanted seedlings root, shallower afterward to warm the water and encourage tillering, then the mid-season drain until the mud cracks and shows a footprint, with deep water held only as a guard against a cold snap.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the staged water depths of a rice year - a few centimeters until rooting, less after, the field drained in midsummer until it cracks and takes a footprint, intermittent watering after, deep water only against cold - which is the calendar our paddies are drawn to. Its limits are that it is present-day extension advice for modern cultivars in a machine-transplanted, herbicide-using system, its figures averaged from twenty-first-century demonstration plots; and that the mid-season drying in particular is documented here as modern practice - it appears in Edo-period farming texts only in places with water enough to re-flood, and spread generally in the twentieth century - so a drained, cracked field in midsummer on our maps is a guess resting on a modern document until a period source carries it.

Used for: the STAGED water depth, verbatim - 田植後活着するまでは水深3~4cmのやや深水 (3-4 cm until rooting), 活着後は水深2~3cmのやや浅水 (2-3 cm after rooting, to warm the water and promote tillering), the 中干し mid-season drain to 田面に小さなヒビが入り、軽く足跡がつく程度 (until small cracks appear and a footprint shows), and the 10 cm / 20 cm deep-water states as an explicit COLD CONTINGENCY - 気温が下がる恐れがある場合は - not a maintained depth. Modern extension guidance, labeled as such wherever cited

zennoh-mizukanri

Zennoh (National Federation of Agricultural Cooperative Associations), 水管理について (https://www.zennoh.or.jp/ty/einou/high_quality_rice/pdf/production_001_05.pdf)

What it is: A rice-growing leaflet, 水管理について ("On water management"), published by Zennoh, Japan's National Federation of Agricultural Cooperative Associations, as part of the advice it gives its member farmers on producing good rice.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for how deep the water in a paddy stands and when it is let out - about 2 to 3 cm held through the twenty days after the ear emerges, and the season-long sequence from deeper water through midseason drying and intermittent irrigation to the final drain a week or so before harvest - because that is what decides whether a given field on the map is drawn wet, drying or dry. The honest limit is era: this is present-day guidance written for cooperative farmers with modern varieties and modern control of the inlet, so we use it for the shape of the rice year, which the crop itself imposes and which is far older than the leaflet, rather than as a record of what a premodern farmer measured.

Used for: the maintained depth through the critical ripening window - 出穂後20日間は、ほ場の水を切らさず、2~3cm程度の湛水状態を保つ (about 2-3 cm for the twenty days from heading); and the full-season sequence やや深水 -> 浅水 -> 中干し -> 間断かん水 -> 湛水 -> 間断かん水 -> 前落水 (final drain 5-7 days before harvest). Modern extension guidance

tabayashi-1987

Akira Tabayashi, "Irrigation Systems in Japan", Geographical Review of Japan Series B 60(1): 41-65 (1987) (https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/grj1984b/60/1/60_1_41/_article/-char/en/)

What it is: A peer-reviewed article by Akira Tabayashi, "Irrigation Systems in Japan", in the Geographical Review of Japan, Series B volume 60 number 1 (1987), pages 41-65. It classifies Japan's irrigation systems by their water source - river, pond, lake, groundwater, torrent - maps where each kind predominates, and traces when each was built, from the Kofun and Nara period works of the west to the Sengoku and Edo works of the east.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the right kind of source for what it is used for - a Japanese geographer's account of Japanese irrigation from the third century to 1867, at national scale and with named systems at village scale, describing the gravity-fed logic that is premodern in origin - so the siting of a reservoir above its fields, the tapering of canals as they are tapped and the separation of supply from drain rest on it directly. Its limits are that its tail describes the modern land consolidation that followed the 1899 law, whose surveyed rectangular grid is the modern arrangement and not ours; that it gives no paddy depth; and that it does not say a storage pond is tapped by a single outlet, so that claim on our maps rests elsewhere.

Used for: tameike siting above its fields; one outlet; canals taper as they are tapped; supply/drain separation at system level

kagawa-tameike

Kagawa prefecture tameike structure pages (https://www.pref.kagawa.lg.jp/tochikai/about_tameike/repair/structure.html)

What it is: Pages from the Kagawa prefectural government's Land Improvement Division describing the structure of a tameike, the irrigation reservoir dammed across a valley head, part by part: the inclined intake on the dam's upstream face, the bottom conduit at its base, and the spillway that keeps a flood from overtopping the dam. Kagawa, dry and densely ponded, has one of the largest counts of such ponds in Japan.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us where each part of a pond sits and what it does, which is what the pond glyph on our maps is drawn from. Its limits are that it is modern engineering and maintenance guidance for ponds as they are kept today - its spillway list includes forms that are unambiguously modern - so we use it for the arrangement of parts rather than for what a premodern pond was built of; that it is one prefecture; and that it says nothing about one pond feeding another below it, so a chain of ponds on our maps rests on other sources or is a guess.

Used for: inclined intake (shahi) + bottom conduit (sokohi); spillway as flood-safety (parent/child pond linkage is not on the page)

mbalib-canal-layout

MBA智库百科, 灌溉渠道系统 (https://wiki.mbalib.com/wiki/灌溉渠道系统)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the command-area placement law - mains on the district's high ground for gravity command of the largest area; every canal tier on the high ground of its own control area; mains/branches along contours and ridges, field channels across them

saitama-minuma-tsusenbori

Saitama City, 見沼通船堀のしくみ (https://www.city.saitama.lg.jp/004/005/006/008/p077111.html)

What it is: A page from the city of Saitama titled 見沼通船堀のしくみ ("how the Minuma boat canal works"), explaining the layout of the Minumadai irrigation system and the lock canal built to join its two arms. It states the arrangement in one line: the east-edge and west-edge canals run along the rim of the plateau, while the Shiba River runs down the center of the lowland.

Why it applies, and its limits: It describes an eighteenth-century Japanese irrigation scheme in the geometry our water layouts use - two supply canals carried high along the plateau rims on either side, and the river draining the lowland between - and it comes from the city that keeps the site. Its limits are that it is a heritage and education page rather than a technical study; that it describes one Kanto system as it survives; and that it gives the geometry only - that the fields could be fed from either side is our reading of that geometry, not the page's words.

Used for: Minuma-dai dividing its head into east-edge/west-edge canals (東縁/西縁) along the plateau rims, with the Shiba River draining the central lowland

jsidre-minumadai

JSIDRE, on Minuma-dai (1728) (https://www.jsidre.or.jp/tabata5-a/)

What it is: An article in the "history of regional development" series published by the Japanese Society of Irrigation, Drainage and Rural Engineering, on the Minuma-dai canal completed in 1728 on the Kanto plain, a shogunal reclamation carried out by Izawa Yasobei of the Kishu school of engineering. It describes the works and the reclamation layout that drained the old reservoir and watered the land in its place.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives us the comb layout our field blocks use, with the supply channels running along the higher rim and the drain down the lowest line through the middle, and it comes from the engineering profession that still maintains these systems. It is a society's popular history article rather than a peer-reviewed study and no author is named, and it describes one very large project, so we take the principle of the layout from it and not the dimensions, which belong to a scheme of thousands of hectares rather than to one hamlet's fields.

Used for: the Edo Kishu-school comb layout - supply on the elevated margins, drainage on the lowest line

maff-water-history

MAFF agricultural-water history PDF (https://www.maff.go.jp/j/nousin/mizu/kurasi_agwater/k_agri/pdf/detail_jp.pdf)

What it is: A twelve-page briefing deck of May 2012 from the Water Resources Division of the Rural Development Bureau of Japan's Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries, titled "On the history of agricultural water and water rights" (original: "農業用水の歴史と水利権について"). It sets out how the right to take irrigation water came to be settled in Japan, the other uses a village canal served besides watering the crop, and worked historical cases, among them the long quarrel over the Takina river in Iwate.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives a ministry's own account of how water disputes ran - on one Iwate river, thirty-six recorded quarrels, some of them deadly - which corroborates the frequency and the severity of the disputes our lore hooks draw on. Its limits are that it is a modern government briefing whose live argument is present-day water rights, with compressed historical cases whose own sources are not reproduced; that the detailed case is one valley in northern Japan; and that it says nothing about an upstream village's leverage over a downstream one - that rule rests on the irrigation study cited beside it, not on this deck.

Used for: Japanese irrigation history background

nies-shiroyone

Shiroyone terraced paddies (NIES) (https://ap-plat.nies.go.jp/inas/goodpractices/tradition/3.html)

Used for: terrace form

japanese-wiki-corpus

Japanese Wiki Corpus + Tsukuba field-trace surveys (https://www.japanesewiki.com/history/Jori%20sei%20(system%20of%20land%20subdivision%20in%20ancient%20Japan).html)

What it is: The English rendering of the Japanese Wikipedia article on jori-sei 条里制, the ancient grid system for subdividing farmland, hosted on the Japanese Wiki Corpus, a collection of Japanese Wikipedia articles translated into English by Japan's National Institute of Information and Communications Technology. The article sets out the geometry: a basic unit about 109 meters square, thirty-six of them making a ri, each unit divided into ten strips, the grid lines generally north-south and east-west but tilted in a fair number of regions, with land first recorded this way in 743.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for that grid module and its proportions, which is the frame our regular field patterns are laid out on and the reason a long-settled plain reads as ruled rather than as a patchwork. Its limits: it is an encyclopedia article, a tertiary summary, so we take the plain geometry and dates and not its interpretation; it is a translation rather than the Japanese original; and jori was an ancient state land system whose regularity survived only where the ground and the centuries allowed, so a grid this tidy suits a broad old plain and not every valley we draw.

Used for: jori-sei grid

beitang-studies

Nature Communications 2023; Jiang-Huai pond irrigation (PMC6695888) (https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-023-39454-w)

What it is: A peer-reviewed article in Nature Communications (June 2023) on reactivating small water bodies for irrigation and drainage in Chinese rice farming. It describes the beitang (陂塘) pond systems of the Jiang-Huai region as a pond-based irrigation paradigm with more than 2,500 years of history, and records that in the 1950s some 8.3 million such decentralized systems served 39 percent of China's irrigated area.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the source for our assumption that a village in rice country waters itself from ponds it holds locally, rather than only from a canal running past it, which changes what a settlement map has to show. It is a modern paper whose real subject is present-day sustainability, and its one big number is a 1950s count rather than a premodern one, so we take the system's antiquity and its village scale from it and treat the share as an indication that the pond mode was ordinary rather than marginal.

Used for: Chinese beitang pond systems as the dominant village-scale irrigation mode in rice China

buck-survey

Buck, pre-mechanization farm survey of China, 1929-33 (https://archive.org/details/landutilizationi0000buck)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: mean dry parcel near 1 mu; mid-Qing Jiangnan holdings scattered over several parcels

senmaida

Shiroyone Senmaida (Wajima, Ishikawa) - the Japanese government's Highlighting Japan feature (gov-online.go.jp, June 2025), the Wajima city cultural-property listing (wajima.hiddenheritage.jp), and JNTO's site page (japan.travel); (https://www.gov-online.go.jp/hlj/en/june_2025/june_2025-01.html)

What it is: The website of the Shiroyone Senmaida Scenic Preservation Council, the body that keeps the terraced paddies at Wajima on the Noto peninsula, whose office sits in the city's tourism division. Its "about" page describes the terrace as it is worked today, by hand, because machines cannot enter: 1,004 basins on about four hectares, an average of about 18 square meters each, the smallest about 50 centimeters square.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the small end of a really worked paddy - basins of a few square meters, the smallest a quarter of one - which is what tells us that no absolute minimum paddy area exists and lets our steepest fields be drawn tiny. Its limits are that it is a preservation body's own promotional page rather than a survey; that it is an extreme case, basins made tiny by a steep slope, so it is a floor and never a typical size; and that its counts describe the terrace's present state, not a documented historical layout - and the page gives the average and the minimum only, so how many basins run about a square meter is our reading, not its figure.

Used for: the SMALL end of a real worked paddy - 1,004 basins on ~4 ha, average ~18 m2, the smallest about half a meter square, and the straw-raincoat anecdote (the readable page is the preservation council's own site, wajima-senmaida.jp; it gives the average and the smallest only). Establishes that no ABSOLUTE minimum paddy area exists, which is why the size floor is a ratio to the fan's own design cell instead

bench-terrace-riser

FAO STI-portal and ICIMOD pages on traditional irrigated / rainfed paddy rice terraces, plus general bench-terracing references (https://sti-portal.fao.org/innovations/traditional-irrigated-rice-terraces)

What it is: A technology profile of traditional irrigated rice terraces on the Food and Agriculture Organization's Science, Technology and Innovation portal, compiled from WOCAT survey data and describing hand-built level bench terraces on steep slopes in Nepal, read alongside pages from ICIMOD and general references on bench terracing. It gives the dimensions of the structural face: risers 0.8 to 1.5 m high at slopes of 80 to 160 percent, stone-faced where stone is available, with a small lip of 20 to 25 cm holding 10 to 15 cm of water on the platform.

Why it applies, and its limits: It supplies the physical distinction our terraced maps depend on, that the tall riser is a retaining wall holding up the soil while a separate low lip holds in the water, which is why micro-basins belong on a hillside and not on a valley floor. The clear limitation is place: these are Himalayan terraces in Nepal standing in for East Asian ones because no comparable published measurements of Japanese or Chinese terrace risers could be read on a public page; the profile also describes a working present-day system that has taken up some modern methods, and parts of its text are automatically compiled, so we use it for the geometry of the structure and not for the details of how it is farmed.

Used for: the terrace riser as a structural retaining face (0.8-1.5 m, stone-faced where available, slope 80-160%) with water held by a separate 10-15 cm lip on the platform edge - the physical difference that makes micro-basins a terrace phenomenon and not a valley-floor one

li-bozhong-jiangnan

Li Bozhong, "The Practice of 'Ten Mu per Farmer' and the Scale of the Traditional Peasant Economy" (Zhongguo Nongshi, 1996) and Agricultural Development in Jiangnan, 1620-1850 - consulted at secondhand via LSE economic-history working papers and the EH.net review (https://eh.net/book_reviews/agricultural-development-in-jiangnan-1620-1850/)

Used for: mid-Qing Jiangnan farms averaged ~10 mu (~1.5 ac at the Ming-Qing mu of ~614 m2) per farm household

skinner-marketing

G. William Skinner, Marketing and Social Structure in Rural China (1964-65) - consulted at secondhand via retrospectives and reviews (https://doi.org/10.2307/2050412)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the standard marketing community - a market town centered on ~18 villages across ~300-500 km2 of farmed hinterland

aric-land-history

ARIC, "History of Agricultural Land Development in Japan" (aric.or.jp) (https://www.aric.or.jp/kiseki/en/tsuchi/index.html)

Used for: the Edo average farm household holding of ~1 cho (~2.45 ac), paddy plus dry

mdpi-kunisaki

Jansing, Mahichi et al., "Sustainable Irrigation Management in Paddy Rice Agriculture: A Comparative Case Study of Karangasem Indonesia and Kunisaki Japan", Sustainability 12(3):1180 (2020) (https://www.mdpi.com/2071-1050/12/3/1180; the page is public))

What it is: Matthew Scott Jansing, Faezeh Mahichi and Ranahansa Dasanayake, a peer-reviewed open-access article in Sustainability (2020) comparing two small irrigation communities - a Balinese subak and Tsunai ward on the Kunisaki peninsula of Oita, whose five or six reservoir systems water fifty hectares for eleven farmers - from farmer surveys, interviews and water tests of the ponds.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives us a real, counted case of how much land a cluster of small farm ponds waters and how many households share it - one ward's systems serving about fifty hectares for eleven farmers - which is the scale at which we draw a pond against the fields below it. It describes irrigation as it is managed today, in one peninsula, with modern equipment and a modern number of farmers; its own counts of the ward's ponds differ between passages; and it states no typical or maximum command area at all, so the upper bound our maps work to is our guess rather than its finding.

Used for: small tameike systems: Tsunai ward's 5 systems / 50 ha / 11 farmers (one case; the paper elsewhere counts six tameike). It gives NO typical command area or upper bound - 'tens of hectares, well under 200' is labeled GUESS

fortune-1843

Robert Fortune, eyewitness account, 1843 (https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/54720)

What it is: Three Years' Wanderings in the Northern Provinces of China by Robert Fortune, the Scottish botanist and plant collector sent to China in 1843; it is his own travel narrative of the tea, silk and cotton districts, with an account of Chinese agriculture and horticulture. We quote his description of where tea was planted: always on the lower and most fertile sides of the hills, never on the low lands, in small gardens of one to five acres, every cottager having his own.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is an eyewitness account of tea growing before mechanization, before state terracing programs, and before the twentieth century rearranged the hillsides, which makes it about as close to a premodern observation of a tea slope as a readable source gets. The limits are that it is one traveler's narrative written for a Victorian British readership rather than a survey, that it covers the coastal and northern provinces he happened to pass through, and that it is Chinese practice standing beside our Japanese material.

Used for: tea on the lower and most fertile hillsides, never the low lands or the barren upper slope

willow-palisade

Qing Willow Palisade statute (border embankment) (https://baike.baidu.com/en/item/Willow%20Palisade/1480401)

What it is: The English rendering of the Baidu Baike encyclopedia entry on the Willow Palisade (柳条边), the Qing dynasty's willow-planted border embankment across Manchuria, including the statute that laid down how the willows along it were to be set.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives a planting specification for a willow-planted earthwork in numbers - three cuttings at each five-chi interval, of stated girth and length - which is what fixes the spacing of willows along an embankment on our maps, as an analog: a Qing frontier barrier standing in for a field dike, which the record labels as such. Its limits are that it is a collaborative encyclopedia entry rather than the statute it repeats; that we read it through the site's machine-assisted English rendering, so the wording of a Qing regulation reaches us at two removes through a translation nobody edited; and that the analog is a frontier work, not a farm one.

Used for: willow whips every 5 chi (~1.67 m) in rows on an embankment. ANALOG only - no polder-dike-specific statute was found

shen-kuo

Shen Kuo, Illustrated Records of Wanchun Polder (Northern Song) (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E4%B8%87%E6%98%A5%E5%9C%A9)

Used for: full dike enclosure as the defining feature of a weitian

fei-xiaotong

Fei Xiaotong, Kaixiangong / the Xichang polder layout (https://archive.org/details/PeasantLifeInChina)

What it is: for its plan of the farm layout in the Xichang polder - the map and passages showing houses standing on the raised polder margin along the interior streams, with mulberry on the same high strip.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is our worked example of how a settlement sits inside deep-water, diked ground: not on a hill but on the only raised line there is, the dike itself. The limits are the same as for the book at large - one delta village observed in the 1930s, in China - and

Used for: houses inside the polder along its interior streams (the deep-water settlement case)

qingming-shanghe-tu

Qingming Shanghe Tu 清明上河図, gate scene (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Along_the_River_During_the_Qingming_Festival)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Along the River During the Qingming Festival (清明上河圖), the early twelfth-century handscroll attributed to Zhang Zeduan, which describes the painting scene by scene - the wheeled wagons and the donkeys and mules moving goods, and the handful of guards at the city gate.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives, through an encyclopedia's account of the painting, the scene at a Song city gate: wagons, a crowd of donkeys and mules, a few guards - open trodden ground with traffic standing in it, which is the arrangement our gate yards copy. Its limits are that we read the scroll through a tertiary summary rather than the painting or its scholarship; that it is Song China, not Japan; that the article records a scholarly dispute over whether the city is Kaifeng or an idealized city, so the scene carries no measurement; and that the hitching posts and the absence of a fence or a lawn on our gate yards are our reading of the picture, not statements on the cited page.

Used for: the city-gate cart yard: wagons, a crowd of donkeys and mules, a few guards - open ground with traffic standing in it (hitching posts, and the absence of a fence or a lawn, are our reading of the picture, not the page's words)

forests-2020

Chen, Lin, Zhang et al., "Village Fengshui Forests as Forms of Cultural and Ecological Heritage: Interpretations and Conservation Policy Implications from Southern China", Forests 11(12):1286 (2020) (https://www.mdpi.com/1999-4907/11/12/1286; DOI 10.3390/f11121286; the page is public) - academic-sources/forests-11-01286.pdf) - read from the GM's downloaded copy in academic-sources/ (mdpi.com refuses automated fetches

What it is: "Village Fengshui Forests as Forms of Cultural and Ecological Heritage: Interpretations and Conservation Policy Implications from Southern China" by Jianling Chen, Weiming Lin, Yaoqi Zhang and colleagues, a peer-reviewed open-access paper in the journal Forests (2020). It reports a 2019 field survey of seven villages, one in each town of Youxi County in Fujian, with interviews of forestry staff, fengshui masters, village officials and clan heads, on what the groves contain, why they survived, and what protecting them would take.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is peer-reviewed work about the exact feature we draw. The figure of roughly two fengshui forests per village that we take from it is not the paper's own measurement: it quotes it, for Fujian province as a whole, from a 2012 Chinese study we have not read, so the number reaches us at second hand. It does not give us grove sizes: its own table records whole-village forest areas only, and it counts no trees or canopies, so the per-grove areas on our maps are labeled guesses. It is also a small interview-based study of the groves that survived in one county of one province, so it describes a remnant landscape rather than the ordinary one of several centuries ago, and China stands in for a setting modeled as much on Japan.

Used for: the two-fengshui-forests-per-village figure (a Fujian-province figure the paper quotes from Xu, Qiu and Wang 2012, in its paragraph on the province; the paper's own survey is seven villages of Youxi County). NOT for grove areas: its Table 1 gives only whole-village forests of over 3, 6 and 20 ha, and it collects no stem or canopy data - the record's per-type areas and counts are labeled GUESS

sphere-2004-water

The Sphere Project, Humanitarian Charter and Minimum Standards in Disaster Response (2004), chapter 2, water supply, sanitation and hygiene promotion (https://ec.europa.eu/echo/files/evaluation/watsan2005/annex_files/Sphere/SPHERE2%20-%20chapter%202%20-%20Min%20standards%20in%20water,%20sanitation%20and%20hygiene%20prom.pdf)

What it is: A chapter of the Sphere humanitarian minimum standards - the water supply, sanitation and hygiene section, circulated here as a PDF on the European Commission's humanitarian aid site - setting out how many users a tap, a handpump or an open well can serve and how far anyone should have to walk to reach one.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives one planning figure - an open well serving about four hundred users - which the record uses only in the negative: a well's yield is never what limits how many wells a settlement has. Its limits are that it is a relief agencies' planning standard for present-day disaster response, not a historical or field measurement; that its four hundred rests on a survival ration of fifteen liters a person a day and a well open eight hours a day, so a settled household cooking, washing and watering stock would exhaust a well at a far smaller number - the figure flatters well capacity and is a ceiling on a ceiling; and that the handbook itself calls these rough guidelines to be used with caution. The bucket rate it assumes is one a premodern rope-and-bucket well shares; the modern part is the ration and the emergency, not the engineering.

Used for: one open well serves ~400 people - capacity is never the binding constraint on well count

aas-rice-technology

AAS, "Rice, Technology, and History" (https://www.asianstudies.org/publications/eaa/archives/rice-technology-and-history-the-case-of-china/)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: rice domesticated in naturally marshy areas; paddy as reclaimed marsh

lacey-regime

Lacey's regime theory for unlined alluvial canals - the wetted-perimeter equation P = 4.8 * sqrt(Q), and the regime power-law exponents (width 0.5, depth 0.33, velocity 0.17 against discharge). Consulted via canal-design references and the Journal of Hydrology review "The width of a bankfull channel; Lacey's formula explained" (2003) (https://doi.org/10.1016/S0022-1694(03)00069-6)

What it is: H. H. G. Savenije, "The width of a bankfull channel; Lacey's formula explained", a research paper in the Journal of Hydrology (2003) giving a physical derivation of the regime formula developed on the irrigation canals of colonial India and Pakistan: the width of a stable channel grows with the square root of its discharge.

Why it applies, and its limits: It supplies the law behind the way our channels taper, which is that width grows as the square root of flow rather than in proportion to it, and it gives an absolute check on drawn channel widths that caught a network drawn five or six times its true hydraulic size. The honest limits are that this is a modern engineering formula, calibrated largely on big colonial-era irrigation canals in sandy soil, applied here to small village ditches, so we use it for the relation between flow and width and not to pronounce the exact width of any one ditch.

Used for: the taper LAW - a channel's width goes as the square root of the discharge it carries, so taper_w interpolates the width SQUARED; and the absolute-width sanity check that found the drawn comb net ~5-6x its true hydraulic size

fao-paddy-duty

FAO irrigation training manuals - "Scheme Irrigation Water Need and Supply" (u5835e) and "Determination of the Irrigation Schedule for Paddy Rice" (t7202e) (https://www.fao.org/4/u5835e/u5835e00.htm)

What it is: Two irrigation training manuals from the UN Food and Agriculture Organization - "Scheme Irrigation Water Need and Supply" and "Determination of the Irrigation Schedule for Paddy Rice" - which teach how to work out how much water a rice scheme must be fed: the large one-off soaking that turns dry ground into workable mud, the water that soaks away through the floor each day, the standing layer kept on the field, and the resulting flow per hectare.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it to size a channel against the fields it actually serves, and that sum is arithmetic of soil, sun and standing water, none of which a premodern valley did differently. The limits are that these are modern teaching documents whose numbers are planning generalizations across many countries, so they give a defensible band rather than the true duty of any particular valley, and that a premodern channel was cut by eye and by custom rather than calculated to a figure.

Used for: the paddy irrigation duty used to size a real channel against a real command area - a net need near 1 L/s/ha (~8.6 mm/day) continuous, plus ~200 mm for puddling, which is the peak that sets a supply canal's section

nougyoudoboku-suikou

のうぎょうとぼく (Japanese agricultural-engineering design notes), 「Design of the water inlet (minakuchi) and the mizujiri (the drain outlet) within a paddy field」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「水田内の「水口・水じり（落水口）」に関する設計について」) (https://nougyoudoboku.com/water-entrance-exit/)

What it is: A second page from the same Japanese agricultural civil engineering site, のうぎょうとぼく, covering the design of the two openings in a paddy plot's bund: the mizuguchi intake that admits water from the terminal channel and the mizujiri outlet that lets it go.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the plot-scale detail our maps draw at every bund - one intake per plot (two when the intakes would stand more than 50 m apart), an intake no wider than about half a meter, its sill just above the field surface and the outlet a little below it - so that water enters at one corner and leaves at another. The same limits apply as to its companion page: it is modern design guidance for engineered plots and concrete or pipe fittings, while a premodern plot's intake was a notch cut in an earth bund, so we take the arrangement and the relative heights and not the materials or the tolerances.

Used for: the mizuguchi as the plot's intake from the terminal channel - one per plot (two if the spacing exceeds 50 m), width within 50 cm, sill 0-10 cm above the field surface, ~0.4 m/s allowable velocity; and the outlet (shirimito / 落水口) as an overflow set 5-10 cm below the field surface

nougyoudoboku-matsutan

のうぎょうとぼく, 「Terminal irrigation channel / small irrigation channel within a paddy field」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「水田圃場内の「末端用水路・小用水路」」) and 「terminal drainage channel / small drainage channel」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「末端排水路・小排水路」) (https://nougyoudoboku.com/terminal-irrigation-canal/)

What it is: A page on the terminal irrigation canal - the last channel before the paddy plot - from のうぎょうとぼく, a self-published site on agricultural civil engineering written by a credentialed agricultural engineer and farmer, a former public servant in the field, posted in 2022 and updated in 2024. It sets out the three-tier supply hierarchy of a consolidated paddy district, the 300 to 600 m spacing of branch canals, and the height of the terminal channel's bed against the field surface; the matching terminal drain is the subject of a separate article on the same site, which this page links but does not contain.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the gravity logic of a paddy's water supply - the order of main, branch and terminal channel and how the last one sits against the field - which wet rice has not changed and which is what our canal trees are drawn from. Its limits are that it is present-day engineering guidance, self-published by a practitioner rather than a ministry standard or a peer-reviewed work; that it assumes consolidated, machine-worked paddy with precast concrete channels, so its 300 to 600 m branch spacing is the dimension of a modern land-readjustment block, the very thing a premodern field lacked, and only the order and the gravity transfer; and that the drainage side - the terminal drain's depth, the drain hierarchy, the sizing from catchment - is not on this page but on the linked companion, so the drainage claims the record took from this key rest on nothing readable at its link.

Used for: the three-tier supply hierarchy 幹線 -> 支線 -> 末端用水路; branch spacing 300-600 m (a consolidation-block dimension); the terminal channel's bed set -5 to +10 cm against the paddy surface (the drainage mirror, the terminal drain's depth and the sizing from 集水面積 are on the site's separate drainage article, which this key does not link)

gb50288

GB50288-2018 灌溉与排水工程设计标准, sections 6.4 and 17.7; plus the Chinese five-tier canal vocabulary 干渠 / 支渠 / 斗渠 / 农渠 / 毛渠 (https://www.antpedia.com/standard/7931413-1.html)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the FIVE fixed canal tiers with the maoqu (毛渠) as the finest, the tier below anything we draw; 斗渠 1,000-3,000 m at 400-800 m spacing and 农渠 400-800 m at 100-200 m spacing; paddy plot (格田) 25-30 m x ~100 m on the plain; 斗/农 bank tops not under 1 m

toro-site

Toro site (登呂遺跡), Shizuoka - Shizuoka City Toro Museum and the Japanese Wikipedia entry (https://www.city.shizuoka.lg.jp/s6725/s005190.html)

Used for: the attested pre-modern paddy net - ~40 plots of 1,000-2,000 m2 over ~8 ha, a single central canal with the paddies ranked either side, 大畦畔 dividing large compartments with small ones inside, and canal + bund revetted with yaita sheet boards 2 m x 30-40 cm x 5 cm

aze-standard

Japanese land-improvement standard designs for 畦畔 (paddy bunds) - Aomori prefecture ほ場整備 standard drawing set and 大和川流域水田貯留技術基準 (https://www.pref.aomori.lg.jp/soshiki/nourin/noson/files/H2904_nnzusyu_H2911syuusei.pdf)

What it is: Aomori Prefecture's standard drawings for consolidated paddy (2017 revision), a public-works design standard: the outer bund drawn as a trapezoid 30 cm high above the higher field, 30 cm wide at the crest, with side slopes of 1:1 to 1:1.2, in three types by the difference in level between the fields it separates, up to 4 m, with berms on the tallest.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives an engineered cross-section for a paddy bund in numbers, which calibrates the most repeated line on a paddy map against the one to two feet the period sources give. Its limits are that it is a post-war land-consolidation standard for machine-formed embankments between fields whose surfaces may differ by meters, a different object from a hand-piled ridge between two level plots, so its figures are a modern engineered minimum and not a measurement of a premodern bund; that a hand-piled bund would have been less regular; and that the enlarged cold-region bund an enlarged cold-region bund of about 50 by 40 cm is not on the Aomori sheet - the berms on it are for tall bunds, and its only frost-heave provision concerns a flume's subgrade - and the other standard the entry names is neither linked nor quoted, so that figure stands as a guess.

Used for: the standard outer bund as a trapezoid of 30 cm top width, 30 cm high, 1:1 side slopes (so ~90 cm at the base); the enlarged cold-region bund of ~50 cm x 40 cm is not on the Aomori sheet and the Yamato standard is unlinked, so it stands as a guess

Attested instances (anchors, not works)

Named real-world cases the rules are calibrated against. They are evidence, but they are measurements rather than publications, so they are listed separately and cited inline by name.

Anchor What it anchors
Suzugamori 74 x 16.2 m (~243 x 53 ft), serving Edo for 220 years - the execution-ground size anchor
Pingyao Market Tower attested at 133.4 m2 plan (~38 ft square); ~60 m mamian tower spacing - the wealthy-county-seat anchor
Xi'an mamian bastions ~20 m wide projecting ~12 m; ~120 m tower spacing (the peaceful tier)
Himeji moats averaging ~20 m (max 34.5 m) - the provincial-city moat tier
Osaka outer moat ~70-90 m - the grand-city moat tier, deliberately ABOVE our range
Fushimi Inari Senbon Torii: ~800 gates over ~400 m - the donation-row regime
Kasuga Taisha / Meiji Jingu / Nagao ranked ichi/ni/san-no-torii 200 m-1.3 km apart - the other regime
Northampton largest excavated urban tannery at 36-37 pits - far past anything in this pool
Longsheng largest terrace 0.62 mu (~0.10 acre); 15,862 terraces in one village
Edo Kokucho time bell billed ~400-410 blocks 'within earshot' - the bell-audibility anchor
Shunde county ~4.6% dike-pond in 1581 while containing townships already over 50% - the scatter was normal

kanazawa-teramachi

VISIT KANAZAWA official travel guide, Teramachi Temple Area (https://visitkanazawa.jp/en/spot/detail_10182.html)

What it is: A visitor attraction listing on VISIT KANAZAWA, the official travel guide published by the Kanazawa City Tourism Association, describing the Teramachi temple district - one of three quarters of the old castle town into which the ruling Maeda family gathered temples when the town was laid out.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the scale a castle town's temple district could reach - some seventy temples close together in one of Kanazawa's three - which is the top of the range our temple quarters are measured against. Its limits are that it is a tourist board's page with no sources; that the seventy is a present-day count of what stands, not a period census; that Kanazawa was the seat of the largest domain in the country and one of its largest castle towns, so its temple stock is a ceiling and not a norm; and that it was three districts, not one.

Used for: ~70 temples clustered in one castle-town temple district

takada-teramachi

Takada Teramachi tourism site / Joetsu Stories (https://joetsu-stories.jp/2025/02/03/takada-teramachi-part-1/)

What it is: An English-language local history article on Joetsu Stories, a bilingual site about the city of Joetsu in Niigata Prefecture, telling how the temple quarter (teramachi) of the castle town of Takada came into being after Matsudaira Tadateru moved his seat there in 1614. It describes the ground west of the castle that was set aside for temples, the first wave of temples that moved in, and what the domain expected of them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives one dated case of a temple quarter made by a lord's decree - about twenty-five temples moved to ground west of the castle at Takada in 1614 - which sets the size of a castle town's temple quarter on our maps and the reading that it doubled as a defensive belt quartering foot soldiers. Its limits are that it is an anonymous local-history blog post that cites nothing; that its count is hedged by its own writer ("probably about 25"); that it is one town in one province at one date, a point in a range and not a rule; and that the reading of "protection" as spiritual support plus billets for soldiers is the writer's interpretation, unattributed.

Used for: ~25 temples relocated to Takada in the 1614 wave

takayama-teramachi

Japan Travel, "Takayama's historic 'Temple Town'" (https://en.japantravel.com/gifu/takayama-s-teramachi-gifu/13893)

What it is: "Takayama's historic 'Temple Town'", a visitor's guide article by Sandra Isaka on Japan Travel, a community travel site written by contributors, walking the reader through the temple quarter on the eastern edge of Takayama and noting who founded most of its temples.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives a size-matched low anchor for a town's temple quarter - Takayama's, with more than ten temples and shrines - which bounds the bottom of the range our town maps draw. Its limits are that it is community travel writing, a visitor's walk-through with no sourcing; that its count mixes Buddhist temples with Shinto shrines, so it is not a temple count; that it counts the quarter as it stands today rather than at any period; and that Takayama, its castle pulled down after 1692 and the town run from the intendancy office thereafter, is a particular kind of town.

Used for: "over 10 temples and shrines" in a SMALL castle town - the size-matched anchor for the city tier

jokamachi-wiki

Japanese Wiki Corpus, Jokamachi (https://www.japanesewiki.com/history/Jokamachi.html)

What it is: The English rendering of the Japanese Wikipedia castle-town article published in the Japanese Wiki Corpus, a collection of translated Japanese encyclopedia articles on history and culture. The passage we use covers the temple district placed at the town's outer rim, whose walled precincts were counted as part of the town's defenses.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives, in English, the temple quarter at the rim of the castle town with its precincts counted as part of the defenses, which is what puts the teramachi where our maps put it. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary at one further remove: the English is a machine-translation research corpus (the bilingual corpus of Wikipedia's Kyoto-topic articles made by NICT, Japan's National Institute of Information and Communications Technology), a frozen snapshot rather than the maintained article, which is why it reads as it does; and that two other entries in this registry point at the same article and at the Japanese original it translates, so the three cannot corroborate one another.

Used for: the teramachi sited at the jokamachi's outer rim, its precincts forming part of the city defenses

pingyao-chenghuangmiao

Wikipedia, City God Temple of Pingyao (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_God_Temple_of_Pingyao)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the City God Temple of Pingyao, the Chenghuang temple in the walled county town of Pingyao in Shanxi, which describes the site as three separate temples standing together on one complex of more than 7,300 square meters and lists them individually.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the shape of a county seat's civic temple - not a single hall but a compound holding several cults side by side, the City God's own temple among them - which is how our town maps compose a precinct rather than dropping one building. It is a tertiary summary used for what stands on the site rather than for a measurement, the surviving buildings are later rebuildings of a much older foundation, and Pingyao was a wealthy town whose temple is a generous example rather than an average county one.

Used for: the City God temple as a complex of three distinct temples on one county-seat site

chinaknowledge-tang-econ

chinaknowledge.de, Tang-period economy (http://www.chinaknowledge.de/History/Tang/tang-econ.html)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: monastic land and dependents funding mills, oil presses and other enterprises

inexhaustible-treasuries

Studies in Chinese Religions 5(2), "Giving while keeping: inexhaustible treasuries and inalienable wealth in medieval China" (https://doi.org/10.1080/23729988.2019.1639463)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the wu-jin-zang interest-earning endowment; Xuanzong's 713 liquidation

tontine-monastery-lending

The Tontine Coffee-House, "Buddhist Monastery Lending" (https://tontinecoffeehouse.com/2024/02/26/buddhist-monastery-lending/)

What it is: A post, "Buddhist Monastery Lending," written by Daniel DeMatos in February 2024 for The Tontine Coffee-House, a financial-history blog running since 2018 that covers the history of financial institutions and innovations. It traces how Buddhist monasteries in China served as lenders and pawnbrokers from the arrival of Buddhism down to the nineteenth century, and closes with a short reading list that includes Jacques Gernet's Buddhism in Chinese Society.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the institutional facts behind a temple that lends - the monastery as the great pawnbroker, and pledged goods held in an associated warehouse or granary for the life of the loan - which is why a temple compound on our maps can reasonably carry a strongroom rather than only a hall. It is a secondary blog summary rather than a scholarly work, so we take from it only what its own cited literature supports, and it is about China; we apply it to a Japanese-flavored setting on the strength of the monastic economy the two shared, not as a statement about any particular Japanese temple.

Used for: monasteries as pawnbrokers; the 1202 Song lay-partnership pawnshop ju

pawnbroking-history

Wikipedia, History of pawnbroking (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_pawnbroking)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "History of pawnbroking", a tertiary summary of lending against pledged goods from antiquity to the present, with a short section on China that runs from the earliest monastic pawnshops through the Song.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use its statement that the earliest Chinese pawnbrokers were established and run by Buddhist monasteries, and its note that Southern Song laypeople formed partnerships with monasteries to open pawnshops, when deciding where a lending house belongs on a map - inside or beside a temple precinct rather than in a market row. It is a tertiary summary whose claims we accept only where its own references support them, and China occupies a few sentences of a global survey, so we take the institutional association it describes and not a chronology or a count.

Used for: pawnbroking limited to Buddhist monasteries prior to the Tang

zhengyi-householder-priests

Grokipedia, Zhengyi Dao; Patheos, Taoism leadership and clergy (https://grokipedia.com/page/Zhengyi_Dao)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: married priests residing in households, hereditary ordination within families

kannushi-wiki

Wikipedia, Shinto priest / Kannushi (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kannushi)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the kannushi, the priest who tends a Shinto shrine, covering the office, its duties, and the way it has been passed down within families.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one structural fact that shapes who lives beside a shrine on our maps - that the office was hereditary, held by a married priest whose children succeeded him, so a shrine implies a resident priestly household rather than a celibate community. It is a tertiary summary and we take its figures only where its own references support them; note too that its most concrete statements are about the modern era, including the abolition of hereditary appointment in 1871, which is well after the period our setting is modeled on and which we use only as evidence of how deeply rooted the practice was before it.

Used for: hereditary shrine office, up to 100 generations; abolished 1871, persists by local preference

jodo-shinshu-marriage

Seattle Betsuin, "Jodo Shinshu and Marriage" (https://seattlebetsuin.org/jodo-shinshu-and-marriage/)

Used for: the Tokugawa exemption permitting a married temple head to keep priestly status

tricycle-temple-wives

Tricycle, "Temple Wives of Japan" (https://tricycle.org/magazine/japan-temple-wives/)

What it is: "Temple Wives of Japan," an interview by Karen Jensen with the scholar Jessica Starling, published in the Spring 2022 issue of Tricycle: The Buddhist Review, a long-running nonprofit Buddhist magazine. It explains the bomori - literally the guardian or protector of the temple, in practice the wife of a Jodo Shinshu resident priest - and the household that a temple in that tradition actually is.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives us what a rural temple contains as a domestic institution: a married clergy exempted by the Tokugawa government so a temple head could be married and still hold priestly status, a wife who runs the temple, and succession from father to eldest son - which is why our temple compounds carry a priest's family dwelling rather than a celibate dormitory. It is a magazine interview rather than a study, and it describes one tradition, Jodo Shinshu, whose married clergy was the exception in the Edo period rather than the rule, so we use it for the shape of a temple household and not as a claim about every sect.

Used for: the bomori institution and eldest-son succession

nishikori-tsunaba

Kotobank, Nishikori tsunaba site entry; Yaotsu town history pages (yaotsu-mall.com) (https://kotobank.jp/word/%E9%8C%A6%E7%B9%94%E7%B6%B1%E5%A0%B4%E8%B7%A1-3061429)

What it is: A Kotobank reprint of the entry on the Nishikori tsunaba from the Heibonsha historical place-name gazetteer 日本歴史地名大系, a scholarly reference compiled from local records. It describes the rope catch across the Kiso river at Nishikori, where the logs floated down from the Kiso forests were stopped at the mouth of the gorge and made up into rafts, and says that when the practice began is not known.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us that a full-river catch stood at the gorge mouth where the loose-log drive ended and the rafting began, which is what puts a boom and a timber yard at that point on our river maps. Its limits are that it is a gazetteer entry reprinted by an aggregator, read in extract, so it supports the site and its position and nothing more; that a yearly log count, the season, the size of the timber magistracy and an origin in the 1340s are on no page we could read, and the entry itself says the origin is unknown, so they stand as guesses; and that one exceptional domain-run river stands in for the ordinary timber trade.

Used for: the full-river rope catch at the gorge mouth (the entry says when it began is unknown; a yearly log count, the season, a 138-official magistracy and a 1340s origin are on no readable page and stand as guesses)

kiso-unzaihou

Rinya-cho (Japan Forestry Agency), Kiso-style felling and transport method pages (https://www.rinya.maff.go.jp/chubu/kiso/morigatari/unzaihou.html)

What it is: A page published by the Chubu Regional Forest Office of Japan's Forestry Agency, part of a public-history series on the Kiso forests, describing the Kiso-style felling and timber transport method - axe felling, the sledge and chute haul down to the water, loose-log drives, and rafting - as it was practiced from the late sixteenth century until 1920. The page notes that its illustrated guide is what the office supposes to be a Meiji-era copy of late-Edo originals, and gives no author.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for how timber reached a town, and it describes that chain in order: the loose-log drive handed off to rafts at the tying station, three men riding each raft, and the run down to the timber storage yard at Nagoya and the bay port beyond, which is what puts a log yard where we put it. It is a government agency's explanatory page rather than a study, and it describes one valley's system, so the crew size and the yard locations are a worked example of how such a trade ran and not a national standard.

Used for: the loose-log drive (the page's terms are kotari-gari and okawa-gari) handing off to rafts at the tsunaba; three-man raft crews; onward transport to the Nagoya and bay-port storage yards

susquehanna-boom

Wikipedia, Susquehanna Boom; PA Conservation Heritage; Lumber Heritage Region (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susquehanna_Boom)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the Susquehanna Boom at Williamsport, Pennsylvania, the seven-mile log boom that ran from 1851 to 1909: some 350 to 400 stone-filled timber cribs, 22 feet high, stretched diagonally across the river, holding as much as 300 million board feet of logs at once.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the anatomy of the largest kind of boom - fixed cribs of stone-filled timber, the enclosure they make, the volume it held - from which the physics of anchoring and enclosure on our timber rivers is taken. Its limits are that it is industrial North America, not premodern East Asia, so the arrangement transfers and the scale does not; that it is a tertiary summary whose figures are only as good as its own references; and that a lifetime total of logs is not on the page at all and is not used.

Used for: seven miles of boom stretched diagonally across the river; ~350-400 stone-filled cribs 22 ft high; 300 million board feet held at once (the lifetime log total is not on the page)

stcroix-boom-nps

NPS, "The St. Croix Boom" (https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/stcroixboom.htm)

What it is: A public-history article by Dan Ott, written in 2013 for the National Park Service's St. Croix National Scenic Riverway, on the log-handling works of the St. Croix river between Minnesota and Wisconsin from 1856 to 1914: the river divided lengthwise into log channels and holding pens beside a maintained navigation channel, the hinged sheer boom, and the lawsuits and negligence rulings the works drew.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the physical arrangement of a working boom - a river divided lengthwise, the logs held in pens along one side and a channel kept open beside them - which is why our timber-town booms run along a bank rather than across the river. Its limits are that it is industrial-era North America, where steam tugs, an army engineers' navigation channel and sawmill-scale volumes share the description with the physical arrangement, so the two have to be separated by hand and only the arrangement transfers; that it is a public-history summary rather than a study or a primary record; and that the navigation lawsuits it records are a modern American statutory and common-law regime with no premodern East Asian counterpart, so they explain that a boom must leave a channel, not who would have sued over it in our setting.

Used for: lengthwise division into log channels and holding pens beside a maintained navigation channel; the hinged sheer boom; navigation lawsuits and negligence rulings

hudson-big-boom

New York Almanack, "The Big Boom: Old Hudson River Chain Recalls Logging History" (https://www.newyorkalmanack.com/2016/10/the-big-boom-old-hudson-river-chain-recalls-logging-history/)

What it is: An article by Glenn Pearsall, "The Big Boom: Old Hudson River Chain Recalls Logging History", published on 18 October 2016 in New York Almanack, an online magazine of New York State history and natural history. It describes the log boom that held floated timber on the Hudson above Glens Falls - hewn timbers bolted together raftwise, the groups joined end to end by chains running over friction rollers, each end anchored to a heavy bank abutment - and the 1859 break that scattered logs forty miles downriver.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for how a boom is built and held, which is what the log booms on our timber-town rivers are drawn from: a floating fence across a current, jointed so it rides the water, tied to solid ground at both banks. Its limits: it is popular history rather than a technical or scholarly account, with the scholarly source one step further out; it is about industrial-era logging in North America rather than East Asia, and its fittings - bolted timber groups, chains run over iron rollers - are nineteenth-century hardware a premodern river works would replace with lashings and rope, so what transfers is the principle of anchoring to fixed ground and not the fastenings; and the Hudson operation was far larger than anything at our settlements, so we take the arrangement from it and not the dimensions.

Used for: boom construction - hewn timbers bolted raftwise, chained end to end over friction rollers to bank abutments; the 1859 break that scattered logs 40 miles

kiba-koto

the tokyo files, "the kiba of Koto-ku"; Hiroshige print notes (Adachi / Brooklyn Museum) (https://thetokyofiles.com/2023/03/20/the-floating-world-of-floating-logs-the-kiba-of-koto-ku/)

What it is: A March 2023 post by Clark Parker on the tokyo files, a personal blog about the geography, design and history of Tokyo, tracing the kiba (木場) of Koto ward - the timber quarter where logs rafted down from upstream were held afloat in a maze of canals and storage ponds and worked by the sawmills around them - from the Edo period to its late twentieth-century conversion into parks, and read here together with museum notes on Hiroshige's prints of the same district.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the shape of a timber quarter - an off-river district of canals and storage ponds, logs kept afloat, rafts poled to the yards - which is what our timber towns' storage grounds are drawn from.
