Its limits are that it is a personal, well-researched but non-scholarly site with no citations a reader can follow; that much of its detail is twentieth-century, the Edo arrangement inferred from its survival and from the prints; and that Edo's timber quarter served the national market of the largest city in the country, so a provincial river town's timber ground is a scaled-down reading of it, not the same thing.

Used for: Edo's off-river timber district of canals and storage ponds; floating storage preserving timber; rafts poled in canals

shangxinhe-gazetteer

Jiangsu provincial gazetteer site on Shangxinhe; Wikipedia/Baidu, Shangxinhe (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E4%B8%8A%E6%96%B0%E6%B2%B3%E9%95%87)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on 上新河镇 (Shangxinhe), the timber town that grew on a Ming-built side channel off the Yangtze at Nanjing, with references to the Qing gazetteer of the two counties. It describes the rafts moored in mass along the banks at the channel's mouth, the timber market that became a town, and the trade's Qing peak; it gives no measurement of the channel.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives a real case of a timber town made by its moorings - rafts along the bank, a market on the shore, a town behind it - which is the arrangement our river timber towns copy. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary standing on gazetteer material; that it is one Chinese river port applied to our timber-town features; and that it carries no measurement of any kind, so a channel length is ours and unsourced.

Used for: Nanjing's side-channel timber market (no length is on the page); rafts moored in mass along the banks; the Qing-peak "constantly sufficient through all seasons" trade

timber-rafting-wiki

Wikipedia, Timber rafting (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timber_rafting)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Timber rafting", a tertiary summary of how cut timber was moved on water before roads and railways, distinguishing rafting - logs bound together and navigated, sometimes with cabins and steering oars aboard - from log driving, and giving raft sizes from several regional traditions.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it to keep two different river systems straight on a map, since a raft needs a wide steady channel and a landing where it can be broken up, while driving loose logs does not, and to keep raft sizes within a plausible order of magnitude. It is a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, and its detailed dimensions come from European rivers such as the Rhine, so we treat them as the outer bound of what a raft can be rather than as a measurement of an East Asian one.

Used for: rafting vs driving as distinct systems; raft dimensions across traditions (Rhine rafts to hundreds of meters, thousands of logs)

Setting canon

l7r.md and budgets.md (the GM's own notes) are cited inline by filename rather than keyed here - they are canon rather than research, and an entry resting on them carries the setting-canon evidence class.

jta-nagayamon

Japan Tourism Agency multilingual commentary database, Nagaya-mon (H30-00651) (https://www.mlit.go.jp/tagengo-db/en/H30-00651.html)

What it is: An entry in the Japan Tourism Agency's multilingual commentary database, written for foreign visitors by the Ministry of Land, Infrastructure, Transport and Tourism, on the nagaya-mon - the long gatehouse range at the street front of a samurai residence - as restored at the Takada family house in Kanazawa, built in the early 1860s. It describes the range's servants' quarters, with a wood floor and a built-in hearth, and its stable with a rammed-earth floor, and records that in the Kaga domain a nagaya-mon was permitted only to samurai of middle rank and above.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the form of the gate range that fronts a samurai compound on our capital maps and the rank rule that governed who could build one, which is what makes the gate a marker of status a reader can read. Its limits are that it is visitor commentary with no sources cited rather than a survey; that it describes one domain's regulation and one surviving building from the very end of the period; and that it names only the servants' quarters and the stable as the range's contents - a gatekeeper's room, a storeroom and street-facing lookout windows are not on the page, so where our maps draw them they are a guess.

Used for: the nagayamon as a perimeter retainer range with a gate cut through it; its contents as the page names them - servants' quarters with a hearth, and a stable (a gatekeeper's room, a chugen room, a storeroom and lookout windows are not on the page)

jta-ashigaru-kaga

Japan Tourism Agency multilingual commentary database, Ashigaru Residences / Samurai Ranks in the Kaga Domain (H30-00660, H30-00647) (https://www.mlit.go.jp/tagengo-db/en/H30-00660.html)

What it is: Two entries from the Japan Tourism Agency's multilingual commentary database, the English texts written to explain historic sites to foreign visitors, covering the foot soldier districts and the ranking of samurai in the Kaga domain around Kanazawa. They give the rows of hedged plots the foot soldiers lived on, each about 165 or 230 square meters and divided between house and garden.

Why it applies, and its limits: Very few sources put a measured plot size on the humblest warrior housing, and these give us both a size and the way enclosure rose with rank from hedge to fence to wall, which is what sets the fringe of our castle towns. The commentary is interpretive writing for visitors rather than a survey, and it says plainly that Kaga's detached foot soldier houses were unusual for the period, most domains housing them in row tenements, so we treat these plots as one domain's practice that our maps may follow rather than a national norm.

Used for: ashigaru kumi-yashiki on the town fringe; 165/230 m2 hedged plots with gardens, flagged as unusual for Japan; enclosure by rank (wall / fence / hedge)

fukui-bushi-jutaku

Fukui Prefectural Archives exhibit, the housing of Fukui-domain samurai (Matsudaira Bunko) (https://www.library-archives.pref.fukui.lg.jp/bunsho/category/tenji/32442.html)

What it is: An online exhibit from the Fukui Prefectural Archives on where the samurai of the Fukui domain lived, built out of the domain's own surviving paperwork and house plans in the Matsudaira family collection. It walks through residence plots by the rank and stipend of the household that held them, and reproduces a dated 1839 plan of the Suganuma house near the Yanagi gate with its measurements written out.

Why it applies, and its limits: A public archive showing the actual Edo-period plans is about as direct as evidence of samurai housing gets, which is why we lean on it for the ladder of plot sizes by stipend and for the detail that the buildings presenting their walls to the street were the servants' long-house quarters. The limits are that this is one castle town, and a large one - Fukui's ladder is being asked to stand for castle-town samurai housing generally - and that an exhibit shows a curated handful of plans rather than a full survey, so the size bands are drawn from a few well-preserved examples rather than from a count of every plot in the town.

Used for: the plot-size ladder by stipend (1,000-1,700 tsubo karo down to 66-96 tsubo clerks); the Suginuma 1839 plan whose street-facing buildings are named as the servants' nagaya

matsue-bukeyashiki

Matsue Buke Yashiki (Shiomi Nawate), museum building documentation (https://www.matsue-bukeyashiki.jp/about/equipment.html)

What it is: The building documentation published by the museum that operates the Matsue Buke Yashiki, a preserved middle-rank samurai residence standing on the Shiomi Nawate street in the castle town of Matsue, describing the house room by room and the gate on its street front.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a surviving building described by the people who keep it, which is why we use it for the nagayamon standing on the street and doubling as the quarters of the gatekeeper and the household servants, and for a room program that includes the servants' service rooms - both things our residence plans have to place. It is one house in one castle town, presented for visitors rather than as a study, and a restored and interpreted one, so it tells us what a middle-rank residence in Matsue is like and not what a typical residence anywhere was.

Used for: a middle-rank residence with its nagayamon on the street; room program including servants' service rooms

hikone-ashigaru

Hikone ashigaru kumi-yashiki documentation (hikone-bunkaisan.net) (https://hikone-bunkaisan.net/ashigaru/)

What it is: A page from 彦根文化遺産 ("Hikone Cultural Heritage"), a heritage site published by the local Hikone Historical Charm Utilization Executive Committee, documenting the ashigaru (foot-soldier) quarters of the Hikone castle town. It covers the preserved street plan and plot sizes, the character of the houses, and the 組 (kumi) grouping by which the ashigaru were organized and along which they were settled - an organization whose descendants still run the neighborhood's fire-prevention and local-governance associations. No author is named and the page carries no date.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a local record of a surviving quarter that tells us the three things our layout needs: that the lowest-ranking retainers were housed on the outermost edge of the castle town as a defensive ring, the size of a single plot, and that men of one unit lived together along a numbered stretch of one street. It is a heritage-promotion page rather than a scholarly study, and it describes one domain - Hikone housed its ashigaru in detached houses with gardens where other domains used barrack rows, so the plot form is that domain's practice and not a national rule.

Used for: ashigaru housing as the town's outermost defensive ring; 5 x 10 ken plots; kumi living together along numbered stretches of one street

shibata-ashigaru-nagaya

Shibata ashigaru-nagaya (1842, Important Cultural Property), Hoppou Bunka museum documentation (https://online.bunka.go.jp/heritages/detail/144338)

What it is: The Japanese Agency for Cultural Affairs' heritage listing for the former Shibata domain's ashigaru nagaya in Niigata, a thatched, hipped-roof terrace of 1842 housing eight foot-soldier families under one roof, 43.6 m along the ridge by 7.3 m deep, designated an Important Cultural Property in 1969; the listing describes one household's share as an entrance, two six-mat rooms, a three-mat room and a kitchen.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a surviving, officially documented building of exactly the type we draw for low-retainer housing, so it fixes the default terraced form and supplies real numbers for it: eight households in one range, about 18 ft of frontage each, roughly nine tsubo of floor apiece. Its limits are that it is one late-Edo building from one domain and that practice varied, since Kaga and Hikone housed their foot soldiers in detached houses with gardens, so the terrace is our default rather than a universal rule, and the listing is a modern conservation record of the building as it survives today.

Used for: the default TERRACED form of low-retainer housing - 8 households under one roof, 24 x 3.5 ken, 9 tsubo per household

bukeyashiki-wiki

Japanese Wikipedia, 武家屋敷 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%AD%A6%E5%AE%B6%E5%B1%8B%E6%95%B7)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the buke-yashiki (武家屋敷), the samurai residence of an early modern castle town. It sets out the zoning of such a town: upper samurai in hedged and walled plots inside the middle moat near the castle, and the row houses and small plots of ashigaru and other low-ranking retainers arranged outside them, often massed at the town's entrances for defense.

Why it applies, and its limits: This is the source for the rule our samurai wards follow, that rank sets distance from the castle and that ranks of small uniform housing belong on the outer fringe rather than inside a great house's fence. It is a tertiary summary and gives no plot dimensions, setbacks or counts, so it decides the pattern while the measurements come from elsewhere, and it describes the Edo-period castle town specifically rather than samurai housing in every era.

Used for: the buke-yashiki plot program and the perimeter nagaya; setback and enclosure conventions

buke-hokonin-wiki

Japanese Wikipedia, 武家奉公人 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%AD%A6%E5%AE%B6%E5%A5%89%E5%85%AC%E4%BA%BA)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on buke-hokonin (武家奉公人), the servants of a samurai household: the chugen, komono, genan and kobito who did the household's odd jobs and attended their master out of doors. It covers their live-in status, their annual contracts and how they were hired.

Why it applies, and its limits: Whether servants show up on a city map as their own little houses or as nothing at all turns on whether they lived in their master's compound or in their own, and this article answers that directly: they were live-in staff on term contracts, hired through labor brokers as a household needed them. That the brokers stood in the merchant quarter is our inference, not the page's statement. It is a tertiary summary about people and their conditions of employment, not about buildings, and it describes a specifically Edo-period arrangement, so the drawn form of the quarters they occupied comes from other sources.

Used for: live-in domestic service on annual contracts (degawari), hiring out of the merchant quarter through brokers; chugen as shared-room staff on call

neixiang-yamen

Neixiang county yamen (内乡县衙), Henan - site documentation and plan descriptions (https://cn.chinadaily.com.cn/a/202103/12/WS604b0287a3101e7ce9743b4c.html)

What it is: A feature article by the Henan Daily reporter Zhang Dongyun, republished on China Daily's Chinese-language site in March 2021, on the Neixiang county yamen in Henan - a county government compound first built in 1304 and rebuilt by a late Qing magistrate, now a museum. It describes the plan in detail: more than 47,000 square meters, eighteen courtyards in succession and over 280 rooms, laid out by Ming and Qing regulation with the offices in front and the residence behind, and it names what stands on each side of the first two courtyards.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the plan of a real county yamen in the words of a reporter walking it - the central axis, the two front courtyards, the Six Chambers, the total area and room count, and what stood east and west of the first court - which is what our magistracy plan is laid out from. Its limits are that it is a newspaper feature rather than a survey or excavation report; that the buildings are a late-Qing rebuild conserved and restored as a museum, so the plan is a preserved one rather than a documented original; that its west line holds the guest hall, the dining hall and the jail, not a clerks' lodging, and it names no sheds against the wall - the record uses Neixiang as the counter-example on both, and the runners' duty courtyard it does name stands on the east; and that it is imperial China rather than Japan.

Used for: the three-axis yamen plan, the two front courtyards, the Six Chambers, the area and room count; the runners' duty courtyard on the EAST; the west line as guest hall, dining hall and jail - the counter-example to a clerks' lodging on the west (吏舍 and banfang sheds are not on the page)

pingyao-yamen

Pingyao county yamen (平遥县衙), Shanxi - site documentation (https://baike.baidu.com/item/%E5%B9%B3%E9%81%A5%E5%8E%BF%E8%A1%99/2409876)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the 1619 gongxiefang clerk lodging built behind the west office range; yamen footprint (~200 x 100 m)

daozuofang

倒座房 / 北京四合院 (Chinese Wikipedia and vernacular-architecture summaries) (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%80%92%E5%BA%A7%E6%88%BF)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article 倒座房 (daozuofang, the "reversed" row), on the southernmost range of rooms in a Beijing courtyard house, whose doors and windows face north into the court so that its back is the blank wall along the street. The article sets out what those poorly lit rooms were used for: guest rooms, the servants' quarters, a family school at the east end and the privy at the west.

Why it applies, and its limits: It answers directly the question we ask of it - who lives in the row a walled compound turns toward the street, and why that face is blank - which governs how we draw the street frontage of a courtyard compound and where the servants go. Its limits are that it is a four-sentence encyclopedia stub whose only two references are popular web pages, so it describes the ideal type of the room and no survey or date stands behind it (the use it gives is uncontroversial and appears in the standard literature on the courtyard house, but that literature is not what this link reaches); and that it describes the Beijing siheyuan in particular, a northern Chinese townhouse form, which we apply to our compounds as a family resemblance rather than as a plan to copy.

Used for: servants' quarters in the street-fronting south row whose blank back wall is the compound's street face; rear service row; wings house family, not servants

pingjiang-tu

Pingjiang tu (平江图), the 1229 stone-carved plan of Suzhou, and its scholarship (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%B9%B3%E6%B1%9F%E5%9B%BE)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on the Pingjiang tu (平江图), the plan of Southern Song Suzhou carved in stone in 1229 - the oldest surviving city plan of its kind in China - with references to the urban-planning scholarship on it. It describes the walled rectangle, long on its north-south axis, the broad moat, and the five gates, each a paired land gate and water gate.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the one dated plan of a whole Chinese prefectural city we can point to, and we take from it two things our city maps decide: that a walled city could be a long rectangle rather than a square, and that a canal city's gates come in land-and-water pairs. Its limits are that we read the carving through an encyclopedia summary rather than the object or a study of it; that it is one southern Chinese prefectural city at one date, a larger and wetter place than most of ours; and that a carved plan is a representation, so what it draws is not automatically what stood.

Used for: the walled rectangle long on its north-south axis; five gates, each a paired land gate and water gate

jokamachi-wiki-corpus

Japanese Wiki Corpus, Jokamachi (translation of the ja.wikipedia article) (https://www.japanesewiki.com/history/Jokamachi.html)

What it is: The same translated castle-town article in the Japanese Wiki Corpus, of how a castle town was arranged: the castle at the center, rings of samurai and foot soldier quarters graded by rank, streets named for the trade that worked them, the temple district on the rim, main roads led past the castle's front to show off the lord's standing, and the total outer enclosure that a few great towns built.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the whole grammar of the castle town in English - the castle at the center, the rank-graded rings of samurai residence, the occupational merchant wards, the temple quarter on the rim, the main road led past the castle's front - which is the skeleton every capital map is laid out on. Its limits are that its examples are the largest Japanese castle towns, a scale that has to be reduced before it reaches a provincial seat; that it is a tertiary summary at one further remove, the English being a machine-translation research corpus (the bilingual corpus of Wikipedia's Kyoto-topic articles made by NICT, Japan's National Institute of Information and Communications Technology) and a frozen snapshot rather than the maintained article; and that two other entries in this registry point at the same article and at the Japanese original it translates, so the three cannot corroborate one another.

Used for: the castle at the town's center; concentric rank-graded zoning (Sange / Kamiyashiki-cho for samurai, Ban-cho / Teppo-cho for ashigaru); occupational machi (Gofuku-machi, Kaji-machi); teramachi at the outer rim as part of the city defenses; main roads routed past the castle's front "to indicate the glory of the ruler"; sogamae total enclosure (Odawara, Osaka)

hirosaki-castle

Hirosaki Castle (Wikipedia; Hirosaki Park official site) (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirosaki_Castle)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Hirosaki Castle in Aomori prefecture, seat of the Tsugaru clan, a tozama daimyo house of 47,000 koku. The article gives the castle's plan in meters (612 east to west by 947 north to south) and the keep as a three-story tower 14.4 m high; it gives no area for the enceinte or the keep.

Why it applies, and its limits: A modest domain's castle is the right anchor for a typical capital, and it makes the point a reader most often gets wrong: the tower is a small part of the castle, not the castle itself. It is a tertiary summary, and what it measures is the surviving park as it stands today, so its dimensions approximate the historical footprint rather than survey it. The hectare figures our castle sizing rests on (about 50 ha with the moats, a keep under one) are not on this page: they came from the park's own site, which we have not been able to cite, so they stand as a guess checked against the 612 by 947 m outline the article does give.

Used for: castle enceinte ~50 ha / 123 acres including moats, at a 47,000-koku daimyo; the tenshu itself ~0.6 ha - the anchor for the median capital's castle_px2 and for "the keep is not the castle"

himeji-castle

Himeji Castle (Wikipedia) (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himeji_Castle)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Himeji Castle, the largest and best-preserved of Japan's surviving castles and a World Heritage site. It gives the complex's overall measurements - a circumference of 4,200 m and an area of 233 hectares (576 acres), 107 hectares inside the middle moat, roughly 950 to 1,600 m east to west and 900 to 1,700 m north to south - along with moat dimensions averaging 20 m wide, 34.5 m at the widest and about 2.7 m deep.

Why it applies, and its limits: These are the numbers that fix the grand end of our castle range, and the moat widths in particular set how broad a defensive water line should read on a map. It is a tertiary encyclopedia summary and we take its figures only where its own references support them; Himeji is exceptional rather than typical, so it is an upper bound and never a median, and its measurements are of the modern site as preserved and restored, including ground whose Edo-period use is no longer visible.

Used for: the grand end of the castle band - 233 ha / 576 acres total enceinte, 4,200 m circumference, 107 ha inside the middle moat; moat dimensions (avg 20 m, max 34.5 m, depth ~2.7 m)

hikone-castle-town

Nakasendo Way, The Story of Hikone Castle Town (https://www.nakasendoway.com/the-story-of-hikone-castle-town/)

What it is: "The Story of Hikone Castle Town", a background article on Nakasendo Way, the route guide published by the guided-walking company Walk Japan; no author or date is given. It narrates Hikone from the Ii clan's founding of the town in 1603 through the Meiji dissolution of the samurai class, covering how rank and defense shaped the street plan and the separation of samurai from merchant quarters, and it cites a census of 1695 counting 15,371 townspeople living in 53 wards, noting that samurai and ashigaru were normally exempt from such counts.

Why it applies, and its limits: That census line is our population anchor for a domain capital - it shows what a substantial domain's town actually held in commoners, and, with the exemption noted, why the samurai population has to be estimated separately rather than read off. It is a tour company's history page with no named author and no references, so we use its census figure as a reported number rather than a verified one, and it describes a single town in a single domain, which we treat as one point on a range and not as a standard.

Used for: the 1695 census figure of 15,371 townspeople in 53 wards at a 300,000-koku domain - the population anchor showing a Rokugani capital is a Hikone-scale market town carrying far fewer samurai

okayama-castle

Okayama Castle site and visitor documentation (https://okayama-castle.jp/learn-history-en/)

What it is: The English history page of the official Okayama Castle site, the castle's own visitor documentation, recounting how Ukita Naoie and his son Hideie built the castle on the hill of Okayama in the late sixteenth century, how the branching channels of the Asahi River were arranged to protect its north and east sides and used to form its moats, and how the castle town was laid out long and narrow between them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is an attested Japanese case of a castle taking a river branch as the moat on two of its flanks, which is the thing our river city and edge castle maps need a real example for. It is a site's own account written to introduce the castle to visitors rather than an archaeological or documentary study, so we use it for the fact and the shape of the river diversion and would not press it for dimensions, dates or anything finer than it plainly states.

Used for: Ukita Hideie diverting a branch of the Asahi River as the moat on the castle's northeastern flank - an attested edge castle

kitsuki-castle

Kitsuki castle town coverage (Japan Today; JNTO) (https://japantoday.com/category/features/travel/kitsuki-castle-town-on-the-sea)

Used for: the castle on a promontory between the Yasaka and Takayama river mouths at Morie Bay, samurai quarters and temples on the surrounding hills - the second attested edge castle

beijing-imperial-city

Imperial City, Beijing (Wikipedia) (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_City,_Beijing)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Imperial City, Beijing", on the walled precinct that enclosed the Forbidden City and the imperial services around it. The passage we take describes the Corridor of a Thousand Steps south of Chengtianmen, with the offices of the government ministries lining either side of it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It answers a layout question for a capital map: the bureaucracy sits on the ceremonial avenue outside the palace wall rather than inside it, which decides where the ministry compounds are drawn. Two limits are worth stating plainly - it is a tertiary summary, and that particular sentence is flagged in the article as lacking a citation, so we treat the arrangement as a plausible shape to draw rather than as a documented fact; and Ming and Qing Beijing is a Chinese imperial capital standing in for our setting's own, which is modeled on both China and Japan.

Used for: the Six Ministries' offices flanking the Corridor of a Thousand Steps outside Chengtianmen - the bureaucracy on the ceremonial avenue OUTSIDE the palace walls

liufang-yamen

Qing local-government scholarship on the 六房三班 organization; Pingyao county yamen documentation (https://zhuanlan.zhihu.com/p/660442182)

Used for: the six fang as ROOMS (side halls flanking the yamen courtyards) rather than separate buildings at county scale; ~300 rooms across Pingyao's courts

nagoya-castle

Nagoya Castle, the English Wikipedia article (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagoya_Castle)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Nagoya Castle, the great Tokugawa castle of Owari domain, describing its enclosures and buildings - the keep in the inner bailey, the Ninomaru palace that served as both the lord's residence and the domain's supreme government office, and the third bailey holding the auxiliary mansions of the lords and the houses of senior retainers.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the arrangement our castle plans follow: the palace at the core as both residence and the working seat of domain government, the keep a separate defensive tower, and the third bailey given over to the mansions of the senior retainers. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, and that Nagoya was one of the largest castles in the country, so it stands for the arrangement of a castle's parts and never for their size at a provincial seat.

Used for: the Sannomaru Oyakata mansions in the third bailey; the goten as the administrative and residential center as distinct from the defensive tenshu

matsumoto-goten

Matsumoto Castle official site, Honmaru/Ninomaru Goten pages (https://www.matsumoto-castle.jp/eng/about/palace)

What it is: The pages of the official Matsumoto Castle site describing the Honmaru and Ninomaru palaces that stood inside the castle's enclosures.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives one Edo-period case of a castle's inner enclosure proving too small for its government: after the 1727 fire the functions moved to the second bailey, and because that was smaller the county office, the town office and the hall where the lord met the village headmen were moved out into the town - the warrant for putting some of a castle town's official buildings outside the walls on our maps. Its limits are that it is the castle's own visitor page, unsigned, undated and without citations; that it is a single case; and that its dates want care, since the page carries at least one internal error in them.

Used for: the county office and town office being moved OUT of the castle into Rokku town when the ninomaru proved too small, and the daimyo/headman conference hall to Agetsuchi town

edo-machi-kido

Edopedia (edoflourishing), machi - towns and villages; Edo nagaya/roji coverage (https://edoflourishing.blogspot.com/2013/10/machi-towns-and-villages.html)

What it is: A page from "Edo - the EDOPEDIA", an encyclopedia blog on the historical city compiled by Gabi Greve as a project of the Daruma Museum, posted in 2013 and titled "machi - towns and villages". Its account of how the city was divided into blocks is drawn from the historian Oishi Manabu's book on Edo place names, and it records that each block was closed by a wooden gate, the kido, open from about four in the morning until about ten at night.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the gate that shuts a block at night and its hours, which is what tells us that these gates belong on a town map at all and roughly where. It says nothing about who held the keys or about the smaller gates on the tenement lanes; those rest on other sources. Its limits are that it is an enthusiast's compilation rather than a peer-reviewed or closely referenced work, whose figures are another book's, so we use it for the shape of the practice rather than as an authority on any figure; that the hours are given flat, with no date inside a period of two and a half centuries; and that it describes Edo, by far the largest city in Japan, which we apply to smaller towns with care.

Used for: the machi-level kido barring each town block (open ~4 am to ~10 pm); block-level collective responsibility for the gate (the finer roji-kido / nagaya-kido, their hours and who held their keys are not on the page)

qing-zhalan

Qing Beijing street-fence coverage (Dashilan street history; Beijing city fortifications; imperial-curfew reporting) (https://www.atlantis-press.com/proceedings/ssha-23/125988752)

What it is: "Dashilan Street: The Fence Culture of Ancient Beijing", a conference paper by Fei Liang, Shaoshuai Liu and Xiaoxi Liu in the proceedings of the 2023 International Conference on Social Sciences and Humanities and Arts, published by Atlantis Press. Drawing on court paintings, old maps and archival photographs, it traces the ornamental street gates and palings (zhalan, 栅栏) that gave the Dashilan commercial district its name, and their role in the curfew and block policing of Ming and Qing Beijing. Reporting on the curfew hours and penalties themselves is gathered under the same key.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is directly about the feature we draw - a fence closing a street at night - and it supplies the reason such a fence existed, which is the curfew that a city's night watch enforced. Its limits are that it is a short conference paper rather than a full study, and that Beijing is an imperial capital and so the strictest case of urban policing in the empire: a provincial town would have had a looser version of the same arrangement, and the specific curfew hours and lash counts come from separate reporting rather than from this paper.

Used for: zhalan (栅栏) palings closing each street at night, cooperating with the curfew and block governance, and Dashilan named for its gate (the curfew hours, the drum and bell times, the lash counts and the sentry points are not on the page)

jokamachi-zoning

Jokamachi surveys (Nakasendo Way, Castle Towns) (https://www.nakasendoway.com/castle-towns-2/)

What it is: The "Castle Towns" essay in the Nakasendo Way series that Walk Japan, a company running guided walks along the old Nakasendo road, publishes alongside its tours. It explains how a castle town separated samurai from townspeople, and notes that the townspeople's wards typically formed narrow strips between different bodies of samurai, laid along the main thoroughfares.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the shape of the castle town's zoning - merchant wards forming strips along the main roads that separated one group of samurai from another, and the precise spatial arrangement that kept samurai and townspeople apart - which is what the ring-and-strip layout of our capitals is drawn from. Its limits are that it is a tour operator's essay for travelers, unsigned and without references, usable for the shape of the arrangement and for nothing numeric; that the same zoning is better anchored in the Japanese encyclopedia article; and that it says nothing about the walls and gates of individual compounds or about the size of merchant plots, so those rest on other sources.

Used for: chonin wards forming narrow strips that SEPARATE different groups of samurai, sited along the major thoroughfares; the precise spatial arrangement separating samurai from townspeople (the per-compound walls and gates, and the size of chonin plots, are not on the page)

edo-josui

Tokyo Waterworks Historical Museum; Tokyo Metropolitan Waterworks, Tamagawa Josui pages; Tamagawa Aqueduct (Wikipedia); IHCSA Cafe, Tamagawa Josui: Edo's Precious Waterworks (https://ihcsacafe-en.ihcsa.or.jp/news/josui/)

What it is: A group of public pages on the Tamagawa Josui, the aqueduct that carried drinking water into Edo from 1653: the Tokyo Waterworks Historical Museum and the Tokyo Metropolitan Waterworks pages on the channel, the Wikipedia article, and an unsigned 2022 article, "Tamagawa Josui: Edo's Precious Waterworks", on IHCSA Cafe, the English culture site of the International Hospitality and Conference Service Association. The IHCSA page is the one we link and the one our English quotations come from; the Japanese passage on the channel's length and fall, the unlined excavation, the buried pipe length and the count of draw-wells come from the other three, which the entry names but does not link. Between them they describe roughly forty-three kilometers of open cut from the Hamura intake to the Yotsuya gate, falling only about twenty-one centimeters in every hundred meters, and then buried stone and wooden pipe inside the city feeding thousands of draw-wells.

Why it applies, and its limits: This is the best-documented premodern water supply in Japan and it answers the questions a city map asks - where an aqueduct is an open channel and where it goes underground, what gradient it needs, what the pipes were made of, who built them, and how the water reached an ordinary tenement's well; the era and the place are exactly ours, so nothing here is a stand-in. Its limits are that these are museum, utility and cultural-outreach pages written for a general reader rather than engineering history, mostly unsigned, so the lengths and gradients are round public figures; that one key bundles four works behind one link, so a reader who follows it cannot read everything the key is credited with; and that Edo was the largest city in the country, so its waterworks is an upper bound that a provincial town would never match.

Used for: the two-part josui - ~43 km of OPEN cut "excavated without timbering" from Hamura to the Yotsuya gate, then ~67 km of BURIED stone (sekihi) and wooden (mokuhi) pipe inside the city feeding 3,600+ draw-wells and cisterns; ship's carpenters as the trade that laid the wooden pipe

kanda-kakehi

Tokyo Metropolitan Library, Ochanomizu Aqueduct, Kanda Service Water Supply Flume; Nippon.com on Suidobashi; Tokyo Waterworks Historical Museum (Kanda Aqueduct stone pipe) (https://www.library.metro.tokyo.lg.jp/portals/0/edo/tokyo_library/modal/index.html?d=5655)

What it is: A digitized Edo-period view with the Tokyo Metropolitan Library's own commentary, showing the Kanda Josui - Edo's water supply - crossing above the Kanda River in a kakehi (懸樋), an open wooden flume carried on a bridge at Ochanomizu, read alongside a Nippon.com piece on Suidobashi and the Tokyo Waterworks Historical Museum's material on the aqueduct's stone pipes.

Why it applies, and its limits: For the question we ask of it - whether a city's water main crossed a river in the open, visibly, where a map would have to draw it - this is about as direct as evidence gets: a contemporary picture, a surviving place name meaning "aqueduct bridge", and a public library's description of both. The limits are scale and singularity: this is the shogunal capital, the largest city in the country, and one celebrated crossing there does not establish that a provincial city of ours would have built the same thing, only that the form existed and was ordinary enough to be a famous sight.

Used for: the kakehi (懸樋) carrying the Kanda Josui over the Kanda River at Ochanomizu as an open flume on a bridge; Suidobashi ("aqueduct bridge") named for it; Hiroshige's depiction - the attested above-ground crossing

osaka-kurayashiki

Dojima Rice Exchange (JPX/ODEX); Japanese Wiki Corpus, Daimyogashi; rice-broker surveys (https://www.jpx.co.jp/dojima/en/index.html)

What it is: The English page on the Dojima Rice Exchange published by the exchange itself (the Osaka Dojima Exchange, through Japan Exchange Group), which recounts how the domains sold their tax rice through kurayashiki, the combined warehouses and residences clustered around Nakanoshima in Osaka, and how the rice bills issued to successful bidders were then traded; the entry also gathers a Japanese Wiki Corpus article on daimyogashi (lending to domain lords against their rice) and surveys of the rice brokers.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us that the daimyo's warehouse-residences stood at the market, in the commercial capital where the rice was sold, rather than at the domain seat - which is why our capitals do not carry rows of them. Its limits are that it is an institution's history of itself, not scholarship; that it describes one exceptional city at the peak of one trade; that the key bundles two other works that are neither linked nor quoted, so nothing readable rests on them; and that the figure of more than a hundred such houses at the early-1800s peak is not in the passage we quote, so it stands as a guess until a page carries it.

Used for: kurayashiki as the daimyo's warehouse-residence AT THE MARKET (Osaka, Nakanoshima) where tax rice was auctioned against rice bills - i.e. the SELLING end, not the domain capital (the count of 110+ at the early-1800s peak is not in the quoted passage)

asakusa-kuramae

Asakusa / Kuramae district histories; Rice broker (Wikipedia) (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_broker)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Rice broker", on the fudasashi of Edo - the licensed brokers of Kuramae in Asakusa, "before the storehouses", who warehoused the stipend rice of the shogunate's retainers, converted it to coin and lent against it, and whose wealth fed the neighboring entertainment district.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the institution our capitals draw beside the government granaries - brokers who turned tax rice into money, clustered before the storehouses - which is what puts a brokers' street next to the granary on our maps. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary carrying the encyclopedia's own notice that it lacks inline citations, so nothing in it can be checked against a reference; that it does not say where the shogunate's storehouses stood - the riverside site on the Sumida our maps assume is not on this page and rests on other sources; and that it describes the capital's brokers, whose scale no provincial city approaches.

Used for: Kuramae (蔵前) "before the storehouses" as the brokers' district; fudasashi warehousing, converting and lending against stipend rice, and their wealth seeding the neighboring entertainment district (that the shogunate's granaries stood on the Sumida riverside is not on the page)

kuramai

Britannica, kuramai; koku-system surveys (https://www.britannica.com/topic/kuramai)

Used for: peasants paying tax rice up to the DOMAIN granary, from which samurai stipends were paid - the collecting-and-disbursing role of a castle town as distinct from the market

shaoxing-towpath

Shaoxing ancient towpath coverage (CGTN; Shanghai Daily); Grand Canal surveys (https://news.cgtn.com/news/3d3d674d7755444d33457a6333566d54/index.html)

What it is: A 2019 travel feature on CGTN, the Chinese state broadcaster's English site, credited to Shanghai Daily and unsigned, on the qiandao towpath at Shaoxing on the Eastern Zhejiang Canal - the stone path along which boats were hauled, in its two forms, one laid on the bank and one carried on piers about half a meter above the water - as it survives and is presented to visitors today. The page dates the canal itself to the fifth century BC and gives the best-preserved stretch as about eight kilometers.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the form of the towpath - a real riverside way, and the slab-on-piers kind standing just above the water - which is what a towpath on our canal maps is drawn from. Its limits are that it is a broadcaster's travel feature rather than a survey or an archaeological report; that it describes a preserved heritage structure as it stands now, whose fabric is later than the institution; that the date, the length and the Marco Polo detail the record once attached to this key are not on the page and rest on nothing readable, so they stand as guesses; and that a Chinese canal stands in for a setting whose surface is often Japanese.

Used for: the qiandao (纤道) towpath as the real riverside way, in its two forms: bank-side, and slab stones on stone piers standing ~0.5 m above the water parallel to the bank (the page dates the canal to the 5th century BC and gives the best-preserved stretch as about 8 km; an 815 CE date, a 40+ km length and Marco Polo's horse teams are not on it)

edo-river-transport

IDE "Japanese Experience" archive on Edo distribution; Nakasendo Way, River and Sea Transport; Oi-kawa crossing histories (https://www.nakasendoway.com/river-and-sea-transport/)

Used for: river transport carrying the tribute rice with a cargo role equivalent to the roads' military/administrative one; Japan's short fast rivers preventing an extensive inland waterway network; and roads NOT following rivers - bridges and ferries prohibited at the Oi-kawa on the Tokaido so the river served as a checkpoint

kotobank-benjo

日本大百科全書(ニッポニカ) 「privy (benjo), the toilet」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「便所」), via kotobank.jp/word/便所-131200 (https://kotobank.jp/word/便所-131200)

What it is: The entry for 便所 (benjo, the privy) from Shogakukan's encyclopedia Nipponica, reprinted on the reference site kotobank. It describes how privies were arranged in different kinds of Japanese household - paired and attached to the main buildings in samurai and court-noble residences, gathered into one small separate building for a compound's rowhouses, set behind the guest parlor in townhouses and farmhouses, and provided in farm households as a single building standing clear of the main house.

Why it applies, and its limits: It answers the placement question a map has to answer - whether the privy is inside the house or a separate outbuilding - and it says the detached farm privy holding both the urinal and the privy was the usual arrangement, which is what we draw. Being an encyclopedia entry it is a tertiary summary and it describes a custom rather than counting it: "usual" is not a percentage, so how often our farmsteads show one is our own choice resting on the form the entry gives.

Used for: the farm privy as ONE independent outbuilding holding the urinal and the privy, "普通".

sinyoken-madori

sinyoken.sakura.ne.jp, 「Changes in the position of the toilet seen from the floor plan」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「間取りからみたトイレの位置の変遷」) (camadori.htm) and cayomo016.htm (https://sinyoken.sakura.ne.jp/caffee/camadori.htm)

What it is: An unsigned essay on a private Japanese website about traditional architecture (sinyoken.sakura.ne.jp), undated, on the floor plans of Japanese houses from the warrior residence of the Kamakura period through the Edo townhouse and farmhouse and on into the urban housing of Meiji and after. It reads published plans and lists three works as references, and it relates the farmhouse privy's position to the barn and the back door.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives where the privy stood in a house of rank - an upper and a lower privy, a form with Kamakura roots - and relates the farm privy to the barn and the back door, which is what places the privy on our farmsteads. Its limits are that it is a self-published essay with no named author and no editorial control, the weakest kind of source; that much of its narrative is post-Meiji and modern, and the passage one of our footnotes quotes is explicitly about urban houses after Meiji, so the era has to be selected out of it rather than assumed; and that the upper-and-lower privy is drawn from the warrior residence, a different scale of place from the farmstead it is used to populate.

Used for: where the farm privy stood - by the naya, at the back door, toward the 背戸口, the 戸口便所.

artic-pigsty-latrine

Art Institute of Chicago, "Model of a Pigsty and Latrines", Eastern Han, object 37716 - (https://www.artic.edu/artworks/37716)

What it is: The Art Institute of Chicago's catalog entry for a Han dynasty tomb model of a pigsty and latrine - a gray earthenware miniature of about 40 by 22 by 28 cm, dated 100 BCE to 100 CE - whose curatorial note records that latrines were customarily built above a pigsty and connected by pipes to a cesspool, comparing the form to models excavated at Huixian. The museum's own page may not open, but its catalog text is public.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives one attested customary structure - the privy over the sty, the muck collected beside it - which is the placement rule that keeps the muck heap and the privy together on our homesteads. Its limits are that it is a catalog note rather than an excavation report; that a tomb model is an idealized miniature; that it is Han China, far earlier than the setting's Japan-leaning period; and that farm-scale pig keeping is a Chinese practice rather than an Edo Japanese one, so what transfers to a Japan-leaning map is the privy-and-muck adjacency, not the sty itself.

Used for: the Han pigsty-privy as one customary structure - the muck clusters with the privy.

boso-no-mura-kigoya

Chiba Prefectural Museum, Boso-no-Mura, the reconstructed farmstead's 木小屋 page (https://www.chiba-muse.or.jp/MURA/facility/page-1521181891660/page-1521294570333/)

What it is: A page from the Chiba Prefectural Museum's open-air museum Boso-no-Mura, describing one outbuilding of its reconstructed late-Edo farmstead: the kigoya (木小屋), the shed that held the charcoal and firewood a household burned. The farmstead is a village-headman-class house assembled from two real houses of the region (the Akiha house of Sago, whose roof plaques are dated 1857, and material from the Naito house of Ichihara).

Why it applies, and its limits: A museum describing its own reconstruction is good evidence for what a farmyard outbuilding was and what went in it, which is what puts a fuel shed on our farmsteads at all. It is a reconstruction of one region's farmhouse presented for visitors rather than an excavation report or a survey; it is a well-off headman's farmstead, composed from two houses, used here as evidence for the ordinary homestead's yard; and the page says nothing about where in the yard the shed stood, so the placement on our maps rests on other reasoning.

Used for: a firewood/charcoal shed on the farmstead. Its PLACEMENT is not on the page.

jawiki-koedame

ja.wikipedia 肥溜め and 下肥 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/肥溜め)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia articles on 肥溜め (koedame, the night soil pit) and 下肥 (shimogoe, night soil used as manure). They describe the pit or buried jar in which a household's human waste was collected and left to ferment before it went onto the fields, giving the two forms it took and the rough size of the jar kind.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us the two forms the night-soil pit took and how big the buried-jar kind was, which is what the pit glyph beside a farmhouse is drawn from. Its limits are that it is a tertiary encyclopedia summary whose descriptive body cites no references at all, so the size figure we take is an uncited statement we rely on for want of anything better; that it frames the practice as a long continuity running from the Edo period into the twentieth century, which is what makes it readable and also what stops it being a period measurement; and that it says nothing about where the pit stood on a plot, so its placement on our maps rests on other sources.

Used for: night soil fermented in buried jars/plastered pits, 1-1.5 m across (no fermentation duration on the page). Location not stated.

mizumaki-goemonburo

Mizumaki town historical museum, 五右衛門風呂 (https://www.town.mizumaki.lg.jp/museum/040/020/050/550/1258.html)

What it is: A page from the historical museum of the town of Mizumaki on the goemonburo (五右衛門風呂), the iron-bottomed bath tub heated by a fire lit directly underneath it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us in a museum's own caption that the cauldron bath was widely used in self-sufficient farming villages, which is what lets an ordinary farmstead on our maps have a bath. Its limits are that it is one object's caption from one town museum in Fukuoka, northern Kyushu, with no survey and no proportion behind "widely"; and that the object it describes is dated by the museum to the postwar Showa years, so the page attests the practice in living memory and reaches our period only as a continuity argument.

Used for: the goemon-buro "used widely in self-sufficient farm villages".

cambridge-animals-china

Animals through Chinese History, Cambridge, chapter "Where did the animals go" (the chapter leans on 1930s survey data by its own account) (https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/animals-through-chinese-history/where-did-the-animals-go/E281204414087F0A9ACCDDCEF5358C08)

What it is: Francesca Bray's chapter "Where Did the Animals Go? Presence and Absence of Livestock in Chinese Agricultural Treatises", in Animals through Chinese History, edited by Roel Sterckx, Martina Siebert and Dagmar Schafer (Cambridge University Press, 2018). It is a peer-reviewed scholarly chapter reading four centuries-spanning Chinese farming manuals to ask whether livestock really vanished from Chinese farms, and argues that they remained ordinary even where the manuals stopped writing about them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It supports the modest claim our farmyards need, that an ordinary smallholding kept a pig and some poultry in the yard, and it does so from an authority that has weighed the counter-argument rather than assumed it. Two honest limits: the picture of the ordinary household leans on twentieth-century survey data, so a 1930s count is standing in for the older practice the treatises describe, and it is about China, while the farmstead our maps draw is Japanese in its surface detail.

Used for: "farmers in most regions of China managed to keep a pig and some chickens in their yard".

qimin-yaoshu-yangji

齊民要術 卷六 養雞第五十九, wikisource (四庫全書本) (https://zh.wikisource.org/wiki/齊民要術_(四庫全書本)/卷06)

What it is: The chapter on raising chickens (卷六 養雞第五十九) of the Qimin Yaoshu (齊民要術, "Essential Techniques for the Common People"), the sixth-century Chinese agricultural treatise compiled by Jia Sixie, read in the Siku Quanshu edition hosted on Chinese Wikisource. Its instruction on housing is direct: set a cage on the ground with a perch inside it rather than letting the birds roost in the trees, where wind and cold thin the big ones and kill the small.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a farming manual from the very tradition our setting is modeled on, telling us how a poultry shelter was meant to be built and why, which is what fixes the ground-level coop in a farmyard rather than a roost in a tree. Three limits worth stating: a manual prescribes good practice and does not record what an average household did; it is sixth-century northern China, far in both time and place from the Edo Japan our maps also draw on; and the text is a much later imperial edition of it, given here in this project's own English rendering of the classical Chinese.

Used for: the chicken roost as a ground-level enclosure with a perch, preferred to the trees.

pitt-zhengzhou-coop

University of Pittsburgh HAA news, "Tao and colleagues publish on a 400-year-old Chinese chicken coop" (https://www.haa.pitt.edu/news/tao-and-colleagues-publish-400-year-old-chinese-chicken-coop)

What it is: A news item from the University of Pittsburgh's Department of History of Art and Architecture announcing that graduate student Yuqing Tao and colleagues had published, in the Chinese journal Popular Archaeology (大众考古), a report on a chicken coop of about the early seventeenth century found during a 2023 salvage excavation at Zhengzhou. The coop was a square structure with six niche-like openings in its west wall, some of them holding eggshell fragments.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is excavated evidence, from late Ming China, of how an ordinary urban household actually housed its chickens, which is the form our farmyard coops are drawn from. Its limits are that it is a short departmental announcement rather than the excavation report itself, that it rests on a single structure at a single town site, and that the page carries no measurements at all, so it can settle what a coop looked like and never how big one was.

Used for: a late-Ming square coop with six niche openings and eggshell. No size given.

zhwiki-liuchu

zh.wikipedia 六畜 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/六畜)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article 六畜 (liuchu, "the six domestic animals"), a short tertiary encyclopedia entry on the classical Chinese grouping of livestock - horse, ox, sheep, pig, dog and chicken - that Chinese writing has treated as a set since antiquity.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one small thing, that the chicken belongs to that classical set, which is what puts a hen-house on a farmstead in a setting built on imperial China and Japan; the article is in Chinese because the category is a Chinese literary one. It is a tertiary summary of a term rather than evidence of how many birds any particular farm kept, so it settles that chickens belong on a farm and nothing at all about their numbers.

Used for: the chicken as one of the six livestock.

tokushima-yashikigami

徳島県立図書館 紀要 50, pp. 131-133, 屋敷神 (https://library.bunmori.tokushima.jp/digital/webkiyou/50/131-133.pdf)

What it is: A short field report, "On the yashikigami of Misato Village" (美郷村の屋敷神について) by Yuasa Yasuo, published in 2004 in issue 50 of the Bulletin of the Awa Academic Society (阿波学会紀要, pp. 131-133) by that society's history section, the Tokushima Historical Society, and posted online by the Tokushima Prefectural Library. It walks house by house through one village in Tokushima Prefecture on Shikoku, recording where each family's household guardian deity is enshrined and what its little shrine is made of.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a house-by-house record of a real village's household shrines - which houses kept one, what it was made of, where it stood, when it was honored - which is what puts a shrine of the right size in the right place on our homesteads, and it gives one measured shrine, a stone box about 40 cm on a side, and one under a persimmon tree 20 m east of the house. Its limits are that it is a 2004 record of a dying custom, taken from elderly informants' memory; that it is one village on Shikoku; and that its statement that the shrine usually stood at the southwest corner is a general remark in its opening paragraph, while its own table of fifteen houses puts the shrines overwhelmingly east and southeast and not one in the southwest - so the southwest corner is a handbook generalization the village itself does not show, and our maps roll the corner rather than fix it.

Used for: the two patterns (every house / old families only), one 40 cm stone hokora, one persimmon 20 m east of a homestead; the SW corner is the report's general statement, while its own fifteen-house table puts the shrines east and southeast and none in the southwest

jawiki-yashikigami

ja.wikipedia 屋敷神; minka-en.com 屋敷神; satologue.com 屋敷神様 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/屋敷神)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 屋敷神 (yashikigami, the guardian deity of a household's own plot), together with matching pages on the same subject from two Japanese folk-culture sites, minka-en.com and satologue.com. They cover the two patterns of household worship and the form the shrine takes, which is almost always a small stone or wooden hokora rather than a building.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us the form of the household shrine - a small stone or wooden hokora, almost never a shrine hall - and where it commonly stood, which the page gives as the northwest or the northeast of the plot without preferring either; the page goes on to say that treating the northeast as the unlucky demon gate is a later overlay from Onmyodo and a comparatively recent custom, so our maps take a corner and not a compass rule. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary which Japanese Wikipedia itself flags as under-sourced and possibly original research, a heavier discount than the usual encyclopedia caveat; that it describes a folk custom that varied by region and household, so it gives a common pattern and not a rule; and that it dates none of it, so nothing here fixes the practice to the era our maps depict.

Used for: the two patterns; a small stone or wooden hokora at the plot's corner, especially the NE 鬼門.

kameyama-yashikigami

亀山市史 民俗編, 屋敷神 (kameyamarekihaku.jp) - (https://kameyamarekihaku.jp/sisi/MinzokuHP/jirei/bunrui3/data3-4/index3_4_1.htm)

What it is: A page from the folklore volume of the 亀山市史 (the official city history of Kameyama, Mie prefecture), published online by the city's historical museum, collecting recorded examples of yashikigami - the small household deity shrines kept within a farmstead's grounds - and where on the grounds each stood.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives real cases of where a household shrine stood - at a corner of the homestead grounds, in a stone wall, south of the main house - which is what confirms that the shrine on our maps belongs at a corner rather than in the open yard. Its limits are that it is twentieth-century folklore fieldwork on a much older practice; that it is one district of Ise; and that it records six individual cases and no tally, so no corner weighting can come from it - the cases it gives put the shrine at the southwest and northwest more often than the northeast - and the corner weights our maps once attached to it stand as a guess.

Used for: a yashikigami hokora at a corner of the homestead grounds - six named cases (SW, NW, SW, south of the main house, NW in a stone wall, SE); the page carries no tally, so no corner weighting comes from it

toyoko-kaki

「Why are persimmon trees planted in the front yards of farmhouses?」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「農家の庭先にはなぜ柿の木が植えられているのか？」), ameblo.jp/toyoko-housing (https://ameblo.jp/toyoko-housing/entry-11418946119.html)

What it is: A blog post of December 2012, "Why are persimmon trees planted in the front yards of farmhouses?" (農家の庭先にはなぜ柿の木が植えられているのか？), on the Ameba blog of Toyoko Housing, a rental-property company. It is a conversational piece about why the persimmon is the dooryard tree of the Japanese farmhouse, citing the Edo-period agronomist Miyazaki Yasusada's encouragement to plant them around the house and listing the tree's practical virtues.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the persimmon standing in every dooryard, the Edo-period agricultural recommendation behind that habit, and the summer shade it throws over the house - the reasons our homesteads get a fruit tree by the door rather than an ornamental one. It is a company blog written for general readers, not a scholarly source: the Miyazaki attribution is reported at second hand, and the "every dooryard" is a modern writer's impression of the countryside, so we use it for the practice and its rationale and never as a measured prevalence.

Used for: a persimmon in every dooryard; Miyazaki Yasusada's encouragement; summer shade for the house.

uekipedia-kaki

植木ペディア カキ (https://www.uekipedia.jp/落葉広葉樹-カ行/カキ/)

What it is: The entry for kaki (persimmon) in Uekipedia (植木ペディア), a Japanese online tree encyclopedia maintained by a person working in the gardening trade, which catalogs ornamental and useful trees by flower, fruit, leaf, growing conditions and landscape use for gardeners and landscapers choosing what to plant.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one measurement, the persimmon's mature height of 3 to 20 m, which sets how large a dooryard fruit tree may be drawn. It is a horticultural reference site rather than a peer-reviewed one, and it is describing the tree as planted in Japan today; height is a species trait that changes little between then and now, but the entry gives no crown width, so the spread of the tree on our maps rests on other grounds.

Used for: persimmon height 3-20 m. No crown width.

326woods-stack

326-woods.com, 薪の保管方法 (https://326-woods.com/2021/02/09/keepwoods/)

What it is: A 2021 how-to post, 薪の保管方法 ("how to store firewood"), on the site of 326-woods, a Japanese firewood supplier. It tells a customer where to put a woodpile and how high to build it, and says that even heavy split wood can be stacked to about 1.5 m and is best kept under an eave.

Why it applies, and its limits: A woodpile is one of the few farmyard features whose height is set by what a person can lift and stack rather than by fashion, so a working supplier's own advice is a reasonable guide to how tall the stack beside a farmhouse should be drawn. It is nonetheless a modern trade page and not a survey of anything old: the 1.5 m figure describes present-day practice with present-day tools and sheeting, and we use it as an upper bound rather than as a measurement of a premodern woodpile.

Used for: a stack to ~1.5 m high, labeled modern.

fei-1939

Fei Hsiao-tung, Peasant Life in China: A Field Study of Country Life in the Yangtze Valley (Routledge, 1939) - https://archive.org/details/in.ernet.dli.2015.76327, full text

What it is: Peasant Life in China: A Field Study of Country Life in the Yangtze Valley by Fei Hsiao-tung (Routledge, 1939), the classic village ethnography of Kaixiangong, a silk-and-rice village on the lower Yangtze delta near Lake Tai. It records the place household by household: how the houses stand along the streams, what land a family held, boats, manure pits, and silkworms reared indoors on stacked trays.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a first-hand, room-by-room account of exactly the kind of settlement these maps draw, and almost everything we take from it - where the manure pit sits, that nearly every household kept a boat, that the village grew along its water, where rice was threshed - is arrangement rather than technology, and so belongs to the older village as much as to the one Fei walked through. The limits are real: it is 1936, with railways, motor roads and a silk economy already disrupted by mechanized filatures, and Fei chose this village because an industrial reform of its silk-making was under way there, which is a bias toward exactly the silk arrangements we take from it; his fieldwork was two months of one summer, by his own statement not a full year's cycle, so the seasonal claims we draw on are a summer observer's; it is one village in one delta rather than a survey; it is Chinese, standing beside the Japanese material for a setting built on both; and the copy we can read is a scanned text whose garbles the footnotes carry.

Read from the downloaded full text, each quote located in it. - II-4 "Nearly every household possesses at least one boat" and XIV-3 "Nearly every household possesses one or more boats except those who are not engaged in agricultural or fishing work." Average holding "about 8.5 mow" (XI), 3,065 mow over 450 adult men

Used for: silkworms reared inside the dwelling on tray stands (five stands of eight 1.5 x 1 m trays per house; every room but the kitchen at the peak); home reeling; nearly every household owning one or more boats and the village laid out along its streams; the village agent boat; manure kept in half-buried earthenware pits behind the house and along the road; mulberry on the 10-30 m raised margin that is also the dyke, houses on it, vegetables beneath it; sheep huts as a 1920s innovation; rice threshed in the open space before the house or the front room

fao-ac264e

FAO/NACA, Integrated Fish Farming in China, ch. 7 "Introduction of Chinese integrated fish farming and some major models" (https://www.fao.org/4/ac264e/ac264e09.htm)

What it is: Integrated Fish Farming in China, a report from the UN Food and Agriculture Organization and the Network of Aquaculture Centres in Asia-Pacific; the chapter we cite introduces the major Chinese models of combining fish ponds with other livestock, including pig sheds built on the pond dike or out over the water and duck ponds with a fenced dry run on the bank.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives us the physical arrangement of a pond that also carries animals - where the shed stands, how the duck run meets the water - which is what a map has to draw. It describes practice as observed on the collective and state farms of the 1980s, so we use it for the form of the thing and never as evidence of how common such a pond was in a premodern village, and the stocking rate it gives (one to five pigs per mu of pond) is a modern intensity we do not draw from.

Used for: the pig shed "constructed on the pond dyke or over the water surface" (ponds under 8 mu), 1-5 pigs per mu; fish-cum-duck ponds with a fenced dry run on the dike and a wet run in a corner of the water. Modern practice (1980s), cited as the FORM, not as premodern prevalence

fao-ac264e-ch9

FAO/NACA, Integrated Fish Farming in China, ch. 9 "Animal raising" (https://www.fao.org/4/ac264e/AC264E11.htm)

What it is: The animal-raising chapter, by Chen Yaowang, of the same report - NACA Technical Manual 7, Network of Aquaculture Centres in Asia and the Pacific, Bangkok, 1989. Where ch. 7 introduces the models, this chapter describes how the animals are actually housed and fed beside the water: where a pigsty stands, how its floor is laid so the waste runs the way it is meant to, and what the manure does once it reaches the pond.

Why it applies, and its limits: It states the PURPOSE of the arrangement a map has to draw - the shed is on the dike so the excreta reach the pond - and it gives the mechanism by which manure is food rather than fouling, which is the question a reader of the map asks. Its limits are the same as its parent chapter's and no smaller: it is training-manual guidance from a Chinese research and training center in the 1980s, prescribing as much as it observes, with cement floors and discharge ditches that a premodern shed did not have, so we take the ARRANGEMENT and the reasoning from it and never a number, a rate or a claim about how common such a pond was before the modern period.

Used for: pigsties built on the pond dike so the excrement is flushed directly into the pond; manure fertilizing the water to produce plankton as natural fish food

fao-ac264e-ch10

FAO/NACA, Integrated Fish Farming in China, ch. 10 "Design and construction of an integrated fish farm" (https://www.fao.org/4/ac264e/AC264E13.htm)

What it is: The design chapter of the same report (NACA Technical Manual 7, Bangkok, 1989): how a fish farm is laid out - the widths of dikes, the arrangement of inflow and outflow canals, where the animal houses stand relative to the ponds and the roads.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the only source read that puts a NUMBER on the thing a dike-pond map draws at every parcel edge - how wide a dike must be, and how much wider when it carries a pigsty rather than only a footpath. That is a constructional requirement rather than a regional fashion, so it travels better than most of this report's figures. Two limits, both real: it is modern design guidance for farms being built in the 1980s, not a survey of what old dikes measured, so it tells us what a dike carrying a shed NEEDS and not what any particular premodern dike WAS; that only the FLOOR of its 5-10 m range bears on a premodern map, because the quoted clause bundles pigsties with "cow sheds piping, or traffic" and the top of the range is plausibly set by carts and pipework a hand-piled mulberry dike never carried, so the source supports "at least 5 m, because it carries a shed" rather than a two-sided corroboration; and its account of pig waste going to septic tanks for fermentation before use describes a modern sanitary practice that sits oddly beside ch. 7's direct-flow shed, so the two are recorded as a tension rather than reconciled.

Used for: a dike carrying a pigsty or cow shed runs 5-10 m wide against 5 m or less for a plain inter-pond dike; duck and goose pens built separately along the dikes; pigsties on dikes or on high ground close to the pond

fao-ac257e

FAO, Report of Consultancy at the Regional Lead Centre in China for Integrated Fish Farming (https://www.fao.org/4/ac257e/AC257E05.htm)

What it is: A working paper by Peter Edwards of the Asian Institute of Technology, Bangkok - NACA/WP/82/6, Network of Aquaculture Centres in Asia, October 1982, hosted in the UN Food and Agriculture Organization's document repository - on the Chinese regional lead center for integrated fish farming, recording what was observed at the ponds and what was recommended. Its subject here is what happens in the water directly below a livestock shed built on the dike.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the ONE source read that addresses where manure enters a pond rather than how much of it does, which is the nearest anything comes to the question this map raises - and the answer it gives is that the problem is concentration at the point of entry, cured by spreading the manure, while the shed stays on the dike. That is why it is cited: it bounds what the record does and does not say about a shed's position. Its limits are that it is one consultant's 1982 report on modern farms rather than a survey, and the anaerobic patch it watched formed under a modern manuring rate, that its remedies (pumps, sprinklers, concrete distribution channels) are modern equipment we do not draw, and that it says nothing whatever about a pond's inlet or its sluice - the specific question asked of it.

Used for: manure distribution techniques needed to avoid anaerobic conditions in the pond next to the livestock quarters; fish losing appetite at the manuring and feeding sites; the shed itself sanctioned on the dike in the same passage

fao-y1187e

FAO, Integrated agriculture-aquaculture: a primer (FAO Fisheries Technical Paper 407) (https://www.fao.org/4/y1187e/y1187e34.htm)

What it is: A primer by Ruben Sevilleja, Jose Torres, John Sollows and David Little for the UN Food and Agriculture Organization (Fisheries Technical Paper 407, c. 2001) on farming crops, livestock and fish together, written as a general introduction rather than as a study of one country. We cite the section on what animal waste actually does in a pond, and its table of where a pen may stand.

Why it applies, and its limits: It states the mechanism in the cleanest form found anywhere - waste feeds the pond along three pathways - and, unusually, it gives the REASONS a pen is put where it is put, which turn out to be the labor of carrying waste and the comfort of the animals rather than anything about contamination. That is directly useful to a map that has to say why a shed stands where it does. Its limits: it is a modern general-purpose manual with no historical claim at all, it is written for present-day smallholders in many countries rather than for any one tradition, and its figures are recommendations for building a pond today, so none of them is evidence about a premodern place. And the use the record makes of its silence - that contamination is nowhere given as a reason for a pen's position - is an argument from what a MODERN manual does not say, which bounds the question rather than settling it.

Used for: wastes acting by stimulating phytoplankton, as substrate for bacterial production, and as feed for zooplankton; pen siting given as a trade-off of labor and animal comfort

qimin-yaoshu-yangzhu

賈思勰 齊民要術 (Jia Sixie, Qimin Yaoshu, c. 540 AD), 卷第六, 養豬第五十八 (https://zh.wikisource.org/zh-hant/%E9%BD%8A%E6%B0%91%E8%A6%81%E8%A1%93/%E5%8D%B7%E7%AC%AC%E5%85%AD)

What it is: The pig-raising chapter of the Qimin Yaoshu, the sixth-century Chinese agricultural treatise, on Chinese Wikisource. The same treatise's poultry chapter is used elsewhere in this record. The chapter is short and practical: how large a pen should be, what ground it wants, what to feed, how to manage a sow and her litter. The bracketed passages are the treatise's own interlinear commentary.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is by a long way the oldest and most direct evidence available on where a Chinese farmer put a pig pen, and it is PRIMARY rather than a modern reconstruction - which matters here because every other source on this question describes twentieth-century farms. It is also the only source we have that speaks to the instinct behind the question, and it answers in the opposite direction. Its limits are real: it is northern Chinese practice roughly a thousand years before the settlements these maps depict, it is prescriptive advice rather than a record of what any farmer did, and it says nothing about fish ponds at all, so it bounds the siting question without settling how a pig and a pond stood together.

Used for: the pen "not disliked for being small" and the place "not disliked for being filthy", with wallowing mud given as a positive - the only siting instruction the chapter carries, and no mention of water

hudson-munoz-2023

Mark Hudson and Irene M. Munoz Fernandez, "Henceforth fishermen and hunters are to be restrained: towards a political ecology of animal usage in premodern Japan", Asian Archaeology (2023), open access (https://pure.mpg.de/rest/items/item_3527987_3/component/file_3528006/content)

What it is: A peer-reviewed open-access REVIEW article - not a report of new fieldwork - on how animals were used in premodern Japan - hunted, herded, kept, eaten - which sets out to test the standard account rather than repeat it. Its concern is the whole archipelago across a long span, and it draws on excavated bone assemblages, genetics and documentary sources together.

Why it applies, and its limits: These maps depict a setting modeled on pre-Meiji Japan as much as on imperial China, so whether a penned pig belongs on a Japanese-flavored map at all is a question the record has to answer, and this is the most current scholarly treatment of it. It is cited here for a NEGATIVE: it lets the map say plainly that a pig on a pond dike is a Chinese form rather than leaving the provenance implicit. The limit that matters most is what the paper is arguing AGAINST: the authors name "Buddhism suppressed pig-keeping" as an assumption under review, so the paper is evidence against the standard account and not for it. The second limit narrows what we may claim from it: the paper records pigs KEPT in early modern Japan - significant finds of Sus bones at the Satsuma residence in Edo, Osaka castle, Hakata and Nagasaki - so the honest negative is about the FORM, a pen on a fish-pond dike, and not about Japanese pig-keeping at all. It says nothing about fish ponds or about pens beside water, so it bounds the question without answering it.

Used for: the two standard assumptions named as assumptions; pork consumption declining from the middle of the first millennium AD; Okinawa as the stated exception and pannage - pigs loose in forest - as the mainland form

fao-ac241e

FAO/NACA, The Integration of Mulberry Cultivation, Sericulture and Fish Farming (https://www.fao.org/4/ac241e/ac241e00.htm)

What it is: The Integration of Mulberry Cultivation, Sericulture and Fish Farming, a 1984 working paper by Hu Bao-tong and Yang Hua-zhu for the Network of Aquaculture Centres in Asia, hosted by the UN Food and Agriculture Organization. It describes the dike-and-pond farming of two Chinese regions, the Pearl River delta and the Lake Tai basin near Suzhou, as practiced on the collective farms of the early 1980s: mulberry grown on raised dikes, silkworms fed on the leaves, and the worm waste fed to fish in the ponds between, with worked examples and farmers' rules of thumb for the proportions.

Why it applies, and its limits: This is the same mulberry-dike-fishpond system our maps draw, and the area proportion it reports - roughly one unit of dike to one of water where the worms alone feed the fish, six or seven to four or three with other feed - follows from the feed cycle itself rather than from any modern input, so it describes the shape of a landscape that had been worked that way for centuries. The limits are that it is a 1980s development report on collective-era Chinese practice, not a historical record; that the pond depth it gives (3.5 m, drawn down in summer) reflects modern stocking and modern earth-moving and is the least premodern of its numbers; that its figures are two worked examples and a rule of thumb, which the paper itself says is "governed by traditional practice" in the field, not regional averages; and that China stands in for a setting modeled as much on Japan.

Used for: the dike:water ratio - "each mu of mulberry plants can yield worm dregs, pupae and waste water to provide feeds and fertilizers sufficient for 1 mu of fish ponds.
