Hence, the ratio of areas of dike and water surface is 1:1", and "the water/dike ratio is 6:4" where other feed is added; pond depth 3.5 m falling to 2.5 m in summer with a 1.5 m slope to the dike top

fao-x6708e

FAO Training Series, Simple methods for aquaculture: Pond construction, ch. 10 "Pond outlet structures" (https://www.fao.org/fishery/docs/CDrom/FAO_Training/FAO_Training/General/x6708e/x6708e10.htm)

What it is: The "Pond outlet structures" chapter of Simple methods for aquaculture: Pond construction, part of the UN Food and Agriculture Organization's training series for small-scale fish farmers. It describes how a pond is emptied and its level controlled, principally the sluice - a protected opening in the dike closed with wooden boards and screened so the fish cannot leave - and the older monk-style outlet.

Why it applies, and its limits: A board-and-slot sluice through an earth dike is a carpentry-and-mud structure with nothing modern in it, and the chapter tells us its form and rough size, which is what a map has to place. The limits are that this is a present-day training manual for small fish farms anywhere in the world, not an East Asian or historical source; that the wooden form we quote is one option on a menu that also offers the same sluice in concrete, brick and plastic pipe; and that its recommended dimensions describe today's small ponds, so we take the shape of the structure from it, not a historical measurement.

Used for: the sluice gate, verbatim: "a protected opening in the pond dike that can be easily closed with wooden boards to regulate water level and can be screened to avoid fish losses" (inside width up to 0.8 m); the monk outlet as the older draining structure

gd-gazetteer-sangji

Guangdong Provincial Gazetteer Office (广东省人民政府地方志办公室), 【粤故事】中国第一个机器缫丝厂与桑基鱼塘有何渊源, 2023-08-16 (https://dfz.gd.gov.cn/index/dqyj/content/post_4237091.html)

What it is: A 2023 article in the 粤故事 (Tales of Yue) series published by the Local Gazetteer Office of the Guangdong provincial government, with no individual author credited, retelling the history of the mulberry-dike fish ponds of the Pearl River delta from the local record: the dig-the-pond, raise-the-dike method, the classic proportion of water to dike, the crops the dikes carried, and the arrival of machine silk reeling in 1873.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the classic proportion of water to dike in the local record's own words, which is what a dike-and-pond block is drawn to. Its limits are that it is a popular, unsigned government retelling written for a general audience rather than scholarship; that the sentence giving the proportion ("three to seven or four to six") never says which side is the water and which the dike, so the direction our maps read is a decision of ours and not a finding of the page; that its narrative runs into nineteenth-century industrial silk reeling, later than our maps; and that southern China stands in for the wet-farming pattern of a Japan-flavored setting.

Used for: the water-to-dike ratio stated as 水基比 「a thirty-seventy to forty-sixty split」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「三七至四六开」) (3:7 to 4:6 - the page does not say which side is water); dikes planted with mulberry (sugarcane and banana are named in the entry but were not found on the page); Chen Qiyuan's 1873 filature as the point where household reeling met the factory

gmrb-2024-sangji

Guangming Daily, 【这些文化遗存现今的模样87】二千多岁的桑基鱼塘仍未老 (赵玙, 侯楠楠), 2024-03-19 (https://epaper.gmw.cn/gmrb/html/2024-03/19/nw.D110000gmrb_20240319_7-01.htm)

What it is: A March 2024 feature in Guangming Daily, a national Chinese newspaper, by Zhao Yu and Hou Nannan, part of a series on cultural heritage still in living use, about the mulberry-dike fish ponds of Huzhou. It describes how the form is made - low ground dug out deep for the pond, the spoil heaped around the edge to build the dike - notes that the ponds are dredged in the seventh and eighth months to rebuild those dikes, and dates the practice from Spring and Autumn origins to a Ming and Qing peak.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the single clearest sentence we found on the physical logic of pond and dike - that the dike is literally the material taken out of the pond - and a maintenance round tied to the calendar, which is exactly the kind of thing that decides how such a landscape is drawn. It is a newspaper feature for a general reader rather than a study, its dating is the paper's own compressed summary, and the site it is standing in is a working heritage landscape today, so the pond it describes is a present-day one whose form we are treating as continuous with the old one.

Used for: the form in one line - 将地势低洼处深挖为鱼塘，塘泥堆在鱼塘四周形成塘基; dredging in the seventh and eighth months to rebuild the dikes (七八月清淤，培固塘基); the Huzhou dating (Spring and Autumn origin, Ming-Qing peak)

dili360-2005-sangji

邓芬, 「Mulberry-dike fish ponds: the commonest of beautiful landscapes is no more」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「桑基鱼塘 最常见的美景风光不再」), 中国国家地理 2005(02) (https://www.dili360.com/cng/article/p5350c3d7e437f08.htm)

What it is: A 2005 magazine feature by 邓芬 (Deng Fen) in 中国国家地理 (Chinese National Geography), "桑基鱼塘 最常见的美景风光不再" (Mulberry-dike fish ponds: the commonest of beautiful landscapes is no more), on the disappearance of the dike-and-pond country of the Pearl River delta. It describes what the landscape looked like: fish raised in the ponds, mulberry and fruit trees on the dikes, banana groves and seas of sugarcane, and the net of creeks running through it all.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the look of the thing - which plants stood where, and how the water threaded between them - which is what a map has to show, and a photojournalistic feature is unusually good at that. Its limits are that it is popular magazine writing rather than research, that it gives no dimensions at all, and that the scene it describes is the twentieth-century remnant the writer remembers rather than a Ming or Qing landscape, so we take the arrangement from it and any measurement from elsewhere.

Used for: the lost traditional landscape's elements - 塘里养鱼，基上种桑树、种果树; 蕉林蔗海; the 河涌 creek net. No dimensions

cssn-sangyuanwei

中国社会科学网, 水乡农谚见证桑园围发展, 2022-09-23 (https://www.cssn.cn/ztzl/jzz/rwln/wh/lnfw/202209/t20220923_5541513.shtml)

What it is: An article by 许结玲 (Xu Jieling), "水乡农谚见证桑园围发展" (Farming proverbs of the water country witness the development of Sangyuanwei), in 人文岭南, September 2022, on 中国社会科学网 (the Chinese Social Sciences Net), on the Sangyuanwei polder in Guangdong. It gathers local sayings about the polder's silk and fish economy and about the sluice that let water through its dike, and quotes 屈大均's late-seventeenth-century 廣東新語 (New Discourses on Guangdong) on the division of work between men and women in a Nanhai village.

Why it applies, and its limits: Sayings and a period observer are good evidence for the ordinary shape of a working day - who traded the fish fry, who fed the silkworms, that pond mud went onto the mulberry - which is the sort of thing that tells us what buildings and yards a polder settlement needs, and the sluice named in a place-name saying confirms the feature we draw. Its limits are that folk sayings are evocative rather than quantitative and undated - the article's own frame runs from the Ming-Qing silk trade to the machine filatures of the 1870s, so when a saying belongs is not established; that they were collected in a heritage article celebrating one polder in the Pearl River delta; and that nothing in them can be turned into a distance or a count. The one dated passage is the seventeenth-century one, which is the better evidence.

Used for: the Sangyuanwei proverbs - 男贩鱼花，妇女喂爱蚕 (men trade fish fry, women feed the worms), 一船蚕丝出，一船白银归 (a boat of silk out, a boat of silver back), 又以塘泥肥桑; the polder's 窦 sluice named in a place-name saying

isis-dykepond

Mae-Wan Ho, "The Circular Economy of the Dyke-Pond System", ISIS, 2006-10-09 (https://www.i-sis.org.uk/DykePondSystem.php)

What it is: "The Circular Economy of the Dyke-Pond System" by Mae-Wan Ho, published on 9 October 2006 by the Institute of Science in Society. It is a summary account of the mulberry-dike and fish-pond farming of the Pearl River Delta in southern China, taking its figures from the research literature on that system (Ruddle and Zhong, Zhong and colleagues, and Deng); it puts the system's evolution over two thousand years and the start of water-control works in the low ground in the mid-fourteenth century.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the dimensions and the working rhythm of the dike-and-pond landscape our delta maps are built from - ponds mostly rectangular, 0.4 to 0.6 hectares and 2 to 3 meters deep, dikes 6 to 10 meters wide, pigs and poultry kept on the dikes, the ponds drained two or three times a year and the bottom mud dredged up onto the dikes to raise and repair them. Its limits: it is a secondary summary from a campaigning organization rather than the original field studies, and those studies have no publicly readable copy, so its numbers rest on work we cannot check; the studies measured the system in the late twentieth century, when its crop mix answered modern markets, even though the dike-and-pond form itself is far older; and it is southern China, not Japan.

Used for: the numbers it carries from Ruddle & Zhong - "most ponds are rectangular, 0.4 to 0.6 ha in area and 2 to 3 m deep. The dykes are usually 6 to 10 m wide"; the late-1980s land use (fishponds 35%, mulberry 12%, sugar cane 18%); "pigs, chickens and ducks are reared on the dykes"; "ponds are drained two or three times a year, and the mud at the bottom is dredged up to put on the dykes"; dike construction from the mid-fourteenth century

ruddle-zhong-1988

Kenneth Ruddle and Gongfu Zhong, Integrated Agriculture-Aquaculture in South China: The Dike-Pond System of the Zhujiang Delta (Cambridge University Press, 1988; URL: none - a print monograph. Cambridge's own subject page title-matches in search but 403s to a fetch, and no catalog record could be confirmed by reading it; a link nobody verified is worse here than an admitted gap, because the interactive map renders it) - unsupported by any readable page: the monograph, its Cambridge Core review (Leeds-Harrison 1989) and the Ruddle et al. 1983 Agricultural Systems paper could not be read; the numbers are taken through isis-dykepond and the search summaries

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: pond size/depth, dike width, the dike livestock, the dredging cycle (as above)

miles-2003

Steven B. Miles, "From Small Fry to Big Fish: Representing the Rise of Jiujiang Township, Nanhai County, 1395-1657", Ming Studies 48 (2003) (https://doi.org/10.1179/014703703788762953) - unsupported by any readable page (paywalled; the search summary gives the subject: the township's rise on the fish-fry trade in Ming Nanhai)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: fish fry as a Ming-era Pearl-delta trade with its own center

mdpi-3860

Liu, Min, Jiao, Liu et al., "Integrated Emergy and Economic Evaluation of Huzhou Mulberry-Dyke and Fish-Pond Systems", Sustainability 10(11):3860 (2018) (https://www.mdpi.com/2071-1050/10/11/3860; it describes no spatial layout) - read from the GM's downloaded copy - an emergy accounting study that describes NO spatial layout

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: nothing

tenmacho-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 伝馬町牢屋敷 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/伝馬町牢屋敷)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 伝馬町牢屋敷 (the Tenmacho jail), on Edo's main prison near Nihonbashi, in use until the 1870s - its extent, the plastered wall and outer moat that enclosed it, its internal divisions, and the categories of prisoner it held.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives a measured anchor for a jail compound - about 2,677 tsubo, roughly 8,850 square meters, behind a solid wall and a moat - and it settles what such a place was for: people awaiting judgment, with life imprisonment and imprisonment as a penalty being the exceptions rather than the rule, which is why our lockups are small holding buildings and not penitentiaries. It is a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, and it describes the shogunal capital's central jail, so a provincial magistracy's lockup is scaled far down from it rather than copied.

Used for: the Edo remand jail's size (2,677坪 ≈ 8,850 m2), its neribei wall and outer moat; that it held 未決囚 pending judgment AND, exceptionally, 永牢・過怠牢 prison-as-sentence cases

chuo-royashiki

Chuo City, 伝馬町牢屋敷跡 (https://www.city.chuo.lg.jp/a0052/bunkakankou/rekishi/tokyobunkazai/royashiki.html)

What it is: A local history page from Chuo City, one of Tokyo's wards, on the site of the Denmacho jail compound (伝馬町牢屋敷跡), the shogunate's central jail in Edo. It gives the compound's area as 2,600 tsubo, about 8,595 square meters, or more, notes the moat and embankment enclosing it on three sides, and describes the numbers held and the execution ground within the walls.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the hard numbers our jail block needs, an area, an enclosure and a population, from the municipality that now owns the ground and maintains the marker. It is a local government heritage page written for residents and visitors rather than a scholarly study, and it describes the largest jail in the shogunal capital, so for a provincial town it is an upper bound to work down from rather than a figure to copy.

Used for: corroborating figure 「2,600 tsubo (8,595 square meters) or more」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「2,600坪（8,595㎡）以上」), moat and embankment on three sides, 300-400 inmates at a time, execution ground in the SE corner

takayama-jinya-city

Takayama City, 高山陣屋跡 (https://www.city.takayama.lg.jp/kurashi/1000021/1000119/1000847/1000954/1000956.html)

What it is: The city of Takayama's official page for 高山陣屋跡 (the site of the Takayama Jin'ya), the one surviving Tokugawa provincial governor's office and compound in Japan, published by the municipality that manages the designated historic site. It lists the buildings and how they are built, notes which structures were moved in from the castle and when, and gives the extent of the protected ground.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the strongest source in the record for what a provincial office contained: an intact compound described by the body that keeps it - the examining court with its white-gravel yard, the storehouses moved from the castle, the office range of 1816 - and its designated area. Its limits are that this is the only survivor of more than fifty such offices, in a mountain province under direct shogunal rule; that parts of what is described are a 1996 reconstruction from an 1830 drawing; and that the area figure, 11,219 square meters, is an exact sum of ten surveyed parcels under a modern legal boundary accumulated across four designations between 1929 and 1989, not a measured Edo footprint - so the compound area our maps derive from it, the total less the front plaza, carries the hedge rather than the source.

Used for: the 吟味所 standing with the 白州 (「The ginmisho (examination hall) and the shirasu are characteristically laid with guri stone (coarse cobble ballast) and roofed.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「吟味所、白州はグリ石敷で屋根のあることが特徴的である」)); the 御蔵 moved from the castle's third bailey in 1695; the designated site figure 「Number/quantity: 11,219.05 square meters」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「員数 11,219.05平方メートル」) (the label reads oddly - recheck against the 史跡 register before relying on it)

takayama-jinya-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 高山陣屋 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/高山陣屋)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 高山陣屋 (Takayama Jinya), on the shogunal intendancy office of Hida province at Takayama, held under direct shogunal rule from 1692 until the Meiji Restoration - the only such office to survive - with two references, the prefecture's 1992 survey report and the national cultural-property register. It records the 1816 rebuild of the office block as one range, the numbered rows of storehouses, the historic-site designation of 1929, and the 1996 restoration from an 1830 drawing.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the shape of a provincial office compound as it survived - the office range, the storehouse rows, the front court - which is what our magistracy plans are laid out from; we use its shape and not its exact dimensions. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary; that this is one atypically preserved compound in a mountain province under direct shogunal rule; and that the buildings it lists describe the post-1996 state, in which the storehouse-keepers' rowhouse, the intendant's residence and the inner rooms are a reconstruction from a drawing rather than standing Edo fabric.

Used for: the 1816 rebuild of the office block as one range - 玄関・吟味所・御役所・大広間; the numbered storehouse rows (一～四番蔵・九～十二番蔵・書物蔵); the 25 intendants over 177 years are on the city's page, not this one

neixiang-yamen-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 内乡县衙 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/内乡县衙)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on the same Neixiang county yamen (内乡县衙), covering its history, its layout and its individual buildings, including the jail in the southwestern part of the compound and the shrine to Gao Yao, the god of prisons, kept inside it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives, with references, the parts of a county yamen our plan draws - the office in front and the residence behind, the jail in the southwest, the jail-god shrine - each on the page in so many words. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary of one site; that the plan it describes is the Qing dynasty's regulation for a local office, embodied in an 1894 rebuild kept as a museum, so a setting modeled on imperial China at large is reading a late and restored example; that the shrine pair on the east line is not on this page and rests on the newspaper feature instead; and that a jail dimension it quotes is larger than the whole compound and is not to be used.

Used for: "前衙后邸" as an instance of the 前朝后寝 system; the jail in the yamen's southwest (「The prison, which sits to the north and faces south, is located in the southwestern part of the Neixiang county yamen.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「坐北朝南的监狱位于内乡县衙的西南部」)); the jail-god shrine to 皋陶 inside the jail; the 衙神庙 and 土地祠 on the east line (unsupported by any readable page - seen in a search synthesis of the same page)

henan-neixiang

Henan provincial government, "China's best-preserved county-level magistrate office in Neixiang" (https://english.henan.gov.cn/2023/11-23/2853070.html)

What it is: A page in English from the Henan provincial government's own website, dated November 2023, describing the county magistrate's office (yamen) at Neixiang, the best-preserved county-level yamen in China. Its standing buildings date from 1894, late in the Qing dynasty, and the compound has been kept as a yamen museum since 1984. The page walks it building by building: the Main Court with its Ming-era kneeling stones for plaintiff and defendant, the guesthouse, the courtyard for the god of the land and the god of the government office, and the prison lying southwest of the Major Court and so "usually called South Prison".

Why it applies, and its limits: A magistracy is the one compound where a player can ask what each building is for, and this describes a real one that still stands, in the order a visitor meets it - which is where our plan's court, shrine and prison positions come from. It is a government page written to attract visitors rather than a measured architectural survey, so we take arrangement and function from it and no dimensions; what a visitor meets is a late-Qing plan as conserved and restored for display; it records one Chinese county seat, whose plan we use as the model for a setting that also draws on Japan; and its wording is the site's own English.

Used for: the prison southwest of the Major Court, "usually called South Prison"

hatakata-men-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 畑方免 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/畑方免)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 畑方免 (hatakata-men), the tax rate assessed on dry fields as opposed to wet paddy in Edo-period Japan. It explains that the dry-field levy was in many places paid in cash rather than in grain, names the Kanto 畑永法 (hata-ei system) as the type case, and notes that the rate was fixed rather than adjusted downward in bad years.

Why it applies, and its limits: It answers a question the map raises directly - why coin circulates in a farming village at all - by showing that the dry-field half of a peasant household's tax obligation was normally settled in money, which means a village growing dry crops needs somewhere to sell them. It is a short tertiary summary of a fiscal practice that varied by domain, and it is specific to Tokugawa Japan; we take the shape of the arrangement from it rather than any rate, and it says nothing about how widely the cash rule held outside the Kanto example it names.

Used for: dry-field tax commonly paid in coin, the 関東畑永法 as the type case (「Tax payment was in many cases made in cash.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「金納で納税が行われることが多かった」) - coin, not specifically copper); the rate fixed and not reduced in bad years

tfd-hongou-fire-history

Tokyo Fire Department, 防火対策の歴史 (Hongo station; https://www.tfd.metro.tokyo.lg.jp/fs/hongou/page_00029.html)

What it is: A page on the history of fire precautions (防火対策の歴史) published by the Hongo station of the Tokyo Fire Department, a short public-education account of how the inhabitants and authorities of Edo guarded against fire, including the water kept ready in ordinary households.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives two things a town map draws - the fire-water kept at the doorway and on the roof of a commoner's house, and the permission for tile, plastered storehouses and plastered houses in the merchant quarter - from the city that invented the regime. Its limits are that it is a fire service's public-education history with no author and no bibliography; that the passage we quote gives the practice without dates or quantities, though the page carries dated ordinances elsewhere; and that it describes Edo, the densest wooden city in the country under a shogunal fire regime, so the habit travels further than the regulation does.

Used for: commoners keeping fire-water routinely at the doorway and on the roof (「The names varied - yosui-oke (water bucket), tensui-oke (rainwater butt), chinka-sui (fire-quenching water) - but commoners kept water ready as a matter of course and set it at the doorway and on the roof.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「用水桶、天水桶、鎮火水と名称はいろいろあるが、庶民は平素から水を用意して、玄関先や、屋根上に設置した」))

gujianchina-taipinggang

古建中国, 古代故宫的防火器材 - "太平缸" (https://www.gujianchina.cn/news/show-5949.html)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: 308 vats in the Forbidden City; vats placed on the axis and before buildings far from the Inner Golden Water River and wells

thepaper-taipinggang

澎湃新闻, 这口大缸能防火？！ (https://www.thepaper.cn/newsDetail_forward_23268207)

What it is: 这口大缸能防火？！ ("This great vat can fight fire?!"), an article in 澎湃新闻 (The Paper) about the bronze and iron water vats standing in the courtyards of the Forbidden City - how many there were, the grades they came in, and the rule that governed where each grade stood.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the water vats of the Forbidden City - 308 originally, 231 surviving, their type and number per courtyard fixed by Qing regulation, set where a timber hall stood far from water - which is the principle our compound yards borrow: standing water where the buildings are. Its limits are that it is a post by a government-affairs account on a news platform, popular writing rather than a study, carrying the statute's figures at one remove; and that the palace was the most heavily regulated and best funded compound in the empire, so the principle carries down to a magistracy yard and the count does not.

Used for: 308 originally, 231 surviving; Qing regulation fixing which vat type and how many per courtyard

tajima-2007-night-soil

Kayo Tajima, "The Marketing of Urban Human Waste in the Early Modern Edo/Tokyo Metropolitan Area", Environnement Urbain / Urban Environment 1 (2007) (https://journals.openedition.org/eue/1039)

What it is: A peer-reviewed article by Kayo Tajima, drawn from her 2005 Tufts doctoral dissertation, in Environnement Urbain / Urban Environment (2007), on the trade in human waste between Edo and the farming villages ringing it. It reads the Tokugawa farming manuals - Nogyo Zensho of 1697, Hyakusho Denki of about 1682 and Koka Shunju of 1707 - alongside the Meiji government's first agricultural inventory of Tokyo prefecture in 1890.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is about the place and the centuries our towns are modeled on, and it describes the arrangements we draw: farmers or their agents came to the door and paid for what the privies held; a village half a day out took one household's whole year of waste under a standing contract and paid in rice; an Edo landlord rather than his tenant owned the tenants' waste and was paid in coin; and a well-placed farmer supplied a daimyo's estate with firewood, charcoal and seedlings in exchange for the right to clean its privies. Its period passages reach us through a modern historian's quotations of the manuals rather than from the manuals themselves, and its prices come from two different worlds: the straw and cash rates around Kanazawa are early eighteenth century, while its tables of quantity and value are from 1890 and 1914, after the land tax was made payable in coin, the roads were paved, iron-axled carts arrived and Western fertilizer chemistry reached Tokyo, so those show a trade already partly modernized. The trade is also a city one, thick within about twenty kilometers of Edo and three to five of an ordinary town; a village beyond that fertilized from its own households, its animals and its woodland instead.

Used for: the annual night-soil contract (tsuke-tsubo at Kanazawa, paid in rice; with the landlord in Edo, paid in cash); upper-class farmers supplying the castle and daimyo estates with firewood, charcoal and seedlings for the right to empty their privies; the reach of the trade (3-5 km of most towns, 20 km of Edo) and the fertilizer boats

guernica-night-soil

Guernica, "Raising a Stink" (https://www.guernicamag.com/raising-a-stink/; the Howell OSU paper it draws on could not be parsed)

What it is: "Raising a Stink", a November 2021 piece by Lina Zeldovich in Guernica, a nonprofit magazine of art and politics, adapted from her book on human waste and sanitation. It describes the night-soil economy of premodern and early modern Japan - human excrement treated as a valuable farm commodity rather than as refuse, with collection contracts, contested collection rights and disputes over price - and sets it against European practice of the same centuries.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives, in a readable English account, the arrangements a map needs to know existed - the annual contract for a household's night soil paid in rice, and farmers supplying a lord's estate with firewood and seedlings for the right to empty its privies - and it names the scholarship it draws on. Its limits are that it is a popular magazine adaptation of a book rather than a study, used for the existence and form of the arrangements and never for prices, volumes or rates; and that the scholar it relies on for the market, Kayo Tajima, published her study of the Edo night-soil trade in an openly readable journal, so this is a retelling standing in front of a source a reader could reach.

Used for: farmers supplying daimyo estates with firewood and seedlings for the privilege of emptying their privies; the tsuke-tsubo annual contract paid in rice

hatchobori-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 八丁堀 (東京都中央区) (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/八丁堀_(東京都中央区))

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on Hatchobori, a district of what is now Chuo ward in Tokyo. Among its local history it records that the many temples standing there were ordered to move to Asakusa in 1635, and that the group residences (組屋敷, kumi-yashiki) of the yoriki and doshin serving under the Edo town magistrate were afterward established on the vacated temple sites - the origin of the constables of period drama calling themselves "Hatchobori".

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one structural fact about how a magistracy was housed: the magistrate's enforcement staff lived together in their own quarter, at a remove from the office where they worked, rather than inside its walls. It is a tertiary summary of a single district in a single city, the article itself hedges the origin story as something that "is said", and Edo was far larger than any settlement we draw, so what we take is the arrangement and not its scale.

Used for: the yoriki/doshin 組屋敷 district of the Edo town magistracy standing apart from the office after the 1635 temple relocation

jinya-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 陣屋 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/陣屋)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 陣屋 (jin'ya), the walled administrative seat of a lord too small to hold a castle, or of a shogunal deputy. It sets out what such a seat contained: the lord's residence, the offices, official houses for retainers, earth-walled storehouses and a drill ground.

Why it applies, and its limits: The list of buildings is exactly what we need to lay out a small domain's seat on a map, and it is the compact statement of a program that longer works assume rather than list. It is a tertiary summary, so it gives the program and not any one seat's measured plan, and the passage we lean on describes a small domain's own seat rather than a shogunal deputy's office, which was a different institution with a different program.

Used for: a small DOMAIN's jin'ya program - residence, office, retainers' houses, storehouses and a 調練場 drill ground (「In the jin'ya of a small han one sees a composition laying out a residence, offices, retainers' official houses and storehouses (dozo), a drill ground (chorenjo), and the like.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「小藩の陣屋では…居館・役所・家臣の役宅や土蔵、調練場などを配置する構成がみられる」)); scope: a domain seat, not a bakufu daikansho

hanko-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 藩校 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/藩校)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 藩校 (hanko), on the domain schools of the Edo period. It describes their founding, their curriculum in the Chinese classics and their compulsory enrollment of retainers' sons, gives the count of 255 schools from a published reference work, and lists the schools domain by domain - a list in which some entries name a drill hall (演武場) among the school's buildings.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us that a domain seat of consequence had a school, which is what puts one on a capital's map. Its limits are that it is a tertiary encyclopedia summary; that the count of 255 is a late-Edo peak, so near-universal coverage is an end state rather than a constant; and, the one that bears on the drawing, that the article never says a drill hall was a standard part of a school - the drill halls are inferred by us from the names of a few particular schools in its list, so a drill hall drawn on every school compound is our generalization and not the source's.

Used for: 255 domain schools at the peak, in nearly every domain; martial arts encouraged and 演武場 among their facilities

edo-three-dojos-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 幕末江戸三大道場 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/幕末江戸三大道場)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 幕末江戸三大道場 (the three great dojos of bakumatsu Edo), on the best-known swordsmanship halls of the city in the last decades of the Tokugawa period, with the year each was founded and the district it was founded in: Nihonbashi Minami-Kayabacho, Nihonbashi Shinagawacho and the foot of Kudanzaka. The page also records that two of the three later moved - to Minami-Hatchobori and to Kanda Otamagaike - and that the grouping itself is a post-Meiji convention that spread from a late-Edo rating verse.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for where a martial hall stood in a large city - on central commercial ground among the townspeople rather than out among the samurai residences - which is a placement rule a map has to get right. Its limits are that an encyclopedia is a tertiary summary, and this one rests on a single kendo-magazine feature from 1977; that a "three great" list is a later convention rather than any census of the city's halls, so it cannot tell us how many a town had; that the addresses are founding addresses and two of the halls moved, so a placement rule reads two different moments; and that these are all late Edo, the very end of the era our setting draws on.

Used for: the three great late-Edo fencing halls and their metropolitan addresses (Nihonbashi, Kanda Otamagaike, Kudan); no date range for the "boom" is given there

hei-jokaku-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 塀 (城郭) (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/塀_(城郭))

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the 塀 (hei) of a castle - the wall built along the top of a castle's stone ramparts and earthworks. It distinguishes framed from unframed earthen walls, notes that each carries a small roof and is pierced with loopholes, and gives thicknesses: a shaku (about 300 mm) or more, with medieval walls as thin as three to seven sun before firearms spread in the late Sengoku period and pushed them to seven sun (about 210 mm) and beyond.

Why it applies, and its limits: A wall's thickness decides how heavy a line we draw for it, and this is a straightforward statement of the castle-class figure together with the reason it grew. It is a tertiary summary whose numbers carry no citation of their own, and it is about castle walls only - a village fence, a temple wall or a townhouse's boundary is a different thing built to a different standard, so the figure is an upper bound for our fortified work and not a general one.

Used for: plastered castle-class earthen walls 「The thickness of the wall is 1 shaku (about 300 millimeters) or more.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「壁の厚さは1尺（約300ミリメートル）以上ある」), with medieval walls as thin as 3-7 sun

kojodan-dobei

攻城団, お城の基礎講座 40. 土塀の種類 (https://blog.kojodan.jp/entry/2020/08/26/180000)

What it is: A 2020 post by the writer Takamaru in "Takamaru's Castle Basics course", a long-running explainer series on Kojodan, a Japanese castle enthusiasts' site. It sets out the kinds of earthen wall used at castles - the framed and plastered wall, the rammed-earth tsuijibei built up in layers inside wooden forms, and the frameless neribei of old tile and stone bedded in clay - with how each was built and how thick it came out.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it to decide how thick to draw a compound wall: a rammed-earth tsuijibei about a meter thick and three high, a frameless neribei perhaps 30 cm. Its limits are that it is an enthusiast's explainer on a castle fans' site with no sources behind it, not an archaeological or conservation report; that the author hedges the 30 cm figure himself while stating the 1 m one flat; and that these are the walls of castles, a different scale of place and a different job for a wall, which we apply to a magistracy or manor compound as an upper bound -

Used for: frameless neribei 「compared with a tsuijibei ... about 30 cm」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「築地塀に比べて…30cm程度」); tsuijibei up to about 1 m thick

sado-bugyosho-fires

Sado magistracy fire history - unsupported by any readable page: search snippets across Sado tourism pages state the 奉行所, built 1603, 「It repeated destruction by fire and rebuilding five times.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「焼失と再建を5回繰り返しました」); the three pages fetched (ja.wikipedia 佐渡奉行, visitsado.com, city.sado.niigata.jp museum) describe only the 2000 reconstruction and say nothing about fires (https://www.asoview.com/spot/15224af2172090002/)

What it is: A listing for the Sado magistracy (佐渡奉行所) on asoview, a Japanese leisure and sightseeing booking site, carrying a short history of the building: put up in 1603 at the start of the Edo period, burned down and rebuilt five times thereafter, remodeled into offices and a school after the Meiji Restoration, named a national historic site in 1929, and destroyed entirely by fire in 1942.

Why it applies, and its limits: One plain point is taken from it - that a magistracy's halls were ordinary timber buildings that burned and were rebuilt again and again, which is why our administrative compounds are drawn as wooden and replaceable rather than monumental. It stands in because nothing closer could be read on a public page: the encyclopedia, museum and tourism pages about the same building describe only its modern reconstruction, so the count of five fires rests on this page alone, and the page is a commercial listing written for visitors, with no sources of its own and its Japanese rendered into English by this project.

Used for: administrative halls as ordinary wooden buildings that burned and were rebuilt repeatedly

fuchu-kosatsuba

Tokyo Jinjacho, 府中高札場 (http://www.tokyo-jinjacho.or.jp/goshahou/fuchukousatsuba/)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: kosatsuba set "at points of heavy passage: barriers and ports, the foot of large bridges, and the entrances and centers of towns and villages"

ogose-kosatsuba

Ogose town, cultural property H26-04 高札場 (https://www.town.ogose.saitama.jp/kamei/shogaigakushu/bunkazai/kaisetsu/explanation_cultural/H26_04.html)

What it is: A cultural property explanation page published by the board of education of Ogose town in Saitama prefecture, describing the site of the town's Edo period kosatsuba, the roofed board on which the shogunate's edicts and regulations were posted. It cites an 1830 provincial gazetteer for where the board stood and records that each of the seventeen villages that make up the modern town had one at a key point, with a surviving place name marking another at the foot of a pass on the Chichibu highway.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives an Edo-period source, at one remove, for where a village's notice board stood: at a key point in each of the seventeen villages, and in the post town itself in the lower town, the political and economic center, at the settlement's southern entrance - which is what puts a board at the center or the entrance of our villages. Its limits are that it is one town's own heritage office reading an 1830 gazetteer, not a comparative study; that Ogose itself is a post station rather than a plain farming village; that the board is gone and the evidence is a gazetteer at one remove; and that the ranked list of candidate sites our rule uses - the center, the shrine precinct, the assembly place, before the officials' houses - is our inference, since the sheet says only "a key point".

Used for: a kosatsuba at a key point in each of seventeen villages, and Ogose's own in the lower town, the political and economic center, at the settlement's southern entrance (the ranked list - center, shrine precinct, assembly place, before the officials' houses - is our inference)

kosatsu-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 高札 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/高札)

What it is: Japanese Wikipedia's article on 高札 (kosatsu), the boards on which laws, price schedules and prohibitions were written up and posted publicly, and on the kosatsuba where those boards stood.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the edict-board as an institution, its siting rule - at the entrance or the center of a town or village, where everyone passes - and Edo's principal boards, which is what puts a notice board on every map and tells us where. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary that carries the encyclopedia's own notice that it lacks sufficient citations, so the siting rule that governs a feature on every map rests on thin references; that it is taken for the institution and the rule and not for a number; and that its counts - the six great boards and the rest - are the capital's, and hedged even there.

Used for: the edict board institution - standing law, rate tables, ban edicts; Nihonbashi as Edo's principal board

adachi-kosatsu

Adachi City museum, 高札 (https://www.city.adachi.tokyo.jp/hakubutsukan/chiikibunka/hakubutsukan/shiryo-kosatsu.html)

What it is: A collection page from the museum of Adachi City, one of Tokyo's wards, about 高札 (kosatsu), the public notice boards on which the Edo-period government posted its edicts. It lists the standing boards of the early Edo period and says where boards were put up: at bridge ends and on the ground before the gates of village officials' houses.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives where the boards stood - at bridge ends and before the village officials' gates - and names Edo's five great boards, which is what puts a notice board in the right place on our maps. Its limits are that it is a short museum page describing objects it holds, with no stated method and no figures; that the Edo region stands for Japanese practice generally; and that it says nothing about who read the board out or how circulars reached a village through it, so those rest on other sources or on our reading.

Used for: boards at bridge ends and before the village officials' gates; Edo's five great boards (who read a board out, and the headman relaying circulars, are not on the page)

caishikou-enwiki

en.wikipedia Caishikou Execution Grounds (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caishikou_Execution_Grounds)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the Caishikou execution grounds in Beijing, the Qing dynasty's public execution site, whose name means "vegetable market". It records that the ground lay at the crossroads of Xuanwumen Outer Street and Luomashi Street, and that crowds gathered there for notable executions.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our maps have to decide whether an execution ground is hidden away or is a named public place, and the Chinese answer is visible here: a market crossroads, chosen so that a crowd would see it. It is a tertiary summary of one site in the imperial capital, so it anchors one Qing site rather than the general Chinese rule - the classical marketplace execution, the qishi of the law codes, is not on this page and rests on other sources - and it is only half of the question our record has to settle, since Japanese practice pushed such grounds out of the settlement entirely.

Used for: the Qing execution ground at the crossroads of Xuanwumen Outer Street and Luomashi Street, "Vegetable Market Execution Ground"; the term 棄市 is not on the page

qiushen-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 秋审 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/秋审)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on the qiushen (秋审), the Qing dynasty's autumn assize, in which the court decided each autumn which death sentences would be carried out. It describes the route a case took: the Three Judicial Offices - the Board of Punishments, the Court of Judicial Review and the Censorate - heard the provincial cases jointly, and the prisoners waiting in provincial jails were held over until the review returned.

Why it applies, and its limits: A magistracy on our maps has a jail and an office, and this gives the shape of what happens in them: a capital case climbs from a provincial cell to the capital and the confirmed sentence comes back down, which is why the jail holds prisoners for months rather than days. Its limits are that an encyclopedia article is a tertiary summary, so we take its procedure only where its own references support it, and that this is Qing Chinese law standing for the setting's justice generally, our setting drawing its officialdom from the Chinese pattern as well as the Japanese one.

Used for: the Qing autumn review of death sentences through 刑部, 大理寺, 都察院 and the emperor - the capital case climbs and the confirmed sentence comes back down

suzugamori-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 鈴ヶ森刑場 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/鈴ヶ森刑場)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 鈴ヶ森刑場, on the Suzugamori execution ground that stood on the Tōkaidō at the southern approach to Edo from the seventeenth century until its abolition in the early Meiji years, and on its northern counterpart Kozukappara on the Nikkō highway.

Why it applies, and its limits: It supplies the placement rule our city maps use for a thing that has to go somewhere unpleasant: execution grounds sat at the entrances to the city, on the great highways, just outside where the built-up area ended, deliberately in the sight of everyone arriving - and that they came in pairs, one at each approach, is the article's own statement. It is a tertiary summary describing one shogunal capital in the Tokugawa period, so we take the siting logic and the pairing and not any measurement; how a much smaller provincial settlement handled the same need is inferred from the principle rather than found here.

Used for: Suzugamori at Edo's southern entrance on the Tokaido, paired with Kozukappara at the northern entrance on the Nikko kaido (「Together with the Kozukappara execution ground, which had been set up along Edo's northern entrance (the Nikko Kaido), it was an execution ground set up along the southern entrance (the Tokaido).」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「江戸の北の入口（日光街道）沿いに設置されていた小塚原刑場とともに、南の入口（東海道）沿いに設置されていた刑場であった」))

kozukappara-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 小塚原刑場 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/小塚原刑場)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 小塚原刑場 (Kozukappara), the execution ground that stood at the northern edge of Edo on the road out of the city, in what is now Arakawa ward in Tokyo. It covers the ground's history, its size, what was done with the bodies left there, and the temple hall founded next to it in 1667 to bury and pray for them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives one of Edo's two great execution grounds - its founding in 1651, its size, the bodies left uncovered, and the hall founded in 1667 to bury and pray for the dead - which is what puts a ground of that kind and size at the edge of our capitals. Its limits are that it is a tertiary encyclopedia summary; that it is the shogunal capital's practice in one era, not a rule for a provincial town; and that the one figure we take from it, the ground's size of about 108 by 54 m, carries no citation on the page, so it is an uncited encyclopedia figure we rely on for want of a better one.

Used for: the two as Edo's 二大刑場 - the page names two major grounds, not three

shiniuba-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 死牛馬取得権 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%AD%BB%E7%89%9B%E9%A6%AC%E5%8F%96%E5%BE%97%E6%A8%A9)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 死牛馬取得権 ("the right to take fallen cattle and horses"), on the hereditary privilege by which the kawata - the outcaste leatherworking communities of Tokugawa Japan - claimed the carcasses of dead livestock across a defined stretch of countryside, a right abolished in 1871.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the outcast community's right to fallen cattle and horses over a defined territory, and its abolition in 1871, which is what ties the hide trade on our maps to a district. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, good for the terms and the outline of the arrangement and not for a number; that it is Tokugawa-specific; and that the territory it describes is a legal and economic one - the article says nothing about where a tannery quarter stood, so its siting in relation to the settlement is our inference.

Used for: the kawata's right to fallen cattle and horses over a defined territory (「A defined district from which fallen cattle and horses were collected was called a kusaba (草場, "grass ground") or a dannaba (旦那場, the ground of one's patrons).」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「死牛馬を集める特定の地域を「草場」ないしは「旦那場」といった」)), abolished 1871

himeji-shironameshi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 姫路白なめし革細工 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%A7%AB%E8%B7%AF%E7%99%BD%E3%81%AA%E3%82%81%E3%81%97%E9%9D%A9%E7%B4%B0%E5%B7%A5)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 姫路白なめし革細工, the white-tawed leatherwork of Himeji - a regional craft in which hides are salted, steeped in the river for several days to loosen the hair, then worked with salt and rapeseed oil, beaten dry and kneaded soft while exposed to sunlight.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the reason a tannery on our maps stands on running water and has open drying ground beside it: the process needs a river for the soak and sun for the finish, so the trade cannot be placed anywhere else in the town. It is a tertiary summary of a named local craft, its account of the process is not dated or attributed, and it gives only "several days" for the soak - so we describe the stage without attaching a length to it, and the practice it records is one district's, not every tanner's.

Used for: the white-tawing process - salted hides soaked in the river "数日間" (several days) to loosen the hair, then salt and rapeseed oil under the sun; NOT the "one week summer, two weeks winter" figure the entry carried

northampton-tannery-1996

Shaw, "The excavation of a late 15th- to 17th-century tanning complex at The Green, Northampton", Post-Medieval Archaeology 30(1), 1996 (https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1179/pma.1996.002; the 36-37 pit figure seen in search snippets of the abstract)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the largest excavated urban tannery (the Western Tannery, 36-37 pits) as an upper comparison

pingyao-shilou-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 平遥市楼 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%B9%B3%E9%81%A5%E5%B8%82%E6%A5%BC)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on the Pingyao Shilou (平遥市楼), the market tower that straddles the middle of South Street at the center of the walled county town of Pingyao in Shanxi, giving its plan, its footprint, its height and the date of the rebuilding that produced the present structure.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the plan, footprint and height of a real market tower at the center of a county town - three bays square, about 133 square meters, 18.5 m high, standing in the middle of the main street - which is what sizes the tower on our county-seat maps. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary and the figures we take are its whole use, while its only reference is an archived page on a commercial site, so the numbers rest on thin support; that the present tower is a Qing rebuild of 1688 rather than an earlier one; and that Pingyao was an unusually rich merchant town whose tower is unusually well preserved.

Used for: 「The ground story is three bays across the front and three bays deep, occupies 133.4 square meters, and is square in plan.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「底层面阔、进深各三间，占地133.4平方米，平面呈方形」), 18.5 m high, rebuilt 1688

dongjing-menghualu-rujia

吴钩, 大宋消防队 (儒家网, https://www.rujiazg.com/article/11577), quoting Yuan Jiong's Southern Song recollection of Kaifeng's fire watch and paraphrasing 東京夢華錄

What it is: An article by 吴钩 (Wu Gou, a popular historian of the Song), "大宋消防队" (The fire brigade of the Great Song), published in 2017 on 儒家网 (Rujiazg, a Chinese website on Confucian culture and history). It reads the Song sources on Kaifeng's fire watch - the twelfth-century memoir 東京夢華錄 (Dongjing meng hua lu) in paraphrase, and, in the passage we quote, the recollection of Yuan Jiong, a centenarian living in Hangzhou after the capital had fallen: a military patrol post every three hundred paces in each ward, and on high ground a fire-watch tower with lookouts above and a hundred soldiers quartered below with buckets, brooms, hooks, saws, axes, ladders and ropes.

Why it applies, and its limits: The quoted passage is a firsthand description of the institution we draw - a watch tower that is its own building, staffed and equipped, standing apart from the ward gate - and that is precisely what we take from it. Its limits are that we are reading the Song testimony through a popular history article rather than in a scholarly edition, with no edition or chapter given; that the passage is a nostalgic recollection of the capital written after it had fallen, so its regularity may be tidier than the city was; and that it describes one great Chinese capital and gives no count of towers for Kaifeng (the tower counts on the page are Southern Song Hangzhou's), let alone for a provincial town.

Used for: 「In every ward, at every three hundred paces, there is a military patrol post; and at a high place there is a fire-watch tower, with men above keeping lookout and, below, a hundred soldiers stationed together with water buckets, sprinkling brooms, hooked saws, axes and levers, ladders and ropes, and the like.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「每坊三百步有军巡铺，又于高处有望火楼，上有人探望，下屯军百人及水桶、洒帚、钩锯、斧权、梯索之类」) - the Song fire-watch tower as a separate institution; no citywide tower count

kyomachiya-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 京町家 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E4%BA%AC%E7%94%BA%E5%AE%B6)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 京町家, the Kyoto townhouse: the narrow-fronted, deep-lot merchant house, its plan and its history. It also takes up the popular story that the shape was a dodge against a tax on frontage width, and rebuts it, explaining that Edo-period Kyoto assessed a total on the block community, which the house-owning townsmen then divided per house.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the shape of an urban lot in a dense premodern city and for the correction to the frontage-tax story that a more popular source repeats. It is a tertiary summary, and it describes one city's building tradition, so our towns take the proportions of the form rather than Kyoto's specific detail.

Used for: the narrow-front deep-lot machiya, and its own rebuttal of the frontage-tax story (「Under the Edo-period tax system of Kyoto, without regard to frontage width, a total sum was first levied on the cho (the block community),」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「江戸時代の京都の税制は、間口幅に関係なく、まず町に対し総額が賦課され」))

kyototuu-unagi

京都通百科事典, 鰻の寝床 (https://www.kyototuu.jp/Life/ProverbUnaginoNedoko.html)

What it is: A page from 京都通百科事典, a Japanese web encyclopedia about Kyoto, on the saying 鰻の寝床 ("an eel's bed") for the long narrow townhouse, giving typical dimensions of about two ken of frontage (roughly 3.6 m) and ten to twelve ken of depth (roughly 18 to 22 m).

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the proportions of the eel's-nest townhouse lot - about two ken of frontage to ten or twelve of depth - which is what our merchant plots are drawn to, and the frontage-tax story as a foil. Its limits are that it is a popular regional web encyclopedia written for tourism and a local-knowledge examination, with no citations of any kind; that it is one city's building tradition; and that both the tax story and the dimensions themselves are given as hearsay - "it is said" - with no source behind either.

Used for: 「a frontage of about 2 ken (about 3.6 m), a depth of 10 to 12 ken (about 18 to 22 m)」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「間口２間（約３.６m）前後、奥行１０〜１２間（約１８〜２２m）」); carries the popular frontage-tax explanation that kyomachiya-jawiki disputes

nagaya-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 長屋 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E9%95%B7%E5%B1%8B)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the nagaya (長屋), the row tenement of Japanese towns - one long building divided across its width by party walls into separate rented dwellings. It covers the form in general and the Edo-period back-alley version in particular: the small unit of nine shaku by two ken, the shared privy in the alley, the shared well, and the townsmen and craftsmen who rented there while the better merchants kept shops on the main street.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the plain definition of the building type we draw along a town's back lanes, and of who lived in one, which is exactly what we take from it. It is a tertiary summary rather than a study, so we use its figures only where its own references support them, and what it describes best is Edo, the largest city in the country, so a provincial town's tenements on our maps are that pattern scaled down.

Used for: the nagaya as one building divided into units sharing walls (「a thing in which a single building is divided in the horizontal direction, each part made an independent dwelling unit」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「1棟の建物を水平方向に区分し、それぞれ独立した住戸とした物」))

hiyokechi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 火除地 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E7%81%AB%E9%99%A4%E5%9C%B0)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 火除地 (hiyokechi), the cleared fire-prevention lots the Tokugawa government laid out in Edo after the Great Meireki Fire of 1657. It records that five burned-over sites were first given over to the purpose, that more were added over time - chiefly northwest of the castle, reaching some thirteen by the Kyoho era - and that in the broad sense the term also covers the widened streets (hirokoji) made for the same reason.

Why it applies, and its limits: It settles the scale at which a firebreak was actually cut: whole blocks and broad streets, opened after a disaster, rather than gaps left between individual houses - which is how our towns show fire precaution. It is a tertiary summary and it is entirely about Edo, a city of a size no settlement we draw approaches, so a smaller town takes the form and the reason from it and not the count.

Used for: firebreak lots cut through Edo after the 1657 Meireki fire - breaks at block scale, not between houses

song-architecture-enwiki

en.wikipedia Architecture of the Song dynasty (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_the_Song_dynasty)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Architecture of the Song dynasty", a survey of Chinese building and city form in the tenth to thirteenth centuries, including the collapse of the walled-ward city plan that the Tang had inherited from earlier dynasties.

Why it applies, and its limits: It carries the one change that decides how a town map looks: a Tang city sorted its residents and its markets into separate walled wards, while a Song city let shops line the street in front of ordinary houses - so a settlement in our setting is drawn as one or the other, and the difference is visible at a glance. It is a tertiary summary, so we take this well-established structural contrast from it and check its own references before using any figure; and it is about China, which our setting draws on alongside Japan, so where the two diverge the Japanese sources govern.

Used for: Tang cities "strictly divided into distinct residential and commercial wards divided by city walls"; under the Song "shops could now line streets in residential areas and did not have to be situated behind precinct walls"

guanxiang-zdic

漢典 zdic.net, 关厢 (https://zdic.net/hans/%E5%85%B3%E5%8E%A2)

What it is: The entry for 关厢 (guanxiang) in Zdic (漢典), an online Chinese character and word dictionary. It defines the word as the streets immediately outside a city gate and the area adjoining them.

Why it applies, and its limits: A dictionary is the right authority for what a word means, and what we take is the meaning: that Chinese had a settled term for the built-up strip outside the wall, which tells us such strips were a normal and named part of a walled city. That is all it supports - it says nothing about what stood there, and the commonly repeated claim that trade and inns concentrated in the guanxiang is not on this page and could not be read on any public page we reached, so nothing on our maps about what fills that strip rests on this source.

Used for: the bare definition - 「the streets outside a city gate and the neighboring area」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「城门外的街道及附近区域」); that commerce clustered there is unsupported by any readable page (Baidu Baike)

jokamachi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 城下町 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%9F%8E%E4%B8%8B%E7%94%BA)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 城下町 (jokamachi, the castle town). It describes how such a town was zoned around its castle, with samurai quarters ranked by their distance from it, merchant and artisan quarters beyond them and a temple district on the outer edge, and how streets were deliberately bent into right-angle dog-legs and stopped with dead ends to lengthen an attacker's approach.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the source of the zoning and the street tricks that give our towns and cities their shape, and it states in one place what scattered town histories show one town at a time. It is a tertiary summary describing the castle town as a general type, so it gives us the pattern rather than any single town's plan, and Japanese castle towns were an Edo-period form, which is later than parts of the setting our maps also draw on.

Used for: samurai quarters ranked by distance from the castle, merchant and artisan quarters outside them, the temple district on the perimeter as part of the defense, and streets kinked and dead-ended to lengthen the approach (「By such means as bending the roads into key shapes (right-angle dog-legs) and providing dead-end alleys, the distance one had to travel to reach the castle was lengthened.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「道を鍵形に曲げたり袋小路を設けるなどすることで、城への到達距離を延長した」))

kichinyado-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 木賃宿 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%9C%A8%E8%B3%83%E5%AE%BF)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the kichin-yado (木賃宿), the cheapest class of Japanese inn, whose name means "firewood-fee lodging" - guests brought their own rice and paid only for the fuel to cook it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the cheapest kind of inn - the firewood-fee lodging on the edge of a post town, a common room, guests bringing their own bedding and rice and paying for the wood - which is what puts such an inn on our town maps. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary that carries the encyclopedia's own notice that it lacks sources; and that its passage about the inns spreading into a city's poor quarter, laborers and the unregistered sleeping a tatami apiece, describes the Meiji-era doss house after the post-station system ended, so the common room and the firewood fee are Edo while the poor-quarter crowding is a later condition being read back into a premodern town.

Used for: the firewood-fee inn - a common room, bedding at the guest's own expense, guests pooling rice and paying the firewood cost to have it cooked

kichinyado-kotobank

コトバンク 木賃宿 (https://kotobank.jp/word/木賃宿-51029)

What it is: A page on kotobank, a Japanese reference site that reprints the entries several published dictionaries and encyclopedias give for a single word, gathering here the entries for 木賃宿 (kichinyado), the cheapest class of travelers' lodging in Edo-period Japan, where a guest paid a small charge for firewood and cooked their own food. The works reprinted on it include Shogakukan's Nipponica and Seisenban Nihon Kokugo Daijiten, Yamakawa's concise dictionary of Japanese history, and Heibonsha's revised Sekai Dai-hyakka Jiten.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us what the cheapest bed in a post town cost and who slept in one - a self-catering inn where the lodger paid for firewood and brought his own rice - which is what puts such an inn on a town map and tells a reader what kind of place it was. Its limits are that these are tertiary summaries of ordinances and records not themselves on the page, so only the dated figures they state can be taken; that the three prices - 3 mon under a 1611 ordinance, a 1658 figure, 24 mon in Edo in 1843 - are two centuries apart across a period in which the coin itself changed value, so they measure monetary change as much as the price of a bed and must never be averaged into one rate; and that the 1843 figure is the shogunal capital's, a different scale of place from the county seat we draw.

Used for: prices - 3 mon a person (1611 ordinance), 6 mon firewood fee (1658), 24 mon roof fee plus 16-24 mon for a futon (1843)

hutong-enwiki

en.wikipedia Hutong (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutong)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Hutong", on the alleys of Beijing that are formed by lines of siheyuan, the walled courtyard houses of the traditional city, and on how such neighborhoods grew.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one structural point that shapes how our town blocks are laid out - the lane is the space left between rows of walled courtyard houses, and a quarter grows by joining one courtyard to the next and then one lane to the next, with smaller passages threading between. Its limits: an encyclopedia article is a tertiary summary, so we take only general description that its own references support and no figure of our own from it; and it is about Beijing, an imperial capital, so the pattern carries over to our towns while the scale and density do not.

Used for: alleys "formed by lines of siheyuan", neighborhoods "formed by joining one siheyuan to another to form a hutong" - the entry's "access routes lined by contiguous courtyard residences" is a paraphrase of this

chinese-city-wall-enwiki

en.wikipedia Chinese city wall (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_city_wall)

What it is: The English Wikipedia survey article on Chinese city walls: their construction from a rammed-earth core faced in brick or stone, their surviving examples at Nanjing, Xi'an and Kaifeng, and their plan, rectangular for capitals and important northern cities but irregular in broken country, where the line followed the terrain.

Why it applies, and its limits: It supports the shape rule our town walls obey, that a wall is a costly thing pulled tight around what must actually be defended and bent by the ground, rather than a tidy circle drawn around empty space. It is a tertiary summary covering the whole country over two thousand years, it gives no costs and no dimensions tied to a settlement's rank, and the economic argument our text makes from the shape is our own reasoning rather than the article's.

Used for: "In areas of rugged relief, however, a square form was usually replaced by one of irregular shape, determined in many cases by topographic conditions"

thepaper-city-walls

澎湃新闻, on the plan forms of Chinese city walls (https://m.thepaper.cn/newsDetail_forward_1270378)

What it is: 圆形、方形、卵形？ ("Round, square, oval? What shape were China's ancient cities"), a 2014 article by the historical geographer Cheng Yinong (成一农) in 澎湃新闻 (The Paper), a Shanghai-based news outlet, surveying the plan forms that Chinese walled cities actually took and why they took them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It supplies the rule our walled outlines follow: capitals laid out in advance were essentially square, while cities in hill and mountain country had to bring the commanding high ground inside the wall for defense and so ended up markedly irregular, Beijing's 凸-shaped plan among the named examples. It is a popular article by a specialist rather than a peer-reviewed paper, and it generalizes over the whole span of imperial China at once, so we take the principle and its named examples from it rather than any measurement.

Used for: hill cities taking the surrounding high points inside the wall and so producing irregular outlines (荆州's oval, 北京's 凸 plan)

xian-wall-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 西安城墙 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%A5%BF%E5%AE%89%E5%9F%8E%E5%A2%99)

What it is: The same Chinese Wikipedia article 西安城墙, here for its account of the rampart itself - 12 m high, 15 to 18 m thick at the base and 12 to 14 m across the top, with 98 projecting towers standing 12 m out from the outer face and rising level with the wall.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the anchor numbers for how a great wall and its towers are drawn - the circuit's length and the area it encloses, the rampart's height and its thickness at base and top, the enemy platforms' projection, width and spacing, set at the range of bows and firearms. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary; that this is among the largest walls ever built, a ceiling rather than a norm; and that the numbers describe the wall as it stands after the brick facing of 1568, the works of 1781 and modern conservation, not a wall measured when it was raised in 1374.

Used for: 「The city rampart is 12 meters high, 15 to 18 meters wide at the base, and 12 to 14 meters wide at the top.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「城垣高12米，底宽15-18米，顶宽12-14米」); 「On the outer face of the city wall are built 98 enemy towers (ditai), projecting 12 meters out from the wall, 20 meters wide, and level in height with the wall.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「城墙的外壁筑98座敌台，延伸出墙12米，宽20米，高与城齐」)

hakone-seki-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 箱根関 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E7%AE%B1%E6%A0%B9%E9%96%A2)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the Hakone barrier, the checkpoint on the Tokaido highway where the shogunate controlled traffic between Edo and the west. It lists the buildings that made up the station: a main guard house with its upper guard room, guards' duty room, rest room and bath house; an opposite guard house with its half-shift duty room, rest room and lockup; stables, a watch post, a public notice board, all enclosed by a fence.

Why it applies, and its limits: For drawing a checkpoint, the useful question is what buildings a barrier consists of, and this article answers it as an itemized list, which is why we use it. It is a tertiary encyclopedia summary, and Hakone was the most important barrier in the country on the most important road - a checkpoint on a lesser highway would have had a plainer program - so the list is a fully equipped ceiling rather than an average. The one thing we wanted from it and did not get is the arrangement: that the two guard houses faced each other across the road is not stated on any page we could read.

Used for: the facility list - 面番所 (上御番所・番士詰所・休息所・風呂場) and 向番所 (所詰半番・休息所・牢屋), stables, 辻番, 高札場, all inside a fence; that the two stations face each other across the road is unsupported by any readable page (search synthesis of hakonesekisyo.jp)

wengcheng-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 瓮城 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E7%93%AE%E5%9F%8E)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article 瓮城 (wengcheng, "barbican"), a tertiary encyclopedia entry on the walled outwork built in front of a Chinese city gate, the half-round and square shapes it takes, and named surviving examples such as the Zhonghua Gate at Nanjing, whose barbicans sit inside the gate rather than before it.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it only for what a barbican is and which forms it takes at a gate, which is the kind of settled definition an encyclopedia carries dependably. It is a tertiary summary written by volunteers, so we take its figures only where its own references support them, and it describes Chinese city gates - the setting's towns take the form from the imperial-Chinese side of their ancestry rather than from Japan.

Used for: the barbican definition - a half-round or square outwork before a gate (Nanjing's 中华门 the inside-the-gate exception)

genbukan-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 玄武館 (search syntheses split between ~3,000 from 清河八郎's roster and "over 6,000") (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E7%8E%84%E6%AD%A6%E9%A4%A8)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 玄武館 (Genbukan), on the swordsmanship hall founded in 1822 at Nihonbashi Shinagawacho in Edo by Chiba Shusaku, later moved to Kanda Otamagaike, which the article ranks among the three great dojos of late-Edo Edo. It records the school's founding, its moves and the Chiba family's service as instructors to the Mito domain.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for a metropolitan dojo with a date and an address, so that one can be put on a street, and for the point that a big city had large private martial schools; it is not used for a count of disciples. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, and that this was one exceptional institution, so it is a ceiling and not a typical dojo. The page says nothing about fees or commercial operation - the Chiba family ran the hall while serving a domain as instructors - so whether such a hall was a paying business rests on nothing readable here.

Used for: the Genbukan as a metropolitan dojo (1822, Nihonbashi then Kanda), one of the three great dojos of late-Edo Edo (nothing on the page about fees or commercial operation); the disciple count is unsupported by any readable page and contested

cdlib-local-elites

Esherick & Rankin (eds.), Chinese Local Elites and Patterns of Dominance, introduction (UC Press e-book, https://publishing.cdlib.org/ucpressebooks/public/book/chinese-local-elites-and-patterns-of-dominance.html)

What it is: The editors' introduction to Chinese Local Elites and Patterns of Dominance, edited by Joseph W. Esherick and Mary Backus Rankin (University of California Press, 1990), a collection of eleven scholarly essays on how local elites held power in late imperial and Republican China. The introduction summarizes the volume's finding that by Qing times much of the gentry had moved into administrative centers and canal towns and drew their income from land they no longer lived on.

Why it applies, and its limits: It answers the question our hinterland has to settle, whether rich houses cluster along the road outside a city gate, and it answers it against the clustering: an urbanized, absentee landlord class collecting through agents leaves the countryside without a ring of grand suburban compounds. Its limits are real: the absentee-landlord pattern it describes is the Lower Yangzi's, which we read as a general Chinese one; the volume's weight is on the nineteenth and twentieth centuries, later than the Ming-Qing and Edo anchor our maps take, so the pattern is being read backward; it is about China rather than Japan; and it describes where elites lived and how they drew rent rather than what their houses looked like.

Used for: "By Qing times, the substantially urbanized gentry were living the leisured life of absentee landlords in administrative centers or the many small towns that lined the canals"; absentee landlords acting through agents and rent bursaries

kaifeng-flood-1642-enwiki

en.wikipedia 1642 Yellow River flood (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1642_Yellow_River_flood)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the 1642 Yellow River flood, in which the dikes above Kaifeng were cut during a siege and the city was destroyed. It gives the traditional casualty figure, some three hundred thousand of the city's three hundred and seventy eight thousand inhabitants lost to the flood and to the famine and disease that followed.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the scale of what a river can do to a walled city in one year - the dikes cut during a siege, and most of the population dead - which is the reason a river city on our maps keeps its distance from the bank. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary of a single catastrophic year, not the ordinary risk of the bank; that its death toll is cited to one modern book on an adjacent subject rather than to the chronicles, and the encyclopedia contradicts itself between this article and its Kaifeng article on the numbers, as the footnote records; that the page gives three accounts of who breached the dikes, so we say only that they were cut during the siege; and that it is imperial China.

Used for: "300,000 of the 378,000 residents were killed by the flood and ensuing peripheral disasters"; the levee breached by the Ming governor during the siege

kaifeng-pmc7048742

Storozum et al., "Geoarchaeological evidence of the AD 1642 Yellow River flood that destroyed Kaifeng", Scientific Reports 2020 (PMC 7048742) (https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-020-60169-1)

What it is: Storozum and colleagues, "Geoarchaeological evidence of the AD 1642 Yellow River flood that destroyed Kaifeng", a peer-reviewed open access paper in Scientific Reports, 2020. The authors read the flood deposits under the modern city and set the 1642 disaster in the river's long record, which brought roughly forty floods to Kaifeng over the past three thousand years.

Why it applies, and its limits: Primary fieldwork on exactly the question a river-city map asks - five excavations through the flood layers of a city that sat below its river - so it is the strongest kind of evidence the record has for the danger of a bank. Its limits are that the frequency we quote (about forty floods in three thousand years) is a background statement in the paper rather than a result of its excavations, which measure the 1642 deposit and what was built over it - still a peer-reviewed statement, which is why we prefer it to uncited counts; that it is one city on the most flood-prone river in the world, the extreme rather than the average; and that it is the Chinese half of a setting also modeled on Japan.

Used for: the Yellow River flooding Kaifeng "around 40 times over the past 3000 years" - the entry's "seven times" is found nowhere

pan-gate-enwiki

en.wikipedia Pan Gate (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Gate)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Pan Gate (盘门), the surviving gate at the southwest corner of Suzhou's old city wall, which describes it as two separate gates in one structure, a land gate for road traffic and a water gate for boats, connecting the city's inner canals and its moat to the Grand Canal.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives a real case of a city gate built as a pair - one opening for the road and one for the canal, the water gate joining the inner canals and moat to the Grand Canal - which is what a canal town's gates on our maps are drawn from. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary whose whole article rests on two references, a city page about a different gate and a travel guide; that it is one gate of one exceptionally canal-dense southern city; that its dating is loose, a Yuan rebuild on an ancient foundation; and that it shows a water gate existed and says nothing about how one was closed.

Used for: "two separate gates, one opening to a road ... and another opening to a canal", Suzhou's Land and Water Gate; the sluiced arch is unsupported by any readable page (chinahighlights)

tetsu-to-hagane-91

History of Iron and Steel Making Technology in Japan, Tetsu-to-Hagané 91(1), JStage (https://doi.org/10.2355/tetsutohagane1955.91.1_2)

What it is: "History of Iron and Steel Making Technology in Japan", a review article by Mitsuru Tate published in 2005 in Tetsu-to-Hagane, the journal of the Iron and Steel Institute of Japan (volume 91, issue 1), tracing the tatara smelt and the refining that followed it across the long history of Japanese ironmaking.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a metallurgist's survey of Japanese ironmaking from the sixth century to 1923, in the field's own journal, cited for a seventeenth-century Japanese process - the two-stage refining in which pig iron from the tatara was worked to wrought iron by the great smithy as a separate operation - so era, place and method line up with the use exactly.
