Its limits are that it is an invited overview in a peer-reviewed journal, a specialist's synthesis rather than reviewed primary research; and that it describes a process and not buildings, so how a smithy stood on a map rests elsewhere.

Used for: the two-stage refining of tatara iron

ohitayama-tatara-enwiki

en.wikipedia Ohitayama Tatara Iron Works (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohitayama_Tatara_Iron_Works)

Used for: dōba, ōkajiba, wari-tetsu; the Chugoku mountains' 80% share

wagner-ming-iron

Donald Wagner, Iron production in three Ming texts (https://donwagner.dk/MingFe/MingFe.html)

What it is: "Iron production in three Ming texts," a page by Donald B. Wagner presenting translations and commentary on Ming-dynasty Chinese accounts of the iron industry, among them Song Yingxing's description of the chao fining hearth and a passage numbering the workforce of one ironworks.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a translated and annotated set of Ming texts on ironmaking by the leading historian of Chinese ferrous metallurgy, cited for the fining hearth as Song Yingxing describes it and for the size of a large iron enterprise's workforce - core setting material, not a stand-in. Its limits are that it is a conference paper on the author's own site rather than a journal article; that the workforce figures come from Qu Dajun's account of about 1680 looking back at the late Ming; and that Wagner's own commentary warns that the passage describes a firm running several ironworks, which Qu Dajun seems to have misread as one, so the figures are an enterprise's and not one works' - a caution our text must carry with the numbers.

Used for: the chao fining hearth as Song Yingxing describes it; the 200 charcoal producers / 200 furnace tenders / 300 miners

wagner-fining-puddling

Donald Wagner, Traditional Chinese fining and puddling (http://donwagner.dk/arch-iron/eu/fining-puddling-china-eu.html)

What it is: "Traditional Chinese fining and puddling," a page by Donald B. Wagner, the historian of Chinese iron and steel technology, on his own site. It describes how Chinese ironworkers turned cast iron into wrought iron: wood, charcoal and broken cast iron charged into a hearth and lit, the half-melted mass stirred with an iron rod under a blast of air, a family of methods known collectively as chao, "stir frying."

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives what happens inside the building - pig iron stirred under blast until it comes out as wrought iron - which is what makes a finery on our maps a finery and not a smithy. Its limits are that it is a specialist's web page rather than a peer-reviewed article; that it is about China, one half of the setting; that it describes a process rather than a workshop; and that the detail we quote comes from twentieth-century field reports of a surviving traditional process - a 1936 account and a 1958 report from southern Henan and Sichuan - which the author treats as continuous with the premodern practice, so it is a modern observation of an old technique and is labeled as such.

Used for: fining as stir-frying pig iron under blast

xuxiebian-han-fining

Cast Iron Smelting and Fining: an Eastern Han site at Xuxiebian, Sichuan (Project MUSE 725769) (https://muse.jhu.edu/article/725769)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the practice running back to the Eastern Han

fao-charcoal-safety

FAO, Simple technologies for charcoal making, ch. 5 safety precautions (https://www.fao.org/4/X5555E/x5555e06.htm)

What it is: The safety chapter of Simple technologies for charcoal making, a technical handbook from the UN Food and Agriculture Organization on small-scale charcoal production. It explains that freshly made charcoal heats itself and can catch fire on its own, that the fine dust is the worst offender, and that a fresh burn should be left in the open air before it is bagged or stored.

Why it applies, and its limits: The chemistry of fresh charcoal is a property of the material and not of the century, so it explains why a charcoal burner's stack and store sit apart from thatched roofs - which is all we take from it. The limit is that this is a modern handbook: the specific waiting times it recommends are today's handling guidance, an anchor for the practice rather than a rule anyone in a premodern village would have stated in hours and days.

Used for: self-heating of fresh charcoal, fines as the worst case, the 24-hour open-air rule and the 8-day threshold

tonya-enwiki

en.wikipedia Ton'ya (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ton%27ya)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on ton'ya, the trade brokers of early modern Japan who acted as wholesalers, warehouse managers and shipment managers, and who became central to the economy of the Edo period (1603-1867).

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it only for what a ton'ya was and what work it did, which is enough to justify drawing a merchant's warehouse-and-office block in a town's commercial quarter and to name it correctly. It is a tertiary summary, so we take its figures and dates only where its own references support them, and it describes an institution rather than a building - nothing about the size, plan or placement of a ton'ya on our maps comes from it.

Used for: the wholesaler-warehouseman of the Edo economy

fires-in-edo-enwiki

en.wikipedia Fires in Edo (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fires_in_Edo)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Fires in Edo", an encyclopedia summary, well referenced to Japanese scholarship, of the repeated great fires of the Tokugawa capital - how often they came, what set them off, when in the year they fell, how the city fought them, and the thick-walled plastered storehouses (dozo) that survived them.

Why it applies, and its limits: We take from it the seasonal pattern - that the serious fires clustered in the dry months under the north and northwest winds, from January to April with March the worst - which is a matter of climate and so applies to a town as much as to the capital; and the construction of the storehouse, an earthen-walled, tile-roofed building a map draws differently from a timber house. It is a tertiary summary, used only where its own references support the point, and it is about the largest city in Japan: the season and the storehouse transfer to a smaller settlement, the scale and frequency of its conflagrations do not.

Used for: the winter clustering of serious fires

sizes-koku

Sizes.com, "What is the unit called a koku?" (https://www.sizes.com/units/koku.htm)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the charcoal hyō as a bale of indeterminate size

nanbu-date-mounds-enwiki

en.wikipedia Nanbu-Date border mounds (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanbu-Date_border_mounds)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the Nanbu-Date border mounds, the earth mounds raised in the early Edo period to mark the boundary between Morioka domain and Sendai domain - a line of about 130 km running from the Ou mountains east to the Pacific - which the shogunate re-confirmed along with the two clans' holdings in 1642.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the fact that a domain boundary in this period was a made thing, marked on the ground by heaped earth, which is why such mounds can appear at the edge of a map. It is a tertiary summary whose details we take only where its own references support them, and it records one boundary in the far northeast standing for the general practice, so we draw the type of marker from it rather than any particular spacing.

Used for: the earth-mound boundary between Morioka and Sendai, reconfirmed 1642

kuniezu-enwiki

en.wikipedia Kuniezu (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuniezu)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the kuniezu (国絵図), the series of provincial land maps that the Tokugawa shogunate ordered every province to produce during the Edo period.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it only for the plain fact that a central government of this kind commissioned province-wide maps, which is what makes a surveyed boundary on our maps a thing such a state would have had. Its limits are that it is a short tertiary summary of a single national program; and that the article lists what the maps showed - mountains, rivers, roads, landmarks, village names, rice yields, castle towns - and says nothing about boundaries drawn on them, so that half of the record's claim rests on other sources or stands as a guess.

Used for: the shogunate's provincial maps (the page lists their contents - mountains, rivers, roads, landmarks, village names, rice yields, castle towns - and says nothing about boundaries drawn on them)

mukoyama-linear-borders

Mukoyama, Naosuke, "The Eastern cousins of European sovereign states? The development of linear borders in early modern Japan", European Journal of International Relations 29(2) (2023) 255-282 (https://doi.org/10.1177/13540661221133206)

What it is: An argument, in the leading European journal of international relations, that early modern Japan developed linear borders of its own rather than importing the idea. It gathers the evidence out of the Japanese historians - boundary disputes, the mounds and stones and trees domains built along agreed lines, the guardhouses that policed them, and the provincial maps the shogunate ordered five times between 1605 and 1835 - and reproduces two period boundary maps and photographs of markers still standing.

Why it applies, and its limits: No other readable source assembles the whole case for drawing a clan border as a LINE: the agreed line, the hundred-odd numbered mounds along 130 km between Morioka and Sendai, the guardhouse of 102 soldiers, the domain currency that stopped at the boundary, and the shogunate’s own instruction that borders be depicted unambiguously on the map. Three limits, each the author’s own admission. He reads the historians rather than the documents, so on any single number the record is at one remove. He states that the change was uneven - furthest advanced in the peripheral outside-lord domains, weakest around Kyoto and Nara, where dozens of lords held ground together - so a border near a capital has weaker warrant than one in the marches. And the paper is about Japan alone; it lists China among its open questions, so it licenses the Japanese half of this setting and not the Chinese one.

Used for: domains building a territorial order of agreed boundaries and mutual exclusion, and the line being drawn on a map as the authentic act

irripro-jiegao-lulu

History of Irrigation - irrigation tools (http://www.irripro.net/en/nd.jsp?id=113) with Baidu Baike Lulu (a weaker reference)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the shadoof and the windlass; the Ming-Qing upgrade to animal power and deeper wells

ide-japanese-experience

IDE, Passing on "The Japanese Experience", rural society (https://d-arch.ide.go.jp/je_archive/english/society/wp_je_unu4.html)

What it is: "Development of local culture and the irrigation system of the Azusa Basin" by Akira Tamaki, published in 1979 by the Institute of Developing Economies as part of the “Japanese Experience of the UNU Human and Social Development Programme” series, and kept in the institute's online archive. It studies the Azusa River basin near Matsumoto in Nagano: the customary rules by which villages shared irrigation water, the difference between the dry fields on the river terraces and the wet paddy below them, and the repeated attempts to bring water up onto the terraces.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the distinction between dry field (hatake) and wet paddy (suiden) and for why a settlement sitting above the water is stuck with the former: the late-Tokugawa schemes to irrigate the Hata terraces were defeated again and again by the downstream villages who would have lost the water. Its limits: it is a close study of one river basin in one province, so its customs are local before they are national, and the terrace-against-paddy contrast is a fact of that valley's landform rather than a general rule; and it runs from the Tokugawa period through twentieth-century modernization, so only its account of the older arrangements bears on our settlements.

Used for: hatake vs suiden; the defeated late-Tokugawa hata irrigation schemes

harie-kabata

Harie Shozu no Sato, the kabata wells (https://ihcsacafe-en.ihcsa.or.jp/news/harie/)

What it is: A 2019 English-language article on IHCSA Cafe, the cultural information site of Japan's International Hospitality and Conference Service Association, written with the cooperation of the local Harie Shozu no Sato committee. It describes the Harie district in Shiga - "the village of living water" - where spring water rises into kabata, built basins inside and beside the houses, and explains how households use that water and how the community looks after it.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the character of a village water point rather than for any measurement: that one source served drinking, cooking and washing, and that the place where water was drawn was also where villagers stopped to talk - which is why a well on our maps is drawn as somewhere people gather and not as plumbing. It is a short institutional piece written with the village's own preservation committee to introduce the place to foreign readers, and it describes a surviving village as it is today, so it is evidence for a continuing practice rather than a documented historical one; Harie is also unusual in having spring water at every door, which most villages did not.

Used for: the domestic and social character of the village well

kabu-ido-commons

Endo, Takahiro, "The Kabu-ido system and factors affecting local groundwater extraction control: case study of a customary groundwater management in Japan", Water History 14 (2022) 169-191 (https://doi.org/10.1007/s12685-022-00302-1)

What it is: A study of one Edo-period institution by which sixteen villages behind a single ring levee on the Noubi Plain limited how many wells they could sink, worked out of the villages’ own papers - the 1812 agreement between the upper and lower villages, and the Nishimatsu Diary, which records the well patrols year by year from 1821. It is the fullest account there is of a farming community policing its own water without an authority above it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the clearest attested case of a community capping its own well count by agreement, which is exactly the practice this project draws more freely than the record attests, so it is evidence worth having against our own decision. Its limits cut that weight down a long way, and all four are the paper’s own. It is ONE district, not a country, and the paper says the institution is little known even in Japan. It is unusual rather than typical - the literature values it as a pioneering example. It runs from 1812 to the 1930s, the last half-century of the Edo period and the sixty years after it. And the wells it caps are ARTESIAN IRRIGATION wells whose free flow waterlogged the paddy of the villages downhill; the grievance is drainage nuisance, not scarcity, and a draw-well in a house yard raises none of it. One correction worth stating: the agreement’s only money runs the other way from a licence fee - the upper villages pay the well inspectors a ryo a year, and no charge for holding a well appears anywhere in it.

Used for: the cap on well numbers, the permit, the villagers’ own patrols, and the government preferring an internal settlement

kochi-seiri-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 耕地整理 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%80%95%E5%9C%B0%E6%95%B4%E7%90%86)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 耕地整理 (kochi seiri, arable-land consolidation), the rearrangement of farm plots into regular rectangles squared to the compass, covering the Shizuoka method first carried out in 1872 and the national consolidation law of 1899.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for a date rather than a measurement: it places the neat rectangular grid of Japanese paddy in the Meiji era and after, which is why the fields on our maps keep the irregular shapes the ground gave them. It is a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, and here what we take is an institution and its statute, which is the kind of thing such an article states plainly.

Used for: rectangular plot consolidation as a Meiji institution - the Shizuoka method of 1872, the 耕地整理法 of 1899/1900, 「the regularization of plot shape and its alignment to the cardinal directions」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「区画の整形化と正方位化」)

nougyoudoboku-keihan

のうぎょうとぼく, 水田圃場における畦畔について (https://nougyoudoboku.com/a-ridge-between-rice-fields/)

What it is: "On bunds in paddy fields" (水田圃場における畦畔について), an article published in December 2022 and revised since on のうぎょうとぼく ("Agriculture and Me"), a Japanese site about agricultural civil engineering written under the name Oimoya by a former prefectural officer in that field. It explains what a bund is and what it does - holding the water in, dividing the plots, giving a footway for weeding and spraying, growing fodder grass - and tabulates the standard cross-section: a trapezoid with a 1:1 batter, 30 cm high and 30 cm across the top, with heavier sections where the drop between plots is large and in cold regions.

Why it applies, and its limits: Those dimensions set the width of every bund we draw and the grassy strip on top of it, and they come from a trained agricultural engineer rather than a general-interest writer. The honest limit is era and method: this is a present-day design standard for consolidated, machine-worked paddy, published on a specialist's own site rather than in a reviewed journal, so we use it as the shape a soil bund naturally takes for the job, which a hand-built one also had to do, and not as a measurement of a premodern field.

Used for: the standard bund - 「The standard is a trapezoid with a 1:1 batter on the sloping faces, a height of 30 cm and a top width of 30 cm.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「法面勾配1:1・高さ30cm・上幅30cmの台形が標準」) (so ~3 ft at the base), cold regions ~50 cm top / ~40 cm high

seijoue-kotobank

コトバンク 正条植 (https://kotobank.jp/word/正条植-85971)

What it is: The entry for 正条植 (seijoue, straight-row rice planting) on Kotobank, a Japanese reference site that reprints entries from published dictionaries and encyclopedias, describing the practice and the national campaign that spread it.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it to date the practice, because it tells us straight-row planting was rare before Meiji and was promoted nationally through the 1890s and 1900s together with the hand-pushed inter-row weeder (田打車), planting ropes and rulers, which is why our paddies are not drawn as tidy grids of aligned seedlings. It is a tertiary reference aggregating dictionary entries, so we take the dating from it rather than fine detail, and because it is written about a Meiji reform, what it tells us about earlier practice comes by contrast rather than by direct description.

Used for: straight-row planting rare before Meiji, promoted nationally in the 1890s-1900s together with the hand-pushed inter-row weeder (田打車), planting ropes and rulers

seijoue-seika

精華町 せいか舎, 正条植え (https://seikasya.town.seika.kyoto.jp/essays/seijoue)

What it is: An essay on せいか舎, the local-history site of the town of Seika in Kyoto prefecture, about 正条植え, straight-row rice planting, and how it spread through the countryside.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a second and independent local voice agreeing with the same dating we take from the dictionary entry, that row planting spread in the Meiji period and brought weeder-based weeding and mid-season cultivation with it. It is a municipal essay written for residents rather than a scholarly study, and it speaks for one district, so we use it as corroboration of the general chronology and not as a measurement of anything.

Used for: seijoue spreading in Meiji and bringing weeder-based weeding and mid-season cultivation with it

kubota-transplanting

Kubota, 稲作の歴史 - 田植え (https://www.kubota.co.jp/kubotatanbo/history/tools/transplanting.html)

What it is: A page from the public-education series on the history of Japanese rice growing published by Kubota, the farm machinery manufacturer, covering transplanting: how seedlings were set out by hand, and how the older scattered pattern the page calls 乱雑植え ("disorderly planting") gave way to marked straight rows laid out with ropes and rolled frames.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the date at which straight-row transplanting spread and for the simple tools that marked the lines, which is what tells us our premodern paddies should still show the older irregular clumps rather than a ruled grid. It is a company's outreach page rather than scholarship, and it is written looking back from modern Japanese farming, so we take its sequence and its rough date and not any figure of yield or spacing.

Used for: the 1890s adoption, ropes and rolled frames marking the lines

kokudaka-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 石高 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E7%9F%B3%E9%AB%98)

What it is: Japanese Wikipedia's article on 石高 (kokudaka), the rice-volume measure used to rate land and to pay retainers, including what one koku was taken to represent and what a retainer was actually issued.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use its rule of thumb - one koku as an adult's rice for a year - to turn a settlement's assessed yield into a number of mouths it could feed, and the article supplies its own hedge, that a standard stipend ran nearer 1.8 koku of brown rice, which keeps the round figure from being treated as exact. It is a tertiary summary whose figures we take where it states them with that qualification, and the koku equivalence is in any case a convention of assessment rather than a measured diet.

Used for: one koku as an adult's annual rice (「One koku corresponds to the quantity of rice that one adult eats in one year.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「一石は大人一人が一年に食べる米の量に相当する」)), with the page's own hedge that real stipends ran ~1.8 koku of brown rice

gokogomin-kotobank

コトバンク 五公五民 (https://kotobank.jp/word/五公五民-64520)

What it is: The Kotobank entry for 五公五民 - "five to the lord, five to the commoner" - the rice tax rate. Kotobank is a Japanese reference site that reprints entries from published dictionaries and encyclopedias, and this one records the shift from a four-to-six split in the early Edo period to a five-to-five split after the Kyoho era of 1716-36.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the nominal tax rate in the words of five published reference works, which is what our rent and yield arithmetic starts from. Its limits are that it is a tertiary aggregator; that the rate is a nominal one, and the sources themselves say the real burden varied with soil, harvest and region; and that one of them hedges the very transition we quote - the shift to five-five after the Kyoho era is "said to have" happened "but is not certain" - so the later rate is a convention rather than a settled fact.

Used for: the tax rate - 四公六民 in early Edo, 五公五民 after the Kyōhō era (1716-36)

satoyama-enwiki

en.wikipedia Satoyama (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satoyama)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on satoyama, the managed border zone between the wooded hills and the farmed flat in Japan, covering its mosaic of woodland, paddy, dry field, grassland and ponds and the ways villagers worked it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the definition of the managed belt between village and mountain, the mosaic of land uses in it, and two practices our maps draw - leaf litter gathered for the paddies and the coppice cut on a cycle. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary flagged as still being translated from the Japanese article; that the one Edo-period statement we lean on, the leaf gathering, carries no citation in the article; that the fifteen-to-twenty-year cutting cycle is stated in the present tense and cited to a 2017 ecology paper about present-day management, so a premodern rotation is being read from a modern study of a traditional practice; and that the word itself is largely a twentieth-century coinage, which is our knowledge rather than the page's.

Used for: the border zone between foothills and arable flat land; the mosaic of forest, paddy, dry field, grassland and ponds; Edo-era leaf gathering for paddy fertilizer; cutting every 15-20 years

louzeyuan-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 漏澤園 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%BC%8F%E6%BE%A4%E5%9C%92)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on 漏澤園 (louzeyuan), the state-funded paupers' burial grounds of imperial China, from Cai Jing's proposal of 1104 through their continuation under the Yuan and Ming. It describes how they were sited on high, open, barren ground behind a fence on all four sides, and how each plot was numbered by the Thousand Character Classic and registered with the dead person's name, origin and dates.

Why it applies, and its limits: It answers, for a city map, where the poor were buried and what that ground looked like: bad land, deliberately walled off, laid out in numbered rows rather than left as an open field. It is a tertiary summary in Chinese, and it describes a Chinese institution, which suits the imperial-Chinese half of our setting more directly than the Japanese half; we take from it the siting and the layout, which its own sources support, rather than any count.

Used for: Cai Jing's 1104 proposal; siting 「high, open, barren ground, with a fence built around all four sides」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「高曠不毛之地，四周建有圍欄」); plots numbered by the 千字文 with name, origin and dates; continued through Yuan and Ming

toribeno-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 鳥辺野 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E9%B3%A5%E8%BE%BA%E9%87%8E)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on Toribeno (鳥辺野), the great burial ground on the eastern hills of Kyoto, one of the three famous funerary fields of the old capital along with Adashino to the west and Rendaino to the north, attested in literature from the Heian period onward in works such as the Tale of Genji and Essays in Idleness.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one point about form rather than any number: that the extent of such a field had no clear definition, which is our warrant for drawing a burial ground as an unbounded stretch of hillside rather than a walled or fenced plot. It is a tertiary summary, so we take from it only what its own references support; it also describes the capital's own enormous burial fields, so it tells us the shape of the thing and never the scale a village graveyard should have.

Used for: the great Kyoto burial ground with 「As to its extent, there is no clear definition.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「範囲について、明確な定義はない」) - an unbounded hillside field

fushimi-inari-senbon

伏見稲荷大社, 千本鳥居 (https://inari.jp/sp/map/spot_07/)

What it is: The official website of Fushimi Inari Taisha in Kyoto, the head shrine of the Inari cult - its visitors' questions page and a map page on the Senbon Torii, the famous tunnels of vermilion gates on its mountain paths. The site gives the shrine's own account of the gates as votive offerings, a custom it dates from the Edo period onward, and its present count of about ten thousand across the whole mountain approach.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us that the gates are donations and roughly how thickly the most heavily endowed shrine in the country came to be lined with them, which is the upper bound a map needs when it draws a shrine approach. Its limits are that it is the institution describing itself, not a survey; that the count is a present-day round number accumulated since the Edo period, not a period census, and only the whole-mountain figure is readable on the site, so any density per stretch of path is derived by us; and that this is the most famous shrine of its kind, so it is a ceiling and never a typical case.

Used for: 「At present about 10,000 torii stand along the whole of the mountain's approach.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「現在は約1万基の鳥居がお山の参道全体に並んで立っています」); gates donated by worshippers from the Edo period onward (the Senbon Torii sub-count is on neither page)

meiji-jingu-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 明治神宮 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%98%8E%E6%B2%BB%E7%A5%9E%E5%AE%AE)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on Meiji Jingu, the large Shinto shrine in Tokyo, covering its grounds, its approach and its buildings.

Why it applies, and its limits: The article counts eight torii along the approach and gives no distances between them, so no spacing figure on our maps comes from it. Beyond being a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, it describes a modern shrine of exceptional scale, which makes it a poor model for the small country shrines our settlements mostly carry.

Used for: the shrine's torii - the page counts 8, not 3; no spacing figures

religion-song-enwiki

en.wikipedia Religion in the Song dynasty (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_the_Song_dynasty)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Religion in the Song dynasty", a survey of Buddhism, Daoism and folk religion in China between 960 and 1279. The one figure we take from it is a dated count: records for the year 1221 gave 400,000 monks and 61,000 nuns in the dynasty, against a population the same article puts at around 100 million.

Why it applies, and its limits: Deciding how many monks a settlement of a given size can hold needs an order of magnitude for the share of a population living in religious orders, and this supplies one attributed to a dynastic record - under half a percent. The limits are real: an encyclopedia is a tertiary summary, so this figure is used precisely because it is stated as coming from a record and no further; a Song Chinese count is standing in for the whole setting; and an imperial count is a count of registered clergy, which is not the same as everyone living in a temple.

Used for: "In 1221, records counted the existence of 400,000 monks and 61,000 nuns in the dynasty" - the one census figure actually read

chang-jiang-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 長江 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E9%95%B7%E6%B1%9F)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the Chang Jiang, the Yangtze. Among much else it lists the separate names the river carries along its course, from the headwater Tuotuo He through the Jinsha and the Chuan, Jing and Yangzi reaches, the last of them taken from a ferry crossing.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is straightforward evidence for something our maps get wrong by default, that a real river is not one name from source to sea but a chain of local names, each belonging to the stretch the people beside it know. It is a tertiary encyclopedia summary, and it describes one enormous continental river in modern usage, so we take the naming habit from it as a principle and not the names or the reach lengths as anything to scale from.

Used for: the reach names - 沱沱河 -> 通天河 -> 金沙江 -> 川江 (宜賓-宜昌) -> 荊江 -> 揚子江 (from the 揚子津 ferry)

shinano-gawa-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 信濃川 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E4%BF%A1%E6%BF%83%E5%B7%9D)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the Shinano River, the longest river in Japan, which notes that the same river is the Shinano within Niigata prefecture and becomes the Chikuma once it passes up into Nagano.

Why it applies, and its limits: It supports a small point of naming that our maps follow, that one river can legitimately carry different names along its course, so a river labeled one thing at a settlement need not be labeled the same thing upstream. It is a tertiary summary about a single modern river, used only for a naming convention it states plainly, and nothing on any map depends on a number from it.

Used for: "「It is called the Shinano River in Niigata prefecture; going up into Nagano prefecture the name changes to the Chikuma River.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「信濃川と呼ばれているのは新潟県域で、長野県に遡ると「千曲川」と呼称が変わる」)"

sumida-gawa-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 隅田川 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E9%9A%85%E7%94%B0%E5%B7%9D)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 隅田川, on the river through eastern Tokyo, including the several older names by which different stretches of it were known - Ōkawa, Asakusagawa, Miyatogawa.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is cited for a small point of naming that matters on a map: one river can carry different names along different reaches and in different mouths, so a watercourse crossing a city need not be labeled the same at both ends. It is an encyclopedia article about one specific river in one city; we use it as an example of the naming habit rather than as evidence about any other river, and where it does not confirm a name we have seen elsewhere we do not claim it.

Used for: 大川, 浅草川, 宮戸川 as names of the same river; 両国川 not confirmed there

yato-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 谷戸 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%B0%B7%E6%88%B8)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 谷戸 (yato, also read yatsu), a tertiary encyclopedia entry on the small spring-fed valleys that bite into the hill country of eastern Japan, how their floors were farmed and how the woods on the slopes above them were used in common.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for why a narrow valley floor makes easily worked paddy once it drains, and for the common rights villagers held over the neighboring woods, which is what puts a firewood wood on the slope beside a valley-bottom field on our maps. It is a tertiary summary, so we take the pattern from it and not a number; it speaks for eastern Japan in particular; and carrying those common rights on from firewood to a reed bed is a step our own record takes, not something the article says.

Used for: yatsu as easily-worked paddy land given drainage; iriai rights over the adjacent woods (firewood - the reed-bed application is the entry's extension)

shitsuden-kotobank

コトバンク 湿田 (https://kotobank.jp/word/湿田-74168)

What it is: The Kotobank entry for 湿田 (shitsuden, "wet paddy"). Kotobank is a Japanese reference site that reprints entries from established published encyclopedias and dictionaries rather than writing its own, so the entry is a dictionary definition of the badly drained rice field that stays waterlogged the year round, with a note on the long conversion of such fields to the drained kind.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the wet paddy as a field class - the poorly drained paddy that never dries - and the direction of its conversion, which is what puts wet paddy on our maps at all. Its limits are that it is a very short tertiary aggregation of dictionary entries, carrying the distinction and the direction of change but no date or proportion; and that the conversion it documents at any scale is the Meiji-and-later national drainage program, a post-1868 undertaking outside the setting, so only the older, western-Japan beginning of the story it mentions bears on our fields.

Used for: the poorly drained paddy that never dries; wet-to-dry conversion beginning in late Yayoi western Japan

hibiya-irie-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 日比谷入江 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%97%A5%E6%AF%94%E8%B0%B7%E5%85%A5%E6%B1%9F)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 日比谷入江 (Hibiya Inlet), the tidal inlet that once reached inland to the foot of Edo Castle and is now dry land in central Tokyo. It records that filling began at the inlet's innermost northern end in 1592 with spoil from the works on the castle's western bailey, went on in earnest from 1603, and was finished in the early Edo period.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is our worked example of a waterfront settlement making new ground out of the earth dug for its own castle, which is the kind of thing that explains why a town's blocks near the water are newer and more regular than the ones behind them. It is a tertiary summary of one episode in one city, its dates are the traditional ones, and Edo's scale is far beyond anything we draw - so what carries across is the sequence, not the acreage.

Used for: infill from 1592 with the spoil of the Nishinomaru works, in earnest from 1603, complete in early Edo

sotobori-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 外濠 (東京都) (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/外濠_(東京都))

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 外濠 (東京都), on the outer moat of Edo castle in what is now Tokyo - how it was dug, which watercourses were pressed into it, and what has since become of it.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for a point about how a fortified city treats its water: the Sotobori was not a ditch dug beside a river but the river itself relocated, the Hirakawa's course moved into the excavation, which is the pattern our walled maps follow when a stream meets a defensive line. It is an encyclopedia article on one specific and unusually large city work - a shogunal capital, not a provincial town - so the principle scales down to our maps only as a principle, and the dimensions of Edo's moat are not numbers we carry across.

Used for: 「The Sotobori River was made by an excavation that relocated the course of the Hirakawa, which flowed into Hibiya Inlet.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「外濠川は、日比谷入江へ注ぐ平川の流路を移設した開削で作られた」) - the Hirakawa turned into the moat

bitchu-takamatsu-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 備中高松城 and 備中高松城の戦い (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/高松城_(備中国))

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on Bitchu Takamatsu Castle, a Sengoku-period fortress in what is now Okayama that stood on a belt of low, wet ground which formed its natural moats. Hashiba Hideyoshi's 1582 siege, in which an earth dam thrown around the castle drowned it in an artificial lake, has its own companion article, which is where the term "swamp castle" and the size of the lake are given.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a plain, well referenced account of a castle whose defense was its marsh rather than its stonework, which is exactly the question our wet defensive belt asks: whether low wetland was treated as a work of fortification and not merely as bad ground. It is a tertiary encyclopedia summary, so we take its figures only where its own references support them; the flooding is a single spectacular siege rather than routine practice; and it tells us that a wet belt was defensively real, not how wide an ordinary one was.

Used for: 「a swamp castle (numajiro) standing on low, wet ground」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「低湿地にある沼城」), 「It was a low, wet zone, and these formed natural moats.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「低湿地帯でこれらが天然の堀を形成していた」); the 1582 water siege raising a 200 ha lake

kagawa-tameike-structure

香川県, ため池の構造 (https://www.pref.kagawa.lg.jp/tochikai/about_tameike/repair/structure.html)

What it is: : that the intake is one facility, an inclined or vertical pipe drawing off stored water into a conduit through the dam base, with a sediment gate at the very bottom for clearing and for the last of the water, and that the water-facing slope of the bank is armored against wave erosion with riprap, stone pitching or concrete facing.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is why our reservoirs get a single intake works rather than scattered openings, and why one stretch of a pond's shore is deliberately bare stone while the rest can be planted, which makes the rule about a pond's margin a matter of position instead of one answer for the whole edge. It is a modern maintenance page: the concrete facing is of our time, the stone pitching and riprap are old, and we take the engineering reason for the armoring rather than the modern material.

Used for: the intake as one facility - 斜樋/堅樋 into the 底樋 discharging to the canal, plus a sediment drain Also (a second entry under the same key, merged from a second entry under the same key): the dam's water-contact slope being armored against wave erosion - 捨石、石張り、コンクリートブロック張 - i.e. one part of a reservoir's shore is bare by engineering, which is why the rule is positional rather than a single answer for the whole margin.

offtake-angle-studies

Diversion-angle hydraulics (a 30° angle cutting sediment entry by up to 64%; no paper read) (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1001627920300706) - unsupported by any readable page: search syntheses of several ResearchGate / ScienceDirect papers - "maximum water discharge and minimum sediment discharge when its diversion angle was 30° or 45° among 90°, 75°, 60°, 45°, and 30°"

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the acute downstream-pointing offtake, 30-45° over 90°

fao-pond-water

FAO, Water for animals ch. 6 surface reservoirs (https://www.fao.org/4/r7488e/r7488e06.htm)

What it is: The surface-reservoir chapter of Water for animals, a UN Food and Agriculture Organization manual on watering livestock. It describes what a dug pond loses - evaporation from the surface, which it puts as high as 2 m a year, and seepage through the floor - and why such a pond needs a spillway to carry storm runoff past the dam.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our maps need to know that a standing pond is not a sealed tank and that it must have somewhere for a flood to go, both of which this states plainly. The limits are that it is a modern development manual written for hot, dry grazing country, so its evaporation figure is an outer bound rather than a number for a temperate rice valley, and that it gives these losses in prose rather than as a worked water balance.

Used for: evaporation (up to 2 m/yr) and seepage as the losses of a standing reservoir; the page states the losses in prose, not as the balance equation

desire-path-enwiki

en.wikipedia Desire path (cites Hampton and Cole 1988 for the fifteen-passage figure) (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desire_path)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Desire path", a general account of the informal tracks worn by people walking the route they prefer rather than the one laid out for them. It states that as few as fifteen passages over a site can be enough to make a distinct trail, and that such a path usually takes the shortest or most easily walked line, citing Hampton and Cole's 1988 handbook Soft Paths.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our maps draw the worn tracks between a farmhouse, its yard and its fields, and this tells us how little traffic it takes to make one visible and what line it follows around a slope or an obstacle, which is exactly the rule we needed. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, and that the fifteen-passage figure comes from a modern wilderness-recreation handbook about soft ground in American backcountry rather than from any study of a premodern farmyard, so we use it as an order of magnitude and not as a threshold.

Used for: "as few as 15 passages over a site can be enough to create a distinct trail"; a desire path "usually represents the shortest or the most easily navigated route", sidestepping slopes and obstacles

ninety-nine-pi-desire

99% Invisible on desire paths (https://99percentinvisible.org/article/least-resistance-desire-paths-can-lead-better-design/)

What it is: An article from 99% Invisible, the American design magazine and podcast, on desire paths - the informal tracks that people wear across open ground where the built route does not go, which the article says can form after as few as fifteen crossings.

Why it applies, and its limits: It corroborates the reading that a path is where feet have found the shortest or easiest line - a mechanism of walking that is not bound to a period - which is the only thing we take from it. Its limits are that it is a design magazine's popular article, modern, Western and non-scholarly, with no East Asian or premodern content; that it supplies none of our distances; and that the one number the record quotes from it, that a path forms after about fifteen crossings, is given there without attribution, so the number's anchor is the 1988 study the record names separately, not the magazine.

Used for: corroborating the desire-line reading

ma-2024-desire-paths

Ma, Brandt, Seipel and Ma (2024), Environment and Planning B - agent-based desire paths (https://doi.org/10.1177/23998083231184884) - unsupported by any readable page: paywalled; its abstract is public and is about a field-of-view angle, NOT turn minimization

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: nothing load-bearing - recorded as the case where a search summary recast a parameter as a finding

pmc7538448-levee

"Earthworm species and density in semi-natural grasslands on rice paddy levees in Japanese satoyama", PMC 7538448 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7538448/)

What it is: A research paper available through PubMed Central, "Earthworm species and density in semi-natural grasslands on rice paddy levees in Japanese satoyama", which counts earthworms in the grassy banks between paddy fields and, in describing its study ground, sets out what those levees are and how farmers keep them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is fieldwork on Japanese paddies, and it states plainly what a levee is for - holding the water in and carrying the farmer and their tools between fields - and that the banks are kept as mown grass, which is exactly the paddy edge our maps draw. Its limits are that it is a modern study whose real subject is soil ecology rather than farming history, and that its account of upkeep includes present-day herbicide use, so we take from it the levee's purpose and its cut grassy surface and not the details of modern management.

Used for: levees "constructed and maintained to retain water in the paddies and to allow the passage of people and transportation of tools"; "farmers generally maintain levee grasslands by periodic mowing"

paddy-field-enwiki

en.wikipedia Paddy field (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paddy_field)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the paddy field, a tertiary summary of flooded rice cultivation, its history across Asia and the physical form of the fields themselves.

Why it applies, and its limits: One sentence in it, describing prehistoric Korean paddies laid out as squares and rectangles separated by bunds about 10 cm high, is where the height of the low earth walls our maps draw between plots comes from. Its limits are kind and place: an encyclopedia is a tertiary summary whose figures we accept only where its own references support them, and the example is Korean and very early rather than Japanese, cited because it is a readable public statement of a bund height and because a bund's height is set by how deep a flooded plot is held, which varies little across the rice-growing world.

Used for: plots "separated by bunds approximately 10 cm in height" (a Korean example)

irri-bund-summary

IRRI Rice Knowledge Bank on bund height (the "15-150 cm" range seen nowhere) (http://www.knowledgebank.irri.org/step-by-step-production/pre-planting/land-preparation/how-to-construct-bunds)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: bunds built ~20 cm to avoid overflow - unsupported by any readable page

visit-toyama-sankyoson

Visit Toyama, the Tonami dispersed settlement (https://visit-toyama-japan.com/en/travel-inspiration/sankyoson)

What it is: The English page on the Tonami Plain dispersed settlement (散居村, sankyoson) from Visit Toyama, the tourism site for Toyama Prefecture. It describes an alluvial fan on which more than 7,000 farmhouses, each ringed by its own kainyo homestead grove, are scattered across roughly 220 square kilometers, a landscape it dates to more than five hundred years of formation.

Why it applies, and its limits: It describes the dispersed-farmstead country of the Tonami plain - thousands of farmhouses each standing in its own fields inside its own grove, and what the grove did for the house - which is the pattern one of our settlement forms is drawn from. Its limits are that it is a tourism organization's promotional page that cites no survey or study; that it describes a living landscape in the present tense and gives modern round counts, used here as an anchor for a premodern pattern; that it is one plain in one prefecture; and that the density our text gives, about thirty farmsteads to the square kilometer, is our own division of its two round figures and not a number the page states.

Used for: farmers building "in the middle of their cultivated rice fields so that they could easily manage the water for their own rice fields"; ~7,000 farmsteads over ~220 km2 with their kainyo groves (the density per square kilometer in our text is our own division of those two figures)

mdpi-sho-fan-groundwater

"Geochemical Study on the Annual Variation of Oxygen Isotope and Chemical Composition of Groundwater in the Sho River Alluvium Fan, Toyama, Japan", Geosciences 11(8):352 (2021) (https://www.mdpi.com/2076-3263/11/8/352; the page is public) - academic-sources/geosciences-11-00352-v2.pdf)

What it is: A peer-reviewed open-access paper in Geosciences (2021) tracing, through a year of oxygen-isotope and chemical measurements, where the shallow groundwater of the Sho River alluvial fan in Toyama comes from.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the ground under the scattered farmsteads of the Tonami plain: the plain is that river's alluvial fan, and the shallow water beneath it is a mixture of river water and rainfall, which is the setting for a farmstead that draws its own water. The paper is a modern geochemical study concerned with where the water comes from rather than how deep it lies, and it does not discuss paddies at all, so the idea that a farmstead there could reach water with a shallow well is an inference of ours and is labeled as a guess where it is used.

Used for: the Tonami plain as the Sho River alluvial fan and its shallow groundwater as a mixture of river water and precipitation (NOT paddy recharge - the paper never mentions paddy); it gives no water-table depth, so 'easily-reached water' is the record's inference, labeled GUESS since 2026-09-07

takeuchi-2010-satoyama

Kazuhiko Takeuchi, "Rebuilding the relationship between people and nature: the Satoyama Initiative", Ecological Research 25 (2010) 891-897 (https://esj-journals.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1007/s11284-010-0745-8)

What it is: A peer-reviewed article by Kazuhiko Takeuchi of the University of Tokyo and the United Nations University, in Ecological Research (2010), setting out the Satoyama Initiative: what the Japanese satoyama landscape is, how villagers worked it, and why it has come apart since the 1960s. Its opening pages give the definition the rest of the field uses - satoyama in the narrow sense as the secondary woodland and grassland beside a settlement, held in common and worked as coppice and meadow for fuel, fertilizer and fodder, and in the broad sense as the whole mosaic of woods, dry fields, paddies, canals, ponds and houses together.

Why it applies, and its limits: The land-use system it defines is the one our villages sit inside, and the author dates its fully wood-and-compost-dependent form to nineteenth-century Japan, before fossil fuels or chemical fertilizer reached the countryside. It is a modern policy review rather than a reading of period records, so we take the definition and the shape of the system from it and no measurement - it carries no cutting cycle or yield we could use, and the litter and the rotation on our maps rest on the 2019 review of satoyama management cited with it. It also corrects the pretty picture in a way worth knowing when reading one of our maps: the same author records that late Edo and early Meiji satoyama was often overused, denuded and eroding, so a well-kept coppice belt is the good case and not the average one.

Used for: the definition of satoyama - secondary woodland and grassland beside a settlement, worked as coppice and meadow for fuel, fertilizer and fodder

jiao-2019-satoyama

Yuanmei Jiao, Yinping Ding, Zhiqin Zha and Toshiya Okuro, "Crises of Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services in Satoyama Landscape of Japan: A Review on the Role of Management", Sustainability 11(2):454 (2019) (https://www.mdpi.com/2071-1050/11/2/454)

What it is: A peer-reviewed review article by Yuanmei Jiao, Yinping Ding and Zhiqin Zha of Yunnan Normal University with Toshiya Okuro of the University of Tokyo, in Sustainability (2019), gathering the published work on how Japan's satoyama landscape was managed and what happened to its plants and animals once that management stopped. Its middle section tabulates the traditional handling of each part of the landscape, from coppice woods and grasslands to thatch fields, paddies and ponds, and what each of them yielded to the households that worked it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It states the practice we draw: farmers cut, coppiced, mowed and carried off the fallen leaves on a repeating round, and the woodland floor stayed light enough for grasses and spring flowers precisely because the litter went to the fields instead of lying where it fell. Because it is a review, what it gives us is the settled picture rather than a first-hand measurement: the individual figures belong to the studies it cites, its "traditional" means Japan before the 1960s rather than the Edo period alone, and the proof that the management keeps the floor open comes from modern ecological surveys of the woods that survive, so the practice is old while the measuring of it is recent. The later half of the paper is about industrialized farming, with chemical fertilizer, consolidated paddies and concrete channels, and has no bearing on these settlements.

Used for: forest litter and compost gathered from the secondary forest for the paddies; the deciduous oaks (konara and kunugi) cut for firewood and charcoal, the coppice logged every 20 to 40 years; cutting and litter removal letting sunlight reach the floor

geography-hub-satoyama

The Geography Hub, "Japan's Satoyama Landscapes" (https://thegeographyhub.com/japans-satoyama-landscapes/)

Not cited: a page with no author, date or sources.

What it is: "Japan's Satoyama Landscapes", an article on The Geography Hub, a general-audience educational geography site that publishes explainers under headings like cultural, historical and biological geography. It carries no named author and no date. The piece surveys satoyama, the managed mosaic of coppiced woodland, paddy, grassland and water that sits between a Japanese village and the mountain behind it, how centuries of cutting and gathering produced it, and how it has fared since.

Why it applies, and its limits: Its subject is exactly ours - the managed coppice country between village and mountain - and the three things we take from it, leaf litter gathered for fertilizer, oak and chestnut cut on a cycle, and the light let onto the floor by cutting to the stump, are ordinary and well attested. Its limits are severe for a source: the page is unsigned, undated and cites no sources at all, so a reader who follows the link can trace nothing further; it mixes the historical practice with its present-day conservation; and closer public sources for the same three claims exist in the scholarly literature on satoyama. Nothing quantitative is taken from it.

Used for: leaf litter to fertilizer, oak and chestnut cut cyclically, sunlight reaching the floor

uehara-2009-agris

Uehara et al. 2009, on Rhododendron in the abandoned satoyama coppice forest (AGRIS record) (https://agris.fao.org/search/fr/records/64724aabe17b74d2224f9430)

What it is: The bibliographic record, in the FAO's international agricultural database AGRIS, for Uehara, Shigematsu, Fujii and Iwamoto, "Succession of shrub-layer vegetation and situation of wild Rhododendron in the abandoned Satoyama coppice forest," Landscape Research Japan 72(5), 2009, pp. 511-516, with the paper's abstract. The study compares a coppice woodland in Hyogo Prefecture cut twenty-five years earlier with one left alone for fifty, counting 42 plant species and 13 floor herbs in the managed stand against 23 species and 3 floor herbs in the neglected one.

Why it applies, and its limits: This is our evidence that a managed coppice has an open, flowering, species-rich floor under a cut canopy while an abandoned one goes dark and bare, which is the difference between a village woodlot we draw as worked ground and a thicket - and it supplies the fifty-year figure for how long neglect takes to show. What we read is the database record and its abstract rather than the full Japanese article, and it is a single paired comparison in one prefecture measured in the 2000s, so we use its direction and rough magnitude, not its species counts, as a general rule about coppice woodland.

Used for: a managed coppice floor under a cut canopy

waldrand-dewiki

de.wikipedia Waldrand (https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldrand)

What it is: The German Wikipedia article on the Waldrand, the forest edge, which sets out the structure of a fully grown one: from the open field inward, a herb fringe, then a belt of shrubs, then the forest mantle of trees whose crowns sweep down low on the field side.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for that three-layer profile, which is how we draw the boundary between woodland and cultivated ground as a graded edge rather than a hard line. It is a tertiary summary, so we take from it only what its own references support; it is also describing central European woodland, and it was consulted because the English-language article on woodland edges did not carry the layer terms - the layering is a general property of a sunlit forest margin, but the species that would fill each layer in East Asia are not taken from here.

Used for: the three-layer edge: herb fringe, shrub belt, forest mantle

pmc7898781-fukugi

"Distribution and utilization of homestead windbreak Fukugi trees", PMC 7898781 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7898781/)

What it is: A research paper available through PubMed Central, "Distribution and utilization of homestead windbreak Fukugi trees", a survey of the fukugi trees planted in strips around houses in the villages of Okinawa, which the authors trace to the Ryukyu Kingdom of roughly three hundred years ago and to the feng shui planning of its rural landscapes.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a survey of a surviving, dated example of the thing we plant on our maps - a maintained belt of trees set around a homestead to break the wind - so the form is attested rather than assumed. The limits are of place and of time: Okinawa is subtropical, and neither its tree species nor its Ryukyuan siting rules are those of the main Japanese islands or of imperial China, so we take the form and not the species or the spacing; and the trees measured today are the survivors of a much older planting, which makes the modern distribution an incomplete record of the old one.

Used for: Okinawan homestead windbreaks planted around 300 years ago

ijc-yamaguni

International Journal of the Commons, "External impacts on traditional commons ..." - the Yamaguni district study (https://thecommonsjournal.org/articles/10.18352/ijc.348)

What it is: "External impacts on traditional commons and present-day changes: a case study of iriai forests in Yamaguni district, Kyoto, Japan" by Daisaku Shimada, a peer-reviewed article in the International Journal of the Commons, volume 8 issue 1 (2014). Working from interviews and archival documents, it traces how the eleven villages of the Yamaguni district hold and manage their customary common forest (iriai), and how those institutions have bent under depopulation and cheap imported timber.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the finding that each of eleven neighboring villages runs its common woodland under its own distinct institutions, with detailed local rules on the method, season and quantity of what each household may take - which is why the villages on our maps differ from one another in how their woodland is held rather than sharing a single scheme. Its limits: it is a modern study whose main subject is recent change, though the institutions it describes are inherited ones; it covers a single district near Kyoto rather than the country; and it describes no boundary marks on the ground, so where a common forest ends on our maps does not come from it.

Used for: "each of the 11 villages in Yamaguni district has its own unique institutions for managing its customary common property forests"; no boundary description

kichijoji-enwiki

en.wikipedia Kichijōji (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kichijōji)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Kichijoji, on the west edge of Tokyo: a planned resettlement of the Musashino plateau after the great fire of 1657, where migrants were granted strips of land more than a kilometer long running from one road to the Tamagawa aqueduct, on poor soil that made every field a dry field, with rice stipends and house-building loans from the state.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives a real case of a village laid out on long strip lots from a road, which is the form one of our settlement types is drawn in. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, lightly sourced on the point we use; that this was a planned, state-subsidized reclamation after a fire, a deliberate special case rather than a village that grew; that it was dry field only, so it carries nothing about a paddy village's form; and that the article says nothing about where the houses stood on the strips - the house at the road end with its fields behind is our placement, drawn from elsewhere, as are the woodlots.

Used for: a village laid out on strip lots from a road

phyllostachys-enwiki

en.wikipedia Phyllostachys bambusoides (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phyllostachys_bambusoides)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Phyllostachys bambusoides, the Japanese timber bamboo known as madake, covering the plant itself and the things traditionally made from its culms and sheaths.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the traditional uses of the great timber bamboo - baskets, fans, food wrappings and clog coverings, the shakuhachi - which is what makes a bamboo grove on a map a working part of a farm rather than scenery. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary whose uses section rests on a single citation, with the encyclopedia's own request for citations on it, so the uses are taken as common knowledge rather than as referenced findings; that the uses are undated, and the article notes that the modern clog trade has moved to another species; and that it says nothing about how big a grove was.

Used for: madake's uses - baskets, fans, sheaths for food and geta, shakuhachi

pmc5723622-bamboo-range

The moso/madake range study, PMC 5723622 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5723622/)

What it is: A 2017 peer-reviewed paper in Ecology and Evolution by Kohei Takenaka Takano and seven co-authors, "Detecting latitudinal and altitudinal expansion of invasive bamboo Phyllostachys edulis and Phyllostachys bambusoides (Poaceae) in Japan to project potential habitats under 1.5C-4.0C global warming". The team surveyed bamboo stands near 145 weather stations across northern and eastern Japan, compared aerial photographs spanning three to four decades, and modeled where the two species could grow under warmer climates.

Why it applies, and its limits: Moso and madake are the two bamboos a Japanese village actually grew, and this is a measured field survey of where in Japan they can and cannot stand, with a stated cold limit, so it tells us whether a bamboo grove belongs beside a given settlement. The limits are that it is a modern survey of a modern and still-spreading distribution, and that its central subject is future warming, so the ranges it projects are not historical ranges and only its present-day survey figures and cold thresholds are safe to use.

Used for: bamboo's distribution and cold tolerance (around -18 to -20 C)

bamboo-growers-hardiness

Grower pages on madake hardiness - completebamboo.com and two others (https://completebamboo.com/species_bamboo/Phyllostachys_bambusoides.html)

What it is: Species pages on the cold tolerance of madake, the timber bamboo: completebamboo.com, an unsigned reference site on bamboo carrying affiliate advertising, and two further pages the record names only as such. They give the temperature at which the species is damaged - about -15 C, or a USDA hardiness zone of 7 - with no method or source stated.

Why it applies, and its limits: A plant's cold tolerance is a constant of the species, so a present-day statement of the temperature at which madake dies bears on a premodern grove in a way a present-day yield would not, and that is the only thing we take from these pages: the northern limit of a bamboo grove on our maps, kept as a spread because the pages disagree. Their limits are that none states a method or a source; that the hardiness zone is a North American classification for ornamental planting, not a statement about managed Japanese stands; and that two of the three pages behind the key are unnamed and cannot be read, so the key rests on one reference site.

Used for: "-15 C, zone 7" and -18 to -23 C - a spread, not a number (one reference site named; the two other pages are unnamed)

tsuijimatsu

tsuijimatsu.com (https://tsuijimatsu.com/)

What it is: The preservation website for the tsuijimatsu, the tall clipped pine windbreak hedges of the Izumo Plain, run by the city of Izumo in Shimane Prefecture through its architecture and housing division. Besides documenting the hedges and the dispersed-settlement landscape they belong to, the site runs pruning workshops and tree-health programs, and carries a page comparing Izumo's hedges with the homestead groves of other regions.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the species of a homestead grove and what each was cut for - the cedar core, the ash, elm, oak, bamboo, persimmon and chestnut around it, leaves and dead wood for fuel, bamboo for the household's gear - which is what our grove is planted with. Its limits are that it is a conservation council's site rather than a survey; that it describes living groves kept under a modern conservation program; and that the passage we quote is on its regional-comparison page and describes another prefecture's groves - the kainyo of Tonami in Toyama - so it is one municipal body's secondhand description of another region, which happens to agree with the Toyama sources cited beside it.

Used for: bamboo in the homestead setting

packer-2017-phragmites

Packer, Meyerson, Skálová, Pyšek and Kueffer, "Biological Flora of the British Isles: Phragmites australis", Journal of Ecology 105 (2017) 1123-1162 (https://besjournals.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/1365-2745.12797; DOI 10.1111/1365-2745.12797) - read, and again from the GM's downloaded copy - the Wiley page shows the full text to a reader with no subscription

What it is: A peer-reviewed review article by Jasmin G. Packer, Laura A. Meyerson, Hana Skálová, Petr Pyšek and Christoph Kueffer in the Journal of Ecology (2017), part of the long running "Biological Flora of the British Isles" series, which gathers what is known about the biology, ecology and habitats of the common reed, Phragmites australis.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use its account of the hydrosere, the succession of vegetation running from open water through the littoral zone to reed, marsh and alluvial forest, to decide which bands of plants a map paints around still water and in what order. The limit is place: it is a flora of the British Isles, so while the reed itself is the same plant that grows across Eurasia, the communities it names are European, and what we take from it is the depth-driven ordering of the zones rather than a species list for an East Asian pond.

Used for: the hydrosere as the paper states it - open water, littoral, reed, marsh, alluvial forest (section IV); it names no sedge or wet-meadow band and never mentions Calamagrostis

kushiro-mire-2014

Kushiro Mire alder invasion, Ecohydrology & Hydrobiology 2014 (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1642359314000706)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: reed marsh invaded by alder where it dries

otanoshike-2004

Otanoshike wetland, Ecological Research 2004 (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1111/j.1440-1703.2004.00644.x)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: grassland with Spiraea shrubs at the marsh margin

mlit-vegetation-classes

MLIT/NILIM river-environment vegetation classes (https://www.nilim.go.jp/lab/fbg/ksnkankyo/mizukokuweb/system/maegaki.files/shiryo2.pdf)

What it is: The vegetation-class reference list of the national river-environment survey run by the National Institute for Land and Infrastructure Management, part of Japan's Ministry of Land, Infrastructure, Transport and Tourism, which defines each plant community the survey records and the ground it grows on.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it to decide what to draw at a wet margin, and it names the communities and their habitats plainly: reed in the lower reaches, the tachiyanagi willow association on the sand and mud deposits of lower rivers, and the alder community on waterlogged ground. It is a modern survey's classification key, so it records what grows along Japanese rivers now, after centuries of embanking and channel work; it gives habitats and not densities or sizes; and it says nothing about how inhabitants cut or used any of these plants.

Used for: the willow (タチヤナギ) and alder (ハンノキ) communities of Japanese wet margins

hotes-wetland-diversity

Hotes, wetland ecosystem diversity, Global Environmental Research 12(1) (https://www.airies.or.jp/attach.php/6a6f75726e616c5f31322d316a706e/save/0/0/12_1-04.pdf)

What it is: A 2007 review article by the wetland ecologist Stefan Hotes, then in the conservation ecology laboratory of the University of Tokyo's Graduate School of Agricultural and Life Sciences, published in Japanese in 地球環境 (Global Environmental Research, volume 12) by the Association of International Research Initiatives for Environmental Studies. It surveys the ways wetlands are classified and what those classifications are worth for conserving and restoring them, and along the way sets out the Japanese wetland types and the plants that characterize each.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for what grows where along a wetland's edge - the large sedges, reed, cattail and alder stands of a nutrient-rich lowland mire - which is the plant zonation our marsh and pond margins are drawn from, and a scientific description of which plants take which ground holds as well for a premodern marsh as for a modern one. Its limits: it is a present-day scientific review, written about Japan's surviving wetlands and about the problem of classifying them rather than about any historical landscape or about how villagers cut and used those margins; it is in Japanese, and we quote it in our own English translation.

Used for: Japanese wetland zonation

lou-2016-floodplain-zones

Yanjing Lou, Yanwen Pan, Chuanyu Gao, Ming Jiang, Xianguo Lu and Y. Jun Xu, "Response of Plant Height, Species Richness and Aboveground Biomass to Flooding Gradient along Vegetation Zones in Floodplain Wetlands, Northeast China", PLOS ONE (2016) (https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0153972)

What it is: A 2016 research article in PLOS ONE by Lou and colleagues, "Response of Plant Height, Species Richness and Aboveground Biomass to Flooding Gradient along Vegetation Zones in Floodplain Wetlands, Northeast China". It measured three herbaceous marsh communities on the Sanjiang Plain and sorted them by how much of the year their ground stands under water: sedge marsh at the streamside, meadow marsh on the middle terraces, and wet meadow grass on the terraces that flood only occasionally.

Why it applies, and its limits: The ladder it measures - which plants hold the ground that floods every spring, which the ground that floods for part of the season, which the ground that floods rarely - is a physical response to water depth rather than a modern agricultural fact, so it is good evidence for what greens the wet margins of a floodplain on our maps. Two honest limits: it is a present-day ecological survey of one plain in northeastern China, not of Japan, and it is entirely about sedges and grasses, so it says nothing whatever about pine or any other tree and cannot be read as evidence about a tree's tolerance of soggy ground.

Used for: the flooding ladder of marsh communities - sedge emergent marsh streamside, sedge meadow marsh at intermediate flooding, reedgrass wet meadow on the terrace (the paper studies herbaceous marsh and says nothing about pine or any tree)

gymnosperm-densiflora

The Gymnosperm Database, Pinus densiflora (https://www.conifers.org/pi/Pinus_densiflora.php) with the Mt. Takao museum page

What it is: Two modern botanical descriptions of Pinus densiflora, the Japanese red pine: the species page in the Gymnosperm Database, a long-running online reference on the world's conifers, together with the species page from the Takao 599 Museum, the natural history museum at the foot of Mount Takao outside Tokyo. Both describe where the tree grows - rocky and sandy slopes, poor and dry soils, from sea level up into the hills.

Why it applies, and its limits: A species' tolerances are a fact of the species, so a modern description bounds what a premodern map may stand a pine on: red pine is a tree of dry, well-drained and disturbed ground, never a reed edge, and both pages also say what a human landscape does with it - the museum page that it is often planted for gardens and windbreaks, the database that its spread in Japan tracked the rise of agriculture. The limits are that the Gymnosperm Database is one editor's compilation rather than a peer-reviewed flora, though it cites its literature; that the habitat sentence our footnote quotes describes the species at its Russian northern edge rather than in Japan; and that neither page is a historical source, so how a village managed its pines rests elsewhere.

Used for: red pine as a tree of dry, well-drained slopes - never a reed edge

maff-drain-shape

MAFF, 排水路の形状・構造と適用条件 (技術書 20-23; https://www.maff.go.jp/j/nousin/noukan/tyotei/kizyun/pdf/04_hojou_hata_gijutsusho20-23.pdf)

What it is: A chapter of the Japanese Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries' land improvement technical standard, issued as a PDF, on the shape and structure of farm drainage channels and the conditions under which each is used. It gives the gradients such channels run at and how the interceptor, collector and outfall channels are laid out relative to the slope of the ground.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the layout of a drainage system on sloping upland fields - an interceptor channel laid nearly along the contours at a gentle gradient, and collectors falling at right angles to it at intervals of a few hundred meters - which follows from the fall of the land and is what our drained hem is drawn from. Its limits are that it is a present-day ministry design standard for upland fields created by modern land-forming earthworks; that the channel sections it standardizes are concrete drains, half-pipes and flumes, designed with tractor access in mind, so they have no premodern counterpart and only the layout and the gradient band transfer; and that its design gradients and sediment works are modern engineering.

Used for: drain layout and gradient band - the contour-parallel interceptor and the collectors at right angles (its channel SECTIONS are concrete and modern and are not used)

senjochi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 扇状地, with MLIT land-classification notes (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/扇状地)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 扇状地 (alluvial fan), on the fan of gravel a mountain stream lays down where it leaves its valley: the three zones of the fan - its apex, its middle and its toe - and the line of springs at the toe where the water that sank into the gravel above comes back to the surface, along which settlement and paddy have clustered since antiquity.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the landform logic our foothill maps follow - dry gravel above, springs and paddy at the toe - and the physics of a fan does not change with the century. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary that carries the encyclopedia's own notice that it needs sources, so the zone terms, though standard in Japanese physical geography, are not attributed to any authority on this page; and that it is landform description only, with no dated settlement in it.

Used for: the three fan zones 扇頂 / 扇央 / 扇端 and the 扇端 spring line where the wet toe begins (the terms are unattributed on the page)

kohai-shicchi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 後背湿地 and 自然堤防 (with GSI notes) (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/後背湿地)

What it is: Japanese Wikipedia's article on 後背湿地 (kohai shicchi, the backswamp) read together with its companion article on 自然堤防 (the natural levee), explaining how a river builds raised banks along its own channel and leaves poorly drained, fine-grained lowland behind them, and how that lowland was farmed.

Why it applies, and its limits: The landform does not change with the century, so a modern description of it serves a premodern map directly, and we use it for what lies behind a river's bank - wet ground, traditionally worked as paddy, extended over time by draining marsh - which decides where our fields and our houses go on a floodplain. It is a tertiary summary, so we take the description of the landform and its traditional use and no number from it.

Used for: backswamp formation behind the natural levee and its land use

kashima-kainyo-1987

The 1987 (昭和62年) survey of Kashima (鹿島), Tonami city, 46 households, as reported by Tonami City's digital archive 砺波正倉, 「The homestead groves (yashikirin) of Tonami City」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「砺波市の屋敷林」) (https://1073shoso.jp/www/sankyo/detail.jsp?id=18666): 「According to a survey in Kashima, Tonami City (46 households) in Showa 62 (1987), of the 1,542 trees in all of 10 centimeters or more in diameter, 735 were sugi, accounting for 48 percent, about half of the whole, an average of 16 per household.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「昭和６２年の砺波市鹿島（世帯数４６戸）での調査によると、直径１０センチ以上の全樹木数１，５４２本の内、スギは７３５本で全体の約半数の４８パーセントを占め、１戸当たり平均１６本であった」) - 1,542 trees of DBH >= 10 cm over 46 households (33.5 per homestead), 735 sugi (48%), 16 sugi per house. The primary 1987 report is not online; .
