(123,647 / 4,773 = 25.9 trees (>= ~3 m tall) per grove-owning household in 2020 - same order of magnitude as 33.5 trees >= 10 cm per homestead in 1987, consistent with the documented post-2000 decline (wind-throw, felling).); https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/esj/ESJ51/0/ESJ51_0_271/_article/-char/ja/ (Sugi as the dominant kainyo species in Tonami; ~18 woody species per homestead when shrubs are counted (the 1987 figure of ~6 species is for >= 10 cm trunks only).); https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/jjsrt/46/1/46_154/_pdf/-char/ja (A single typical grove (S family, 2020) also has sugi as the dominant species by basal area; confirms a body of Tonami grove surveys from ~1975 onward and names the Research Institute bulletin where the 1987-era survey data would have been published.)

What it is: A 1987 survey of the homestead groves (yashikirin, locally kainyo) of Kashima in Tonami city, Toyama prefecture, counting the trees in the grounds of 46 households - 1,542 trees of at least 10 centimeters trunk diameter, of which 735 were sugi, an average of 16 sugi and about 33 trees per homestead - as reported by Tonami city's own digital archive 砺波正倉 in its account of the city's homestead groves.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the best hard count of a homestead grove's stand the record could read - trunks, species and heights, household by household - and it is what the size of our groves and the dominance of cedar in them rest on; a later city count and two published ecological studies of Tonami groves agree with it in order of magnitude, which is why we trust the scale rather than the exact number. Its limits are that it is a 1987 measurement of a living practice, and the same page says the composition had changed markedly over the preceding thirty years, with ornamental garden species brought in to replace the cedar, so a 1987 species list is already part modern garden; that the Tonami plain is the dispersed-farmstead snow country of Hokuriku, where an isolated farmstead's grove is a wind and snow break at the developed end of the Japanese range, not a national average nor the form a nucleated village takes; and that the primary 1987 report is not online - what a reader opens is a 1996 institute publication's excerpt on the city archive.

Used for: the real scale of a homestead grove stand

cssn-sangji-yutang

中国社会科学网, 从生态美学视角研究桑基鱼塘 (https://www.cssn.cn/ztzl/jzz/rwln/wh/lnfw1/202209/t20220923_5541481.shtml)

What it is: An article by 吴建新 (Wu Jianxin) of South China Agricultural University, "从生态美学视角研究桑基鱼塘" (Studying the mulberry-dike fish pond from the perspective of ecological aesthetics), in 人文岭南, September 2022, on 中国社会科学网 (the Chinese Social Sciences Net). It describes the dike-and-pond farming of the Pearl River delta, in which low ground is dug out into fish ponds and the spoil raised into planted dikes, and gives the proportions and the crops the dikes carried.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the proportion of dike to water and for what grew on the dikes, which is what decides how a dike-and-pond block is drawn and tinted, and it is written by a scholar for an academic institute's public site rather than by a promoter of the landscape. Its limits are that the "six dike, four pond" figure is a remembered classic proportion rather than a measured average; that the piece is an essay on aesthetics and heritage rather than a field survey; that the practice is specific to Lingnan in southern China, so we treat it as one attested way of farming wet ground and not as a norm for the whole setting; and, on date, that by the article's own account the all-mulberry form only settled in the late Qing - earlier dikes carried fruit, tea and vegetables as often as mulberry - so a map set in an earlier era should draw mixed dikes rather than the fully formed six-four landscape.

Used for: the classic ratio named as "基六塘四" (dike six, pond four - note the order); early dikes planted with mulberry, tea, vegetables and fruit, fruit-dike ponds the commonest; mulberry dominant from the late Ming in Nanhai and Shunde

cssn-jiangnan-weitian

中国社会科学网, 古代江南圩田开发及其社会效应 (https://cssn.cn/lsx/lsx_zgs/202502/t20250217_5844872.shtml)

What it is: An article by 庄华峰 (Zhuang Huafeng), a professor of history at Anhui Normal University, "古代江南圩田开发及其社会效应" (The development of polder fields in premodern Jiangnan and its social effects), published in 中国社会科学报 (Chinese Social Sciences Today, the newspaper of the Chinese Academy of Social Sciences) in February 2025 and carried on its portal 中国社会科学网. It traces how the Lake Tai plain of the lower Yangtze was turned into diked polder fields from the Five Dynasties and Song onward, quoting the Song officials who described the system.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the kind of source we want for how a wet-rice polder actually worked - channels inside the dike, gates and sluices on the outside, opened in drought and shut in flood - and for the spacing of the embankment channels that gives a polder landscape its grid, and it carries the Song-era wording rather than a modern paraphrase. Its limits are that it is a specialist's newspaper article rather than the primary texts themselves, so we are reading Song descriptions at one remove with no edition or chapter given; that it synthesizes seven centuries, so a detail is not tied to a date unless the quoted text ties it; and that this is Chinese Jiangnan standing in for our setting's rice country, which leans as much on Japan.

Used for: 「Within there are watercourses and canals; without there are gates and sluices. In drought the sluices are opened, drawing in the benefit of the river's water; in flood the sluices are shut, keeping out the harm of the river's water.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「中有河渠，外有门闸。旱则开闸引江水之利，涝则闭闸拒江水之害」) - channels inside, gates on the outside, opened in drought and shut in flood; the tangpu spacing 「a transverse embankment-channel (hengtang) every five or seven li, a longitudinal channel (zongpu) every seven or ten li」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「五里七里一横塘、七里十里一纵浦」)

sdlib-shunde-jitang

顺德图书馆, 发现顺德·经济篇 - 桑基鱼塘 (https://www.sdlib.com.cn/home/article/detail/id/741.html)

What it is: An article by the journalist 李健明 (Li Jianming), published in the 珠江商报 (Zhujiang Business Daily) in July 2005 and republished by the Shunde district library in its local-history series, on the dike-pond landscape of Shunde in the Pearl River delta: the dike built from the pond's own spoil and planted, the crops it carried, and the spread of the system from the Song polder through the Ming silk boom to its Qing peak.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the making of the dike-and-pond block in plain words - dug from the pond, raised from the spoil, planted - and a chronology of the landscape's spread, which is what our delta maps draw and when. Its limits are that it is a newspaper's local history, written to celebrate a local heritage rather than as scholarship; that it quotes period gazetteer phrases without naming which gazetteer, so they cannot be checked at this remove; and that it is subtropical southern China, not Japan.

Used for: the dike built from the pond's own spoil (「The mud is taken out and heaped over the four sides to make the dike; the hollow left sunken in the middle becomes the pond.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「取泥覆四周为基，中凹下为塘」)), planted with mulberry ("基种桑"), hemp, soybeans, peanuts and melon trellises; the Song 桑园围, the Jiajing spread, the Xianfeng-Tongzhi peak

usu-windbreak

Utah State University Extension, Windbreak Benefits and Design (https://extension.usu.edu/forestry/publications/utah-forest-facts/005-windbreak-benefits-and-design)

What it is: "Windbreak Benefits and Design," a Utah Forest Facts extension bulletin published by Utah State University Extension, which explains what a planted windbreak does and how far its shelter reaches, expressed as multiples of the windbreak's own height.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use its rule of thumb - shelter extending downwind up to thirty times the height of the trees, with full protection of a large area needing a windbreak every ten to twenty heights - to decide how far apart sheltering tree belts stand and how much ground one belt can be said to protect. The limits are place and era: this is a modern American agricultural extension document about Utah, written for tractors and center-pivot country, so what we take from it is the physics of air flowing over a barrier, which does not change with century or continent, and never its planting recommendations or species.

Used for: "Windbreaks reduce wind speeds up to 30 times their height (H) downwind"

miragenews-polders

Mirage News, "China's Water Heritage: Significance of Polders" (XJTLU; https://www.miragenews.com/chinas-water-heritage-significance-of-polders-955573/)

What it is: A news item on Mirage News reporting research from Xi'an Jiaotong-Liverpool University on the history of China's polders, the embanked field islands of the lower Yangtze, quoting the researcher directly.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is our warrant for the shape of a polder landscape - by the early twentieth century the large enclosures had been subdivided into small islet-like "fish-scale" polders, and it was only after 1949 that their organic outlines were replaced by standardized rectangles for industrialized farming - which is why our maps draw irregular polder blocks instead of a grid. It is a press release republished as news rather than the study itself, so it is a summary in the researcher's own words that a reader cannot check against data; and its dates run later than our setting, so we read it as the end point of a long development rather than as a picture of any one earlier century.

Used for: "By the early 20th century, they were further compartmented into even smaller islet-like polders, dubbed as fish-scale polders or yulin wei (鱼鳞圩), for their shapes as seen on aerial photos"; after 1949 "the organic forms of polders were replaced by standardised rectangular patterns for industrialised agricultural production"

fusekoshi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 伏越 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/伏越)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 伏越 (fusekoshi), the inverted siphon - a canal carried in a closed conduit beneath a river or another watercourse instead of over it. The article describes the technique and names historical examples, among them the Shibayama fusekoshi on the Minuma-dai irrigation canal and the Ugamori fusekoshi conduit in the Ogaki ring-dike country.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one narrow thing: that carrying one channel under another was a real method in old Japanese waterways, and not a modern trick we invented for a map. It is a tertiary encyclopedia summary, so we take from it only the existence of the technique and the named historical works it points to, never a dimension, a gradient or a construction detail - and the examples it gives are large public irrigation projects, which is a bigger class of work than a village would undertake on its own.

Used for: the inverted siphon as a method "historically seen in old waterways" - 見沼代用水の柴山伏越, 大垣輪中の鵜森伏越樋

suirokyo-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 水路橋 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/水路橋)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 水路橋 ("aqueduct bridge"), on the structures that carry a watercourse over a river or a valley, with Japanese examples such as the Tsujun bridge, the Meisho irrigation works and the Suirokaku arcade in Kyoto.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it to establish the form itself - that when a canal has to cross a valley the answer is a bridge carrying the water, and what such a bridge looks like - which is a piece of furniture our irrigated landscapes need and which readers rarely expect. It is a tertiary summary and does not date its examples, so it tells us the type exists and how it is built and nothing about when any given one was; the named examples are also nineteenth-century or later works, so we draw the form and not their engineering.

Used for: the aqueduct bridge carrying water over rivers and valleys (通潤橋, 明正井路, 水路閣 - the page does not date them)

yashikirin-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 屋敷林 (居久根 redirects here) (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/屋敷林)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 屋敷林 (yashikirin, "homestead grove"), a tertiary encyclopedia entry on the belts of trees Japanese farmhouses planted around themselves against wind and snow, covering the regional names for the same thing - the northeastern igune (居久根) and the Izumo tsuijimatsu among them - along with the species used and the sides of the house they stand on.

Why it applies, and its limits: This is the backbone of how we plant a farmstead: which sides carry the grove (north and west against the wind, the east left open for the entrance and its ornamental garden), which trees make it up (sugi, matsu, hinoki and keyaki), and the part that turns a fact into a choice - that the Izumo form once enclosed the whole circumference of a house and was later cut back to a hook covering only the north and west, which is why our maps treat a full ring and a two-sided belt as two correct answers rather than one. It is a tertiary summary rather than a survey, so it gives us forms and species names and not counts or measurements, and the practice is Japanese and regional, the north-and-west rule following a wind that a warmer corner of the setting need not share.

Used for: igune on the north and west of the homestead as windbreak and snowbreak; the species named - スギ, マツ, ヒノキ, ケヤキ (three evergreen conifers and one deciduous broadleaf; the page never says "evergreen-heavy") Also (a second entry under the same key, merged from a second entry under the same key): the east side as the entrance with garden and ornamental trees against tall cedar on the west and south; and - the load-bearing part - the Izumo tsuijimatsu, which "enclosed the entire circumference of the house" before Meiji and was later reduced to a 鉤型 hook shape "covering only the north and west sides". That is what makes full-ring versus two-sided a KNOB rather than one right answer.

kuwa-jawiki

ja.wikipedia クワ (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/クワ)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on クワ, the mulberry (genus Morus): its botany, its species, and its cultivation for silkworm rearing, including the note that although a mulberry can grow into a tree of ten meters or more, most are bushes and cultivated ones are usually trained low.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us that cultivated mulberry is trained as a low shrub rather than let grow into the tree it can become, which is why a mulberry plot on our maps is waist-high bushes and not an orchard. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary; that it gives no planting density and no cultivated height in figures, so our height and spacing are guesses; and that it states the low-training habit as a present practice without dating it, so its use for a premodern plot is an assumption the page does not itself carry.

Used for: cultivated mulberry 「training as a low shrub is common」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「低木仕立てが多い」) - kept as a low shrub; the height and density figures are unsupported by any readable page (search syntheses: low-trunk mulberry under 70 cm, 1.2 m an optimal feeding height; 800-1,000 plants per mu in Jiangsu/Zhejiang, 5,000-6,000 in Guangdong)

aburana-jawiki

ja.wikipedia アブラナ and zh.wikipedia 油菜 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/アブラナ)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article アブラナ and the Chinese Wikipedia article 油菜, both on rape (oilseed rape, the yellow-flowered brassica grown for oil and as a green vegetable). The Japanese page's calendar - seedlings transplanted before winter, the crop cut in March and April - is that of nabana, the flowering-greens vegetable grown as a market crop; the Chinese page describes rape sown into the harvested paddy and plowed in before the next transplanting, a present-day Taiwanese double-crop practice.

Why it applies, and its limits: Between them they give the shape of the rice-and-rape year - rape in the paddy over winter, cut in spring before the rice goes in - which is what lets a plot on our maps carry two crops. Their limits are that they are tertiary summaries; that the calendar we quote is a modern market-garden regime for the vegetable, transplanted at spacing, not the broadcast oilseed stand our maps draw, so its dates are taken as plausible for oilseed rape rather than shown for it; and that the green-manure practice is an explicitly modern Taiwanese one, not an attested premodern rotation.

Used for: rape sown in autumn, overwintering, cut in March-April (the food crop's calendar); rape sown into the harvested paddy and plowed in before the next transplanting (a Taiwan double-crop practice) - neither page gives the rice months

wanli-fishpond-summary

The Shunde (顺德) dike-pond figures, VERIFIED on readable pages on 2026-08-28 : 40,084 mu of ponds in 1581 (万历九年) and 58,094 mu in 1642 - 广州日报 (https://gzdaily.dayoo.com/h5/html5/2023-07/05/content_871_829964.htm) and the Shunde Archives timeline; the 4.6% / 6.7% shares are those figures over the 8,700 顷 (870,000 mu) of cultivated land 吴建新 gives from 广东通志 (https://gzdaily.dayoo.com/h5/html5/2023-07/05/content_871_829964.htm) - derived, not read as such; 「By the end of the Guangxu era, the grain fields within the county made up less than one tenth of the total cultivated area, most of it having become fish ponds.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「至光绪末年，县境禾田占总耕地面积不到十分之一，大部分成为鱼塘」) (https://www.163.com/dy/article/D4OSJLAR0514D0GJ.html); the late-1980s survey - the main dyke-pond area 86,632 ha, 35% fishponds, 25% irrigated rice (https://www.i-sis.org.uk/DykePondSystem.php); the 1581 taxable fishponds of Nanhai, Shunde and Panyu, ~160,000 mu, from 珠江三角洲农业志 (https://www.gz.gov.cn/zt/lmgzssst/content/post_10195754.html). Longshan (龙山) was NOT over half pond in 1581: the archives give 8,124 of 44,947 mu = 18% in 1581 (https://www.163.com/dy/article/D4OSJLAR0514D0GJ.html); "over half" (75%, 乡之塘倍于田) is the Qianlong-Jiaqing figure; the 1581 wording traced to an uncited blog copied into zh.wikipedia 基塘农业.

What it is: Four pages of three kinds on the extent of the Shunde dike-pond zone: a 2023 Guangzhou Daily feature carrying the Ming county figures (40,084 mu of dike-pond in 1581, 58,094 in 1642); a Guangzhou municipal portal page quoting the delta's agricultural gazetteer for the 1581 taxed-pond total; a page by an aquaculture trade account on the NetEase platform retelling the Shunde archives' timeline, its own sources given as the archives, the agriculture bureau and unattributed internet material; and an article by Mae-Wan Ho on the Institute of Science in Society's site reporting a late-1980s Guangzhou Institute survey of the dike-pond area.

Why it applies, and its limits: Between them they give the scale of the sixteenth-century dike-pond landscape - how much of a county was pond and dike - which is what sets how far the pattern spreads on our delta maps. Their limits are that they are journalism, a government portal, a trade account and an advocacy site rather than scholarship, so every Ming number reaches us at one or two removes from the gazetteer or archive that holds it; that the 1980s figure is an advocacy site's report of a survey we have not read, and a modern measurement of a landscape that kept growing; and that the shares our text gives are our own arithmetic - the pond figures are on a readable page, but the county's cultivated-land total that makes the denominator is on none we can read, so the percentage rests on the registry's word and is labeled as such.

Used for: the scale of the sixteenth-century dike-pond zone - the pond figures are on a readable page, the county's cultivated-land total that makes the denominator of our percentages is not

chang-morphology-walled-capitals

Sen-dou Chang, "The Morphology of Walled Capitals", in Skinner (ed.), The City in Late Imperial China (http://web.stanford.edu/~mel1000/sen.pdf)

What it is: Sen-dou Chang's scholarly chapter "The Morphology of Walled Capitals", in the geographer G. William Skinner's edited collection The City in Late Imperial China (Stanford University Press, 1977). It is a comparative study of the physical form of Chinese walled administrative cities at every rank, county capitals included: the models their street nets follow between the gates, the drum tower at the crossing, and the cultivation, ponds and lakes that occupied surprisingly large parts of the ground inside the walls. The copy available online is a page-image scan.

Why it applies, and its limits: This is the strongest source behind the way our county seats are laid out, a thin skeleton of trunk streets with lanes elsewhere, walls sized to a city's administrative rank rather than its population, and a real share of the enclosed ground left unbuilt as siege insurance; Chang treats county capitals by name, so for the county seat it is a direct source and not a stand-in. Its limits are stated in our own text: the only proportion Chang gives is the lake at Jinan taking about a fifth of the walled area, so the street share and the share of unbuilt ground our maps use are this record's synthesis rather than his measurement; the public copy has no text layer, so every passage quoted from it is our own reading of the scan; and for the hamlet and village tiers his cities are a larger and grander class of place than what we draw.

Used for: the sparse street net of a Chinese county seat, the deliberately unbuilt intramural reserve, the civic program's composition and its place at a city's center; the chapter gives no percentage of the interior for civic buildings, and none is claimed from it, not measured

tedai-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 手代 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/手代)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 手代 (tedai), on the clerks of that name - both the shop clerks of merchant houses and the junior staff who served under a Tokugawa intendant in a rural district office - covering where they came from and what they did.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us who the clerks of an intendant's office were - mostly the sons of village and town officials, hired from peasants and townsmen versed in rural administration - which is what fills the clerks' quarters on our magistracy plans with the right kind of household; it also gives one establishment, Fukuyama domain's twelve clerks under three intendants, four apiece, with their stipends. Its limits are that it is a thin tertiary article whose only citation concerns modern commercial law, so its Edo material is uncited; that a recruitment pattern is not a measurement; and that the one staffing figure is a domain's, not a shogunal intendancy's, so if it is ever used it is used as one example.

Used for: 「Most tedai are the children of village officials and town officials.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「手代の多くは、村役人・町役人の子弟である」) - most tedai were children of village and town officials, hired from peasants and townsmen who knew the district; nothing about birth order

mingguanci-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 名宦祠 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/名宦祠)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on the mingguanci (名宦祠), the shrine in which a locality offered sacrifice to former officials of the place who had governed it well.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for what the building is and where it belongs - a state institution from the early Ming onward, standing in the Confucian temple precinct east of the Dacheng gate rather than inside the magistrate's own compound - which is how we place and label it on a town plan. It is a tertiary summary whose statements we take only where its own references support them, and it describes the Chinese institution, which our setting adapts rather than copies.

Used for: 「The mingguanci (shrine of eminent officials) serves to offer sacrifice to local officials who had achievements in government.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「名宦祠用以祭祀有政績的地方官」) - the hall venerating local officials of merit, standing at the Confucian temple (east of the Dacheng gate), Ming Hongwu onward - a state institution, not a yamen's own predecessor shrine

shoinzukuri-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 書院造 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/書院造)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 書院造 (shoin-zukuri), on the formal residential style of the warrior and priestly household that began late in the medieval period and was completed early in the Edo period: the study with its alcove and shelves, the formal rooms in a row, the veranda and the garden they open onto.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the orientation rule our compounds follow - the garden on the south of the formal rooms, beyond the veranda - which fixes the show face of every compound we draw. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary describing an ideal type rather than a surveyed house; that the article carries its editors' own notice that it lacks adequate citations, worth saying when a rule that fixes every compound's face is taken from it; and that it is silent about the north service side, so the kitchen and the servants' quarters rest on other sources.

Used for: 「On the south side of these consecutive rooms, a garden spread out beyond the irikawa (the outer aisle inside the eaves).」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「これら連続した室の南側には、入り側を介して庭が広がっていた」) - the garden on the south of the formal rooms; nothing on the north service side

economy-song-enwiki

en.wikipedia Economy of the Song dynasty (replaces the Grokipedia page of the same title) (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Song_dynasty)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Economy of the Song dynasty", a survey of Chinese agriculture, industry, trade and money in the tenth to thirteenth centuries. We use its account of the iron trade: Robert Hartwell's estimate that per capita iron output rose sixfold between 806 and 1078, and the description of iron-smelting households working with government support such as charcoal supplies and skilled craftsmen.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives us the scale and the organization of premodern heavy industry - how a smelting operation was staffed and supplied, and how much iron a large premodern state could make - which is what sizes and sites an ironworking quarter on a town map. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, and the article itself reports that Donald Wagner questions Hartwell's method, so we treat the tonnage as a contested estimate; and a dynasty-wide output figure has to be scaled down hard before it says anything about one town's forges.

Used for: "Historian Robert Hartwell estimates that per capita iron output rose sixfold between 806 and 1078, such that, by 1078 China was producing 127,000,000 kg (125,000 t) in weight of iron per year"; Bao Qingtian on iron households given "government support, such as charcoal supplies and skilled craftsmen"

loess-plateau-enwiki

en.wikipedia Loess Plateau (replaces the Grokipedia History of agriculture in China) (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loess_Plateau)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the Loess Plateau of northern China, covering the region's wind-blown soils, its erosion, its agricultural history and its climate, including the concentration of the year's rain into June, July and August.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for that one climatic fact, because a summer-concentrated rainfall is what makes storage ponds, bunds and flood channels worth drawing in a dry-looking landscape. It is a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, and it covers one region of northern China, not the whole of the country our setting draws on.

Used for: "40-60% of the precipitation concentrate in June to August" - the summer concentration; the 400-800 mm range is not on the page

kawara-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 瓦 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/瓦)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on kawara (瓦), fired roof tile, covering its manufacture, its types and the course of its spread through Japanese building from the temples of antiquity to ordinary houses.

Why it applies, and its limits: This settles a decision every map makes hundreds of times - what an ordinary townsman's roof looked like - by stating that commoners' houses were board-roofed as a general rule throughout the Edo period and that tile only became widespread at its very end, which is why our roofs are boards and thatch and our tile is reserved for temples, gates and the wealthy. It is a tertiary summary, so we take the direction of the trend rather than any proportion from it, and "the end of the Edo period" is later than the eras our setting draws on, which makes this a reason to draw less tile rather than a license to draw some.

Used for: 「Even once the Edo period had come, board roofing was the general rule for the houses of ordinary commoners, and it was at the end of the Edo period that tile roofing spread.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「江戸時代になっても一般庶民の家屋は板葺きが一般的で瓦葺きが普及したのは江戸時代末期のこと」) - commoners' houses board-roofed; tile widespread only at the end of Edo

kashi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 河岸 and 雁木 (港湾) (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/河岸)

What it is: Two Japanese Wikipedia articles: 河岸 (kashi), on the river landing that grew into a warehouse and merchant district from the medieval period to its Edo peak, and 雁木 (gangi), a short stub on the stair-shaped landing structure that lets people and cargo go up and down as the water level changes, in whatever material it was built.

Why it applies, and its limits: They give the form of a working river landing - the stepped bank where cargo is worked, the warehouses and merchants behind it - which is what our river towns' waterfronts are drawn from. Their limits are that both are tertiary and below the usual encyclopedia bar - one carries a notice that its sources are insufficient, the other is a stub - so neither has references of its own to carry a claim; and that the form is taken from them and no date or dimension.

Used for: the kashi as a river port and landing that grew into a warehouse and merchant district; gangi as the stairs at a landing used to load and unload cargo

chaoguan-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 鈔關 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/鈔關)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on the chaoguan (鈔關), the Ming and Qing tax barriers that levied transit duty on goods, carts and boats. It records where they were placed, at points where goods gathered and at strategic junctions of the water and land routes, and that by the Wanli era, late in the Ming, only eight major stations remained, seven of them strung along the Grand Canal.

Why it applies, and its limits: We cite it for a negative that matters to the drawing: Chinese customs practice put the barrier on the trade route, not at an ordinary town's gates, so our gate-throat inspection post cannot claim Chinese precedent and rests instead on the Japanese sekisho. That is an argument from the article's silence - a page about barriers on canals says nothing about county-seat gates, and a reader who follows the link will find no sentence ruling them out - so it is labeled as our reading. It is a tertiary summary of a fiscal institution rather than a study of gate architecture, and it is used to rule a layout out rather than to justify one.

Used for: 「A tax barrier (shuiguan) was a checkpoint established by the government, during China's Ming and Qing dynasties, at places where goods gathered and dispersed and at strategic points on water and land routes.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「税关是中國明清兩代，政府在货物集散地和水陆要冲设立的关卡」) - Ming-Qing customs barriers at commodity centers and strategic water and land routes (the eight great chaoguan on the Grand Canal), not at a county seat's gates

takayama-coverage-summary

Takayama Jin'ya site and building areas (https://www.city.takayama.lg.jp/kurashi/1000021/1000119/1000847/1000954/1000956.html) - unsupported by any readable page: a search synthesis gives 「The site area is 3,000 tsubo, the building area 1,000 tsubo.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「敷地面積は3,000坪、建物面積は1,000坪」) (~33% coverage); the 御蔵 「a grain storehouse (dozo) among the foremost in the country in both age and scale」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「年代、規模共に全国有数の穀物土蔵」)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the jin'ya's built coverage and the granary's standing, both unread

fukagawa-kiba-summary

Fukagawa kiba, the Edo lumber yards - unsupported by any readable page: fifteen timber wholesalers bought ~300,000 m2 in 1701; the yards were ordered out of the city after the 1657 Meireki fire because in-town timber yards spread fire (https://www.mokuzai-tonya.jp/03about_us/history/index.html)

What it is: The history page of a Japanese timber wholesalers' trade body, recounting the origin of Kiba, the lumber quarter of Fukagawa in Edo: after the great fire of 1641 the spread of the flames was blamed on the high timber stacks of merchants scattered through the city, the merchants were gathered onto Eitai-jima, another great fire followed in 1657, and Kiba Fukagawa-cho was laid out in 1703 over some 90,000 tsubo of made ground.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives us the thing we want to draw - a lumber yard as a large works on the water, deliberately pushed out of the built-up core because stacked timber carries fire through a wooden town - and it is a Japanese account of a Japanese quarter. The limits are that it is an industry body's own promotional history rather than a scholarly one, with no references a reader can follow, and that it gives an area for the whole quarter and none for a single yard, so the size of the yard on our maps is our own estimate.

Used for: the lumber yard as a waterfront works far larger than a shophouse, sited out of the built core

nanjing-wall-enwiki

en.wikipedia City Wall of Nanjing (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Wall_of_Nanjing)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the City Wall of Nanjing, the Ming circuit built around the first Ming capital, describing how it was laid out in a winding, free form that followed the city's broken topography, used the river as its natural moat, and so departed from the square or rectangular walls of Beijing and Xi'an.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives one clear case of a capital wall that follows the ground rather than the square - bending around lakes, hills and the river that serves as its moat - which is the form that lets our maps bend a wall where the terrain demands. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary whose history and construction sections carry the encyclopedia's own requests for citations, a sharper discount than the usual one; that it is an imperial capital, so no dimension transfers to a provincial seat; and that it gives no total length for the circuit, so the 35 km figure often quoted for this wall is on no page we could read and our text says so.

Used for: the Ming circuit built "in a winding, free style, based on the city's complicated topography" with the river as its moat; the 35.267 km length is unsupported by any readable page (aggregator sites)

shanghai-xiancheng-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 上海县城 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E4%B8%8A%E6%B5%B7%E8%80%81%E5%9F%8E%E5%8E%A2)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on 上海县城, the old walled county town of Shanghai, describing among other things the wall first thrown up around the town in 1553 against pirates coming up the river, and its roughly circular rather than square outline.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for two things about a walled town: that a Chinese county seat could go unwalled for a long time and then be walled in a hurry under a specific threat, and that such a wall could take a round outline rather than the textbook square. It is a tertiary summary taken where its references support it, about one town in the Yangtze delta, and while the record likes the idea of a wall raised very fast, the article does not put a season on it, so the speed we imply is ours rather than the source's.

Used for: 「In 1553, in order to fend off the wokou (Japanese pirates) coming up the river, a city wall encircling the town was built for the first time.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「1553年为抵御溯江而上的倭寇第一次修建了环城的城墙」) and its 圆形轮廓; "one season" not stated

hyojosho-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 評定所 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%A9%95%E5%AE%9A%E6%89%80)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 評定所 (Hyojosho), on the Tokugawa shogunate's high council of judicature - the body made up of the town magistrate, the temple-and-shrine magistrate, the finance magistrate and one member of the shogun's senior council, which heard important matters of government and the suits that crossed several magistrates' jurisdictions.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for a single fact of placement: the Hyojosho stood at Tatsunokuchi outside Edo Castle, on ground that is now Marunouchi, and not within the castle walls - which is why a capital on our maps puts its high court outside the lord's enceinte. Its limits: it is a tertiary summary, taken only where it states a plain checkable fact of location and composition; it describes one specific Tokugawa institution in one city, which our fictional capitals resemble rather than copy; and it is in Japanese, quoted here in our own translation.

Used for: the Hyōjōsho standing 「Tatsunokuchi, outside Edo Castle」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「江戸城外の辰ノ口」) - OUTSIDE Edo castle at Tatsunokuchi (present Marunouchi 1-chome)

tamagawa-josui-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 玉川上水 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E7%8E%89%E5%B7%9D%E4%B8%8A%E6%B0%B4)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 玉川上水 (Tamagawa Josui), on the aqueduct built in the 1650s to carry drinking water from the Tama River across the Musashino plateau into Edo - its route and the ridge lines it follows, its total length and fall, the water-watch station at the city edge, and how the water was distributed once inside.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our capital-tier maps have to show how a very large premodern city was watered, and this gives the whole shape of the answer: an open cut of some forty-three kilometers dropping about ninety meters, run deliberately along the high ground so gravity does the work, then a control point at the city boundary and buried wooden and stone pipes beyond it. It is a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, and it describes one exceptional work built for the shogun's own capital, so it marks the top of what a premodern state could manage rather than ordinary practice; the article also gives no total length for the buried pipe network, so we do not state one.

Used for: 「a total length of 42.74 kilometers across an elevation difference of 92.3 meters」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「高低差92.3メートルの間に全長42.74キロメートル」); distribution into the city from the 水番所 at Yotsuya Ōkido; wooden and stone pipes underground below it (no total km given)

suidobashi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 水道橋 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%B0%B4%E9%81%93%E6%A9%8B)

Used for: the bridge renamed for the Kanda josui's 御茶ノ水懸樋 crossing after the Meireki fire

edo-36-mitsuke-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 江戸城三十六見附 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%B1%9F%E6%88%B8%E5%9F%8E%E4%B8%89%E5%8D%81%E5%85%AD%E8%A6%8B%E9%99%84)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 江戸城三十六見附 (the thirty-six mitsuke of Edo castle), listing the guarded gate posts around the castle, many of them built as a single unit with a bridge thrown across the moat. The article notes that the figure of thirty-six is a popular saying and that other counts, such as sixty-six or ninety, are argued.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for how a great castle's approaches were watched - a guardhouse paired with a bridge at each crossing of the moat - and for the conventional round number a place of that size was said to have. Its limits are that an encyclopedia is a tertiary summary, and that the article's own hedge is the point: thirty-six is a traditional figure rather than a count of gates, so we treat it as a convention and not as a total to reproduce.

Used for: 「It is popularly said that Edo Castle had 36 mitsuke (gate guardposts),」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「俗に江戸城には36の見付があったといわれ」) - and the page's own hedge that the real count was higher (「there are various theories, such as 66 or 90」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「66、90など諸説あり」))

karamete-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 搦手 and 大手門 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%90%A6%E6%89%8B%E9%96%80)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia articles on the karamete (搦手) and the ote-mon (大手門), the terms for a Japanese castle's rear approach and its main front gate.

Why it applies, and its limits: A castle in our maps needs a back way out as well as a ceremonial front, and these articles establish the plain vocabulary and the plain geometry - the karamete-mon stands opposite the ote-mon. That is all they establish: they are tertiary summaries of a generic form, they name no particular castle, and they say nothing about the divination or orientation reasoning that might have decided which side was which, so any such reasoning on our maps is ours and not theirs.

Used for: the karamete-mon as the rear gate opposite the ōte-mon (generic; Edo not named; no divination reasoning)

xian-moat-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 西安城墙 (moat section) (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%A5%BF%E5%AE%89%E5%9F%8E%E5%A2%99)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article 西安城墙 (the city wall of Xi'an), in the section on its moat - a tertiary encyclopedia entry on the Ming dynasty wall that still rings the old city, and on the water ditch outside it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the dimensions of a great city's moat - 20 m wide, 10 m deep, water all year - which is the top of the range our moats are measured against. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary; that this is a former imperial capital, the top of the scale and not a provincial town's ditch; and that the figures describe the moat as it stands today, dredged and landscaped in the modern era - the article dates the wall's construction and rebuilds but gives no period source for the moat's size, so the number is a modern measurement of a much-repaired work rather than a Ming dimension.

Used for: 「On the outer side of the city wall there is a moat, 20 meters wide and 10 meters deep, holding water all year round, which together with the wall forms a complete defensive system.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「城墙的外侧有护城河，宽20米，深10米，终年积水，和城墙共同构成完整的防御体系」)

osaka-castle-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 大坂城 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%A4%A7%E5%9D%82%E5%9F%8E)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on Osaka Castle (大坂城), a tertiary encyclopedia summary of the castle's site, its successive constructions and its history, including the plateau it stands on and the rivers running below it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives one geographic statement our river-and-castle maps use - the Yodo and its tributaries below the northern plateau serving as natural moat and as the water drawn into the castle's moats. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, and the sentence we quote carries no reference in the article, so we take it as well-known geography and nothing more; and that Osaka is the largest castle in Japan, exceptional even for a capital-tier map, so the arrangement transfers and the scale does not.

Used for: the Yodo and its tributaries below the northern plateau 「It served the function of a natural moat and was at the same time used to draw water into the moats inside the castle.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「天然の堀の機能を果たすとともに、城内の堀へと水を引き込むのに利用された」)

suzaku-oji-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 朱雀大路 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%9C%B1%E9%9B%80%E5%A4%A7%E8%B7%AF)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 朱雀大路 (Suzaku Ōji), on the great central avenue that ran from the palace gate to the southern gate of Heian-kyō and of the earlier Japanese capitals, with the corresponding avenue of Chang'an in Tang China for comparison.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the width of the ceremonial avenue of an imperial capital - 82 m for Heian-kyo, 147 m for Tang Chang'an - which is the top of the scale our widest streets are measured against. Its limits are that the Heian figure is a width prescribed in a legal code, not a measured one, and the page's own archaeological citation gives a different estimate for a different boundary, about two meters apart, so the number is a convention rather than a confirmed measurement; that the page's principal reference is a popular book rather than an excavation report, and the Chang'an figure reaches us through a Japanese tertiary page rather than a Chinese archaeological source; and that this is the grandest street of an imperial capital, which no town of ours approaches.

Used for: Heian-kyō's avenue 「width 82 meters (Engishiki)」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「幅82メートル（延喜式）」); Chang'an's 「width 147 meters」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「幅147メートル」)

tokaido-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 東海道 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%9D%B1%E6%B5%B7%E9%81%93)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 東海道 (the Tokaido), on the highway between Edo and Kyoto - its post stations, its administration under the shogunate, and the orders that shaped its surface, its width and its markers.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the 1604 order that made the highway five ken wide, about nine meters, which is the upper bound our trunk roads are measured against. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, and the paragraph we use carries no citation on the page; that the Tokaido was the busiest highway in the country, so its width is a ceiling; and that a decree records what was ordered rather than what was built - the same paragraph reports that the post stations' mandated porters and horses never reached the ordered numbers.

Used for: 「In the second month of Keicho 9, Okubo Nagayasu and others were ordered to make the width of the highway 5 ken,」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「慶長9年2月に大久保長安その他に命じて街道の幅員を5間とし」) - 5 ken (~9 m, ~29.5 ft) in 1604

changan-enwiki

en.wikipedia Chang'an (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang%27an)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Chang'an, the Han and Tang capital at modern Xi'an, which treats the two cities - built on different ground, centuries apart - as one subject. It describes the Tang city's walled residential wards and its two controlled markets opened and closed by drum and gong, the deliberately irregular rectangle of the Han city's outline, and the fact that Japan's ancient capitals at Nara and Kyoto were laid out in imitation of the Tang plan at a smaller scale.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives us both the walled ward as a real urban form and the historical bridge that lets a Chinese plan sit under a Japanese-flavored setting, since the Japanese capitals were consciously copied from this one. It is a tertiary summary, and it describes a planned imperial capital of extraordinary size, so it anchors form and not scale - never a size or a count for the places we draw. Two cautions: the irregular outline it describes is the Han city's, so it says nothing about whether a Tang or later wall was square; and the number of Tang wards is a figure the page gives only as "about 110", which is what our text follows.

Used for: walled wards and the market drum; the Tang ward count is unsupported by any readable page (109-110 usually; "108" the popular figure)

kuramae-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 蔵前 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%94%B5%E5%89%8D)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 蔵前 (Kuramae), the Edo district named for the Asakusa Okura, the shogunate's great rice storehouse ground on the Sumida River, in what is now Taito ward in Tokyo. It describes the eight numbered channels cut into the right bank of the river and the rows of rice storehouses that faced them.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the form of an official granary quarter - eight numbered boat channels cut into the river bank with rows of rice storehouses facing them - which is what our granary quays are drawn from. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary with sparse citations, and the channel sentence itself carries none; and that this was the shogunal capital's central granary, some 36,000 tsubo with 67 storehouses, an exceptional scale from which only the form transfers to a provincial seat.

Used for: 「At the Asakusa Okura, eight channels, counted from upstream as number one, number two, and so on, were cut on the right bank of the Sumida River,」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「浅草御蔵は、隅田川の右岸に上流から一番、二番と数える8本の堀を作り」) - eight numbered channels into the storehouse ground

horikawa-nagoya-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 堀川 (名古屋市) (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%A0%80%E5%B7%9D_(%E5%90%8D%E5%8F%A4%E5%B1%8B%E5%B8%82))

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the 堀川 (Horikawa) of Nagoya, a canal running from Ise Bay to the neighborhood of Nagoya Castle. It records the canal's traditional origin: cut at the founding of Nagoya in the early Edo period, commonly said to have been dug in 1610 under Fukushima Masanori, to carry building materials up to the castle then under construction.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is our type case for the transport canal a city digs for itself - the reason a straight channel runs from a harbor to a construction site and then keeps carrying freight long after the works are done. It is a tertiary summary, the attribution of the digging is one the article itself reports as what "is often said" rather than as established, and the Horikawa today is a modern urban waterway, so we take the purpose and the moment of cutting rather than any measurement of it.

Used for: dug in 1610 under Fukushima Masanori for the castle's construction materials; the 舟入堀 count of nine in Edo is unsupported by any readable page (ja.wikipedia 舟入 is a disambiguation page)

sento-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 銭湯 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E9%8A%AD%E6%B9%AF)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the 銭湯, the public bathhouse, covering the institution and how many of them a city carried, including more than 600 in Edo during the Bunka era of 1804 to 1818, when its population stood at roughly one to 1.2 million.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the one number the record calls its cleanest case for scaling a service by population - more than six hundred bathhouses in Edo in the early nineteenth century - which is what sets how many bathhouses a city of a given size carries. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, and the sentence we take carries no citation in the article and is written as reported tradition ("it is said there were") rather than as a sourced count; and that Edo was the largest city in Japan at its height, so the step down to a provincial city is ours.

Used for: 「In the Bunka era (1804-1818), across the whole of Edo, whose population was about 1,000,000 to 1,200,000, there were more than 600 bathhouses (sento).」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「文化年間（1804〜1818年）には、人口約100万〜120万人の江戸全体で600軒以上の銭湯があった」) - the record's "523 in 1810" is a search-summary figure

shichiya-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 質屋 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%B3%AA%E5%B1%8B) - read - no Edo count on the page; the "2,700+" is unsupported by any readable page, also in town-deep-audit.md's darumapedia pointer

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: pawnbroking as the commoner's bank

edo-sanza-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 江戸三座 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%B1%9F%E6%88%B8%E4%B8%89%E5%BA%A7)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 江戸三座 (the three theaters of Edo), on the playhouses licensed to stage kabuki by the Edo town magistrate's office from the middle of the period onward. It records that the city once had a great many playhouses, which were consolidated to four and finally to three.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives us the institution we need for a town map - that a theater is a licensed establishment, that the magistracy decided how many there were, and that the number ended up very small, capped because a big timber hall in a townhouse block was a fire the city could not fight - which is why a settlement gets one playhouse or none rather than a row of them. Its limits are that an encyclopedia is a tertiary summary, and this article carries the encyclopedia's own warning that its sources are unclear; that the licensing was an Edo city policy under one government, so we take the arrangement rather than the number itself; and that the count is dated - four houses by the 1670s, three only from 1714 - so "three" is not true of the whole period.

Used for: the three playhouses licensed by the town magistracy after consolidation from many, to four, to three

kasoba-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 火葬場 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E7%81%AB%E8%91%AC%E5%A0%B4)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the kasoba (火葬場), the crematory, covering the history of cremation grounds in Japan and their relations with the neighborhoods around them, including the Edo-period record for Asakusa and Shitaya.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the two things our maps take about cremation: that temples burned the dead in their own precincts, and that the shogunate moved Edo's precinct crematoria together to the city's edge in 1667 after the smoke reached a shogunal grave visit. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary whose substance elsewhere is modern facilities and regulation; and that the episode is the shogunal capital's - twenty-odd temple crematoria in two wards, moved as a group by government order, in a city of a million - so a provincial town's one or two burning grounds is an inference from it rather than a reading of it.

Used for: by ~1650 the precinct crematoria of some twenty-odd temples in Asakusa and Shitaya; ~20 temple crematoria moved to the Kozukappara area in 1667 after smoke troubled a shogunal visit; the 1873 proposal to ban cremation over smoke and odor (rescinded ~1875)

bernstein-fire-and-earth

Andrew Bernstein, "Fire and Earth: The Forging of Modern Cremation in Meiji Japan", JJRS 27/3-4 (2000) - unseen-japan.com/meiji-japan-cremation-ban-attempt/ (2026-08-28): "In May 1873, they suggested relocating crematoriums, notably the Senju temple ones, outside the city's 'red line' (shubiki, 朱引)"; "the severe stench injures people's health" (https://doi.org/10.18874/jjrs.27.3-4.2000.297-178)

What it is: Andrew Bernstein's article "Fire and Earth: The Forging of Modern Cremation in Meiji Japan", published in the Japanese Journal of Religious Studies 27/3-4 (2000), a scholarly account of the Meiji government's short-lived national ban on cremation and its repeal. Only the abstract is on a public page; the specific episode we cite - an 1873 proposal to move the crematoria, notably those at the Senju temples, outside the city's "red line", on the argument that the stench from burning bodies injured health - is quoted from a later account of the article rather than from Bernstein's own text.

Why it applies, and its limits: It bears directly on where a city puts the burning of its dead and why, which is the rule a capital map needs when it places a crematory ground relative to the built-up area. Two limits: Meiji Japan is later than the period our settlements are drawn from, so it shows an older arrangement coming under pressure rather than the arrangement itself; and because the passage comes from a secondary retelling and not from a page carrying Bernstein's own words, we treat the 1873 proposal as reported rather than as read in the article.

Used for: the May 1873 shubiki proposal and the stench rationale - unsupported by any readable page as to Bernstein's own text

changmen-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 阊门 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E9%98%8A%E9%97%A8)

What it is: The Chinese Wikipedia article on Changmen (阊门), the northwest gate of Suzhou. It describes the district that grew up outside the gate, which in Ming and Qing times was, by the article's account, the busiest commercial quarter in China, with streets radiating outward from the gate and shops in the thousands.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our city maps have to decide how much of a place lives outside its wall, and this is a concrete, named case of a suburb that outgrew the walled town beside it, with the street pattern that goes with it. It is a tertiary summary making an unattributed superlative claim, and Suzhou at its height was one of the wealthiest commercial cities of the empire, so it shows the extreme end of extramural growth rather than what an ordinary county seat would have outside its gates.

Used for: the Ming-Qing Changmen district 「the most flourishing commercial street district in all of China」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「全中国最繁盛的商业街区」), its radial streets outside the wall and tens of thousands of shops

nisshinkan-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 日新館 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%97%A5%E6%96%B0%E9%A4%A8)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the Nisshinkan (日新館), the domain school of Aizu, which stood immediately west of Aizu-Wakamatsu castle on a ground of about 120 ken by 60 ken and was among the foremost such schools in the country, with a recitation hall for boys up to fifteen, a swimming ground and an observatory. The article also covers the modern reconstruction, which serves as museum and as archery and martial-arts hall.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the dimensions of a domain school's ground - about 120 by 60 ken for the original Aizu school - which sets the size band a school occupies on our capital maps, and it shows archery and martial halls standing on such a ground. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary carrying the encyclopedia's own notice that it lacks sufficient sources, which matters because we take a dimension from it; that Aizu's was one of the foremost domain schools in the country, so it bounds the top of the range; that the halls it names belong to the 1987 reconstruction rather than the original; and that it states no rule pairing a lecture hall with a drill hall.

Used for: the reconstructed school's 弓道場・武道場; the lecture-hall pairing as doctrine is not stated

bodaiji-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 菩提寺 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%8F%A9%E6%8F%90%E5%AF%BA)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the bodaiji (菩提寺), the temple whose sect a household follows generation after generation and at which it holds the memorial rites for its ancestors. It also covers the related danna-dera and the Edo-period danka system that bound each household to a temple.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our small urban temples are drawn as places with named patron households rather than as public monuments, and this is the article that defines that relationship. Its named examples are at the very top of society - the Tokugawa house's temples at Kan'eiji and Zojoji, the Date house's Zuihoji - so applying the pattern to the modest households of a town carries it down several tiers, and the registration system it describes is an Edo administrative institution, where an earlier setting inherits only the patronage. It is a tertiary summary of a religious institution, definitional rather than quantitative: it fixes what a small temple is for and who pays for it, and carries nothing about how large such a temple is, where it sits on a street, or how many a town holds.

Used for: the lineage temple 「a temple to whose doctrine a household has given its allegiance generation after generation, and at which it holds memorial rites for the enlightenment of its ancestors」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「一家が代々その寺の宗旨に帰依して、先祖の菩提を弔う寺院」); 檀那寺 under the danka system

sando-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 参道 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%8F%82%E9%81%93)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the sando (参道), the approach road to a shrine or temple. It gives the term both narrowly, as the path inside the sacred bounds within the torii or the temple gate, and broadly, as the whole road running to the shrine from somewhere busy such as the line of a highway; it also notes the torii-front and gate-front towns that grew up along such approaches, and highways whose course was bent to bring them nearer a shrine.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the relation between a shrine's approach road and the highway - the road running from the busy highway to the hall, the gate-front town along it, and highways bent to bring them nearer a shrine - which is what places a shrine and its approach on our town maps. Its limits are that it is a short tertiary article carrying the encyclopedia's own notice that it lacks sources, and none of its three references is attached to the sentences we quote, so it is an unsourced definition rather than a referenced finding; that it is a general Japanese case with no date; and that it supports the relation between road and hall and no distance or width.

Used for: the approach 「a road running to a shrine or temple from a place of heavy foot traffic such as the line of a highway」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「街道筋など人通りの多いところから社寺に至る道」); highways sometimes bent to bring them nearer a shrine

azolla-enwiki

en.wikipedia Azolla (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azolla)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Azolla, a genus of small floating water ferns. Its ecology section describes the fern's use on flooded rice paddies, where its symbiotic cyanobacterium fixes nitrogen from the air and the rotting mat releases that nitrogen into the water for the rice.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it to explain why a flooded paddy might be drawn carrying a green film rather than clear water, which is a visible surface feature with a reason behind it. It is a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, and the productivity numbers it quotes come from modern agronomy, so we cite the practice and the mechanism rather than any yield.

Used for: Azolla planted on flooded paddies, "the rotting plant material ... releases nitrogen into the water for the rice plants"; the Anabaena symbiont is in the page's biology section, not in the passage read

daxiangguo-enwiki

en.wikipedia Daxiangguo Temple (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daxiangguo_Temple)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the Daxiangguo Temple, the great imperially patronized Buddhist monastery in Kaifeng, the Northern Song capital. It gives the temple's history and its size at its height, when it is said to have managed sixty-four departments with several thousand monks, and the rebuilt temple's grounds at about 540 mu (some 36 hectares).

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it as an upper anchor for how large a capital monastery could get, which is what keeps our own biggest temple from being drawn too small. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary which the encyclopedia itself flags as below its quality standards; that the sentence we quote is cited to the temple's own website, an institution's description of itself rather than a count or a study; and that this was one exceptional court-supported monastery, so it bounds the top of the range and says nothing about an ordinary temple.

Used for: the Kaifeng monastery managing "64 departments ... with several thousand of monks" - more than the record's "1,000+"

adashino-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 化野 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%8C%96%E9%87%8E)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 化野 (Adashino), on a district at the western edge of Kyoto. It records that the place was formerly used for exposure burial, that it later became known as a cremation ground alongside Toribeyama, and that Adashino in the west, Toribeno in the east and Rendaino in the north were called the city's three great burial grounds.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one thing only: where a city put its dead, which turns out to be at the edge, in named districts on the outward side of the settlement, and that is a placement rule a map can follow. It is a tertiary summary of one city's arrangement, so it tells us the shape of the custom rather than any measured distance, and Kyoto is a capital, so the pattern is applied to smaller places by analogy rather than by evidence.

Used for: 「formerly a place of wind burial」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「かつては風葬の地」), later a cremation ground with Toribeyama; one of Kyoto's three great burial grounds (西の化野・東の鳥辺野・北の蓮台野)

fukada-kotobank

コトバンク 深田 and ja.wikipedia ヨシ (https://kotobank.jp/word/%E6%B7%B1%E7%94%B0-123763)

What it is: The Kotobank entry for 深田 (fukada, "deep paddy"), a Japanese online reference that reprints entries from published dictionaries and encyclopedias; here it defines the word as a paddy whose mud is deep, a swamp paddy (沼田, numata). It is cited together with the Japanese Wikipedia article on ヨシ (yoshi, the common reed), which covers reed beds and their cutting.

Why it applies, and its limits: Japanese dictionaries are good evidence that the deep-mud wet paddy we draw was a distinct and familiar kind of field, common enough to have its own everyday word. The limits are that a dictionary defines a word rather than measures a field, so there is no depth, no extent and no date here, and that the encyclopedia and Wikipedia entries beside it are tertiary summaries; anything more than the term and its sense comes from other entries in the record.

Used for: 深田 「A paddy whose mud is deep. A marsh paddy (numata).」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「どろの深い田。沼田」) - the deep-mud paddy; reed cutting after the rice harvest as a seasonal custom; the Meiji-drainage persistence and the iriai framing not on either page

magariya-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 曲屋 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%9B%B2%E3%82%8A%E5%AE%B6)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the magariya (曲屋), the L-shaped farmhouse of northern Japan in which the stable is built onto the dwelling as a wing of the same roof.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the L-shaped farmhouse with the stable under the same roof, projecting from the dwelling and warmed by the kitchen hearth, which is what our cold-country farmhouse variant is drawn from. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, though a well-cited one; that it is a regional house type of Iwate, not the ordinary farmhouse of the country; that the stable's projection from the SOUTH face is the article's description of the Tono basin in particular, where the article also records both right- and left-handed forms, so the compass side is a local observation and not a property of the type; and that the article associates the form with the upper stratum of the village - headmen and prosperous farmers - so drawing it as the ordinary farmhouse would misstate its standing.

Used for: 「on the south side the stable, joined to the lower end of the main house, projects out,」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「南側に厩が母屋の下手に接続して突出し」) - the stable projecting on the south, joined to the house, warmed by the kitchen hearth; nothing on the well

kayabuki-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 茅葺 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%8C%85%E8%91%BA)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on kayabuki (茅葺), thatched roofing, describing the materials used, how a thatch roof is built and maintained, and the constraints the material puts on the roof's form.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our farmhouses are thatched, and this article gives the physical reason their roofs are drawn steep: thatch is pitched steeply to shed rain, and the coarser and thicker the stems the wider the gaps and so the steeper the pitch required. The limit is that it gives no number - no angle, no degrees - so the specific pitch on our roofs is our own choice within a requirement the article states only in words, and as a tertiary summary its claims are ones we take where its own references support them.

Used for: thicker thatch 「a steeper pitch is required」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「より急勾配が要求される」) - the steep pitch as a material requirement; no degree figure

weinei-he-summary

圩内河 canal forms - unsupported by any readable page: a 中国国家地理 feature seen in search: 「The channels take many kinds of form - cross-shaped, T-shaped, crescent-shaped, bow-shaped and so on ... The two ends of a channel run right up to the embankment of the polder field, and sluice gates are built to control water storage and to detain floodwater.」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「河道有十字、丁字、月状、弓状等多种形状 ... 河道两端直达圩田堤岸，并建闸控制蓄水和滞洪」) (https://www.dili360.com/cng/article/p5fb1f39c3bbad41.htm)

What it is: A November 2020 feature article by Huang Tao in 中国国家地理 (Chinese National Geography), a popular geography magazine, on the polder fields (圩田) of southern China - farmland made by throwing up a dike around low-lying ground beside a river, a lake or the sea. It describes the constant of the form (a dike at the low place, fields enclosed within, water held without) and the way a polder reads from the air as a chessboard of dikes, channels and roads.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for that plain definition of a polder and for the chessboard look of one from above, which is exactly what a general-audience geography magazine reports well. It is written for readers rather than reviewed by scholars, and it describes polders as they survive today; the more particular claim that a polder's internal channels take crescent and bow shapes and are gated at their ends is not on the page that can be read, so nothing on our maps rests on it.

Used for: the crescent and bow canal forms and the gated ends of the polder's internal channels

dikepond-erosion-summary

Dike-pond landscape 1949-2020 (Shunde) (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.aquaculture.2021.737741) - unsupported by any readable page: "size of ponds increased while that of dikes decreased between 1967 and 2016"; the 20 m -> <4 m magnitude not seen

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the direction of consolidation - ponds growing, dikes shrinking

rice-rape-calendar-summary

Yangtze rice-rape rotation calendar - unsupported by any readable page: rape sown late September to mid-October, harvested early April (southern Jiangxi) to early May (Jiujiang); rice transplanted mid-April to early May (https://www.ecoagri.ac.cn/article/doi/10.12357/cjea.20240340?viewType=HTML)

What it is: A paper in the Chinese Journal of Eco-Agriculture (中国生态农业学报) by Liu Dan, Tian Jun and Duan Licheng on how a warming climate has shifted rice planting within the rice and rapeseed rotation of Jiangxi Province, built on weather records from 85 stations and concentrating on the years 2011 to 2022. It prints the district-by-district calendar: rapeseed harvested in early April in the south of the province and early May at Jiujiang, with rice transplanted from mid-April to early May.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives dated points in a plot's year for a rice-and-rapeseed rotation - rape harvested in early April in southern Jiangxi and early May at Jiujiang, rice transplanted from mid-April to early May - which is what decides what a plot on the map is carrying in a given month. Its limits are that it is a modern calendar, and the paper's own subject is that warming has moved these dates, so an older calendar sits later than the one it prints; that one southern Chinese province stands in for our rice country; and that it carries only the spring transitions - it gives neither the rice harvest month nor the rapeseed sowing window, which the record marks as unsourced.

Used for: the spring transitions of a rice-and-rape plot - rape harvested early April (southern Jiangxi) to early May (Jiujiang), rice transplanted mid-April to early May; the rice harvest month and the rapeseed sowing window are not on the page

hancho-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 藩庁 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E8%97%A9%E5%BA%81)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 藩庁 (hancho), the administrative seat of an Edo-period domain. It explains that domains ranked as castle-holders and above kept their seat in the castle itself, while domains of the lower ranks - castle-holder-in-name and castle-less lords, who were not permitted to maintain a castle - built a mansion somewhere in their territory and made that the seat of government instead.

Why it applies, and its limits: This settles a question a map has to answer before anything else: whether a domain's government sits inside a fortress or in a walled mansion in a town, and the answer turns out to follow from the lord's rank rather than from the size of the domain. It is a tertiary summary of a legal distinction, so it tells us which building held the government and nothing about what that building looked like - no plan, no size, no arrangement of offices, all of which we take from elsewhere.

Used for: 「The han of castle-holding daimyo and above had it at the castle; the han of joshu-kaku rank and below - the joshu-kaku and the castle-less daimyo - were not permitted to maintain a castle, and so placed a mansion within their territory and made it the han's administrative seat.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「城持大名以上の藩は城に、城主格以下の城主格と無城大名の藩は城を構えることが許されなかったことから、領内に屋敷を置いて藩庁とした」) - a castle-holding han's seat was AT the castle; a castle-less han's was its jin'ya

honmaru-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 本丸 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%9C%AC%E4%B8%B8)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the 本丸 (honmaru), the innermost bailey of a Japanese castle. For the early modern period it describes what stood there: the 奥御殿 (oku-goten), the inner palace where the castle lord and his family lived, and the 表御殿 (omote-goten), the outer palace that was the seat of government, with the keep and turrets built alongside as required.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the division our castle plans are built on - the lord's residence and the domain's government office are two halves of one compound, one private and one where business is done - which is what a player is really asking about when they click on the inner bailey. It is a tertiary summary generalizing across many castles and two centuries, so it describes a common arrangement rather than any particular castle's plan, and it supplies no dimensions.

Used for: 「In the early modern period the honmaru held the oku-goten, where the castellan and his family lived, and the omote-goten, which was the government office,」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「近世における本丸は、城主とその家族が暮らす奥御殿と政庁である表御殿を置き」) - the goten's public half named as the 政庁

kakegawa-castle-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 掛川城 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%8E%9B%E5%B7%9D%E5%9F%8E)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on Kakegawa Castle (掛川城), a castle in what is now Shizuoka prefecture that headed a domain of roughly 51,000 to 59,000 koku. It describes the castle's history and the buildings of its enclosures, including the ninomaru goten, the palace in the second enclosure.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives one small-to-middling domain's castle where the everyday business of government was transacted in the second-bailey palace rather than the keep, which is what puts the working offices of our capitals in the bailey. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary; that the building named is the rebuild of 1861, a very late Edo datum standing for the whole period; and that two castles are a thin base for a rule - the other, Kawagoe, is a different article this key does not link.

Used for: 「the ninomaru goten, which was the office of government business」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「政務所である二ノ丸御殿」) at a ~51,000-59,000 koku domain - the administration office in the ninomaru goten; ja.wikipedia 川越城 (170,000 koku) likewise puts the lord's residence and a 武具方役所 in the ninomaru

karo-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 家老 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%AE%B6%E8%80%81)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the karo (家老), the senior retainers who formed the standing council of a daimyo's house and ran its administration.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our castle towns have to place the great retainer mansions and to decide where government was actually done, and this article supplies both in general terms: the karo decided matters in joint council at a hall such as the hyojosho, and their mansions stood near the castle. Its limits are precision and kind - it is a tertiary summary, "near the castle" is not a distance, and it names no ring or block of the town, so the concentric arrangement our maps draw is our reading of a general statement rather than something this article says.

Used for: the karō deciding in council (「they assemble at the hyojosho or the like and decide after joint deliberation」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「評定所などに集合して合議の上で決裁する」)) and keeping their mansions "城の近く" - near the castle; no ring named

goyobeya-kotobank

コトバンク 御用部屋 (https://kotobank.jp/word/%E5%BE%A1%E7%94%A8%E9%83%A8%E5%B1%8B-66403)

What it is: The Kotobank entry for 御用部屋, reprinted from published Japanese reference works. It defines the term as the room inside Edo Castle where the senior councillors and their deputies sat and conducted the business of government, and adds that the room where a domain's senior retainers did their work - both in the domain office at home and in the domain's Edo mansion - went by the same name.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for a single idea: that the seat of a government contained a dedicated room in which its executive council actually met, rather than the ruler simply holding court wherever they were. Its subject is the shogun's own castle, so for a provincial seat it is an analogy carried down a tier rather than direct evidence, and a dictionary definition gives no size, no position within the building and no plan - only that the room existed and what it was for.

Used for: the executive council room inside the seat of government

mineta-2007-tameike

嶺田拓也, 「Basic knowledge for ecosystem consideration (part 3): the flora of paddy fields and irrigation ponds」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「生態系配慮の基礎知識（その3）― 水田とため池の植物相 ―」), 農業農村工学会誌 (Journal of the JSIDRE) 75(8), 2007, pp. 745-748 (https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/jjsidre2007/75/8/75_8_745/_pdf)

What it is: An article by Mineta Takuya in the Journal of the Japanese Society of Irrigation, Drainage and Rural Engineering (Japanese: 農業農村工学会誌) 75(8), 2007, part of a series on the ecological basics of farmland, dealing with the plants of paddy fields and of irrigation ponds.
