Why it applies, and its limits: It is the one source with numbers behind our rule that a farm pond's shallow shore is reeded, giving the zoning of reed and cattail by water depth and bank slope, and it is what shows that a working reservoir does not have a bare margin: a Kagawa study it reports found more emergent and floating-leaved species where ponds are dredged and their algae cut, and it is the ponds that have fallen out of use that lose their variety, so a working reservoir does not have a bare margin. It is a professional-society review reporting recent surveys of ponds still in use rather than primary evidence of its own or evidence about any earlier century, and the correlation it reports comes from a single prefecture, so we take a pattern of where plants stand from it and not a figure about how a premodern pond looked.

Used for: the finding that CONTRADICTS "a managed reservoir has a bare margin" - a Kagawa Prefecture study found a statistically significant POSITIVE correlation between emergent- and floating-leaf plant species counts and the practice of dredging silt and cutting algae, and it is the ponds whose water use has stopped that lose diversity; and for the zonation of reed and cattail (ヨシやガマなどの大型抽水植物) by water depth and slope. The one number-bearing source behind the rule that a tameike's shallow shore is reeded.

tameike-jawiki

ウィキペディア日本語版 ため池 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E3%81%9F%E3%82%81%E6%B1%A0)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article ため池 (tameike, "retention pond"), on the irrigation reservoirs that store water for rice fields across Japan - how they are built and managed, how the embankment is kept sound, and what grows on it as a result.

Why it applies, and its limits: It describes how a reservoir's embankment is kept - cultivation forbidden, the bank mown and burned, and the sun-loving grassland herbs that result - which the record offers as a confirmation of the rule that reeds keep off a raised, tended bank. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, and the passage we use carries no citation on the page, so its weight comes from the prefectural and ministry sources cited beside it; that the practice is a maintenance regime with no date attached; and that the page never mentions reeds - it contrasts the tended bank's herbs with the tall grasses and kudzu of a neglected one - so "herbs, not reeds" is our inference from the regime and not the page's statement.

Used for: the embankment being managed by forbidding cultivation and by mowing and controlled burning, TO KEEP THE BANK STRONG, and the dry-grassland herbs (not wetland plants) that grow there as a result - the independent confirmation of the GM's rule that reeds keep off a raised planted bank. An encyclopedia article, cited for a plainly-stated maintenance practice that the prefectural and MAFF sources corroborate.

maff-tameike-shizen

農林水産省, 自然環境に果たす役割 - 2. ため池 (https://www.maff.go.jp/j/council/seisaku/nousin/seibibukai/gijutu_syoiinkai/h14-1/pdf/data3-2.pdf)

What it is: A document prepared for an advisory committee of the Japanese Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries on the part irrigation reservoir ponds play in the natural environment. Its figure shows the bands of plants that succeed one another on a gently sloping shore, from waterside woodland through wet meadow, emergent plants such as reed, cattail and wild rice, then floating-leaved plants and finally submerged ones.

Why it applies, and its limits: That banding is what we use to draw the edge of a village pond, and it is ecology rather than history, so it changes little between a modern pond and a premodern one of the same shape. Two limits: the paper is a modern government document about present-day ponds, and the zonation figure is credited to a book we were not able to read, so we cite the banding from this document at one step from its original.

Used for: the land-to-water zonation of a gently-sloping natural shore - riparian forest, wet-meadow, emergent, floating-leaf, submerged - with the emergent belt named as ヨシ・ガマ・マコモ. Note the diagram credits 桜井義雄『水辺の環境学』, which was NOT read; the zonation is cited from this document, one step from that figure.

nies-tameike

国立環境研究所, ため池の生物とその環境 (https://www.nies.go.jp/biology/data/tameike.html)

What it is: A page from Japan's National Institute for Environmental Studies, "The life of farm ponds and their environment" (ため池の生物とその環境), comparing the wildlife of irrigation ponds in different condition - about six dragonfly species in a pond swallowed by the town, walled in concrete and choked with nutrients, against twenty-nine or thirty over the year in a pond ringed by worked woodland and full of water plants.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the check on an easy mistake, that a pond a village works must be the barren one: the impoverished pond in this comparison is the urban concrete one, while the farmed pond in its living landscape is the rich one. It is a modern ecological account of present-day ponds, so we take the direction of the contrast - managed is not the same as bare - and none of its counts appear on any map.

Used for: the low-diversity end of the spectrum being the urbanized, concrete-armored, eutrophic pond (six dragonfly species) rather than the farmed one - the check that "managed" and "bare" are not the same axis.

inamino-tameike-museum

いなみ野ため池ミュージアム, 水生植物 (https://www.inamino-tameike-museum.com/pond-creature/aquatic-plants.html)

What it is: A page on aquatic plants from いなみ野ため池ミュージアム (the Inamino Tameike Museum), the public-education body that documents the dense cluster of irrigation reservoirs on the Inamino plateau in Hyogo prefecture. It describes the shallow embanked "dish ponds" of the lowland and the plants that ring them and cover their water.

Why it applies, and its limits: It corroborates the pond-margin planting on our maps at exactly the right scope - not wetlands in general but tameike, the built irrigation ponds themselves - with reed, Manchurian wild rice and lesser cattail forming colonies at the rim and floating-leaf plants over the open water, and it comes from an institution documenting a real, still-working pond cluster. Its limits: it is an outreach page written for visitors rather than a scientific survey, and it describes those ponds as they are now, including the eutrophication of the modern water; so it is a good guide to which plants belong at a pond's edge and a poor one to how much of them there would have been; it is in Japanese, quoted here in our own translation.

Used for: reed, wild rice and lesser cattail forming colonies around LOWLAND tameike specifically - a tameike-scoped corroboration of the zonation, from an institution documenting a real pond cluster in Hyogo.

ohmi-yoshi

公益財団法人淡海環境保全財団, ヨシとは (https://www.ohmi.or.jp/yoshi/about/whats/)

What it is: A public information page, ヨシとは ("What the reed is"), from the Ohmi Environmental Conservation Foundation (公益財団法人淡海環境保全財団), a public interest foundation in Shiga prefecture whose work is the environment of Lake Biwa. It describes the common reed and the long standing practice of cutting the winter-withered stems for use.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is our evidence that a reed bed is a harvested resource rather than scenery - cut in winter for yoshizu blinds, thatch, summer shoji and standing screens, and left to deteriorate when nobody cuts it - which is what a reader is told when they click the reeds on a map. Two honest limits: it is a conservation foundation writing for the public, not scholarship, and everything it describes is a lake or river reed bed on Lake Biwa, so we cite it for lake and river reeds only; nothing we could read puts that harvest economy on the margins of a farm pond, and our record says so rather than stretching this page to cover it.

Used for: the reed-harvest economy - winter cutting for yoshizu screens, thatch and summer shoji, and an uncut bed going 荒れる - and, just as importantly, for the SCOPE LIMIT recorded against it: this foundation is named for Lake Biwa and every example on the page is a lake or river reed bed. It is cited as evidence about lake and river reed beds ONLY. Nothing found puts that economy on tameike margins, and the research entry says so rather than extending it.

edago-ja

"枝郷" (edago, branch village), ja.wikipedia (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%9E%9D%E9%83%B7)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 枝郷 (edago, "branch village"), on the Edo-period relationship between an original village, the hongo, and the new settlement that split off from it. It explains that the branch's assessed yield was in most cases apportioned through the parent village's headman, and that there were nonetheless branch villages with their own headman and group elders, treated on a par with the parent.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our maps have to decide whether a small outlying hamlet has any officer of its own or is administered from the village down the road, and this is the attested form for exactly that, including the exceptions - which is what lets us mark our setting's flat rule as a deliberate departure rather than pass it off as history. Its limits are serious for a source doing that work: it is a short encyclopedia stub with no citations at all, and Japanese Wikipedia's own editors have flagged it as unverified and possibly original research, so the distribution it asserts ("in many cases ... but there were also ...") rests on nothing a reader can follow; the dictionary entry it links at Kotobank is the closer public source. It describes a particular Japanese tax and registration system, so we use it for the shape of the arrangement and never for a rate or a proportion.

Used for: the hongo/edago parent-and-branch settlement form, its commonness ("in most cases" the branch's assessed yield came from the parent's headman), AND the attested exceptions - branch hamlets with their own shoya and kumigashira - which is what makes Rokugan's flat "a hamlet has no headman" a deliberate deviation rather than a finding

ndl-hongo-edago

National Diet Library Collaborative Reference Database, entry on hongo/oyamura and edago/komura (https://crd.ndl.go.jp/reference/entry/index.php?id=1000166660&page=ref_view)

What it is: An entry in the National Diet Library's Collaborative Reference Database, where Japanese librarians publish researched answers to reference questions with the works they consulted. This one explains the parent village (hongo or oyamura) and its branch settlement (edago or komura): the cadastral register and the tax notice stood in the parent's name, the branch could pay its land tax only through the parent, and even a petition to the lord needed the parent's consent.

Why it applies, and its limits: It states the fiscal mechanism of a branch hamlet's dependence on its parent village in plain words, from the right country and the right era, which is what the record needed for the parent-and-branch relationship on our maps. Its limits are that it is a librarian's compiled answer from reference works rather than original research, a chain of dictionaries and a city history; and that the answering library and every work it cites are Amagasaki-local, so one district's reference literature stands in for the general Tokugawa rule.

Used for: the fiscal mechanism of the dependency - land registers and tax quotas in the PARENT village's name, the branch able to pay only through it - which is the reason a branch hamlet had no headman of its own standing

jstage-admin-natural-village

"The Relation between Administrative Village and Natural Village in the Edo Era", Japanese Journal of Human Geography 19(2) (https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/jjhg1948/19/2/19_2_117/_article)

What it is: Tadashi Kondo, "The Relation between Administrative Village and Natural Village in the Edo Era", a research article in the Japanese Journal of Human Geography, volume 19 issue 2, 1967. It examines how the villages the Edo tax system recognized related to the settlements that actually existed on the ground, and finds that a taxed village on a plain was usually a single settlement while one in the mountains gathered several, more of them the deeper into the hills it lay.

Why it applies, and its limits: It answers a question the record asks about terrain rather than rank - that a taxed village on a plain was usually one natural village while one in the mountains gathered several, and more the deeper into the hills. Its limits are that it is one district's pattern rather than a national rate, as the paper's own abstract says; that it is scholarship of 1967, which later Japanese historical geography may have refined; and that we have read and quoted its English abstract only, not the Japanese full text.

Used for: that multi-hamlet administrative villages were commoner in mountains than on plains - a terrain pattern, and the closest thing found to a frequency statement. Cited as evidence that the record does NOT rank settlement forms

kokudaka-en

"Kokudaka", en.wikipedia (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kokudaka)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on kokudaka, the rice-denominated assessment by which Japanese domains, fiefs and stipends were rated.

Why it applies, and its limits: This one is cited for a caution rather than a fact: the article's own account of the assessment converting other crops and produce into their rice equivalent is what stops us from reading a domain's rice rating as evidence that its land was mostly wet paddy, which would put paddy on maps that should carry dry field. It is a tertiary summary, used here only for the definition of a fiscal convention, which is the kind of thing an encyclopedia states plainly, and for no measurement at all.

Used for: what it does NOT establish. Kokudaka's rice denomination is a FISCAL convention - "the value of other crops and produce converted to their equivalent value in terms of rice" - covering dry field as well as paddy, so it is not evidence of wet-rice land-use dominance.

nanushi-en

"Nanushi", en.wikipedia (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanushi)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the nanushi (名主), the Edo-period village headman who administered a village under a district magistrate, with a number of group heads (kumigashira) and one or more peasant representatives (hyakushodai) beneath him - one nanushi to a village.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for that ratio, one headman to one village, which is the general rule our settlements follow. It is a tertiary summary, and it has a specific gap that matters here: it never says whether its "village" is the natural cluster of houses or the larger administrative unit that could contain several, which is the very question at issue, so it fixes the ratio and leaves the unit open.

Used for: one headman per (administrative) village as the general rule - and recorded as NOT resolving whether "village" there means the natural hamlet or the unit containing several, which is the question at issue

kinoshita-1995

Kinoshita, "Household Size, Household Structure, and Developmental Cycle of a Japanese Village: Eighteenth to Nineteenth Centuries", Journal of Family History 20(3), 1995 (https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/abs/10.1177/036319909502000302)

Not cited: no publicly readable page carries the passage the record relied on.

Used for: the nearest historical anchor for ~5 to a household - the abstract synopsis reads that mean household size "rose from about five to six persons" over 1760-1870. ONE village in northeastern Japan and a rising trend, not a national constant; the map's figure rests on the setting's canon instead

hayami-encyclopedia

"Hayami, Akira", encyclopedia.com (https://www.encyclopedia.com/social-sciences/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/hayami-akira)

What it is: A reference-work biography of the Japanese historical demographer Akira Hayami, written by the historical demographer Osamu Saito for the Encyclopedia of Population and republished on encyclopedia.com. It describes how Hayami brought the methods of European historical demography to the Japanese 宗門改帳 (shumon aratame cho), the annual religious-investigation registers of households and population, and summarizes what those registers showed about marriage, fertility, mortality and household size in Tokugawa Japan.

Why it applies, and its limits: Household size is the number that turns a population into a count of houses on a map, and this entry vouches for the source those numbers come from and for the direction of travel - households in the regions studied got smaller over the period. It is a tertiary summary of somebody else's scholarship rather than the scholarship itself, and it gives no absolute household figure at all, so we use it only to confirm the method and the trend and take actual numbers from elsewhere.

Used for: confirming the shumon aratame cho register methodology and a downward household-size trend in the regions studied. Recorded as giving NO absolute figure

l7r-median-domain

The GM's campaign notes, l7r.md, "The Median Domain" and "Place Names" (URL: none - the GM's own unpublished writing, at /host-l7r-repo/setting/l7r.md)

What it is: The setting's own notes, written by the game master, in the sections "The Median Domain" and "Place Names". They set out how many hamlets and villages a typical domain holds, how many inhabitants live at each tier, the size of a household, where a hamlet's headman lives, and how a village and its district come to share a name.

Why it applies, and its limits: This is canon rather than evidence: where the historical record is silent, contradictory or simply messier than a map can show, the setting's own notes decide, and every settlement count and tier on these maps follows from them. There is no historical limitation to state, because the notes are not making a historical claim.

Used for: the settlement-tier counts a median domain holds (~1,296 hamlets, ~216 villages, ~40% of inhabitants in a hamlet), five to a household, a hamlet's 50-100 for 10-20 households, that a hamlet's headman lives in the main village, and that a village and its district share a name. The setting's canon, which decides these maps where the historical record is silent or messier

wang-ochiai-2022

Wang Jingying and Ochiai Chiho, "Spatial composition and building techniques of farmhouses prone to windstorms: a case study in Arakawa Village, Shiga Prefecture, Japan", Journal of Asian Architecture and Building Engineering 21:6 (2022), DOI 10.1080/13467581.2021.1972810 (https://doi.org/10.1080/13467581.2021.1972810) - the article is Gold open access (CC BY).

What it is: Wang Jingying and Ochiai Chiho, "Spatial composition and building techniques of farmhouses prone to windstorms: a case study in Arakawa Village, Shiga Prefecture, Japan", a peer-reviewed open-access article in the Journal of Asian Architecture and Building Engineering (2022). It reconstructs the house lots of one windstorm-prone village on the west shore of Lake Biwa from a Meiji-era land register - 61 lots, 46 of them households - verifies them by field survey and interviews, and analyzes where each building type stood on its lot.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a measured study of where the buildings of a Japanese farmstead stood, which is what places the outbuildings on our homesteads, and it is the source of one number the record uses as it stands - that 72.7% of the toilets sat on the southeast-to-south side of the lot, which the authors attribute to sunlight speeding the fermentation of night soil - and of one negative, that its wind-siting finding concerns storage buildings and retirement houses and says nothing about siting a privy downwind. Its limits are that it is one village and a small count behind the percentage; that the village was chosen because it is windstorm-prone, so its wind-driven siting is a strong case rather than a typical one; that the layouts are Meiji-era, after the Restoration and so later than the setting, though from a register rather than from houses surviving today; and that the toilet finding is the authors' explanation, not a tested cause.

Used for: the 72.7% of toilets sited southeast-to-south for solar warmth to speed fermentation of night soil - the figure the GM ruled be used literally as the seat share. Also used for what it does NOT say: its wind-siting finding covers storage buildings and retirement houses rather than toilets, and it contains no leeward/downwind/odor/hygiene language, which is what contradicted the proposal to seat privies downwind.

coggins-minor-2018

Chris Coggins and Jesse Minor, "Fengshui Forests as A Socio-Natural Reservoir in the Face of Climate Change and Environmental Transformation", Asia Pacific Perspectives 15:2 (2018) (https://jayna.usfca.edu/asia-pacific-perspectives/pdfs/1-coggins-minor-fengshui-forests.pdf) - read from the PDF itself (pages 1-20) after the fetcher could not decode its text stream.

What it is: Chris Coggins and Jesse Minor, "Fengshui Forests as A Socio-Natural Reservoir in the Face of Climate Change and Environmental Transformation", a peer-reviewed article in Asia Pacific Perspectives 15:2 (2018). It is a field study of the village woodlands of southeastern China, mapping where each grove sits in relation to its village and arguing that these small protected forests carry an outsized ecological and social weight.

Why it applies, and its limits: It establishes what a village grove is: not a belt around the settlement but a set of separate patches at particular positions, behind the village on its hill, at the water head and the water mouth and in the mountain gaps, with the paddies lying open in front. It is a modern study of the groves that have survived to be surveyed, in one part of China, so it records the pattern rather than a full premodern inventory, and it stands for a practice our maps also give to Japanese-flavored villages, where the same siting logic is assumed rather than separately attested.

Used for: the finding that a Chinese village's fengshui forest is a SYSTEM OF SEPARATE PATCHES rather than a perimeter belt - back / master-mountain (zhushan), water-head, water-mouth (shuikou) and mountain-gap groves, sited "at specific topographic positions corresponding to especially energetic winds (mountain gaps) and water (cutbanks)", with the paddies in the open ground between. This is what contradicted the question as we first posed it.

honda-1915-via-miura-2019

Honda Seiroku, Hoanrin Zorinho (1915), quoted verbatim in Miura Osamu, "Invention Process of a Technical Term Yashikirin and Its Diffusion Process in Geography", Quarterly Journal of Geography 71:3 (2019), pp. 120-127 (https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/tga/71/3/71_120/_pdf)

Used for: the FOUNDING technical definition of yashikirin, which restricts the belt to two named sides - 屋敷林ハ...農家ノ周圍特ニ西ト北トノ両側ニ設ケラルヽモノ, "established mainly on the two sides, specifically west and north" of an isolated farmhouse. The source of the rule that an absent flank is not a gap. Cited through Miura because Honda's 1915 text was reached in his verbatim quotation of it, not separately.

igune-jawiki

ウィキペディア日本語版 居久根 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%B1%85%E4%B9%85%E6%A0%B9)

Used for: 多くの居久根は屋敷の北側と西側に存在し - most igune stand on the north and west sides of the farmstead - corroborating Honda's two-sided definition in the dispersed-settlement (Tohoku) case.

purdue-ncr191

Purdue Extension, "Wind and Snow Control Around the Farm" (NCR-191) (https://www.extension.purdue.edu/extmedia/ncr/ncr-191.html)

Used for: the engineering half of the rule, offered as modern evidence about what a FUNCTIONING belt looks like rather than as history: "when high-velocity air passes through a constriction, its velocity increases", and an access opening through a windbreak is built "the same height and porosity as the rest of the windbreak fence" rather than left bare. It gives no width, which is why our 30 ft threshold is labeled a rendering convention.

gd-fengshui-types

Guangdong Provincial Forestry Administration, on the four types of village fengshui forest (水口林 / 龙座林 / 垫脚林 / 宅基林) (URL: none - the page at lyj.gd.gov.cn/news/special/forum/content/post_1876507.html 404s and no live address for it was found; the text below is a search summary of it) - unsupported by any readable page: the page at lyj.gd.gov.cn is gone and only a search summary of it could be read; kept because it corroborates coggins-minor-2018 independently and in categorical forestry-bureau language; nothing rests on it alone.

nabunken-azemame

Nara National Research Institute for Cultural Properties, Asuka Historical Museum, "畦豆" (2023-06) (https://www.nabunken.go.jp/asuka/info/2023/06/post-444.html)

What it is: A short explanatory article of June 2023 from the Asuka Historical Museum of the Nara National Research Institute for Cultural Properties on azemame (畦豆), the soybeans sown along the bund of a paddy field.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives, from a national research institute's museum, the practice of sowing beans along the paddy bund after transplanting and harvesting them with the rice - once done across Japan, now rare - which is what puts bund beans on our paddies. Its limits are that it is a short piece for a general audience with no proportion of bunds sown and no household count; that its "once across Japan" carries no date, so it fixes the practice in no particular century; and that the post itself says these bund plants are seen especially in the terraced hill country, where the bund is a larger share of the farmland than on the flat - so our flat-valley paddies are the case the source calls the less bund-intensive one.

Used for: azemame - soybeans sown on the paddy bund after transplanting and harvested with the rice, grown in a row along the bund; once nationwide, now rare

wikipedia-soybean

Wikipedia, "Soybean", Description (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Soybean", and in particular its Description section - a tertiary encyclopedia entry on the plant, its habit and its cultivation.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the two facts a bean plot on a map turns on - that the plant is an annual, replanted every year, and how tall it stands - and the first is a constant of the species. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary; and that the height it gives, 50 to 125 cm, describes the crop as bred and grown now - the article gives a separate modern-cultivar figure and says nothing about landraces - so the drawn height is a modern-crop range used because no premodern measurement of the plant could be read.

Used for: a mature soybean plant is 50-125 cm tall; an annual

cropfarming-soybeans

cropfarming.org, "What do soybeans look like growing in the field?" (https://cropfarming.org/what-do-soybeans-look-like-growing-in-the-field/)

What it is: A growers' advice page on cropfarming.org, an unsigned farming trade site, headed "What do soybeans look like growing in the field?" It describes the soybean plant as a field crop looks: an erect, bushy plant on a central stem, two to four feet tall, with broad, pointed, medium green leaflets.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it only for the shape and the color of the plant, so that a bean plot on a map is drawn as something a reader could tell from rice, and a plant's habit and leaf color are not much changed by the century. Its limits are that it is a commercial advice page with no named author, no references and an affiliate-link business model, so nothing on it can be traced to a study; that it describes modern varieties in the American Great Plains and Corn Belt, bred shorter and more even than the landraces an East Asian farmer would have sown, so we treat the height band as an approximation rather than a measurement of our setting's crop;

Used for: the plant is erect and bushy on a central stem, 2-4 ft tall; the leaflets are broad, pointed and medium green - the color the bead is drawn darker than

saijo-mizu-rekishikan

Saijo City (Ehime) Water History Museum, on digging a well (https://www.city.saijo.ehime.jp/site/mizunorekishikan/lineup3-8-3.html)

What it is: A page from the Water History Museum run by the city of Saijo in Ehime Prefecture, part of a series on local groundwater, explaining how a well is dug by hand: a vertical shaft about a meter across, wide enough for a digger to get down inside it, sunk by human labor until it reaches the aquifer.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the true diameter of the thing our oversized wellhead glyph stands for, from a municipal museum describing the well-digging of its own famously watery district, and the figure is not really local - a shaft has to be about that wide for a digger to work at the bottom of it. The limits are that a museum's public-education page is a summary written for visitors rather than a survey, and that it describes the technique in the present tense, so it is the dimensions of hand-digging we take from it and not a dated historical measurement; the English here is this project's translation of the Japanese.

Used for: a hand-dug well's shaft is about 1 m in diameter, large enough for a person to enter - the true size the oversized wellhead glyph stands for

kotobank-idoyakata

Digital Daijisen and Nihon Kokugo Daijiten, "井戸屋形", via kotobank (https://kotobank.jp/word/井戸屋形-435033)

What it is: The Digital Daijisen and Nihon Kokugo Daijiten entries for 井戸屋形 (idoyakata, the well house), gathered on kotobank, each defining it as a simple structure of posts set beside a well with a roof on top of them and nothing more.

Why it applies, and its limits: Our well glyph puts a small roof over the well head, and these entries confirm that the roofed well of Japanese practice really was that plain - posts and a roof, not a shed. A dictionary gives the form of a thing and not how common it was, so how many of a settlement's wells are drawn roofed is not decided by this source.

Used for: the well house is posts beside the well with a roof on them and nothing more - the form the glyph's roof square depicts; its prevalence is not stated

sendai-igune-list

Sendai City, 居久根 conservation page, which links the city's PDF list of trees suited to an igune (https://www.city.sendai.jp/ryokuchihozen/kurashi/shizen/midori/hyakunen/hozen.html; )

What it is: The city of Sendai's conservation page for igune, the farm windbreak groves of the Sendai plain, which links the city's own PDF list of tree species suited to one, sorted by height: tall trees of 15 m and over (sugi, black pine, the hinoki group, keyaki), sub-canopy and middle trees around 10 m, and shrubs around 3 m.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the species and the height classes of a real windbreak grove - tall trees of fifteen meters and more, a middle story around ten, shrubs around three - which is what sets the drawn height and the mix of our homestead belts. Its limits are that it is a modern city's conservation list, guidance about what to plant and keep rather than a survey of what stands; that it is one region's list - the cold, snowy, salt-windy Sendai coastal plain, where the black pine is a coastal species - standing in for a windbreak anywhere in the setting; and that the list is a PDF that does not render as text for every reader who follows the link.

Used for: the igune species list behind the windbreak belt's composition (homesteads.html, the windbreak height section's sources).

minami-2022-igune

Minami, Yonezawa and Okaze 2022, the Osaki igune flow-field study, J-STAGE (https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/jass/38/2/38_37/_article/-char/en) - : the abstract places the 10 m igune "on the prevailing wind direction side" and names no compass side

What it is: Kento Minami, Chinatsu Yonezawa and Tsubasa Okaze, a 2022 paper in the Journal of the Japanese Agricultural Systems Society (open access on J-Stage), which models the wind around one dispersed farmstead's igune - its homestead grove - in Osaki, Miyagi: a computer simulation of the flow field over a digital model of the site built from drone imagery, with the trees represented as a canopy. The paper describes the grove as standing on the west and north sides of the site, against the west-northwest winter wind, with tall cedar and dense bamboo on the west.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives a measured-looking picture of what a homestead belt does to the wind and, in its description of the site, which sides the belt stands on, which is what our belts' height and siting rest on. Its limits are that it is a computer simulation of the flow rather than a measurement, with only the geometry taken from the site; that it is one modeled house, so "west and north" is that farmstead's arrangement against that site's wind and not a general rule; and that the belt modeled is a surviving modern one, which the paper itself says has been in decline since the 1970s.

Used for: the igune as a 10 m windbreak on the wind side of a dispersed farmstead.

kanto-gakuin-tsuijimatsu

Kaneko Tomoya, 「Tsuijimatsu (築地松): environmental design rooted in the climate and land of Shimane」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「築地松：島根の気候風土に根ざした環境デザイン」), Kanto Gakuin University column (https://kyousei.kanto-gakuin.ac.jp/column/kaneko-tomoya/20140911-1906/; a search snippet of it carries the claim: black pines on the north and west sides of the house against the northwest winter wind)

Used for: Izumo tsuijimatsu standing on the north and west sides of the house.

isa-auf-fukugi-2011

Chen and Nakama, on the Fukugi homestead windbreaks of Okinawa, Arboriculture & Urban Forestry 37(1) (https://auf.isa-arbor.com/content/37/1/19) - "House-embracing Fukugi trees were functionally laid out with denser forests on the northern side of the houses and on the sides facing the sea"; "the greatest number of large Fukugi trees stand to the north of the house, followed by the eastern side"

What it is: A peer-reviewed article by Chen and Nakama on the Fukugi homestead windbreaks of Okinawa, in Arboriculture & Urban Forestry volume 37 issue 1 (2011). The authors surveyed the old Fukugi trees still standing around houses in several villages, recording their heights and which side of the house each one stands on.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the shape and orientation of a homestead windbreak - a line of trees set around the house, thickest on the sides the damaging wind comes from, most of the large trees to the north and then to the east - which is how the shelter belts around our farmsteads are drawn. Its limits: it measures trees that were planted long before the survey, so it records a surviving form rather than a documented historical practice; and Okinawa is subtropical and typhoon-swept, standing in here for the main islands of Japan, so the species and the exact wind sides are local while the principle we carry over is shelter on the weather side.

Used for: the homestead windbreak as a line of trees on the house's wind sides (Okinawa).

ido-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 井戸 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/井戸) - the shaft's lining: 「If the ground is firm a bare dug shaft will do, but where it is liable to collapse a well lining (idogawa, 井戸側) becomes necessary.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「地盤が堅固であれば素掘りでもよいが、崩れやすい場合には井戸側が必要となる」)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 井戸 (ido, "well"), covering how wells in Japan are dug, lined and owned.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the construction of the shaft that the well glyph in a yard stands for - firm ground will hold a bare dug shaft, while ground liable to collapse needs a lining (idogawa) - and for the related point that wells were often held jointly rather than by one household, because digging one was expensive. Its limits: a tertiary summary, taken only for plain description of the thing itself; it describes Japanese wells in general, modern ones included, and dates nothing it says, so it does not establish that the lined shaft or the shared well is the Edo-period form rather than a general Japanese one; it says nothing about where in a yard a well is placed, so that decision on our maps rests on other sources; and it is in Japanese, quoted here in our own translation.

Used for: the well's construction (the lined shaft);

hasa-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 稲架 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/稲架) - 「They are most often built in the paddies and dry fields, mainly after the harvest.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「主に収穫後の田畑に作られることが多い」); the regional names (稲掛け, 稲機, はさ/はざ/はせ/はぜ/はで)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the 稲架 (hasa), the wooden rack of poles and crossbars on which cut rice is hung head-down to dry after the harvest. It describes where the racks are put up, notes that some districts leave a rack standing in place rather than building one each year, and lists the many regional names for it (稲掛け, 稲機, はさ, はざ, はせ, はで and others).

Why it applies, and its limits: Drying racks are one of the few farm fixtures that change the look of a settlement's fields for a few weeks a year, and this is a plain description of where they usually stand - mainly in the paddies and dry fields after the harvest - which is what we needed in order to place them. The article says "mainly", not "only": it supports the field as the usual place and is silent about the farmyard, so keeping racks out of our yards is our reading, not its rule. It is a tertiary encyclopedia summary, and it describes Japanese practice as recorded in the modern era; hand-harvested rice was dried this way for centuries, but the article does not date any of its statements, so we take from it only the general placement and not any figure.

Used for: the rice-drying rack as a post-harvest fixture of the fields - the record's "or the yard" is not on the page.

nakasendoway-dosojin

Nakasendo Way, "Dosojin" (https://www.nakasendoway.com/dosojin/) - dosojin "are closely associated with roads, travelers and local boundaries", "found most often in the more isolated parts of the highway ... and near the mountain passes"

What it is: "Dosojin", a page on Nakasendo Way, an English-language guide to the history and culture of the Nakasendo highway published by Walk Japan Ltd., a walking-tour company. It describes dosojin - the small carved stone guardians of roads, travelers and local boundaries, often a paired couple - and where they are found, most often on the lonelier stretches of the highway and near the mountain passes, along with the related Jizo and the horse-headed Kannon, the guardian of horses and travelers.

Why it applies, and its limits: It settles the one thing we need, which is where such a stone stands: on the road and at a boundary rather than inside a farmyard. It is a tour operator's cultural guide written for walkers, not a scholarly work, and it describes one highway in particular, so we use it for placement and for what the stones are, never for a count or a measurement.

Used for: dosojin standing on the road and at boundaries, not inside the farmstead.

jaanus-yashikimon

JAANUS (Japanese Architecture and Art Net Users System), "yashikimon" 屋敷門 (http://www.aisf.or.jp/~jaanus/deta/y/yashikimon.htm) - "The front gate of a warrior's manor house"; the illustrated example is the former Tōjin yashiki gate at Kōfukuji, Nagasaki, 1740.

What it is: The entry for yashikimon 屋敷門 in JAANUS, the Japanese Architecture and Art Net Users System, an online dictionary of Japanese art and architectural terms. It defines the word as the front gate of a warrior's manor house and illustrates it with the former Tojin yashiki gate of 1740, now at Kofukuji in Nagasaki.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for vocabulary - what the formal front gate of a walled residence is called and what one looks like - when naming and drawing the gates on our compound plans. Its limits: it is a specialist dictionary, which is to say a compact summary rather than a survey, and its illustrated example is a single eighteenth-century gate; and the term as JAANUS defines it belongs to a warrior's residence, so nothing about a farmstead's gate or about what such a gate meant for a farming household's standing can be sourced to it.

Used for: the gate vocabulary (yashikimon) in the outbuildings pass; .

bambubatu-madake

Bambu Batu, Phyllostachys bambusoides (https://bambubatu.com/phyllostachys-bambusoides-japanese-timber-bamboo/) - "Cold hardiness: Approximately -5 to -10°F (-20 to -23°C)"

What it is: A page on madake from Bambu Batu, an educational bamboo blog carrying affiliate links for tools and bedding, giving the species' cold tolerance in a quick-facts box - about -5 to -10 F, -20 to -23 C - and a line on frost damage to the culms.

Why it applies, and its limits: A plant's cold tolerance is a constant of the species, so a present-day figure for the temperature at which madake is damaged bears on a premodern grove, and this page sits at the tolerant end of the spread the record keeps open. Its limits are that the figure is an unsourced quick-facts entry on a blog, with no method behind it, not a measurement; and that it describes cultivated plantings today, chiefly outside East Asia.

Used for: madake hardiness (one of the three grower pages behind bamboo-growers-hardiness).

practicalplants-madake

Practical Plants, Phyllostachys bambusoides (https://practicalplants.org/wiki/phyllostachys_bambusoides/) - "tolerating temperatures down to about -18°c, but it dislikes prolonged exposure to hard frosts"

Used for: madake hardiness (the second of the three grower pages behind bamboo-growers-hardiness).

ridgelineimages-gsi

Ridgeline Images, "Reading GSI Topographic Maps" (https://ridgelineimages.com/maps/reading-gsi-topographic-maps/) - the bamboo-forest symbol described as its own category beside broad-leaved forest, coniferous forest and the bamboo-grass (sasa) zone

What it is: "Reading GSI Topographic Maps", a guide on Ridgeline Images, a website about hiking and mapping in Japan, which walks through the legend of the topographic map series published by the Geospatial Information Authority of Japan. It shows the bamboo grove carrying its own symbol, listed separately from broad-leaved forest, from coniferous forest, and from the dwarf bamboo (sasa) zone of the high ridges.

Why it applies, and its limits: It shows that Japanese topographic maps give bamboo its own symbol beside broad-leaved forest, conifers and bamboo grass, which is the convention our maps borrow when they draw a grove differently from a wood. Its limits are that it is a hiker's blog explaining the legend rather than the agency's own legend document, though that document is public and linked from the page and is the closer source; and that it describes the present-day map series, so it is evidence for how Japanese maps show bamboo now and not for any older practice - a map drawing convention, not a physical claim.

Used for: the bamboo stand drawn as its own class of ground, distinct from woodland and from dwarf bamboo.

mlit-senjochi-kurashi

MLIT land-classification teaching material, 「Alluvial fans and the lives of the inhabitants」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「扇状地と人々の暮らし」) (https://nlftp.mlit.go.jp/kokjo/inspect/landclassification/land/teaching_material_1.pdf)

What it is: A teaching document from the land-classification survey material of Japan's Ministry of Land, Infrastructure, Transport and Tourism, "Alluvial fans and the lives of the inhabitants" (original: "扇状地と人々の暮らし"), explaining how an alluvial fan is built and how settlement and cultivation arrange themselves across it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It states the landform pattern our foothill maps follow - villages and paddies at the toe of the fan where water can be had, the dry middle of the fan given to crops that need little water, mulberry among them - in a ministry's own teaching material. Its limits are that it is modern educational material stating a general pattern rather than a study of any historical village; that the deck says water "can be obtained" at the toe and never speaks of springs, and says nothing about antiquity, so "the spring line" and "since ancient times" are the record's gloss and not the source's; and that its second half is about debris-flow hazard on fans, so the fan-life material is a few slides of a broader deck.

Used for: the fan-toe spring line as the place villages and paddies sit on an alluvial fan.

woods-westbrook-2006

Woods, Westbrook et al., "Hydrologic interactions between an alluvial fan and a slope wetland in the central Rocky Mountains, USA", Wetlands 2006 (https://www.cheriewestbrook.ca/publications/16523-hydrologic-interactions-between-an-alluvial-fan-and-a-slope-wetland-in-the-central-rocky-mountains-usa) - "Continuing ground-water discharge into the upper part of the wetland limited the water level declines"; stream seepage "is the primary source of ground-water recharge for the alluvial fan"

What it is: A peer-reviewed hydrology paper by Woods, Westbrook and colleagues, "Hydrologic interactions between an alluvial fan and a slope wetland in the central Rocky Mountains, USA", published in the journal Wetlands in 2006, which traces where the water in a wetland at the foot of an alluvial fan actually comes from.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the mechanism behind a marsh drawn at the edge of a fan - groundwater discharging out of the fan keeps the wetland wet, and seepage from the stream is what recharges that groundwater - so the chain from stream to fan to marsh holds together on the map instead of each piece being placed by eye. Its limits are place and era: it measures one site in the Rocky Mountains in the 2000s, not an East Asian one, so we cite it for the physics, which does not change by country, and never for a measurement of a particular place. Only the abstract and the summary sections are on a public page, the full paper being behind a subscription.

Used for: slope wetlands at a fan's perimeter fed by the fan's groundwater - which the stream recharges; the chain is kept whole.

tedori-fan-pwe

"Analysis of water balance in the Tedori river alluvial fan areas of Japan", Paddy and Water Environment (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10333-013-0373-3; the search summary carries the finding: irrigation water, chiefly paddy, is the main input to the fan's groundwater, and a paddy area smaller by more than 30% could not maintain the water table)

Used for: paddy-field seepage as a major recharge source of fan groundwater.

minka-en-yashikigami

minka-en.com, 屋敷神 (https://minka-en.com/shisetsu/shisetsu_7/) - 「In one corner of the homestead, and especially at the kimon (鬼門, the northeast corner), there is a small shrine (hokora, 祠) of stone or of wood or with a straw-thatched roof, and ...」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「屋敷の一隅、特に鬼門（きもん）（北東隅）に、石や木、わら屋根の祠（ほこら）があり」)

What it is: The official site of the Fukushima City Minka-en, a municipal open-air museum of relocated folk houses; the page is the museum's own guide entry for its yashikigami exhibit, describing the household shrine as it was kept - a small hokora of stone, wood or thatch at a corner of the plot, the northeast corner especially.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the form and the customary corner of the household shrine in a museum's own words, which is what the shrine glyph on our homesteads and its corner placement are drawn from. Its limits are that it is a museum caption about its own exhibited example, not a survey, with no frequency; and that the museum is in Fukushima, so it speaks for Tohoku practice - one more northern source on a page that leans on them.

Used for: the household shrine in a corner of the yard, especially the NE (鬼門) corner, as a small stone or wooden shrine.

satologue-yashikigami

satologue.com, 屋敷神様 (https://satologue.com/living/758) - 「In the small shrine (hokora) standing in a corner of the plot there dwells the homestead deity (yashikigami-sama).」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「敷地の一角に佇む小さな祠には、屋敷神様がいます」)

What it is: A page on satologue.com, a present-day Japanese site that writes about country living and rural custom, describing the yashikigami, the deity of a farm household's own ground, and the small hokora shrine that stands in a corner of the plot to house it.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for two plain facts of a custom that is still visible on farms today and that we draw on every homestead: the shrine is tiny, and it sits at a corner of the grounds rather than at the center. It is a lifestyle site and not a survey or a historical document, so it is good for what the thing looks like and not for how common it was or how old the practice is, and because the page names no compass direction, which corner we put it in is our own choice.

Used for: the shrine's small size and its corner-of-the-grounds placement.

shimogoe-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 下肥 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/下肥) - 「Fresh night soil is harmful to crops, so when it is used as fertilizer it is stored for a set period and left to rot down and mature.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「新鮮な屎尿は作物にとって有害であり、肥料として用いる際には、一定期間貯蔵して腐熟させる」); the storage vessel is the 肥溜め

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on 下肥 (shimogoe), human night soil used as fertilizer, which explains that fresh night soil harms crops and so is stored for a set period to rot down and mature, in a pit called the 肥溜め.

Why it applies, and its limits: It answers why a farmstead has a manure pit at all and why the stuff sits there instead of going straight onto the fields, which is what justifies drawing a separate pit on the plot rather than treating it as part of the privy. It is a tertiary summary whose statements we take where its own references support them, and it gives no storage times or pit dimensions, so the size we draw the pit at is our own decision.

Used for: night soil stored to ripen in the manure pit and used on the fields.

woodland-edge-enwiki

en.wikipedia "Woodland edge" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodland_edge) - describes the edge generically ("trees are often different from those inside the wood, as well as hedge vegetation, brambles and low-growing plants") and does NOT carry the herb-fringe / shrub-belt / forest-mantle layers

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Woodland edge", a short tertiary encyclopedia entry describing in general terms how the trees and plants at the margin of a wood differ from those growing inside it.

Why it applies, and its limits: This one is cited for an absence rather than for a figure. The layered vocabulary we use for the edge of a wood - herb fringe, shrub belt, mantle - comes from the German-language article on the same subject, and the English page is named so that a reader following the obvious English link is not left thinking those three layers were drawn from it.

Used for: the negative the record states: the English page does not carry the three-layer terms; waldrand-dewiki does.

shizen-teibo-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 自然堤防 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/自然堤防) - 「On alluvial lowland, long-established settlements are sited first of all on the natural levees, which are also used as dry fields.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「沖積低地において、古くからの集落はまず自然堤防上に立地し、また畑として利用される」), against the 後背湿地 behind it

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 自然堤防 ("natural levee"), on the low ridge of coarser sediment a river builds along its own banks when it floods, and on the wetter backswamp (後背湿地) that lies behind it on an alluvial plain.

Why it applies, and its limits: This is the single most useful fact for laying out a lowland village: houses and dry fields go up on the levee, which is the only naturally drained ground for miles, and the paddy goes into the backswamp behind - a rule the article states directly for long-established Japanese settlements. It is an encyclopedia summary of settled physical geography rather than a survey, so we take the siting principle from it and not any measurement; and it describes Japanese alluvial lowland, so it applies to our river-plain settlements and says nothing about hill or fan country.

Used for: settlement and dry fields on the natural levee, paddy in the backswamp.

lianou-zhwiki

zh.wikipedia 莲藕 (https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/莲藕) - cultivation 「It is cultivated mainly in acid marshland or in paddy fields (lotus fields).」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「主要在酸沼地或水田（莲田）栽培」); no acreage, mu or percentage figure for the share of land under lotus

Used for: the negative the record states: the lotus area share was found nowhere (archetypes.html).

izumo-taisha-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 出雲大社 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/出雲大社) - 「Its height is 8 jo (about 24 meters), a size out of all proportion for a shrine.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「高さは8丈（およそ24メートル）と、神社としては破格の大きさである」); no plan dimension such as 二間四方

Used for: the 24 m height of the main hall, and the absence of a floor dimension (buildings.html).

taisha-zukuri-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 大社造 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/大社造) - the 1744 Izumo hall 「it is enormous, at about 24 m」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「約24mと巨大である」); no plan dimension

Used for: the same negative - height only, no floor dimension (buildings.html).

shixue-yuekan-louzeyuan

陈朝云 / 张晓芊, 「A study of Song-dynasty louzeyuan (漏泽园, the pauper burial grounds) and of social relief」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「宋代漏泽园及社会救助研究」), 史学月刊 (Henan University), the journal's own listing page (https://sxyk.henu.edu.cn/info/1014/7333.htm) - 「The name "louzeyuan" is first seen in the third year of the Chongning era of Emperor Huizong of Song (1104).」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「"漏泽园"名称始见于宋徽宗崇宁三年（1104年）」)

Used for: the 1104 date of the louzeyuan public burial grounds (cities/capitals.html).

ifc-307-bonfire

International Fire Code section 307.4, open burning - read in a state adoption (New York State Fire Code 2010, 307.4.1) at UpCodes (https://up.codes/s/open-burning-and-recreational-fires) - "A bonfire shall not be conducted within 50 feet (15 240 mm) of a structure or combustible material unless the fire is contained in a barbecue pit"

What it is: Section 307.4 of the International Fire Code, the model fire-safety code published by the International Code Council and adopted, with local amendments, across much of the United States. We read it in one such adoption - the 2010 New York State Fire Code, section 307.4.1 - reproduced on the code-hosting site UpCodes, the publisher's own site being closed to us; the clause forbids a bonfire within fifty feet of a structure or combustible material unless the fire is contained in a pit.

Why it applies, and its limits: It supplies one number, the working clearance a modern regulator requires around a large open fire, from which the cleared ground around a capital's pyre is derived. Its limits: it is a present-day American code, not a historical or East Asian source, and it governs a recreational bonfire rather than a funeral pyre; the fifty feet is a regulator's safety margin keyed to a code-defined fire, not a measurement of anything; and the record's own finding is that Edo's crematoria were moved for their smell rather than their sparks and that the real premodern practice was containment - a stone hearth inside a walled ground - so this distance stands in for a containment practice the record documents, as a reasoned choice on our part and not as evidence about any real settlement.

Used for: the 50 ft bonfire setback the pyre-ground clearance is derived from (cities/capitals.html).

ushijima-2020-manchu

Ushijima, Akira; Yang, Dan; Nakazono, Mahito; and Mishima, Sachiko, "Spatial composition and premise arrangement of traditional Manchu village in Northeast China", Japan Architectural Review 3(3) (2020) 346-358 (https://doi.org/10.1002/2475-8876.12146)

What it is: A measured field survey of one Manchu village in Liaoning, by an architectural team that worked there between 2013 and 2017: sixteen days of survey and interviews, nineteen buildings and twenty houses drawn, and all 296 housing lots in the village classified by the street they front, where their gate stands, what they measure and how their outbuildings sit. It is the authorized English of a paper first published in Japanese by the Architectural Institute of Japan.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is honest evidence that a nucleated farm village in East Asia lays deep walled lots in rows along an east-west street with subordinate lanes crossing it, and honest evidence of what such lots measure - about one to two, mostly 30 m or less wide by 50 m or less deep, commonly 300 to 500 square metres, with the working yard in front of the house rarely deeper than 20 m. What it cannot be used for is anything that depends on the village not having been rebuilt: its own central finding is that the plan was remade by a Chinese state resettlement in about 1968, which is when the ground behind the lots was opened and given streets. Everything it reports about the older form comes at second hand from two mid-twentieth-century works. The building culture is Manchu and Han northeastern, shaped by the kang and by a cold-climate demand for south-facing rooms, and the authors note the Manchu form had become hard to tell from the Han by 1933. It is not evidence about Japan and not directly evidence about the premodern period.

Used for: the hierarchy of ways in a nucleated village and the shape and size of a housing lot

tian-dike-pond

Tian, M., "Seeing from Above: Observation of Contemporary Dike-Pond Landscape", Landscape Architecture Frontiers 7(4) (2019) 130-138 (https://doi.org/10.15302/J-LAF-1-050004)

What it is: A short bilingual essay in a Chinese landscape-architecture journal, looking at the Pearl River Delta’s dike-pond country from the air and arguing that its character is being lost. It is written in Chinese and English on facing text, the journal supplying the English and the author being one of its two translators.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is where the description of the delta as a mosaic of built ponds threaded by natural rivers actually comes from - a phrase this record had been carrying for some time attributed to a different paper, which does not contain it. Two limits matter. It describes the landscape as it IS, and its argument is that the mosaic is going: from the 1960s a state improvement program squared the irregular ponds into rectangles, so the mosaic it praises is read off 1960s aerial imagery rather than surveyed in a premodern century. And it is an essay rather than a study - it measures nothing, counts nothing and cites no survey, so it can carry the FORM of the landscape and must not be asked for a number.

Used for: the Pearl delta read as a mosaic of constructed ponds among meandering natural rivers

terauke-seido-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 寺請制度 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/寺請制度) - 「Of the 6,000 Jodo-sect temples in existence at the time of Genroku 9 (1696), those founded from the sixteenth century onward accounted for nine tenths.」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「元禄9年（1696年）当時存在した6000か所の浄土宗寺院のうち、16世紀以降の創建が9割を占めていた」); no all-sect total of Edo temples on the page

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article 寺請制度 (terauke seido), on the Tokugawa requirement that every household register with a Buddhist temple, and on the growth in temple numbers that followed it.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for a single figure that tells us how recent a dense temple landscape actually is - six thousand Jodo-sect temples in existence in 1696, nine tenths of them founded from the sixteenth century onward - which is the check on how many precincts a settlement of a given age should carry. It is a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, the count covers one sect rather than all of them, and the page carries no all-sect total, so we do not put one on a map.

Used for: the 6,000 Jōdo temples of 1696, and the absence of a total (religion-and-death.html).

nogyo-zensho-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 農業全書 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/農業全書) - 「an agricultural manual published in Genroku 10 (1697)」 (translated from the Japanese by this project; original: 「元禄10年（1697年）刊行された農書」). , so the record's "the readable manual saying it" about night soil is not supported by this page (it may be true of the book, not stated here)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the Nogyo Zensho (農業全書), the farming manual published in 1697, the tenth year of Genroku - the standard general treatise on Japanese agriculture of its day.

Why it applies, and its limits: It identifies and dates the manual named in our note on why night soil was aged before it went on a field, and a period farming manual is the right kind of authority for what a farmer was told to do. The limit is exact: this encyclopedia page describes the book and gives its year but says nothing about night soil or manuring, so it supports the manual's identity and date and not the practice - the passages about letting dung rot to ripeness before use are quoted on the encyclopedia's separate page for night soil.

Used for: the farming manual named in the night-soil withdrawal note (homesteads.html); the year and the night-soil attribution are flagged for the GM.

kaifeng-enwiki

en.wikipedia Kaifeng (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaifeng) - "Five major floods devastated the city between 1375 and 1416, and flooding occurred more than 300 times from the Southern Song Dynasty to the late Qing dynasty"

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Kaifeng, the Chinese city on the Yellow River that served as an imperial capital and has been repeatedly buried and rebuilt by floods. The passage we use counts the disasters: five major floods between 1375 and 1416, and more than three hundred flood events from the Southern Song to the late Qing.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the shape of Kaifeng's history under the Yellow River - the repeated floods that buried the city layer on layer - which is the extreme case a river-city map is measured against. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary, and the flood count we quote from it carries no citation on the page, so it is an uncited encyclopedia statement, with the peer-reviewed frequency in the peer-reviewed flood study cited beside it the better anchor; that Kaifeng under a perched, silt-choked river is the worst case of river-city danger and not the average; and that it is the Chinese half of a setting also modeled on Japan.

Used for: Kaifeng's flood count (cities/river-cities.html).

fao-ac180e-rice-fish

FAO, Fish Culture in Rice Fields, chapter 7 "Culture Operations" (https://www.fao.org/4/ac180e/AC180E07.htm) - "the height of the dry bund should be at least 25 cm, but a height of 60 cm is recommended". , and the basin-irrigation chapter (fao.org/4/s8684e/s8684e03.htm) speaks of "permanent bunds" of 60-90 cm and never of a "dry bund"

What it is: A chapter of Fish Culture in Rice Fields, a technical manual published by the UN Food and Agriculture Organization on raising fish in flooded rice paddies. The "Culture Operations" chapter covers how such a field is prepared and managed, including the earth bund that holds the water in, which it says should stand at least 25 cm high and better still 60 cm.

Why it applies, and its limits: A paddy bund is a wall of piled mud whose height is set by how deep the water must stand, which has nothing to do with modern machinery, so the figure carries back to a premodern field. The honest limits are that this is a present-day extension manual rather than a historical record - the same chapter assumes modern fertilizer, pesticides and bred varieties; that it was written for tropical fish-farming projects in Africa and Southeast Asia rather than for a Chinese or Japanese paddy; and that a bund built to keep fish in is taller than one built only to keep water in, so we treat its 60 cm as the upper end of the range a paddy bund occupies rather than as a measured premodern height.

Used for: the permanent dry bund and its height, as quoted in the bund entry (homesteads.html); the chapter's name is corrected here and flagged for the GM.

minka-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 民家 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/民家) - a general page on commoner dwellings; no roof pitch and no ridge height on it

Used for: the negative the record states beside kayabuki-jawiki: neither the 45-degree pitch nor the 6-7 m ridge is on the pages read (homesteads.html).

fengshui-woodland-enwiki

en.wikipedia Fengshui woodland (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fengshui_woodland) - the concept ("A Fengshui woodland, Fungshui woodland, or Fengshui forest, known in Chinese as 風水林"), regional totals and the 57-village survey mentioned; NO per-village grove count and NO grove size on the page - the "~2 groves per village, 1-2 ha" the record states beside it is not this article's (the survey papers are)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article "Fengshui woodland", a short encyclopedia entry on the patches of woodland kept behind and around villages in southern China and Hong Kong for geomantic reasons, giving the Chinese term 風水林 and some regional totals.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one thing only - that the village grove is a recognized, named class of woodland rather than a decoration we invented. It is a tertiary summary whose figures we take only where its own references support them, and it carries no count of groves per village and no grove area at all, so the sizes and numbers on our maps come from the survey papers and, where those run out, are marked as guesses.

Used for: the fengshui forest as a named village-woodland class (vegetation.html); not for its count or size.

hu-2011-fengshui-patches

Hu, Li, Guo et al., "Values of village fengshui forest patches in biodiversity conservation in the Pearl River Delta, China", Biological Conservation 144 (2011) (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0006320711000450; the full text is paywalled: doi 10.1016/j.biocon.2011.01.023; unsupported by any readable page until then) - the ABSTRACT is public on that page and was read - 3403 plants/ha, 49.1 m2/ha, 32 well-protected patches, one 1200 m² transect each

What it is: A peer-reviewed article by Hu, Li, Guo and colleagues, "Values of village fengshui forest patches in biodiversity conservation in the Pearl River Delta, China", in Biological Conservation volume 144 (2011). The authors surveyed thirty-two well-protected village fengshui forest patches, sampling one 1,200 square meter transect in each, and report the species richness, stem density and basal area they found.

Why it applies, and its limits: A fengshui forest is the protected wood a village keeps at its back, which is exactly the feature our homestead and village groves are modeled on, and this is a direct measurement of how thickly such a grove actually stands (about 3,400 stems and 49.1 square meters of basal area per hectare, which the authors themselves put on a level with well developed evergreen broadleaf forest). Its limits: only the abstract is on a public page, so we take nothing beyond the figures the abstract states; the survey is of patches that survive in one region of southern China in the twenty-first century, and choosing the well-protected ones means these are the healthiest examples rather than an average grove; and southern China is not Japan.

Used for: the stem-density and basal-area figures of intact fengshui groves (abstract); the record's 'closed canopy' gloss was replaced by the authors' own comparison to well-developed evergreen broadleaved forests

afcd-ncsc-9-06

Agriculture, Fisheries and Conservation Department (Hong Kong), "Fung Shui Woods in Hong Kong", Advisory Council on the Environment, Nature Conservation Subcommittee paper NCSC 9/06, December 2006 - with an Annex listing all 115 surveyed woods by species richness and GIS-measured area in m² (https://www.epd.gov.hk/epd/sites/default/files/epd/english/boards/advisory_council/files/ncsc-paper-06-09.pdf)

What it is: A December 2006 paper by Hong Kong's Agriculture, Fisheries and Conservation Department to the Nature Conservation Subcommittee of the Advisory Council on the Environment, reporting a 2002 to 2004 survey of 115 fung shui woods - the native forest patches behind villages that villagers preserved, with fruit trees and useful plants at their edges - each wood's boundary drawn on a survey map and its area computed by GIS, listed in an annex.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the one large measured set of village grove areas the record could read - the annex over which our median, mean and spread of grove sizes are computed - and so it is what sizes the groves on our maps. Its limits are that the 115 woods were chosen as the most representative sites and the paper says the survey was not exhaustive, so our figures are statistics over a selected sample that plausibly drops the smallest and most degraded woods; that the paper scopes the feature to southern China, so its standing in for the wider East Asian practice is our assumption rather than its claim, made because no comparable Japanese survey could be read; that it is a modern survey of the woods that survived; and that the boundaries were drawn by judgment where a wood merged into secondary forest, so each area carries a convention.

Used for: the one large measured area distribution of village fengshui groves - the record's arithmetic over the Annex: median 9,866 m², mean 11,026 m², 58 of 115 under 1 ha, 45 between 1 and 2 ha, 12 at 2 ha or more, range 606-53,170 m²

hk-herbarium-fsw

Hong Kong Herbarium (AFCD), "An Overview of Fung Shui Woods in Hong Kong" (https://www.herbarium.gov.hk/en/special-topics/fung-shui-woods/an-overview-of-fung-shui-woods-in-hong-kong/index.html)

What it is: "An Overview of Fung Shui Woods in Hong Kong", a page published by the Hong Kong Herbarium of the government's Agriculture, Fisheries and Conservation Department. Fung shui woods are the patches of trees traditionally kept standing behind a village for the good fortune they were held to bring, and the page summarizes a field survey begun in 2002: about 116 such woods, averaging a hectare, ranging from 600 square meters at the smallest to 6 hectares at the largest, with 80% of them lying below 100 m elevation.

Why it applies, and its limits: The village grove is a feature we draw on nearly every map, and this gives the one thing hardest to guess - how big it actually is, and how much the sizes vary. We use a Hong Kong survey because no comparable measured survey of Japanese homestead or village groves could be read on a public page; it is southern China rather than Japan, and subtropical, so the tree species differ, and that the siting custom is the same is our assumption rather than the page's finding. It is a modern survey of the woods that have survived to be counted, not of what once stood; and the page rounds its figures and cites no study of its own, so where it disagrees with the underlying 2006 paper we follow the paper.

Used for: the survey summary - 116 woods, average 1 ha, 600 m² to 6 ha, 80% below 100 m; the 2006 paper's GIS table is primary where the two differ

afcd-hkbio-8

Yip, Ngar, Yip, Liu and Lai, "AFCD Survey of Local Fung Shui Woods", Hong Kong Biodiversity 8 (March 2005) 2-5 (https://www.afcd.gov.hk/english/publications/publications_con/files/hkbonewsletter8.pdf)

What it is: An article by Yip, Ngar, Yip, Liu and Lai reporting the Hong Kong government's survey of local fung shui woods, published in the Agriculture, Fisheries and Conservation Department's newsletter Hong Kong Biodiversity, issue 8 (March 2005). It describes what these village woods are, notes that they typically form a crescent behind a village, and explains how the surveyors drew each wood's boundary.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a government field survey of the actual feature we draw - the planted or protected belt of trees standing behind a village - by the people who measured it, which is why we take the crescent form and the boundary convention from it. The woods surveyed are in Hong Kong, in subtropical southern China rather than Japan, and they are the twenty-first-century remnants of a much older practice, so the form is better attested by this source than any historical extent is; the surveyors themselves note that boundaries were drawn when there were significantly fewer large trees than the wood once held.

Used for: the form - a crescent behind the village - and the boundary rule the area survey used

yuan-liu-2009

Yuan Juanwen and Liu Jinlong, "Fengshui forest management by the Buyi ethnic minority in China", Forest Ecology and Management 257(9) (2009) 2002-2009 (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0378112709000632; the full text is paywalled: DOI 10.1016/j.foreco.2009.01.040)

What it is: A peer-reviewed article by Yuan Juanwen and Liu Jinlong, "Fengshui forest management by the Buyi ethnic minority in China", published in Forest Ecology and Management in 2009, on how Buyi villages in Guizhou province hold and manage the sacred groves kept for a settlement's fengshui.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the shape of a village's sacred forest holdings in a real case - two fengshui parcels per village in the two villages studied - which is what sets how many groves a village on our maps keeps. Its limits are that it is two villages of one ethnic minority in one southwestern province, so a shape and its extremes rather than an average; that it is a count of what survived in the 2000s, after a century in which collectivization and forestry law reshaped village forests; and that the 133-hectare lineage forest the record quotes as the large tail of the range is not this paper's own measurement - it is a Han lineage's forest in Fujian, cited secondhand in the paper's introduction from another author - so the outlier comes from a different province and a different community.

Used for: two fengshui parcels per village in two Buyi villages (Guizhou); the 133 ha Letu lineage forest as the large tail

park-2013-maeulsup

Park Mee Jeong, Kim Sang Bum, Jang Choul Soon, Shin Min Ji and Kim Eun Ja, 「A study on the construction of a GIS database of traditional village groves and on the analysis of their distribution characteristics」 (translated from the Korean by this project; original: 「전통마을 숲의 GIS-DB구축 및 분포 특성 분석에 관한 연구」), 농촌계획 (Journal of Korean Society of Rural Planning) 19(1) (2013) 149-164 (https://koreascience.kr/article/JAKO201311637857073.pdf) - Korean analogue

What it is: A peer-reviewed Korean study by Park Mee Jeong, Kim Sang Bum, Jang Choul Soon, Shin Min Ji and Kim Eun Ja in the Journal of the Korean Society of Rural Planning (2013), which assembles a mapped database of 462 traditional village groves and field-surveys 100 of them, reporting each grove's area, its distance from the village and the kind of place it stands in.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the only publicly readable survey we found that actually measures the planted grove at a village's mouth, and its numbers are what our maps size that belt of trees by - most groves under a hectare, and the village-entrance grove, the sugumagi that blocks the water mouth, averaging around seven tenths of a hectare and accounting for 42 of the 100 surveyed. Two limits stated plainly: it is Korean rather than Japanese or Chinese, cited because no comparable public survey of Japanese village groves could be read, and Korea is offered as a fair analogue because it shares the region's geomantic siting tradition and its village scale; and the groves measured are the ones that survive today, so a present-day area is only an approximation of the original planting.

Used for: the 462-grove GIS census (national mean 10,375 m², 356 under 10,000 m²) and the 100-grove field survey by location type: the village-entrance grove (동구숲, the sugumagi water-mouth blocker) mean 7,149 m², 42 of 100

kweon-youn-2021

Kweon Deogkyu and Youn Yeo-Chang, "Factors influencing sustainability of traditional village groves (Maeulsoop) in Korea", Forest Policy and Economics 128 (2021) 102477 (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1389934121000836; open access) - Korean analogue

What it is: A peer-reviewed article by Kweon Deogkyu and Youn Yeo-Chang, "Factors influencing sustainability of traditional village groves (Maeulsoop) in Korea", published open access in Forest Policy and Economics in 2021. It surveys twenty-four red pine village groves around Mt. Jiri in Namwon, South Korea, reporting each grove's area and how many trees it holds along with the social conditions that keep such groves alive.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the closest thing to a measured answer we could read on a public page to the question of how big a village's planted grove actually is and how many trees stand in it, so it sets the size band our groves are drawn to. Two honest limits: it is Korean, standing in for Japan and China because no comparable open survey of Japanese or Chinese village groves could be read, though village grove planting is a shared regional practice; and it measures groves as they survive today, which records what has lasted rather than what was originally planted.

Used for: 24 red-pine village groves around Mt. Jiri: mean 2,197 m², range 250-6,330 m²; "less than 10 to more than 100" trees per grove

ma-2017-fengshui-carbon

Ma Lei, Shen Chunyu, Lou Duo, Fu Shenglei and Guan Dongsheng, "Patterns of ecosystem carbon density in edge-affected fengshui forests", Ecological Engineering 107 (2017) (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0925857417304548; the full text is paywalled: DOI 10.1016/j.ecoleng.2017.07.037)

What it is: A peer-reviewed article by Ma Lei, Shen Chunyu, Lou Duo, Fu Shenglei and Guan Dongsheng, "Patterns of ecosystem carbon density in edge-affected fengshui forests", published in Ecological Engineering in 2017. It measures forty-five plots of 40 by 40 m across five surviving fengshui forest fragments around Guangzhou in southern China, reporting among other things how much tree trunk area each hectare of grove carries.

Why it applies, and its limits: The basal area it reports is our best readable measure of how densely a village's protective grove actually stands, which is what decides whether a grove is drawn as scattered trees or as a solid canopy. Its limits are real: the full text is behind a paywall, so we use only the abstract and the publicly visible passages; it measures subtropical Guangdong fragments as they survive today, so it is a modern measurement of the remains of an old practice, and a southern Chinese figure applied to a setting that leans Japanese.

Used for: basal area 30.0-52.5 m²/ha in five Guangzhou fengshui fragments (45 plots of 40 x 40 m)

zhongshan-fsw-2016
