孙红梅、张冬冬、修小娟 (Sun Hongmei and Xiu Xiaojuan of the Guangdong Zhongshan State-owned Forest Resource Protection Center, 广东省中山市国有森林资源保护中心; Zhang Dongdong, the corresponding author, of the Fuxin City Forestry Bureau, Liaoning, 辽宁省阜新市林业局), 「The medicinal plant resources of the fengshui groves of Zhongshan City」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「中山市风水林的药用植物资源」), 热带生物学报 (Journal of Tropical Biology) 7(3) (2016) 368-372 (https://rdswxb.hainanu.edu.cn/cn/article/doi/10.15886/j.cnki.rdswxb.2016.03.016; DOI 10.15886/j.cnki.rdswxb.2016.03.016)

What it is: A peer-reviewed survey by Sun Hongmei, Zhang Dongdong and Xiu Xiaojuan, "The medicinal plant resources of the fengshui groves of Zhongshan City" (中山市风水林的药用植物资源), published in 热带生物学报 (Journal of Tropical Biology) in 2016; the authors work for the state forest resource protection center of Zhongshan in Guangdong and for the forestry bureau of Fuxin in Liaoning, and the fieldwork behind it covers that one municipality's fengshui groves.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it not for its botany but for the count it had to make first: 66 groves in 66 natural villages totaling 269.85 hectares, which works out at about four hectares a village and gives us a typical grove to draw against the very large outliers other studies report. Its limits are place and era - the Pearl River Delta is subtropical southern China, warmer and wetter than the setting's Japanese model, and the groves were measured as they stand now, so the figure describes the survivors of a century of clearing around them rather than what a village once held.

Used for: a Pearl River Delta municipal census of fengshui groves - 66 groves in 66 natural villages, 269.85 ha in total (about 4.1 ha each, the record's arithmetic)

huangshan-daily-jiekou

黄山日报 (Huangshan Daily), 「Huangshan's exploration of the forest-chief system」 (translated from the Chinese by this project; original: 「林长制的黄山探索」), 2022-01-01, mirrored by the Yangtze River Delta CPC news site (http://www.yrdcpcn.org.cn/c189/20240913/i238685.phtml; journalism, not scholarship) - read in the GM's browser machine translation, saved to academic-sources/ForestChiefSystem.txt - the host refuses this container and the Chinese original was not read

What it is: A newspaper article from 黄山日报 (the Huangshan Daily), "林长制的黄山探索" ("Huangshan's exploration of the forest-chief system"), dated 1 January 2022 and mirrored on a Yangtze River Delta party news site. It reports on a modern forest-stewardship program in Anhui province, and in passing describes the water-mouth grove standing at the entrance to Jiekou village in Shexian.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one concrete measure of a water-mouth grove - over a hundred mu (roughly seven hectares), nearly a thousand old trees, and fifty-six yews more than five centuries old - and the record treats that as the extreme end of the range rather than the ordinary case; a stand that has been kept at a village mouth for that long is evidence about the form even though the article around it is current news. Its limits: it is journalism rather than scholarship and it is promotional about a government program; the numbers are the reporter's, unsourced; the article is in Chinese and we read it in machine translation, so the wording is not the newspaper's own English; and it describes the largest example anyone in the region can name, so nothing in it should be read as typical.

Used for: the Jiekou (Shexian) water-mouth grove - over a hundred mu with nearly a thousand ancient trees and 56 yews over 500 years old, described as the largest in East China: the tail, not the type

meander-enwiki

Wikipedia, "Meander" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meander)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on meanders - the bends a channel makes as it winds across its own floodplain. It gives the geometry of a bend (its radius against the channel's width, its wavelength, its sinuosity) and the erosion and deposition that cut one, and it credits the radius figure to Leopold and Langbein's 1966 Scientific American article on river meanders.

Why it applies, and its limits: It carries the one number our ditches are drawn from - how tight a turn running water makes, measured against the channel's own width - together with the bank behavior that produces it. Its limits are that it is a tertiary encyclopedia article rather than the survey work behind it; that the radius it gives is the curvature a FREE channel settles into over years by cutting its own outer bank, while a farm ditch is dug and kept to a line, so its curve is set by whoever dug it and by the field boundary it runs along rather than by the water; and that it gives no figure for a maintained earthen ditch at all, so the step from a river's bend to a ditch's is this project's reasoning and not the article's.

watersa-junction-angle

Water SA, "Numerical study of junction-angle effects on flow pattern in a river confluence located in a river bend" (https://scielo.org.za/scielo.php?script=sci_arttext&pid=S1816-79502016000100006)

What it is: A 2016 paper in Water SA, the journal of South Africa's Water Research Commission, reporting a three-dimensional numerical model of a river confluence that sits inside a river bend. It runs the model across junction angles from 30 to 115 degrees and reports what each angle does to the zone of separated flow at the mouth, to the streamwise velocity near the inner wall, to the strength of the secondary spiral flow, and to the bed shear stress inside the junction.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one thing: the DIRECTION in which a junction's angle changes the way a side channel enters a larger one, which is what sets the two feet of a river-fed moat where it meets its river. Its limits are worth stating plainly - it models a confluence located in a bend, so its figures (a 59% rise in peak bed shear between 30 and 115 degrees) belong to that geometry rather than to a straight reach; it is a numerical model rather than a survey of a real waterway; it is modern hydraulics, so it describes what water does and says nothing about what a premodern builder intended or achieved; and the slack water behind the mouth and the attack on the far bank that our record reads from it are not among its findings. One limit deserves to be spelled out, because it bears on whether the direction we take from the paper carries at all: the model holds the tributary's width and its share of the discharge fixed, at the proportions of a laboratory flume, whereas the offtake that feeds a moat draws a share of its river's flow smaller by orders of magnitude - a trickle beside a current rather than one stream meeting another - so we cannot be sure even the direction of the effect survives the change of scale.

daihachiguruma-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 大八車 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/大八車)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the daihachiguruma, the large two-wheeled hand cart of Edo-period Japan - a tertiary encyclopedia entry covering what the cart was, who was allowed to use it, where it was allowed, and the smaller beka-guruma of the Kamigata region.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one thing only, the legal confinement of the freight hand cart to Edo, Owari and Sunpu, which is what makes a cart the exception rather than the norm on a country lane, and for the article's note that freight carts were not permitted on the Tokaido until the Meiji period. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary rather than a document or a study; that it speaks for Japan alone, one half of this setting; and that it is about the regulated cities and highways, so it tells us what was forbidden outside them and nothing about what an ordinary village lane looked like.

stone-routes-enwiki

en.wikipedia, Stone routes (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_routes)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the stone routes, the stone-paved ox-cart roads laid in Japan to carry goods and building materials into Kyoto - the stone is the road's surface, not its cargo - a tertiary entry that gives the road's construction: its width, the two rows of laid stones the wheels ran on and the earth track between them for the beasts.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives the one measured wheeled way we could read for premodern Japan, 2.7 m of route with a 90 cm center for the bulls, which sets the upper bound our village lanes are kept under: if a purpose-built cart road was nine feet across, an ordinary farm track was not wider. Its limits are that it is a tertiary encyclopedia article citing no survey in the passage we quote; that a paved road built to carry freight into the capital is the most exceptional kind of way there is and is not evidence of what an ordinary lane measured; and that it is Japanese, while China is this setting's leading reference.

lowtech-chinese-wheelbarrow

Low-Tech Magazine, "The Chinese wheelbarrow" (https://solar.lowtechmagazine.com/2011/12/the-chinese-wheelbarrow/)

What it is: A long illustrated article from Low-Tech Magazine, an independent magazine on pre-industrial and low-energy technology, on the Chinese one-wheeled barrow - what it carried, how it was built, and the road network that grew up around it rather than around the cart. It quotes and credits its two main authorities by name, Rudolf Hommel's 1937 fieldwork on Chinese hand tools and Joseph Needham's history of Chinese technology.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is what our whole picture of a Chinese country lane rests on - narrow paths wide enough for a walker and a barrow, cart tracks only on the eastern plains, and paths built for pedestrians, carrying-pole porters, barrow-pushers and litter-bearers - which decides the vehicle we draw a lane for. Its limits are that it is a popular magazine article rather than a peer-reviewed study, so it is a readable route to Hommel and Needham rather than a primary source itself; that it is an advocacy publication whose standing thesis is that pre-industrial transport was more capable than it is remembered to be, so it reads its evidence in that direction; that it carries no width measurement at all - the paths it describes are 「scarcely wide enough for foot passengers and wheelbarrows」, which is a description and not a figure, so the width we draw a lane at is ours and not the article's; that its observations are early twentieth-century fieldwork used as a witness to a much older practice; and that its cart-track sentence speaks for the eastern plains, not for the rice-growing south our settlements are modeled on.

itabashi-kotobank

Encyclopedia entry s.v. 板橋, aggregated at kotobank (https://kotobank.jp/word/板橋)

What it is: The dictionary and encyclopedia entry for 板橋 (itabashi) at kotobank, which aggregates the major Japanese reference works, giving the word's plain sense: a bridge made of boards.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it to fix the name of the small crossing our maps draw over a farm ditch, so that the glyph and the word a reader clicks mean the same object - a planked deck and not an earthen one. Its limits are that it is a dictionary definition and nothing more: it gives no construction, no size, no period and no picture of how such a bridge was used, so everything else our entry says about the plank comes from elsewhere.

dobashi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 土橋 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/土橋)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the dobashi, the earthen-decked bridge - a tertiary entry describing how one is built, logs laid side by side and earth thrown over and trodden down until the surface is level, and noting that in the Edo period plank bridges were limited to an important few while earthen bridges were the great majority.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the other half of the naming question: it tells us what a dobashi actually is, which is what lets our record say that the plank bridge and the earthen bridge are two different objects rather than two words for one. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary with no measurements and no dating of the technique, and that it is Japanese, so it names the Japanese form of a thing our maps place in a setting drawn from both China and Japan.

shakkanho-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 尺貫法 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/尺貫法)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the shakkanho, the traditional Japanese system of weights and measures, a tertiary entry that tabulates each unit against the metric system - among them the tsubo at 400/121 square meters, the se at thirty tsubo, and the tan at ten se.

Why it applies, and its limits: Every area a Japanese source states in tsubo, se or tan has to be turned into square meters before it can be drawn, and this is the conversion we use; the figures are exact definitions rather than measurements, so there is little for a source to be wrong about. Its limits are that it is a tertiary encyclopedia article; that the article's own values are the legally fixed modern definitions of units whose local sizes varied before they were standardized, so a premodern document's tsubo may not be exactly this tsubo; and - the one that can put a figure badly wrong rather than slightly - that the tan was redefined in the course of Hideyoshi's cadastral survey, from 360 bu to the 300 bu this page's tan assumes, so an area a pre-1590s document states in tan and we convert with this table comes out about a fifth too small. The tsubo itself is stable across that change, so an area given in tsubo is safe.

kateisaien-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 家庭菜園 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/家庭菜園)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the household kitchen garden, a tertiary entry which records that ground a Japanese household cultivates to feed itself is called yashikibatake, the homestead field. It also gives the plot a short history: its origin can be traced as far back as the Yayoi period, and by the Edo period such a plot stood inside the residences of the warrior class as well as those of farmers.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the object and its name - the bed beside the house that grows a household's own food, as distinct from the fields it farms for the tax and the market - which is what the garden on our homesteads is, and its two dates are what tell us the plot is far older than the modern gardening the rest of the article is about. Its limits are sharp and worth stating: the article gives no area and no count, so it cannot support the size band our maps hold a garden to, and that band is labeled a guess on the page; its history is two sentences rather than an account, so it fixes that the plot existed early and nothing about what it looked like; and it says such cultivation is often done on odd scraps of ground away from the residence as well, so it does not by itself put the bed against the house.

fao-farm-structures-cattle

FAO, Farm structures in tropical climates, chapter 10, Cattle housing (https://www.fao.org/4/s1250e/s1250e11.htm)

What it is: The cattle-housing chapter of Farm structures in tropical climates, a design manual published by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations for builders and extension workers in the tropics. It gives the space a beast needs - a shade structure at 2.5 to 3 square meters per animal, a free stall 2.2 m long by 1.2 m wide for a milking cow of 500 to 600 kg, a bull pen with 12 to 15 square meters of shaded rest and 20 to 30 of exercise yard.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the readable answer to a question the historical record was silent on - how much room one large draft animal takes under a roof - and it is what keeps our byre shed sized against a stated figure rather than guessed at. Its limits are three, and the first two are stated on the page beside the figure. It is a MODERN standard, written for modern stock and modern welfare expectations, and a twentieth-century dairy cow is not an Edo-period ox or a water buffalo. It is written for a different part of the world - the manual addresses builders and extension workers in tropical Africa, so nothing in it is a record of East Asian practice, and the animals it sizes for are the ones kept there. And the third is a matter of DIRECTION: these are welfare minimums, what a builder today is told to provide, not measurements of what anyone built, so treating them as a target makes the shed we draw generous rather than accurate, and the error runs one way.

tnau-cattle-housing

TNAU Agritech Portal, Animal Husbandry, housing space for cattle (https://agritech.tnau.ac.in/animal_husbandry/animhus_cb_housing_space.html)

What it is: A page of the Agritech Portal of Tamil Nadu Agricultural University, an Indian agricultural extension resource, tabulating the floor space livestock housing should allow by class of animal - for adult cattle, 3.5 square meters under cover and 7.0 square meters of open area, with a manger 60 to 75 cm.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a second modern figure for the room one adult beast needs, and it is what we check the FAO manual against: the two come out on the same order of size, which tells us neither is idiosyncratic. Its limits are the manual's and two more. The agreement is NOT independent corroboration of anything premodern - both pages are normative housing standards saying what a keeper ought to provide for modern stock, so both may be generous for the same reason, and two such standards agreeing says they share a convention rather than that an old byre measured what they recommend. The page is also unsigned and carries no references, so no figure on it can be traced back to a study. And it is a recommendation rather than a survey of what exists, written for southern Indian husbandry, so it stands in for East Asian practice on the strength of the animal rather than the place.

hinomi-yagura-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 火の見櫓 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E7%81%AB%E3%81%AE%E8%A6%8B%E6%AB%93)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the 火の見櫓 (hinomi-yagura), the fire-watch tower of Japanese towns and villages: a raised lookout platform used to spot a fire early, summon the fire brigade and sound the alarm to the neighborhood, generally carrying a hansho bell at its top. It covers how the towers developed alongside the firefighting system from Edo-period Edo, how far across the country they had spread by the early Showa era, and the town-by-town code by which the bell was rung.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the plain definition of the structure our city maps draw and of what the watch on it was for, and it is the only source in the record that dates the institution and its spread, which is what settles whether a small unwalled seat would carry one. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary rather than a study; that it describes Japan alone; that much of the article is about the surviving steel towers and the modern volunteer companies, so the premodern form has to be taken from its historical passages and not from what it shows standing today; and that its two datings do different work and must not be run together - it puts the ORIGIN of the tower in Edo-period Edo, the shogunal capital, but the SPREAD of the form across the country in the early Showa era, which is the twentieth century, so the fact that such towers became common everywhere cannot be used to put one in a small premodern settlement.

hansho-kotobank

kotobank 半鐘 (https://kotobank.jp/word/%E5%8D%8A%E9%90%98-118280)

What it is: Kotobank's entry for 半鐘 (hansho), aggregating standard Japanese reference dictionaries: the small bronze hanging bell hung on a fire-watch tower, and the code by which the number of strokes told a hearer what kind of incident had happened - for a fire, a continuous peal for a near one, fewer strokes the further off it was. The entries also date the usage: one of the dictionaries puts the hanging of the bell on a fire-watch tower from the Edo period onward, and the historical dictionary among them carries an attestation of the word from 1686.

Why it applies, and its limits: It supplies the one thing our fire-tower explanation needs beyond the tower itself: that the bell carried information by its cadence rather than simply making a noise, which is why a watchman on a tower is worth drawing, and it puts that bell on a tower within the period our settlements are pitched in rather than leaving it undated. Its limits are that it is a short tertiary dictionary entry with no citations; that its 1686 attestation is of the WORD in another of its senses, the bell of a striking clock, the earliest example it gives for the fire-alarm sense being dated 1706; and that it describes Japan only.

machibikeshi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 町火消 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E7%94%BA%E7%81%AB%E6%B6%88)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the 町火消 (machi-bikeshi), the townsman fire companies of Edo: the ordinance establishing them, issued in 1718 by the South Town Magistrate Ooka Tadasuke on the advice of the block headmen and of Ogyu Sorai; the 1720 revision into forty-seven iroha companies west of the Sumida and sixteen more for Honjo and Fukagawa, one company to about twenty blocks; and the arrangement by which the companies stood under the town magistrate's command while each block bore their cost.

Why it applies, and its limits: It tells us who actually manned, paid for and commanded an urban fire watch, which is the point our setting departs from deliberately, and it shows that the departure is narrower than it looks, since command sat with the magistrate historically as well. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary; that it is about Edo alone, the largest city in the country; and that it describes an institution of the long Tokugawa peace, so its scale and its politics do not transfer to a provincial city of the earlier, more martial period these maps are pitched in.

dongjing-menghualu-wikisource

孟元老, 東京夢華錄 卷三 (Chinese Wikisource) (https://zh.wikisource.org/wiki/%E6%9D%B1%E4%BA%AC%E5%A4%A2%E8%8F%AF%E9%8C%84/%E5%8D%B7%E4%B8%89)

What it is: The third chapter of 東京夢華錄 (Dreams of Splendor of the Eastern Capital), Meng Yuanlao's recollection of daily life in Kaifeng, the Northern Song capital, written in the twelfth century after the city had fallen, given here in the original literary Chinese on Chinese Wikisource. The passage we use describes the military patrol posts set through the wards and lanes and the brick-built 望火樓 fire-watch tower, with its lookouts above and its quartered soldiers and firefighting gear below.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is a primary account, by someone who lived in the city, of exactly the institution our city maps draw - a permanent fire-watch tower with a manned station under it - in a dense premodern East Asian capital, which is the strongest kind of evidence the record can have for it. Its limits are that it describes one imperial capital at the very top of the urban hierarchy and not a provincial seat; that it is a nostalgic memoir composed from memory after the city was lost, so its figures are recollection rather than record; and that it says nothing about smaller towns, so it cannot be read either way on what an ordinary market town kept.

omamori-enwiki

en.wikipedia, Omamori (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omamori)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on the omamori, the amulet a Japanese shrine or temple gives out - what it is, how it is made and carried, and how it is understood by the people who receive one.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the transaction rather than the object: the amulet comes from the shrine or temple itself, and what the recipient gives back is understood as a donation to it, which is why our maps show no third-party stall selling charms at a temple gate. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary; that the word the article itself uses for the exchange is "sold", so "bestowed" is our own gloss on it and not the page's; that our conclusion goes one step further than anything the page states - it never says that nobody else sold charms at a gate, and we are reading that from its silence; that the passages about factory manufacture and shop-sold versions describe MODERN commercialism, not the premodern practice our maps depict, so they cannot be read back into the setting; and that it describes Japan alone.

monzenmachi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 門前町 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E9%96%80%E5%89%8D%E7%94%BA)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the monzen-machi, the town that grows in front of a large shrine or temple: how it forms when tradesmen and craftsmen dealing with the institution and its pilgrims gather at the gate, and how its trades changed over time.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is our evidence that the approach to a great temple carries a settlement of its own, which is what puts shops, inns and lodging on a city map beside a temple rather than scattered anywhere. Its limits matter to how we read it: the article attributes the eating houses and souvenir shops a modern visitor pictures to the RAILWAY era, and names inns (hatago) as the premodern trade, so the crowded food-and-souvenir street is later than the period we draw; and it is a tertiary summary describing Japan.

qingming-enwiki

en.wikipedia, Qingming Festival (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qingming_Festival)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Qingming, the Chinese tomb-sweeping festival: the visit to the ancestors' graves, the cleaning and maintenance of the ground, and the offerings burned there - incense, joss paper, spirit money and paper replicas of goods.

Why it applies, and its limits: It gives us what actually happens at a Chinese grave, which is what makes a burial ground a tended place on our maps rather than waste ground, and it names the paper goods whose makers we put among the funeral trades. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary; that it describes the festival as practiced rather than a historical period, so it cannot date anything; and that it says nothing about where the goods were made or sold, nor how far from a mound the tending reached, which is the figure our drawn collar would need.

tudigong-enwiki

en.wikipedia, Tudigong (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudigong)

What it is: The English Wikipedia article on Tudigong, the Chinese earth god, and the small shrines kept for him - in villages, in the countryside, and inside larger temples and houses.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for the shrine's PLACE: the article shows such a shrine standing within a village's entrance gate, which is what puts the village shrine at the way in on our maps rather than at its center. Its limits are sharp: the instance it shows is a photograph caption of one walled village in Hong Kong, not a general statement; the article's own PROSE gives a different typical placement, describing such shrines as commonly located beneath the main altar of a temple or below the door of a house, so a reader of the page would come away with the household and temple shrine rather than the village gate; and nothing on it connects the earth-god shrine to the water-mouth specifically, so the join our record makes between the two is our own reading and is labeled as such.

cssn-citang-panyu

中国社会科学网, 漫谈番禺广府祠堂 (https://www.cssn.cn/ztzl/jzz/rwln/wh/whcy/202209/t20220923_5541419.shtml)

What it is: An article by 朱光文 (Zhu Guangwen), published in the magazine 人文岭南 (Renwen Lingnan) number 98 and dated 2019-11-27 where it is reposted on the website of the Chinese Academy of Social Sciences, about the Cantonese (Guangfu) ancestral halls of Panyu, in Guangdong: how such a hall is laid out on its axis, with a pond or pan-pool and an open plaza before its front gate, and what the plaza is used for.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is our evidence that a hall of this kind fronts a hard open plaza where the lineage gathers and holds its ceremonies, which is why our maps keep swept open ground before a religious building instead of packing houses to its door. Its limits are that it describes the Cantonese ancestral HALL of one southern county, not the earth-god shrine we pair it with and not northern practice; that it is a popular article on an institutional site rather than a survey; that it is written about halls that are still standing, and where it does sort them by period it is dating the BUILDINGS, while every sentence it gives about the plaza carries no period at all - so it says what such a forecourt is for and nothing about when the arrangement began or how old the one we draw would be; and that it says nothing about grain-drying, markets or opera on that ground, which our record had claimed and no longer does.

ryobosei-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 両墓制 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E4%B8%A1%E5%A2%93%E5%88%B6)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on ryobosei, the two-grave system: the burial place where the body is interred, often in mountain forest away from habitation, and the separate visiting-grave with its stone pagoda, frequently inside a temple precinct, which holds neither body nor bone.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the practice that lets our maps put a settlement's burial ground at the edge of the fields while the stone that a family visits stands at the district's temple - two places for one dead person, which is otherwise hard to credit. Its limits are stated on the page and we keep them: the custom is concentrated in the Kinki region and merely scattered elsewhere, so it is a regional form rather than a national norm, and the article is a tertiary summary.

chinju-no-mori-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 鎮守の森 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E9%8E%AE%E5%AE%88%E3%81%AE%E6%A3%AE)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the chinju no mori, the forest attached to a Shinto shrine: a wood established and maintained within the precinct and around it so that it surrounds the sanctuary, the approach and the place of worship.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is why the ground around a shrine is drawn swept but the trees around it are drawn standing: the grove is deliberately KEPT, the opposite of cleared, so our sweeping rule touches only the loose scatter and never the wood. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary with no measurements - no depth, no species count, no distance from the hall - so it gives the principle and none of the numbers, and that it describes Japan.

kofukuroman-sanmai

福井県伝承浪漫, 県内各地サンマイ写真（火葬場・土葬地）【福井県の葬送Ⅳ】 (https://kofukuroman.com/sousou4-sanmai/)

What it is: A page of 福井県伝承浪漫, one enthusiast's folklore website about Fukui prefecture, on the sanmai - the burial and cremation ground of a Fukui hamlet - with field photographs, the local names, and the observation that the sanmai stands at the edge of the settlement while the visiting-grave is at the temple within it.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is our readable witness that ordinary hamlets buried their own dead at their own fringe, which is what sets a burial ground at the edge of a settlement on our maps, and it describes the cremation site as a dug pit with no building, which is what keeps our drawn cremation ground bare. Its limits are considerable and worth naming: it is one person's compilation with photographs rather than a survey, written and published without editorial review, so nothing on it has been checked by anyone but its author; it gives no areas or distances at all; the grounds it photographs are twentieth-century survivals rather than sites dated to any premodern century, so they witness a custom that lasted into living memory rather than one we can date; and it speaks for one prefecture.

bochi-jawiki

ja.wikipedia 墓地 (https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%A2%93%E5%9C%B0)

What it is: The Japanese Wikipedia article on the graveyard as a category, which distinguishes the kinds - among them the village graveyard, held in common and managed by the inhabitants of a settlement - and notes that new graveyards of that kind are no longer permitted under present-day Japanese law.

Why it applies, and its limits: We use it for one fact: the village graveyard held in common by its own inhabitants is a recognized kind of place, which is what justifies drawing one ground for a settlement rather than scattered family plots. Its limits are that it is a tertiary summary largely concerned with modern regulation, that it gives no size, no siting rule and no period for the village form, and that the household threshold and the grandfathering our record once claimed are on it nowhere.

tanigawa-1992

Tanigawa Akio, "Excavating Edo's Cemeteries: Graves as Indicators of Status and Class", Japanese Journal of Religious Studies 19/2-3 (1992) (https://nirc.nanzan-u.ac.jp/journal/6/article/832/pdf/download)

What it is: A peer-reviewed article in the Japanese Journal of Religious Studies, freely readable in full, reporting the archaeology of Edo-period cemeteries excavated in Tokyo. It sorts the graves into categories by construction - stone chambers, wooden coffin graves, jar burials in wooden chambers, plain jar burials and coffins - and gives the major axis of each kind, from 65 cm to 2.4 m.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the only measured evidence our record has for how much ground one grave takes, which is what our burial-ground sizes are built up from. Its limits must be stated wherever it is used: the paper gives the size of a GRAVE PIT and no area per burial, no density and no cemetery area - the one large figure in it is the excavation trench, not a graveyard - so the step from a pit's width to a ground's acreage is this project's arithmetic; and it is Edo-period Japan, so it says nothing about Chinese full-body burial.

tokyohakuzen-yoyogi

東京博善, 設立前の歴史 (https://www.tokyohakuzen.co.jp/anniversary/history02/)

What it is: The institutional history page of Tokyo Hakuzen, a Japanese crematory operator, on the cremation grounds that preceded it. It quotes the Edo-period gazetteer Shinpen Musashi Fudoki-ko for the Yoyogi cremation ground: 900 tsubo in extent, with three houses of those who worked there.

Why it applies, and its limits: It is the one readable figure we have for the size of a cremation ground serving a great city, and it is what our drawn grounds are scaled against. Its limits are worth being plain about: it is a company's own history quoting a gazetteer rather than the gazetteer itself (and it misprints the title), so the figure reaches us at one remove; it is a single ground serving metropolitan Edo, the largest city in the country, so it is an upper anchor and not a typical one.
